## Prelude

Endings pile up until they become a geography of their own. They move out of the enclosure of history: history expands and intensifies until distances can no longer contain it, so geography itself changes to make room for its endings—and then prepares to receive its beginnings.

But the history made up of these endings is not the same thing as “world history.” That expression has misled many people. It has thrown them outside the paired movement of beginning and end, until they began speaking in other people’s language while imagining it was their own. In this way they carried us back to the ancient confusion of tongues, a time long since passed. What followed was an age of distinct languages: independent from one another in one sense, joined in another.

When God tried to give humanity a single language, He failed. The result was the confusion of tongues, followed by the independence and separation of languages.

And just as God failed to gather all the world’s endings beneath one banner, so any individual, anywhere and at any time, will fail if he tries to pack the world into his own bag. The world is bigger than our bags. The finite cannot contain the infinite. The one contains multiplicity, so no particular “one” can claim to be the absolute One. Parmenides laboured in vain.

The Sufis—and before them the people of the Tao—spoke of the unity of being. But they built that unity on the multiplicity of the one. It is a scattered unity. We call it unity only because everything returns to the Absolute. The Absolute is a hypothetical one from which things begin. From there they multiply and diversify, splitting outward as existence expands and pushes ever farther from unity into difference and distinction.

Anyone who tries to force all that multiplicity back into a single thing will break himself apart, with blood pouring from his mouth and nose. Such people are believers in what might be called the “unity of the existent.” In earlier times they were a type of subjective idealist: they denied the world’s actual existence and dissolved it into the Absolute.

Today they are the neighbours of the rich.

And by “the rich” I mean the West. They want the world to become one: beginning in Europe and ending in North America. They speak of the unity of the existent in defiance of the Real and at the expense of truth.

We, by contrast, join beginning to end and end to beginning precisely when we insist on our difference and seek to leave the Western concentration camp.

Our beginnings lie in our own history, whose birth is spread along our great rivers, from the Nile to the Yellow River. Our rivers are the longest and mightiest of rivers, and the richest in meaning and selfhood.

In defending that meaning and that sense of self, we expose the Eurocentric myth. We resist the outside powers—the rich and those who live beside them—and try to restore our poor to awareness.

Though in truth, the poor never lost their awareness.

We did.

We are the small minority who read translated truths and call what we read “knowledge.” We cut ourselves off from the knowledge of ordinary people and call it ignorance, as though illiteracy and ignorance were the same thing.

Ignorance can be found in the illiterate and the learned alike. Illiteracy is not another word for ignorance. Indeed, ignorance can sometimes be the companion of learning.

We embrace our history so that we can begin from it—that is, from its endings.

The Chinese communists began at the end of the Tao, where Taoism became Marxist and Marxism became Taoist. For the most part, this happened without conscious knowledge or deliberate intent.

The reason is that the Chinese do not live with a sharp divide between past and present. Their past continues inside their present. Anyone who lives among them for some time does not need to read their history in order to encounter it.

Our task is different. We have to act knowingly and deliberately, because six centuries of foreign occupation have cut us off from our history. Anyone who lives among us still has to read our history, because we do not embody it as the Chinese—and East Asians more generally—do.

A Chinese person may curse the past under the pressure of modernity or vulgar materialism, as happened during the Cultural Revolution. But even while cursing the past, he continues to live it.

Had the Chinese been able to leave their past behind as the Russians did, China today would look like Russia. Yet the gulf between them is enormous.

And this is despite the fact that the Russians never had a past comparable to China’s. Leaving it behind was easier because it was much shorter.

Our task, then, is conscious and deliberate: to return to our heritage, which means beginning from the end.

The martyred scholar followed this path. Indeed, he was the one who opened it after translated culture had kept it closed.

I follow his path. At my best, I am one beat of his living pulse, surging outward into the open field of possibility.

Sufism is one of those endings from which we can begin again.

Several paths branch out from it, and struggle brings them together. In its social form, Sufism stood against three powers: the power of the state, the power of religion, and the power of money.

We will see that this tradition came to an end in the ninth century of the Islamic calendar with Abd al-Karim al-Jili. Much of what followed was little more than dervish practice. At its best—particularly among the Persian Sufis writing in Persian—it became a romantic kind of Sufism: literature rather than thought.

Literature can stand on its own without a system of thought behind it. This is why we speak of Islamic thought, but not of “Islamic literature” in the same sense. We speak instead of Arabic literature, Persian literature, Kurdish literature, and so on.

The Sufism that came to an end was the Sufism of struggle.

We want to recover it as one source of strength in our present struggle against the powers that dominate us. What concerns me above all is its fight for the poor: against the state and its servants, religion and its clergy, money and those who own it.

The point is to begin from this Sufism of struggle and build a grassroots communism—what I call **communalism**.

This communalism formed the thread running through Sufi struggle from Ibrahim ibn Adham to Abd al-Qadir al-Jili, or al-Kilani. Its practical principle is to turn private wealth into common wealth and put it to work meeting people’s everyday needs: food, clothing, housing, and medical care. Anything left over can go to the state for its other responsibilities.

Alongside this struggle, we want to form a free human personality: free from the bonds of state and religion; shaped by communal, cooperative, and collective values; simple in its needs and not driven by pleasure; living moderately, neither in hardship nor in luxury; honest, self-respecting, and above petty behaviour.

This book as a whole is meant to strengthen the communist conscience of our younger generations. It is meant to help them move out of the enclosure of doctrine and into the open space of moral feeling, and to unsettle habits they have learned from a culture built on translation.

To be a good communist, a person needs a **communist heart**, not merely communist ideas.

Indeed, communism has little to do with abstract thought. It is not, in itself, a branch of culture. Some of the worst kinds of communist are intellectuals.

Communism—or communalism, as I prefer to call it—is a human moral stance: love of people, and hatred of domination by the state, the rich, and religious authorities.

This comes from inside a person. It does not require some additional body of knowledge.

It may, however, need to be strengthened and awakened by culture and knowledge of the right kind: a culture that has not been domesticated, that is spiritually alive rather than hardened into doctrine, and that remains faithful to the demands of human nature.

The sources of such a culture are many and varied. We find them in Chinese wisdom and Islamic Sufism, and also among a handful of exceptional Western sages—few as they are—whom we will meet in one of these orbits.

We need these people.

But we do not need them merely for their culture or their ideas. We need them as examples and as prophets: figures whose example reaches into us, fills us with moral feeling, and makes our awareness more universal as it widens our consciousness and the horizons of our thought.

This book therefore contains a short, carefully chosen list of what I call the **prophets of communalism**, beginning with Laozi and ending with the Swedish writer Ivar Lo-Johansson.

What we can learn from these people is worth as much as what we learn from all the works of writers, philosophers, and natural scientists put together.

So I would advise anyone who wants to develop a communist conscience to begin by reading writers, philosophers, and natural scientists, and then complete that education with Tao, Sufism, and the wisdom traditions.

Learn the names of the people in our list, along with the brief account I have given of each one. Let them become your prophets, guiding you through the unknown terrain of truth and human life.

In this way you can acquire the culture and knowledge expected of an intellectual, then counterbalance the damage that culture and knowledge can do with social awareness and a communist conscience rooted in ordinary people.

You do not have to agree with these prophets in every belief or opinion. As I have said, what matters is the **stance**, not the doctrine.

People can share the same moral feeling while disagreeing over doctrine. Their intellectual sources and ways of thinking may differ completely while they remain united in their love of people and their grief for human suffering.

These orbits are devoted to Islamic Sufism and directed toward that purpose.

But I have not confined myself to its social dimension. I have also allowed myself to read Sufism through its two other great dimensions: the intellectual and philosophical, and the passionate or romantic. In this way, the book can take in the phenomenon as a whole.

I have arranged this reading in orbits, and the orbits around letters.

The letters are where I begin and where I end.

In truth, the letters surround me; I do not surround them.

I move in their orbits.

They do not move in mine.

Peace to the people of truth, wherever they are and whatever language they speak.

Peace to humanity, and to the friends of humanity.

And peace to those who came before us and those still to come: God’s hidden ones and the pillars of the earth, who straighten what has gone crooked and keep the earth from corruption.

**Mid-January 1997**

**Hadi al-Alawi al-Baghdadi**
**Maarrat al-Numan**

## The Orbit of Nūn (ن)

> **Translator’s note:** Al-Alawi builds the Nūn, Mīm, and Bāʾ orbits around Arabic words that begin with those letters. Because their English equivalents do not preserve the same initials, this translation keeps the Arabic letter in the major orbit title but gives the subsection headings in plain English. Where the letter itself is the subject, it remains explicit.

### Light

And fire too.

Fire is present within water, according to our master Muhyi al-Din, because existence is a mixture of the two.

Light, meanwhile, is what allows created things to be unveiled and seen as they truly are. Light has one instrument: the sun. This belongs to every creature alike. But light has another instrument that is not shared by all, and that is the heart.

There is a difference between the two.

The sun rises quickly. Within a few hours it passes from red to yellow and then to white. The heart rises slowly. It needs struggle and discipline. That is why it is rare, and why its light belongs only to some.

But once the heart arrives, it remains in union. At that point its name changes from *qalb* to *fuʾād*. The word *qalb* carries the sense of turning and changing, whereas the *fuʾād* remains steady in its orbit.

This is a subtle point our masters, may God have mercy on them, did not notice. But it follows directly from what they taught. They are its source and foundation even if they never said it themselves.

The Sufis were called **the people of light** because of a saying attributed to the Messenger of God:

> Whoever wishes to look upon a servant whose heart God has filled with light, let him look upon Haritha.

So Haritha is described as having a heart filled with light.

Another saying has him declare:

> When light enters the heart, it opens and expands.

The Messenger of God did not actually speak these words. They were spoken on his behalf.

Our masters often make the Creator and His messengers say what they believe belongs to the perfection of a revelation that never truly came to an end.

Our master Shuayb Abu Madyan explained this through a distinction between **descent** and **continuing descent**. The first came to an end. The second continues until the Day of Resurrection.

These are the two faces of revelation.

Descent belonged to the prophets. Continuing descent belongs to the saints.

And descent remains incomplete without continuing descent.

The literalists never understood this. They confined themselves to the first kind of revelation, and this narrowed their share of the Real.

The world cannot run on prophetic revelation alone.

The proof is that it continues to move. Its movement depends on needs that the Creator did not address through prophetic revelation, yet it moves all the same.

It is the saints who keep it moving.

Saints appear in every place, just as they appear in every age. In those parts of the earth that heaven did not favour with a prophetic revelation, the saints performed the work performed elsewhere by prophets.

In many respects they went further than the prophets.

Some people of the inner path even believe that saints make fewer demands on people than prophets do.

When they speak of parts of the earth that received no prophetic revelation, they sometimes say that by doing without revelation these lands were spared a great deal of trouble and hardship.

One of our masters put it this way:

> The messengers made the road to God longer.

What he meant was the outward obligations imposed on believers as conditions of reaching God: the burdens religious people must carry before they can enter the kingdom of heaven.

One prophet among us, however, noticed this problem and stopped it.

That prophet was Jesus.

*Isa* is the Arabic name for Jesus. He is called the Messiah, a word originally derived from the Syriac *Mshiha*, meaning one who has been anointed with sacred oil. His real name was Emmanuel.

The use of sacred oil for anointing is itself a Babylonian rite.

Our religions are one family. They go back to a single origin, because all of us in this part of western Asia and northern Africa belong to one civilisational formation.

But unity does not mean sameness. This civilisation is varied rather than solid and absolute. Its names and attributes differ so greatly that they sometimes come into conflict.

Perhaps the master who blamed the prophets for lengthening the road to God failed to distinguish Jesus from the Church.

Jesus did not impose elaborate rituals or demand heavy acts of worship from his followers. In fact, he came partly to lighten the ritual burdens of Judaism. He abolished the rituals of the Sabbath without abolishing the Sabbath itself.

Nor was Jesus the one who established Sunday as the weekly holiday. That was Paul.

The weekly day of rest was not an ancient Christian invention. The Babylonians called it *yawm nūḥ libbī*—“the day when my heart rests.”

Their word *nūḥ* does not mean mourning, weeping, or wailing. It survives in the expression Iraqis still use today: “My heart knows no *niyāḥa*,” meaning no rest.

In classical Arabic the same root can also suggest turning or inclination. *Lisan al-Arab*, for example, gives the expression “he did not *nāḥahu* with anything good,” meaning that he gave him nothing—as though giving someone something good were a way of giving him rest.

The weekly holiday is an Iraqi Semitic institution. The Hebrews took it over and placed it on Sunday.

The civilisations of the Far East did not know the weekly holiday. They first encountered it through Muslims.

When the son of Amina came, he found Jewish ritual heavy and the Church burdened with obligations. Referring to the followers of both religions, he said of himself:

> Those who follow the unlettered prophet whom they find written about in the Torah and the Gospel—he commands them to do what is right, forbids them from what is wrong, permits them what is wholesome, forbids them what is foul, and lifts from them the burdens and chains that weighed upon them.

He could say this because he abolished the heavy Sabbath restrictions and reduced the ritual of his own weekly holiday, Friday, to the midday prayer.

He also modified a number of harsh rules contained in their law.

The same claim cannot really be made against the Church. Its rituals, although later inventions in relation to Jesus, remain lighter than the rituals of Islam.

Islamic ritual is lighter than Jewish ritual.

Still, the road to God remains long in Judaism, Islam, and the Church.

With Jesus it is short and direct.

It is long with Buddha, Confucius, and Hinduism.

It is short with Sufism and Taoism.

Sufism, Taoism, and Jesus all meet in their direct relationship with heaven.

The less religion a society has, the easier its journey becomes, because religion can only realise itself through obligations.

I do not know why this is so.

I have never managed to understand what our master Karl Marx meant when he said that the essence of religion is a call to simplicity. Religion is complicated and weighed down with ritual.

Perhaps he drew this conclusion from the example of Jesus, who is an exception among religions.

Even ethical teachings that naturally lean toward simplicity become complicated when they come into contact with religion, or when some change turns them into a creed.

This happened to Taoism when Taoist religion grew out of Taoist philosophy. Philosophy was turned into ritual and burdened with superstition.

The same happened to Sufism. At the beginning of the Ottoman period it turned into dervish practice, and the dervishes acquired rituals the earlier Sufis had never known.

By distinguishing between descent and continuing descent, our companions were trying to repair some of the harm caused by prophetic revelation when it lengthened the road for people in certain areas of life.

That required major changes to the whole structure of religion.

One of them once stood during the pilgrimage season and watched the crowds surging around him, circling the Kaaba, chanting the pilgrimage formula, touching the sacred stone.

He was horrified by what he saw.

“What has the son of Amina done to these people?” he said.

From living among the Sufis, I have seen that they regarded the religious obligations as heavy: five prayers every day, a full month of fasting, pilgrimage to the House from the furthest reaches of the earth, with both obligatory and voluntary forms and with the reward increasing according to the difficulty.

They thought all this was heavy **for ordinary people**, not for themselves.

In fact, their approach to obligations was the reverse: they made them heavier for the select and lighter for ordinary people.

By “the select,” they meant first of all the people of unveiling. They might also include people of learning, who were expected to pay what they called the tax of knowledge.

Those who held political power were never counted among the select. They belonged to the worldly powers.

Our companions were precise about names, because, as they liked to say, they learned the names before they were taught to Adam.

They had:

**the select**, meaning themselves;

**ordinary people**, or “creation,” as they preferred to call humanity;

and **the worldly powers**, meaning the people of political authority and wealth.

The bond between the select and ordinary people remains intact.

The bond between both of them and the holders of power and money is broken.

There is something here that may smell slightly of hierarchy between the select and the ordinary. As I have said, our masters treated obligations differently: they made them heavier for themselves and lighter for others.

They called this the **tax of knowledge**, the **tax of reason**, or the **tax of understanding**.

The same principle applied to material comfort.

The Sufi pole chooses poverty. He does not seek out fine food or a comfortable life. But he is delighted when he sees ordinary people enjoying life, eating good food, and wearing soft clothes.

One disciple tells the following story.

He once saw his master buying a roasted chicken, bread, and side dishes.

He thought to himself:

“Could our master really be eating things like this without our knowing?”

So he followed him without being noticed.

The master eventually reached a house and knocked on the door. A woman came out whose poverty was plain to see.

He handed her the chicken and went back the way he had come.

The disciple then understood that the master had not bought it for himself.

The point is not that the sheikh had somehow become intellectually superior to this poor ordinary woman, so that he could live without chicken while she could not.

It belongs instead to a class ethic in which the select carry a direct responsibility toward ordinary people.

The Sufis took this principle from Ali ibn Abi Talib.

Ali described two forms of asceticism.

For ordinary people, he prescribed moderation: a decent life without denying oneself wholesome pleasures.

For leaders, he prescribed poverty and deprivation.

His statements on this are well known. They appear in *Nahj al-Balagha*, in al-Kafi, in al-Makki’s *Qut al-Qulub*, and in Ibn Abd Rabbih’s *al-Iqd al-Farid*.

The **imam** can be a ruler.

In that case the obligation follows from his political responsibility for the people as a whole.

But the imam can also be the scholar, or what today we would call the intellectual or thinker.

This is the meaning the Sufis have in mind when they speak of asceticism.

For them, knowledge must be tied to action, and thought to conduct.

The character of an intellectual therefore depends on whether he lives according to what he thinks.

This is the opposite of the modern slogan:

> Say your piece and move on.

The Sufi pole says his piece and does **not** move on.

He knows that words arrive only when the person who speaks them arrives with them.

As for the old poet who said:

> Act on my words, though I fall short in what I do;
> my words may help you, and my failure will not harm you—

well, that is the sort of thing poets say.

Our companions count poets among the worldly powers.

Thought produced by a thinker who fails to live by it cannot exercise the influence it should. It begins to resemble merchandise offered for sale.

This may be different in pure science and technical work.

A scientist may produce a sound theory explaining some feature of nature while himself belonging entirely to the worldly powers: indifferent to ordinary people, closely tied to the state, taking from it and giving to it.

That does not make his scientific explanation false.

For this reason our Russian master Vladimir Lenin continued to support the Russian scientist Pavlov even though Pavlov had supported the tsar.

If Pavlov had been a poet or writer, he would not necessarily have received the same protection.

Thought and literature depend on a social position. Without one, they become luxury goods, objects of pure beauty.

People certainly need beauty.

But they can find beauty elsewhere, in the work of writers and poets who are socially committed and morally sound.

There is no comparable substitute for the scientist’s knowledge.

There is another difference.

Thought and literature stand close to consciousness and conscience.

Scientific work, by contrast, often results from a more mechanical process. It may require great skill in one field without requiring a broad human awareness.

A scientist may possess knowledge without possessing reason in the larger sense.

A writer or thinker cannot create anything without reason.

Our companions therefore imposed a tax on reason, but not on technical knowledge.

This is behind sayings such as:

> Reverent fear is a condition of knowledge; awe is a condition of direct knowing.

The scholar fears the Creator.

The knower stands in awe of Him.

Fear contains an element of being afraid. Awe does not.

The knower feels awe without fear.

They also say:

> In intoxication, the knower witnesses the state. In sobriety, he witnesses knowledge.

The emphasis falls on the state rather than knowledge, and on intoxication rather than sobriety.

For the decisive act of the knower is witnessing: that moment when the higher kingdom reveals itself to his heart while he is in ecstasy.

The ordinary scholar does not witness.

He lacks the comprehensive reason required for it and remains enclosed inside the profession in which he specialises, knowing little beyond it.

Frederick Engels noticed something similar when he said that the scientist, once outside his laboratory, is half illiterate.

Perhaps this helps explain why prophets, saints, sages, and reformers do not usually emerge from the ranks of scientists.

They come instead from the unlettered—as Muhammad and Jesus did—or from the ranks of thinkers, as with the Russian philosophers.

Self-denial, then, becomes an obligation for people of reason once their culture reaches a certain level and becomes liable to taxation.

That threshold is generally what I call **universal culture**, or more precisely, the culture of the **universal intellectual**.

Al-Jawahiri described Abu al-Ala al-Maʿarri in these terms:

> What I honoured in you were qualities that endure:
> freedom of thought, deprivation, and anger.

Deprivation is the mark of the universal intellectual, not of the poet.

Al-Jawahiri said it only because his poetic sensitivity allowed him to catch the finest things, turn them into verse, and then move on.

The universal intellectual accepts deprivation because of his responsibility toward ordinary people.

This responsibility is especially important in Chinese and Islamic wisdom.

No one emphasised it more strongly than the Taoist philosophers, beginning with the great master Laozi.

Among us, Ali ibn Abi Talib placed this obligation on rulers.

The Sufis placed it on sages—the universal intellectuals.

Their teacher in this was Amir al-Anbari, one of the earliest figures of Islamic intellectual culture.

The first Qadari theologians followed his path. They were thinkers and politicians at once and therefore made deprivation a shared obligation of both thinker and political leader.

One point should be made here.

Ali ibn Abi Talib applied this doctrine specifically to rulers, not to opponents living outside power.

He was not an ascetic before he became caliph.

He lived the moderate life he recommended for ordinary people and was not known for extreme austerity or deprivation before taking office.

The same was true of Umar ibn Abd al-Aziz.

Abu Dharr al-Ghifari and Salman al-Farisi were different.

They embraced deprivation while still in opposition, with no share of political power.

The Sufis followed Abu Dharr and Salman.

The Sufi is an opponent of power. He has no authority of his own, yet he still makes deprivation obligatory upon himself.

What is the purpose of this deprivation?

In the case of a ruler, Ali explained it by saying that he must serve as an example to his poor subjects so that poverty does not destroy them.

This explanation is rather vague.

Another text makes the point clearer.

It comes from Ali’s letter to his governor in Basra, Uthman ibn Hunayf, after Ali heard that the governor had accepted an invitation to a banquet.

Ali wrote:

> There may be someone in the Hijaz or Yamama who has no hope of obtaining even a loaf of bread and who has never known a full stomach. Should I be satisfied merely to be called Commander of the Faithful while refusing to share with them the hardships of time, or to be their example in the roughness of life?

The reason, then, is that he governs poor people whom he cannot make prosperous or provide with enough. He therefore imposes deprivation upon himself so that his way of life does not set him apart from theirs.

But what if he succeeds in making them prosperous?

What if they are no longer deprived?

No one seems to have asked Ali that question.

Someone really should have.

Unfortunately, there were no journalists at the time to pursue rulers and politicians with this sort of persistent questioning.

Journalism is one of the achievements of modern Western civilisation—modern, because the ancient West did not know it.

In any case, in our own age deprivation became a distinctive practice of the leaders of socialist revolutions: the Bolsheviks and the communists of East Asia.

It remained particular to them rather than to ordinary rulers and political leaders because it belongs to communalist consciousness.

The Chinese communists continued this practice even after they had succeeded in feeding the entire population.

Their deprivation, however, was not like that of the Taoists.

They simply lived as the people lived—in food, clothing, and housing.

During my long years among them I found almost nothing in their material life that separated them from ordinary people.

I often saw an ordinary citizen wearing better clothes than a cadre, leader, or official.

One of the oddities was that my own salary at the time was higher than the salary of Mao Zedong and his successor Hua Guofeng.

I received five hundred yuan.

Each of them received four hundred.

That was before I entered the service myself.

There is another reason rulers should practise asceticism, one Ali ibn Abi Talib did not mention.

Power itself breeds corruption.

Ali used to say:

> Whoever possesses power becomes a despot.

In other words, power leads to tyranny.

He did not say it leads to corruption.

Yet it is also the greatest cause of corruption.

A person in power begins to permit himself things he would never permit others. He comes to believe—as many ordinary people do too, not only flatterers and courtiers—that ruling others gives him some natural superiority over them.

He rules them; they do not rule him.

So he grants himself a freedom of action they do not possess.

Part of that freedom is the freedom to take more wealth than ordinary people receive.

All this depends on treating political office as a personal gain and a means of livelihood.

That understanding began with power itself.

Ali rejected it and tried to establish the opposite idea.

He would tell the men he appointed:

> Your office is not food placed before you. It is a trust placed upon your neck.

This is the origin of the common Muslim saying:

> Authority is a burden of responsibility, not an honour.

These sayings disappeared into the wind until modern communists arrived and tried to put them into practice as a requirement of what they called “people’s government.”

To counter the corrupting effects of power, then, the privileges of rulers must be abolished and their standard of living made equal to that of the people.

But this is only half the solution.

A ruler may renounce material privilege and still refuse to renounce power itself.

A wise Abbasid poet described the problem:

> Some souls can do without the pleasures of living,
> but not the pleasure of commanding and forbidding.

That is what Ali meant when he said:

> Whoever possesses power becomes a despot.

The cure is to educate political people through Taoist and Sufi culture, so that the knot of power and its poisons are removed from their hearts.

A person should become equally able to live as ruler or ruled, commander or commanded.

This solution is different from democracy.

Democracy is a treatment for despotism.

It ensures that rulers are elected and restrains them through constitutions and parliaments. In this way it can block personal dictatorship and absolute rule.

But even in electoral parliamentary states, politicians compete for power with astonishing ferocity and often treat victory as the great purpose of their lives.

Election campaigns are frequently violent for this reason and, when conditions allow it, can become genuine battles.

In states established by communists, a desire to enjoy power also appeared even where rulers continued to practise material deprivation.

A ruler would not leave office unless forced.

If no one forced him, he stayed until the end.

The Cultural Revolution in China may itself have been one result of this tendency.

During the final decade of his life, Mao Zedong showed an increasingly strong desire to rule alone. This led to the removal of nearly all the other pillars of the leadership.

Liu Shaoqi, Zhu De, Peng Dehuai, and others died forgotten and cast aside by the very state they had built with their minds and their hands.

Hence the need for Taoist and Sufi education: to produce rulers and leaders capable of renouncing not only the pleasures of comfort, but also the pleasures of commanding and forbidding.

Some popular leaders absorbed too much Western culture, and this was a major cause of the problem.

During the years of the Cultural Revolution, Mao increasingly resembled a European-style political leader or intellectual rather than the Chinese Taoist type he and his comrades had more closely resembled before that disaster.

### A Subtle Point

When deprivation is imposed upon the select, it falls into the natural order of things.

This was one of the corrections our companions made to religion.

Religion, in theory, imposes asceticism on both ordinary people and the elite. In practice it generally imposes it on ordinary people while exempting the elite.

For us, deprivation is a tax imposed on those who concern themselves with public affairs: people of culture and people of politics.

I have already explained its purpose.

There is also a psychological point.

Anyone who takes responsibility for the affairs of others cannot succeed unless he strips himself down and lets things go.

If he eats excessively, seeks out delicacies, and spends his life moving from one pleasure to another, those things begin to occupy him. They become his meaning and his share of the world.

His class commitment breaks down.

All that remains is reading and the outward badge.

Such a person becomes like a merchant with goods to sell. He works on his merchandise, advertises it, makes it attractive in people’s eyes, and finally sells it for a profit.

But if he strips himself of excess, accepts deprivation, and lets go of comfort, his spirit grows stronger.

He becomes able to endure hardship and remain steady in times of upheaval.

More than that, he gains honesty and credibility in public affairs.

When he openly calls for wealth to be shared rather than concentrated, people believe him.

They do not say that he is making this demand only so that he can seize the spoils for himself.

His self has already been divided among the people. Nothing remains for him alone.

As Sheikh Abu Madyan Shuayb said:

> No one reaches pure freedom while any remnant of himself still remains.

In this sense, deprivation becomes the condition under which the select work to abolish deprivation among ordinary people.

As I have said, this belongs to the natural order.

The deprived should be few.

Those who are not deprived should be many.

In other words: **poverty for the minority, prosperity for the majority.**

The prevailing arrangement is exactly the opposite: the majority go hungry while a minority grow rich.

People of politics and culture form only a very small number. They are not a people, a mass, or a class. They may not even form what can properly be called a social group, except loosely, because their numbers are often smaller than what would normally be needed to make one.

Yet today they are the people who enjoy society’s wealth while the public goes without.

This violates the laws of the Real on which the whole world stands.

As Muslims say:

> By justice the heavens and the earth were established.

Let us return to our companions and their dislike of burdensome rites and rituals.

I said that they found these burdensome **for ordinary people**.

The Taoists before them had felt the same way.

So had the Mohists, followers of Mozi, who lived in the fifth century before Christ.

Mozi condemned the extravagance of the Confucians in such matters and accused them of demanding from people more than they could bear.

The Greek philosopher Epicurus—one of the very few Western philosophers I would count among the sages—made a similar criticism.

Yet none of these people lightened the burden for themselves.

In fact, they practised more than was required.

The Sufis prayed beyond the obligatory prayers.

They fasted outside Ramadan.

They repeatedly travelled to Mecca.

The same man who reproached the son of Amina for imposing the pilgrimage on others would himself perform additional pilgrimages beyond what was required.

There are two sides to this.

The first follows the rule we have already seen: lighten the burden on ordinary people and increase it for the select.

Our companions were intensely sensitive to the suffering caused when people were made to carry excessive obligations while receiving little in return.

Their love for people, their fear for them, and their defence of their rights inspired them.

And they counted this work itself among the pleasures they enjoyed.

The sheikh who carried the roasted chicken to that poor family undoubtedly took more pleasure in giving it to them than he would have taken from eating it himself while they remained hungry.

We too take our pleasure from such things.

The second side is that our companions were all, in one way or another, **deists**.

They believed in God but denied prophecy as a necessary mediation.

Their deism was based on direct communion: they believed themselves to be in contact with heaven.

We will return to this in another section of this orbit.

A deist affirms divinity but denies prophecy. He therefore rejects religion while continuing to worship.

His worship takes place without mediation.

That is, it is performed without elaborate ritual forms.

Its purpose is not to obey the rules of a religion.

It has two aims.

The first is communion with heaven.

The second is spiritual discipline.

Communion with heaven takes two forms: prayer and meditation.

It is said of Abu Dharr al-Ghifari that most of his worship consisted of meditation.

This report is believable, because Abu Dharr had already been one of the God-seekers before Islam.

Our companions perform prayer in the form prescribed by their religion and may even multiply those prayers, because prayer for them is a means of entering direct communion with heaven.

For that same reason they generally avoid Friday and congregational prayer except as an outward precaution, or out of fear.

Congregational prayer does not give them what they seek: solitude with the Creator.

The first among them known to have withdrawn from Friday prayer was their early teacher Amir al-Anbari.

Al-Maarri’s successor once answered those who asked why he did not attend Friday prayer in one of his *Luzumiyyat*:

> They ask why I do not attend the Friday prayer,
> through which we hope for pardon from God, or nearness.
>
> What good would my presence do
> when I would merely jostle among their finest
> like mangy camels?

He was being deliberately provocative and wanted to insult their so-called finest people.

He did not state the real reason for avoiding the prayer.

That was a hidden secret too difficult to explain openly.

Alongside prayer, they turn to meditation.

Meditation was also the worship of the Taoists.

The Chinese had no religion organising their relationship with the Creator, and so meditation became their prayer to the Absolute.

Spiritual discipline is achieved through fasting.

Our companions fast during Ramadan and outside it.

Al-Maarri said that he broke his fast only on the two major feast days.

Islamic fasting requires complete abstention from food during daylight hours, rather than abstention merely from certain foods as in Christian fasting.

It is therefore a strict form of spiritual discipline.

But I am surprised that the Sufis allowed themselves to eat meat.

Only Amir al-Anbari and Abu al-Ala departed from them on this point.

I can hardly understand how the others managed it.

I ask the same question of the Taoists.

And, indeed, of universal intellectuals in our own age such as Goethe, Karl Marx, Plekhanov, Vladimir Lenin, and Lunacharsky.

The same problem even extends to Jesus.

There is clearly no agreement among universal intellectuals on vegetarianism.

For my part, I regard it as an obligation for them, though not for ordinary people.

It cannot be imposed universally because the plant life of the earth is not sufficient to feed all its inhabitants.

The Sheikh of Maarra went too far when he turned vegetarianism into a doctrine and preached it to others.

It would have been enough for him to observe it himself.

We are compelled to provide meat for the people, partly because we cannot persuade them not to eat it and partly because, as I have said, plants alone cannot feed billions of human beings.

The secret of pilgrimage to Mecca can be understood through the desire for nearness.

In Mecca stands the House of God, built by Abraham, according to belief, as the starting point from which his descendants would begin their inward journey toward heaven.

Our companions do not raise troubling questions about religious obligations.

As I have said, they practise these obligations as a way of strengthening their direct communion with heaven.

The Hijaz is the birthplace of Islam and the home of the son of Amina, heavy with memory.

The Sufis are plants of this soil and the distilled essence of its civilisation.

In one sense they are the intellectual opposite of that civilisation.

But at the deepest level of the self and the heart, its most active element lives through them.

They are not its enemies.

They are its rebellious children, rising against it in order to rescue it from the worldly powers and return it to the ideal Abu Dharr al-Ghifari and Amir al-Anbari wanted for it.

When they travel to the House on pilgrimage, they become captives of a memory stretching across centuries.

There, the gates of heaven open to them.

They enter its kingdom and feel themselves closer to it than they could anywhere else.

But the House is not merely the stone structure standing before them.

If it were, their circling around it would make no sense.

The true House is something in their conscience that happened to take visible form in a building.

People mistook the building for the House.

It is only an image.

Abu Yazid expressed this idea when he said:

> I used to circle the House seeking it. When I reached it, I saw the House circling around me.

This contains a movement toward the divinisation of the human being.

It releases the person from servitude to the Creator—from the command “Be.”

Abu Yazid also said:

> The first time I made pilgrimage, I saw the House.
> The second time, I saw the Lord of the House.
> The third time, I saw neither the House nor its Lord.

—*Talbis Iblis*, p. 244

This statement raises problems that are not easy to solve.

It is said that Abu al-Qasim al-Junayd al-Baghdadi wrote a book explaining Abu Yazid’s ecstatic sayings.

The book has not survived.

The author of *al-Lumaʿ*, who had read it, says that Junayd’s explanations did not solve the difficulties. They only made them harder.

Junayd outwardly sought compromise.

His inner position can be seen from the verse he quoted when someone asked why he remained still during a gathering of mystical music:

> You see the mountains and think them motionless, yet they pass like clouds.

I have reflected on Abu Yazid’s statement.

It seems to me that on his first visit to the House he was still at the beginning of the path.

He saw it as ordinary believers see it and circled it as they do.

By his second visit, however, he had advanced further.

A door that had been closed opened before him.

Through it he looked with inner sight and saw what others could not.

The Sufi’s inner sight comes so close to physical sight that what appears to the inward eye can seem to be visible to the outward eye.

Through this eye, seeing by means of the heart, Abu Yazid saw the Lord of the House.

Al-Bistami had seen Him many times during different phases of his life.

Later he advanced still further and began to say, as reported in *Talbis Iblis*:

> Moses wanted to see God. I never wanted to see God. He wanted to see me.

A story reported by Abu Talib in *Qut al-Qulub* and by al-Ghazali in *The Revival of the Religious Sciences* confirms the same idea.

A disciple once asked his master:

“Would you like me to show you Abu Yazid?”

The disciple replied:

“I have no need to see him. I have seen God.”

The master answered:

> To see Abu Yazid once is better for you than to see God seventy times.

From here we can move to Abu Yazid’s third visit, when he saw neither the House nor the Lord of the House.

My interpretation is that by this point he had reached the furthest stage of his ascent.

The pole becomes so absorbed that everything around him disappears. He sees nothing, neither with the inward eye nor with the outward one.

At this point all things vanish into the being of the pole.

He himself becomes the House of the world, and all things circle around him.

At this stage the pole has acquired the cosmic power that places him face to face with the three authorities: religion, the state, and money.

He clothes himself in awe.

He steps outside the constraints of the command “Be.”

He moves from the condition of being created toward the condition of creating.

He becomes the symbol of a power that cannot be defeated.

This is the secret of why al-Bistami was less afraid than the warriors of the age of ignorance.

Pilgrimage nevertheless remains important to the Sufi no matter how far his spiritual states advance.

As I have said, pilgrimage calls him back toward the place of origin.

It awakens in him a longing for beginnings, like rainwater making its way back to the sea—but only after watering the earth, leaving it flowering and green beneath its shade.

We can hear this longing in the words of one pilgrim whom distance prevented from travelling:

> You who are going to the Chosen One of Mudar,
> you have travelled in bodies, while we travelled as souls.
>
> We remained behind through weakness and excuse,
> and whoever remains through weakness
> is like one who travelled...

Prophecy is the verbal noun belonging to **prophet**.

The linguists connected the Arabic word *nabi* with *nabaʾ*, “news.”

But the word exists in Hebrew, which predates Arabic in the sequence of the Semitic languages, so the derivation itself is ancient.

The corresponding Hebrew term is *neviʾut*.

This is the parent term in the concept.

There is also “messenger” and “message,” but these are borrowed expressions.

Some Muslims nevertheless place the messenger above the prophet.

A messenger receives revelation through Gabriel.

A prophet receives inspiration directly.

A messenger possesses a scripture.

A prophet does not.

For this reason Moses was considered a messenger while his later successors among the Children of Israel were prophets rather than messengers.

In ordinary usage, however, **prophecy** came to include all these meanings because it had become a self-contained concept, whereas **message** acquired many other meanings.

Muslims still say “the Messenger of God” when referring specifically to Muhammad.

The phrase is used more often of him than “the Prophet.”

That is why the call to prayer says:

> I bear witness that Muhammad is the Messenger of God.

It does not say:

> I bear witness that Muhammad is the Prophet of God.

The deists rejected prophecy.

Among them were philosophers, Sufis, and independent thinkers such as al-Maʿarri and Ibn al-Rawandi.

The philosophers of Islam were deists, with the possible exception of al-Kindi, who did not go very deeply into philosophical questions and devoted much of his attention to the natural sciences.

The list includes al-Razi, al-Farabi, Ibn Sina, Ibn Rushd, Ibn Tufayl, Ibn Bajjah, and Sadr al-Din al-Shirazi.

As for the Sufis, their great poles were all deists in this sense.

Al-Hallaj openly rejected mediation—that is, prophecy.

The same position appears among other poles of both social Sufism and philosophical Sufism.

Statements of this kind even appear among poles usually classified as orthodox believers, such as Abd al-Qadir al-Jili.[^ms-v1-001]

The deists differed among themselves over prophets, prophecy, and divinity.

Al-Razi rejected prophecy outright and accused prophets of lying.

He even wrote a book about their falsehoods called *The Frauds of the Prophets*.

For this reason al-Razi did not begin his books by blessing the Prophet. He simply opened with the formula “In the name of God.”

He performed religious obligations only when forced to do so for appearances.

His God was one of five eternal absolutes:

the Creator,

the soul,

prime matter,

absolute time,

and absolute space.

This God does not resemble the God of religion.

He does not sit upon a throne.

He has no angels.

He is pure abstraction.

Yet al-Razi still entered into communion with Him through prayer and meditation.

Al-Maarri followed a similar path.

He accused the prophets of lying and deliberately misleading people.

Like al-Razi, he also said that religions were a cause of war among human beings.

The identity of al-Maʿarri’s God is less clear.

At times He has the attributes of the God of religion.

At other times He merges with the Aristotelian First Mover.

At still other times al-Maʿarri seems to doubt whether such a being exists in either form.

Al-Maarri’s God is not just.

In this respect He resembles nature, which al-Maʿarri accused of irrationality and aggression.

Unlike al-Razi, however, al-Maʿarri observed religious obligations such as fasting and prayer and even added more than was required, much like the Sufis.

But he did not attend Friday prayer or perform the pilgrimage.

Apparently he had no memories drawing him toward Mecca, whose disappearance he predicted in one of his *Luzumiyyat*:

> Quraysh and Mecca will one day be spoken of as “once they were,”
> just as people now speak of Jadis and Tasm.

The Sufis reject prophecy **as mediation**.

The Sufi enters into communion with heaven directly and does not want to reach it through an intermediary.

I have already quoted their saying:

> The messengers made the road to God longer.

This amounts to a rejection of religion.

Yet the Sufi does not accuse prophets of lying.

Nor does he attack them.

We find nothing among the Sufis resembling a logical refutation or denial of revelation, because they never entered fully into abstract philosophical logic.

Their knowledge came through **taste**, as Ibn Arabi explains:

> The sciences of those who know through taste cannot be transferred and cannot be retold.

They abolished revelation in one sense by removing the intermediary from it.

Gabriel became a symbol to be interpreted rather than a concrete intermediary who had to appear in person.

Once mediation had been removed, they began treating the prophets as members of their own wider family.

They gave Muhammad a special place among prophets but dealt with him, in effect, as an equal or colleague.

They never criticised him in the manner of the heretical theologians or philosophers.

Instead they reproached him when he did something they found inconvenient.

In such moments they often used his familiar name Abu al-Qasim or called him “the son of Amina.”

Ibn Sabʿin, according to Ibn Shakir al-Kutubi in *Fawat al-Wafayat*, said:

> The son of Amina narrowed something wide when he said, “There will be no prophet after me.”

This is reproach, not criticism.

Ibn Sabʿin is simply wishing that his companion had not closed the door in his face and prevented him from carrying forward what had already been begun.

Prophecy is a phenomenon whose existence cannot simply be denied.

It performs a large historical role.

That is how the Sufis understood it.

But they also believed it continued.

They had two views of this continuation.

The first was Ibn Sabʿin’s objection to the doctrine that prophecy had ended forever.

The second was more widely shared: the saint completes the work of the prophet.

The **saint**, here, is the Sufi pole.

The Sufis disagreed over whether the saint is equal to the prophet, superior to him, or below him.

Ibn Sabʿin’s position suggests equality, because the saint completes the prophet’s work.

Believing Sufism usually places the saint below the prophet.

But Abu al-Ghayth ibn Jamil, as quoted in *The Perfect Human*, and Abd al-Qadir al-Jili, as quoted in *Qalaʾid al-Jawahir*, openly place the saint beyond the prophets.

The first says:

> We entered a sea at whose shore the prophets stopped.

The second says:

> Company of prophets, you were given the title; we were given what you were not given.

Both men are normally classified among the believing Sufis.

This shows how loose the boundaries become once we look at the great poles themselves.

Their meaning is perfectly clear.

The Sufi stands ahead of the prophet.

The prophets stopped at the shore of knowledge and did not dare enter its depths.

They received the title.

The Sufi received knowledge denied to them.

It is almost as though prophecy were a matter of luck: some people receive the name without deserving it while others, more worthy of it, are denied it.

In any case, the saint may be superior to the prophet.

For some Sufis this suggestion grows into what we might call a gesture toward the throne itself.

A story transmitted by the commentator on *Nahj al-Balagha* from Abu Said al-Kharraz points in this direction.

Abu Said said:

“I saw the Messenger of God in a dream and said, ‘Messenger of God, forgive me. My love for God has distracted me from loving you.’”

The Prophet replied:

> Blessed one, whoever loves God has loved me.

(3/27)

This amounts to an open admission that Abu Said does not love the Messenger of God.

The dream belongs to the Sufi rejection of intermediaries.

The Sufi knows no one but God and loves no one but God.

He therefore needs no intermediary to introduce Him and no road or means through which love must travel.

Their rejection of mediation also contains a hint that prophecy itself falls short of full arrival.

We have already seen them reproach the messengers for making the road longer and multiplying its demands.

The saint contains something the prophet lacks.

And not only because the prophet hesitates to enter the deep sea.

The prophet’s heart also lacks longing.

In *Hayat al-Qulub*, God says to David:

“You ask many things of Me, but you never ask Me to give you longing.”

David replies:

“My Lord, what is longing?”

The prophet does not know longing.

Follow the logic through and the conclusion is that the prophet does not love God.

If the prophet’s relationship to God is stripped of love, it becomes a relation of pure servitude.

A slave does not love his master.

The Sufis say a great deal about servitude, but by tying it to love they remove the very thing that defines ordinary slavery: two fundamentally unequal sides.

The pure slave hardly exists in their system.

He is the believer, not the knower.

The prophet is a believer.

The Sufi is a knower.

The Sufi’s relationship with the Creator is a relationship of knowledge that has moved beyond servitude by reaching the stage of longing—a stage neither the ordinary believer nor the prophet reaches.

Some people associated with Sufism rejected this suggestion.

Others interpreted it away.

But the great poles of direct knowledge generally agree in placing longing inside servitude and thereby transforming it.

We will return to the paths of servitude in another orbit.

This feeling also comes from a hidden comparison between what the Sufi pole possesses and what the prophet possesses.

Perhaps the Sufis read the sacred books and found that they contained little beyond outward knowledge and none of the secrets contained within the Sufi universe.

In this they stand alongside many Muslim thinkers more broadly: philosophers, Sufi poles, theologians of the class of Ibrahim al-Nazzam, and independent thinkers such as al-Maʿarri who belong to no school or sect.

The comparison also extends to personal conduct.

Their early teacher Amir al-Anbari is said to have considered himself superior to the household of Abraham.

The basis of this superiority was simple: he did not marry and did not eat meat.

He therefore considered himself stronger than they were in giving up pleasures and more determined in extinguishing physical desire.

The later Sufis did eat meat, unlike this early teacher, but they did not overeat or chase after delicacies in the way the prophets of the Torah are described as doing.

Some married and others remained single.

As a rule, however, they did not take several wives and did not keep concubines.

In this they followed the line of Abu Dharr, Salman, and Amir: one wife and no slave women.

Prophets were permitted to do otherwise.

The Sufis treated such practices as belonging to the worldly powers.

At times it can seem almost as though they placed prophets, kings, and rich men into a single basket.

Yet they were not so lacking in judgment as some modern critics who seem incapable of making distinctions.

Kings and rich men, in their view, are wholly harmful to people.

Prophets are different.

They were people with causes to preach.

They reformed and corrupted.

They were right and wrong.

When the Sufis declared themselves to have gone beyond the prophet, they did not bury him in the same grave as the king and merchant.

Love for the prophets entered their hearts.

Not a grain of love for kings or rich men did.

The prophet they loved most was Jesus.

Like them, he did not marry.

He owned nothing beyond what he needed to survive.

He wandered from place to place, called for justice for the poor, and denounced the rich.

As for Muhammad, I have said that he was one of them.

And precisely because he was one of them, they rejected his guardianship over them.

They turned directly toward heaven, by themselves, without returning to him.

Yet they were deeply in love with his Book.

It became one of the means through which they entered communion.

In my view, this has much to do with the artistic power of the Book.

The Sufis had risen high in the world of art.

Ecstasy dominated them.

They possessed a profound sense of beauty that could move from the beauty of a word to the beauty of a woman.

They found no speech more beautiful than this astonishing Book.

There is another reason.

They did not possess Muhammad’s power or the force with which he seized history.

He handled history with his own hands almost as a boy handles a ball, then sent it rolling outward, defying the ordinary laws of inertia until it eventually reached them.

They found themselves moving within its orbit.

They could not escape its crushing wheel.

What distinguished them from others was their awareness of the force they were caught inside and their resistance to necessity.

Eventually they acquired a will that had defeated the household of Abraham, and they used it to correct the orbit.

If one of them had possessed the power of a prophet, he might have been able to create a new history beyond anything the prophets themselves achieved.

The Sufi who entered the deep sea and left the prophet standing behind him on the shore would then combine the force of prophecy, the breadth of knowledge, and the dignity of will.

He would reach the level of absolute action.

No one has reached that height.

Not even this movement of ours...

### So What Did They Believe About the Creator?

Zhuangzi once wondered whether the world had a maker.

He remained uncertain and reached no final conclusion either way.

The Taoists did not trouble themselves greatly with the question.

For them, the Tao was the supreme Absolute.

It exists both outside existence and within it.

It differs from things and merges with them.

It moves, and from its movement things come into being.

It is both mover and moving, unlike Aristotle’s mover, which does not move.

The Tao has no separate divine attributes.

Nor does it grant existence to things from outside.

It is the principle within things.

It expands through them and they take form.

It contracts and they come to an end.

Perhaps it is best understood as a force flowing through existing things.

This is why Zhuangzi placed the Tao in water, fire, wood, earth, gold, air, blood, and dung—that is, even in animal waste.

Everything returns to one source.

The Taoists call it the Tao.

The Sufis call it the Creator or the Real.

Materialists call it prime matter.

All of these are absolutes.

The Tao is absolute.

The Creator is absolute.

Prime matter is absolute.

The atom and the nucleus are also absolutes in the sense that they endure within things.

So when Zhuangzi places the Tao in dung, what can it be other than the primal matter that flows through dung and makes it what it is?

Yet the Tao remains absolute even while present in dung.

A compound is not the same thing as the simple element from which it is composed.

When the simple enters into a compound, the simple remains simple and the compound remains compound.

The same is true of atoms and nuclei within larger structures.

Dung is filthy and polluted.

The Tao present within it is pure.

This is a subtle point that can be grasped only by a clear mind inhabited by dialectics.

It is also perfectly familiar to physicists and chemists.

The natural sciences have long learned to compose and decompose things, separate elements from compounds, identify the properties of each, and avoid confusing realities merely because they are combined inside the same material form.

The Tao, then, is not a creator.

So what is **al-Bāriʾ**, the Creator?

The Arabic word comes from the verb *baraʾa*, meaning to create or bring into existence.

Genesis begins:

> *Bereshit bara Elohim et hashamayim ve-et ha-aretz.*

In the beginning God created the heavens and the earth.

And in the Quran:

> He is God: the Creator, the Maker, the Giver of Form.

The meaning is essentially the same as **creator**.

Yet the Sufis preferred the term **al-Bāriʾ** in some places and **God** or **the Real** in others.[^ms-v1-002]

They rarely used the ordinary word “Creator.”

They normally pronounced *al-Bāriʾ* without emphasising the final glottal stop.

This movement from “God” toward “the Creator” or “the Real” may signal an attempt to give the Sufi God a special meaning closer to the idea of an Absolute around which things move and to which they return, without this Absolute standing apart from them.

We can therefore compare the Sufi Creator or Real with the Tao.

Both can be understood as a force flowing through existing things rather than standing outside them.

This is the idea of **unity of being**.

For the Taoists, its highest pole is the Tao.

For the Sufis, it is the Real.

The Sufi conception of the Creator differs from the ordinary religious conception.

The Real exists in no particular direction and is contained by no place.

Yet both Taoists and Sufis, when meditating, often turn themselves toward the sky.

This may suggest some hidden recognition of the God of religion.

But there is another way of understanding it.

The Sufis do not bind themselves to pure reason.

Their culture is based on taste and unveiling.

The movement of the heart mixes with the judgments of the mind.

Their experience is emotional as much as rational.

The movement of the heart belongs to their spiritual life, including their relation to the unknown supreme reality.

The Taoists share the same relation.

It lies outside ordinary reason.

It is not governed by philosophical logic.

It belongs, in the words of our master Muhyi al-Din, to:

> the sciences of taste, which cannot be transferred and cannot be retold.

This produces a difficult contradiction.

Direct spiritual communion seems to require a personal being with whom one enters into contact.

But unity of being denies precisely such a separate personal being.

The Sufis hold onto both sides.

They affirm unity of being in its strict philosophical sense.

They also enter into communion with what they experience as a presence.

Here we encounter the figure of the **philosopher-sage**: someone in whom the logic of the philosopher and the intuition of the sage exist together.

Philosophy is a strict method of thought.

Wisdom is a form of moral feeling tied to society—that is, tied to social action and personal conduct.

The philosopher-sage lives spiritually and thinks rationally at the same time.

The results of these two activities may differ.

The truths a philosopher-sage reaches through the Sufi path are ultimately the same truths the philosopher reaches through logical reasoning.

But spiritual life belongs to conduct rather than abstract thought.

It takes hold of the sage and fills him with happiness during the contemplative solitude in which he communes with the Absolute.

Happiness is one result of this spiritual life.

It is what people call divine love or Sufi ecstasy.

We will discuss it in the Orbit of Mīm.

But happiness is not the only result.

There is another result that belongs even more closely to this all-encompassing inward state:

**the ability to act and confront power in the field of social struggle.**

The philosopher-sage struggles.

The pure philosopher generally does not.

His strict logic brings him closer to the practitioners of pure science.

This type is found more often in Eastern than Western philosophy.

The overwhelming majority of Chinese philosophers were philosopher-sages.

The same description applies to the great poles of Islamic Sufism.

It does not apply to the philosophers of Islam.

They were philosophers in the pure Greek style.

They enriched rational culture but left little mark on social culture.

This may explain why so many of them worked for rulers, while the Sufis kept their distance from political authority.

The sage’s spiritual life—what I also call the life of the universal intellectual—gives him the energy he needs to confront the rich.

His inner bond with the Absolute frees him from the ordinary intellectual’s anxieties and allows him to move with greater freedom, without constantly calculating profit and loss.

This is the force that equips the Taoist when he confronts an empire.

Laozi says:

> When the emperor is crowned
> or the three ministers are installed,
> do not send a gift of jade
> or a team of four horses.
> Stay where you are
> and present the Tao.

When you confront an empire with the Tao, you can challenge it and it cannot do anything to you.

Ordinary people say that God created a light and then created Himself from that light.

The Taoists and Sufis did something similar.

They created an Absolute for themselves and then united with it.

In doing so, they created for themselves an authority higher than the authority of the state.

But this was not creation from nothing.

The Taoist sage and the Sufi begin by identifying themselves with the spirit of the universe: that obscure presence which seems to surround existing things or settle inside the sage’s heart.

It fills him with happiness and gives him a power charged with tremendous force.

He possesses it in his solitude, which stands against the outward state while identifying itself with ordinary people—or with the Creator—through its union with the Absolute.

The power of the state comes from soldiers and money.

The power of the sage comes from something far harder to attack: a tightly bound union between ordinary people and the cosmic Absolute.

The state has limited reach.

The sage’s source of strength cannot easily be broken apart or made to disappear.

This cosmic force is not a product of pure intellect.

The scientist and pure philosopher do not possess it.

Aristotle, the greatest of philosophers, belonged to the world of the state.

Al-Hallaj wanted to overturn states.

The force we are speaking about is more like an explosion carried by the spirit of the universe into the spirit of the Sufi pole.

It floods his conscience and lights torches inside him.

They illuminate the unknown regions of existence.

He contemplates their beauty and no longer needs the attractions offered by the outward state.

He rises above its rulers.

As I have explained, this power reaches him from two sources joined together in his separation from worldly authority:

the spirit of the universe,

and the spirit of ordinary people.

The second is connected to the communal consciousness of the Sufi pole.

We will discuss this in the Orbit of Mīm.

The spirituality of the sage is the opposite of religion.

The Sheikh of Maarra said this clearly and deserves credit for exposing a hidden secret that runs against what most people assume.

Religion and spirit are different things.

Religion establishes a physical relationship with an embodied God:

a visible and tangible idol among pagans,

a man sitting upon a throne among the religions of revelation.

This is why the religious personality is fundamentally physical and sensory.

The religious man carries a worldly creed in nearly every detail, including its picture of the next world.

The afterlife is imagined as material and bodily pleasure: enjoyment experienced through the body, either completing the pleasures of this world or compensating for their absence.

The spirit has little role in such a relationship.

Al-Maarri noticed this pleasure-seeking side of religious personality.

Through his own life he also created the opposite image: the spiritual unbeliever.

Spirituality belongs specifically to the sage.

Sensual pleasure, by contrast, is shared by the religious man and the man of state.

Religion and the state meet on this ground.

Religion differs only by promising happiness in both worlds.

The religious man receives both.

The man of state receives this world alone.

The sage’s spiritual formation comes before his work on behalf of ordinary people, as we will explain in a later chapter.

His rational development, by contrast, is an intellectual process.

We have already distinguished between **social Sufism** and **philosophical Sufism** through two great poles:

al-Hallaj and Ibn Arabi.

Al-Hallaj wrote little apart from his poetry and his *Tawasin*.

These belong mainly to Sufi literature, with a small amount of heretical theology.

Ibn Arabi, by contrast, wrote enormously.

His works form an encyclopedia of philosophy, science, and metaphysics.

They are enough to place him among the great generation of Islamic philosophers.

He may even have been the most innovative or creatively fertile of them.

He should be regarded as the greatest philosopher of Sufism.

No other pole of philosophical Sufism produced anything like his volume of work.

He should also have been included in the history of philosophy as well as the history of Sufism.

The reason he was not is that the history of Islamic philosophy remained captive to Orientalist classifications.

The Orientalists were the first to write that history, and they were poor readers of Arabic texts as well as being shaped by a racism that prevented them from seriously tracing the original elements of Eastern thought.

Ibn Arabi, for his part, did not enter deeply into social struggle.

He had no strong emotional bond with ordinary people.

His relations with rulers remained open, although he did not actually enter their service.

Nor did he display any clear tendency toward political opposition.

Yet he did carry the banner of equality among human beings and the removal of hierarchy between religions.

This position is established decisively in *The Bezels of Wisdom*, one of his later and more mature works.

In making this argument he was following a Sufi tendency shared by the great poles.

Outside Sufism, al-Maʿarri is almost alone in joining them.

Al-Hallaj read more and wrote less.

The great figures of social Sufism were major intellectuals in the sense that they possessed wide knowledge of the various branches of learning available in their time.

But al-Hallaj’s mission was not primarily intellectual or scholarly.

It was social.

He should be placed in the line of the prophets of social revolution alongside Jesus, Mazdak, Abu Dharr, and Hamdan.

Moments of abstract reasoning are rare in al-Hallaj.

He is almost entirely spiritual force, present intensely in time and place.

You will not find systematic scientific or philosophical thought in the *Tawasin*, apart from a heretical deism expressed in Sufi rhetoric without logical structure.

But it blows open the human interior and sets fires inside it.

That is the special power of Sufi speech when it comes from a social pole of the class of al-Hallaj.

The *Tawasin* is an example of what this type of person says.

The same burning current runs through the fathers of social Sufism from Ibrahim ibn Adham to Abd al-Qadir al-Jili.

I have said that spiritual life and rational thought can exist side by side in the same pole, though they may also separate.

Ibn Arabi leans more toward reason.

Al-Hallaj leans more toward spirit.

Ibn Sabʿin combines the two.

Combining them does not necessarily mean that one overwhelms the other. Each can preserve its own space.

Yet I have observed that the more a person develops intellectually, the less concerned he often becomes with ordinary people.

Ibn Sabʿin’s position in the field of social struggle is higher than that of al-Hallaj and al-Jili.

We have observed something similar in the case of Ibn Arabi.

This, in truth, is another of the trials of reason.

The further the mind advances and fills itself with knowledge, the further it can drift from people.

This is often true of the natural scientist and the pure philosopher.

It is rare to encounter either in the turmoil of social struggle.

Perhaps when people of knowledge and reason come to know more and more truths, they become disgusted by what they regard as the ignorance of ordinary people and begin to set themselves apart.

The word “common” then becomes an insult associated with ignorance.

But people often fail to distinguish ignorance from illiteracy.

Ordinary people may be unable to read and write without being ignorant.

They practise trades and crafts that require intelligence and skill.

Anyone who possesses intelligence and skill cannot simply be called ignorant.

He may be illiterate if he cannot read and write.

That is a different matter.

Ordinary people also possess social, class, and political awareness.

Historically, their political awareness was sharpened by a deep tendency to resist the state.

Ordinary people do not love the state.

The state finds far more supporters and servants among people of learning and intellect.

In the modern period, ordinary political awareness has also included national consciousness opposed to colonialism.

These are forms of awareness and knowledge that may have no equivalent among educated and cultured circles.

Ordinary people are therefore not a burden upon reason and conscience.

They are, in fact, one of their sources.

The universal intellectual of earlier ages understood this subtle truth about ordinary people and did not set himself apart from them.

It is worth noticing that the Sufis rarely complain about the injustice or ignorance of the public.

Such complaints are common among poets, writers, natural scientists, and similar people.

The Sufis direct most of their complaints instead toward the literalists—the jurists.

I thought for a long time about Sadr al-Din al-Shirazi’s complaints against “the public.”

He was a philosopher-sage, even though philosophy outweighed wisdom in him.

He kept his distance from the state and stood apart from those fellow philosophers who approached it.

A man like this would hardly have complained about ordinary people, who had never persecuted philosophers.

Eventually it became clear to me that by “the public” he meant the public **among people of learning and intellect**—those scholars accustomed to plotting against one another.

The chief of the philosophers did not withdraw to the village of Kahak to escape ordinary people.

He fled there to escape people of his own kind.

### Further Note

The letters have informed me that what I said about why Taoists and Sufis turn toward the sky during meditation is not quite enough.

There is another subtle point.

The sky appears to us to be **above**.

It seems high.

It is almost as though we were sitting inside a square, with ourselves on its bottom edge, two sides enclosing us to the right and left, and the final side above our heads.

So when one of us wants to enter into communion with the spirit of the universe, he raises his head—just as he raises it when looking at the stars.

Even in the age of space travel, people imagine that a rocket is launched **upward**.

In reality there is no single direction in the universe that can properly be called up or down.

Our philosophers who denied that God occupies any direction understood this perfectly.

But the image has become habitual.

More than that, it is imposed on us by experience itself.

Anyone who wants to look at the sun or the Pleiades must raise his head.

## The Orbit of Mīm (م)
### Opposition

Our masters did not use the word **opposition**. It is a term invented by our own generation. But they practised the thing itself, as a way of breaking free from the powers that bind us.

Opposition means cutting ties with the state and those who run it, and calling for—or working toward—its transformation. The state is a devil made up of devils. Its actions are devilish, and so is the conduct of its masters. Those who deal with it, and whom it deals with, become part of the same devilry. Together they make up the worldly powers that inhabit the realm of conflict, sensation, and appetite. They are the pillars of injustice and the source of darkness, and anyone who joins them is heading for ruin.

One of our women once looked down at the vizier Ali ibn Isa as he passed in a huge and imposing procession. She asked who this magnificent man was, though she knew perfectly well.

Then she said:

“This is a man who fell from God’s sight, so God afflicted him with what you see.”

Ali ibn Isa had one advantage over the other ministers of the state: he seems to have done less harm than most of them. It is said that when he heard what the woman had said, he gave up his position and left Baghdad to live beside Mecca. To live beside Mecca, in their language, was to live beside God, and such a person was therefore called a **neighbor of God**.

To fall from God’s sight meant to enter the service of the state.

The Sufi tradition of opposition reaches back to the earliest period of Islam. Opposition is one of the great forces of Islamic political history, and it shares that history with power itself.
