Contrary to the games played by Orientalists and by those among us who repeat them in our own language, rulers did not shape this history alone. Opposition fills half of it, perhaps more.

Nor did our historical sources devote themselves simply to recording the history of governments, as some ignorant intellectuals claim. They recorded political events, and political events are made by both sides: the authorities and their opponents.

When a Muslim historian writes the history of a state, he also records its enemies and the people who rose against it. This is why history was not normally written as a sequence of rulers. It was written year by year.

Works arranged around rulers tended to be shorter ones, such as al-Masudi’s *Meadows of Gold* and al-Yaqubi’s history. The great chronicles that tried to contain history between two covers were organised by year: al-Tabari, Ibn al-Athir, Ibn Kathir, al-Dhahabi, Ibn al-Imad.

Sufi opposition was primarily an **intellectual commitment** that stood outside everyday politics, because the Sufis were not professional politicians.

Al-Hallaj was the great exception. He joined an organised political movement, and it cost him his life. The killing of al-Hallaj belongs in the same family of events as the killing of Husayn, the crucifixion of Christ, and the death of Babak.

Our people did not repeat his experiment. Their personalities were too strongly shaped by the life of thought and culture for that kind of political adventure.

The universal intellectual is rarely good at politics. Plekhanov and Lunacharsky floundered in it, and I do not know how far Vladimir Lenin succeeded. These men were masters of a culture that broke through boundaries, but for that culture to become political it needed the experience of their less cultured colleague, Joseph Stalin.

I do not want to close off the possibility we seek. The two have to meet: the experience of the politician and the mind of the intellectual. Only then can we build the ideal society and make sure it survives.

The Sufis found something more useful and more lasting. They made opposition into an **intellectual commitment**, part of their work as intellectuals.

It is largely to them that we owe the establishment and spread of this tendency within Islamic culture. In its most active part, Islamic culture is a culture of opposition. Poetry is the main exception.

Poetry, by itself, is not really an intellectual or cultural act. It comes from instinct, and reason need play no part in it unless the poet also becomes intellectually self-aware and gives his instinctive work a rational dimension. That is rare. You find it only among exceptional poets such as Bashshar in the old period and Adonis in ours.

Some jurisprudence also falls outside the tradition of opposition, though certainly not all of it. The great jurists who served the state were fewer than the great poets who served it.

Most theology belongs to the opposition. Social Sufism belongs to it completely. Philosophy is neutral.

But the strongest tradition of opposition is found in Sufism, where it forms a remarkable counterpart to Taoist opposition.

The Sufis confronted three powers:

**the state, religion, and money.**

The Taoists confronted two:

**the state and money.**

There was no comparable struggle over religious authority in Chinese thought, because China knew religion mainly among tribal or ethnic minorities. The Han—the Chinese nation itself—had no religion.

The Sufi commitment to opposition is broad, detailed, and exact. It leaves no loophole for the intellectual.

Our people establish it through stories and anecdotes, some of them invented by a myth-making imagination and put to work for purposes more important than factual accuracy.

It is said that one master had a pregnant wife. Whenever the ruler’s procession passed their house, she would climb onto the roof to look at it. The master kept telling her not to, but she would not stop.

One day he said:

“This child of yours will enter the ruler’s service.”

And so it happened. She gave birth to a boy who later became a man of the state.

So it was forbidden not only to work for the ruler, take his money, or serve him, but even to share in the smallest thing connected with him.

Merely watching the ruler’s procession was included in the ban.

They even attributed to Ahmad ibn Hanbal a prohibition against making use of the ruler’s torchlight.

Women would sometimes take advantage of a ruler’s procession passing their homes and spin yarn by the light of its torches. The procession was usually long and moved slowly, so it gave them plenty of light.

This kind of severity is not normally associated with Ibn Hanbal, who permitted prayer behind an immoral imam. But our people regularly put the teachings they wanted to establish into the mouths of the Prophet or the great religious authorities.

The prohibition against using even the ruler’s torchlight is extreme. But once the state is understood as a purely devilish structure, there is nowhere else for the argument to go.

Otherwise Rabia al-Adawiyya would not have lost her heart after sewing a tear in her shirt by the light of the ruler’s torches.

She remained without her heart for some time. Then she remembered what she had done, tore out the stitches, and her heart returned to her.

Another story puzzles me even more.

One of them says that he wandered lost in Sinai for fifteen days. Eventually he found his way and met a soldier who gave him a drink of water. After that, hardness returned to his heart for thirty years.

He seems to be confessing that some military element dissolved into his bloodstream and left him, in certain situations, a little harsh with people.

But the Sufi is not harsh toward people. His harshness is directed toward the worldly powers.

The Sufis created what might be called an **opposition complex** within intellectual culture. It made intellectuals think long and hard before doing anything that might bring them closer to the state.

That complex survived into our own time and still shapes intellectual choices to a certain extent.

It remained very strong until the 1950s, then began to weaken as pressure from Western culture increased and liberalism spread among intellectuals.

This happened alongside the oil revolution. Intellectuals were drawn toward governments—especially the oil states—and a large class of wealthy intellectuals emerged.

The oil factor has carried corruption deep into contemporary culture, while the intellectual increasingly treats Western culture as his sole source of education.

Western culture remains caught in its feudal and then capitalist inheritance, while the communal share of that inheritance is small compared with Eastern culture and its two great streams, Chinese and Islamic.

We need to broaden our education in the oppositional heritage of Islamic culture and its Chinese counterpart. Only then can we rebuild the personality of the intellectual with new elements that help him withstand Western disruption and recover his role in social struggle.

The insistence on boycotting the state is an indirect form of incitement against it.

But the Sufis did not repeat the experience of al-Hallaj. They did not usually collide directly with the state. They were content to turn opposition into an ideology.

Even so, individual Sufis sometimes crossed the line from teaching opposition to confronting the ruler themselves.

Abd al-Qadir al-Jili—al-Kilani—may be regarded as the most dangerous Sufi figure in this field after al-Hallaj.

He lived in Baghdad during the time of the later caliphs, after the city had freed itself from Seljuk control.

Ordinary people surrounded him like a wall. He seems to have drawn strength from them in challenging the caliph.

He had organised social activities to help the poor of Baghdad, believing that feeding the hungry was the greatest work a human being could do.

He financed this through lawful money sent to him by people of means.

When the caliph tried to exploit these activities and use them as a source of funding, Abd al-Qadir returned his gifts with a rebuke: he would not accept money taken from people’s blood.

The caliph avoided a confrontation with him because he feared entering into conflict with the people of Baghdad, the one place where he still exercised real power.

For readers outside Iraq, it is worth adding that his district—known in his time as Bab al-Azaj and today as Bab al-Shaykh, after him—still carries a heritage of defiance.

Its toughs and rebels have always resisted state authority.

Iraqis still look at someone from Bab al-Shaykh with a certain awe or wariness, shaped by the special reputation of the neighbourhood and its people.

The state is the first front of opposition.

The second, closely tied to it, is money.

Money comes from the state, trade, or land.

Money has a power of its own. Sometimes it struggles against state power; sometimes it joins with it.

The power of money seeks independence from the state. In modern language this is called the **feudal tendency**.

It succeeded in the West, where feudal power established an authority of its own outside the cities.

It did not succeed in the East because of the centralised state and the state’s large role in economic life.

At times, during periods of disorder and the collapse of an existing state, feudal power might survive long enough to create an independent sphere for itself. But it never lasted.

A new state would soon move to extend its authority over the whole country and reassert central control before the feudal lord could fully consolidate his own power.

At the major turning points of history, the alliance between state power and the power of money remained the dominant pattern.

The Sufis did not distinguish, in their attitude toward wealth, between lawful and unlawful money.

This was a general revolutionary feature of the opposition, and its distant roots go back to the dispute over hoarding between the poor and the rich among the early Muslims.

There were two main positions.

The first held that the verse condemning hoarding had been superseded, in submission to the absolute authority of the religious text. But even those who took this position argued that wealth above a certain level became mixed with what was unlawful.

A statement attributed to Jaafar al-Sadiq puts the limit of unlawful wealth at twenty thousand dirhams. In dinars, this would be somewhere between one and two thousand.

To understand what this meant, remember that the purchasing power of a single dinar at that time was enough to support an ordinary family for a month.

Baghdad was an exception because its cost of living was extraordinarily high.

Al-Mubarrad reports in *al-Kamil* that the Kharijite Shabib once received word that one of his followers possessed seventy thousand dirhams.

He was horrified.

“No Muslim should have seventy thousand,” he said. “This is a temptation.”

The report does not tell us whether he believed this amount of money must necessarily be mixed with unlawful wealth, or whether he objected simply to so much being concentrated in the hands of one person.

The second position held that the verse against hoarding had never been superseded. This was the view associated with Abu Dharr.

Its followers were few, because the textual case for supersession by the verse of almsgiving seemed clear.

But radical opposition movements such as the Qarmatians placed formal limits on ownership. A person’s share was restricted to what he could obtain through his own manual labour, not through commerce, because their state monopolised trade.

Manual work would never produce anything approaching the level of forbidden hoarding.

The Qarmatians set aside the religious law as a whole, so they did not regulate their economy through its legal rulings.

Whatever the source of wealth, lawful or unlawful, the Sufis placed the rich among the worldly powers that had to be boycotted.

The boycott extended even to sitting with them and making friends with them.

A Sufi was not praised for befriending a rich man.

In this they put into practice Christ’s judgment excluding the rich from the Kingdom of God.

Sari al-Saqati extended it further, to include the **neighbors of the rich**.

We do not know whether he literally meant people living next door to the rich, or rather those who mixed with them and worked in their service.

The second interpretation is more plausible if we remember how strongly symbolic language dominates Sufi texts.

In our own time, “neighbors of the rich” can be extended to include politicians and intellectuals who defend the power of capital, or what some of them call the **market economy**.

For our people, the market itself belongs to the worldly powers.

The Sufi is not a slave of the market in this world or the next, as our master Abu Yazid tells us.

The break recommended by Sari therefore governs our relationship with these political and intellectual servants of the market.

Our position rests on two principles.

The first is that the Sufi must leave wealth behind, because gnosis requires non-possession.

Their definition of the Sufi is:

**someone who owns nothing.**

And also:

**someone whom nothing owns.**

In the language of the pre-Islamic Arabs, the Sufi is **liqāḥ**: he owns nothing and is owned by no one.

This definition brings together the two powers, the state and money.

The *liqāḥ* is someone the state does not own.

The Sufi is someone who owns no wealth.

Logically, therefore, he is someone whom neither a thing nor a person can own.

The worldly powers own one another.

The state owns the people of the market because they depend on the state to keep their market running.

The people of the market own the market.

But the market, in turn, owns them.

So they are not free.

That does not mean they are unhappy in this world.

Quite the opposite.

In practice, they monopolise happiness for themselves while ordinary people live in misery.

We do not say, as religious people do, that the human being was created for the next world. We therefore do not ask people to accept their lot patiently so that they can receive eternal happiness on Judgment Day.

We do not know whether Judgment Day will ever come.

Our people themselves have doubts about it.

For that reason they do not postpone people’s rights.

Their whole effort is directed toward giving people comfort in this world.

If Judgment Day comes, then that will be an additional happiness for them.

And if it does not come, they will at least have received their rights here.

Among us there is complete agreement that **rights must not be postponed**.

You may hear statements suggesting postponement from some people who are counted among us.

But not everyone counted among us truly belongs to us.

Some contemporary writers divide Sufism into categories such as believing Sufism and atheist Sufism, Shi‘i Sufism and Sunni Sufism.

Sufism cannot be classified in this way.

It exists only under its known conditions. If even one of those conditions is lost, the name and the identity are lost with it.

We divide it instead into **social Sufism** and **Sufism of knowledge**. Later, in the section on Love, we will speak about **Sufism of feeling**.

Sufism is concerned with the Divine. It is neither believing nor atheist, neither Shi‘i nor Sunni.

Those who speak the language of postponed justice may be believers, Sunnis, or Shi‘is.

But as Sufis, they do not qualify.

Every people has its impostors.

Freedom requires leaving ownership behind.

The Sufi is free because he owns nothing.

That is why the word *Sufi* became so closely identified with the word *poor*.

But leaving ownership behind does not mean simply abandoning wealth to those who already possess it.

It is a beginning—a first condition—for taking wealth out of the hands of those who monopolise it.

When the Sufi gives up money, he is gathering the strength needed to make wealth common and prevent it from being concentrated.

We will return to this in the section on Communalism.

But if this is the Sufi’s condition, how does he live?

He has to live if he is going to continue working to take wealth from those who monopolise it and turn it into common wealth.

The Sufi lives by working.

There are two kinds of work: work by hand, and work in the market.

They ranked them in that order.

Manual work comes first.

If that is not possible, then work in the market. This is the worse of the two choices, but it is still better than idleness.

That is how al-Makki ranks them in *The Nourishment of Hearts* (2/5).

Work in the market did not mean commerce in the ordinary sense. The Sufis were not very good at that.

It usually meant opening a small shop that produced enough to support a family, if the person had one, or simply to support himself.

This was easier for them.

Sari al-Saqati had a shop in the Baghdad market.

Manual work included crafts, trades, and service.

One form of service was orchard watch, guarding gardens. Ibrahim ibn Adham worked at this.

Their crafts were generally simple: weaving palm leaves, engraving, weaving cloth, and similar work.

A person might inherit money from his father or mother. If it was a large amount, he might give most of it away and keep enough to support himself for the rest of his life.

Or he might distribute all of it, or refuse it altogether, if the amount was too large.

Naturally, they did not take money from the ruler.

But money might occasionally reach them from some branch of government—from a lower official who occupied a special position within the state.

This did not happen much with the Sufis themselves. It did happen with some of the earlier saints, such as Sufyan al-Thawri.

Sufyan was an oppositional jurist who lived and died in hiding from the Abbasids. He belonged to the period before Sufism fully flourished after the second Islamic century.

Sufi biographies still include him among their people because he lived in their manner, with asceticism and simplicity, and never married.

He supported himself from a fixed sum of money that lasted him throughout his life.

There are two accounts of where this money came from.

One says he inherited around ten thousand dirhams.

He is reported to have said:

“If I did not have it, the Abbasids would have walked all over me and squeezed me dry.”

The particular word used here does not appear in the dictionaries, though it survives in Iraqi speech. Iraqis say that a person *yatamandal* when he walks slowly and with a certain swagger.

The other account appears in Yaqut’s entry on Yahya ibn Khalid al-Barmaki in *The Dictionary of Literary Men*.

It says that Yahya regularly gave money to Sufyan, and Sufyan prayed for him.

This account deserves to be accepted because the Barmakids secretly supported the opposition.

The two accounts can therefore reinforce rather than contradict one another.

The Sufis themselves rarely had opportunities like this because, as we said, they did not usually take part in politics.

Sufyan was a professional political activist. He took part in the Basra uprising led by Ibrahim ibn Abd Allah against al-Mansur, which was why he spent the rest of his life in hiding.

We may perhaps find a similar case—only as a possibility, though a plausible one—in the relationship between al-Hallaj and the minister Ali ibn Isa.

Ali ibn Isa was one of the great ministers of the Abbasid state.

He was accused of sympathising with the Qarmatians.

Al-Hallaj became active in Baghdad during his period in office. He was arrested and given several lashes, then released and left alone.

He remained safe until Ali ibn Isa was dismissed.

Al-Muqtadir then appointed Hamid ibn al-Abbas, who oversaw al-Hallaj’s trial and killing.

Our people later created the story we heard earlier about Ali ibn Isa: the woman rebukes him, he renounces everything, and he settles in Mecca.

In reality, he died in Baghdad after losing office.

But the story may hint at some kind of relationship with al-Hallaj.

The Sufis might also accept support from a merchant who met very specific conditions.

Abu Yazid al-Bistami, for example, was supported by a merchant named Ibrahim Muadhan.

Abu Yazid’s biographer says that this man was so scrupulous, honest, pious, and devout that he refused to sell cloth on cloudy days, saying that cloth looked better in clouded light than it did on clear days.

Through his generosity and constant support, Ibrahim earned a special place in Abu Yazid’s heart.

Abu Yazid said:

“God has a friend named Ibrahim, and we too have a friend named Ibrahim.”

He also said:

“Some people joined us with themselves. Some joined us with their money. Some joined us with their hearts. Ibrahim Muadhan joined us with himself, his money, and his heart all together.”

He brought contentment and fullness, honour and standing.

Without things like this, hearts would scatter and life itself would hardly be worth anything.

Muadhan sent enough every day to meet the needs of one hundred of Abu Yazid’s companions.

From this we can understand that Ibrahim Muadhan himself entered the path and specialised in the cloth trade. Abu Yazid seems to have assigned him, among all his companions, the particular role of providing for the group.

The simplicity of Sufi life reduced their need for money.

They wore clothes only for modesty and warmth.

They did not spend time choosing furniture for their homes.

Their food was kept within the limits required to stay alive.

Their biographers exaggerated how little they ate.

This does not agree with the laws of medicine, because eating too little leads to malnutrition and directly harms the body.

I have personal experience of this.

For a number of years I reduced the amount I ate and cut down the range of foods in my diet. I also stopped eating meat.

Signs of malnutrition began to appear in my body. My lips became swollen.

Alarmed, I went to my learned physician Abd al-Sattar al-Rifai and to my older brother Kazim, who specialises in pharmacy.

They agreed that the problem was malnutrition and gave me a dietary programme that remained vegetarian.

The symptoms disappeared and my health improved.

This experience made me much more critical of the stories told about how little our masters ate and how simple their diets were.

If those stories were literally true, the same thing that happened to me would have happened to them, and they would not have survived.

Yet most of them lived to old age.

What we can reasonably accept is that they did not indulge in food the way rich people and men of the state did.

They did not live to eat.

They ate to live.

And that correct formula—**eat in order to live**—helped them live longer than rich people and men of the state, who burn through their lives in pursuit of appetite.

#### Flash

I said that leaving ownership behind is a first step toward taking property away from the worldly powers and making wealth common among people.

Anyone who remains attached to ownership cannot reach that goal.

During the long struggle between ownership and communalism, men and women have entered the path, repeated all the right words about poverty and wealth, and adopted doctrines calling for shared property.

But they failed to cut their attachments.

They behaved like people who leave to God what is God’s and to Caesar what is Caesar’s.

They committed themselves to politics but did not commit themselves to deprivation.

When some of them reached power in the name of the poor, they immediately began to behave like hoarders.

The reason is that they entered the path with something of the old self still clinging to them.

They continued to live among pleasures and possessions.

They had never tasted deprivation, so they never learned its flavour or discovered its pleasure.

For our people, deprivation is the happiness sought by the traveller and the knower.

Anyone who cannot be happy with deprivation cannot make the deprived among ordinary people happy.

Instead, he begins competing with them for their share of the wealth that belongs entirely to them.

Deprivation is the greatest condition placed on anyone among us who exercises power.

Ali ibn Abi Talib chose it.

Umar ibn Abd al-Aziz chose it.

Hamdan ibn al-Ashath and his successors in eastern Arabia chose it.

In our own time, the leaders of the Russian Bolsheviks and the sages of Chinese communism chose it.

Because of it, enormous benefits became available to ordinary people, especially where these experiments lasted a long time, as among the Qarmatians and the Chinese.

Hunger was lifted, and people received their share of society’s wealth.

Conditions later deteriorated in Russia and China when the next generation turned away from the principle of deprivation and began to rule like hoarders.

The psychological basis of deprivation is expressed in an Iraqi proverb:

**“The full man makes tharid slowly for the hungry.”**

*Tharid* is made by breaking bread into pieces and soaking it in the broth of cooked meat. It is one of the traditional staple dishes of the Arabs.

A saying even calls it the lord of all foods.

The proverb means that someone who is already full takes his time preparing food for the hungry because he cannot feel their hunger.

We take wisdom from the people just as the people take wisdom from us.

Experience and knowledge move in both directions.

The only authority that can guarantee food to the hungry is one whose leaders choose to deprive themselves of the privileges and pleasures of rulers.

Only such an authority can do it.

#### Aside

In *The Nourishment of Hearts*, we are told that Sulayman al-Taymi stopped eating wheat because it was ground in a watermill that charged people for its use.

Water belongs to everyone.

No one has the right to trade in it, whether it is being used for drinking, washing, or as a source of mechanical power.

The same book tells another story.

A woman brought Bishr al-Hafi a basket of grapes.

“These are from my father’s vineyard,” she said.

He returned them.

“Glory be to God!” she said. “Do you doubt my father’s honour? Do you doubt that the vineyard lawfully belonged to him and that I inherited it? Your own name is written as a witness on the deed of sale.”

“You corrupted the vineyard,” he replied.

“How?”

“You watered it from the River of Tahir.”

Tahir ibn al-Husayn belonged to the powerful Tahirid family of the second and third Islamic centuries.

The river bearing his name had been dug as part of state irrigation projects.

Using its water was forbidden for several reasons.

First, people were charged for it, even though water is common property.

Second, such excavation projects were carried out through forced labour, or by making workers carry burdens beyond their strength while paying them less than they deserved, along with other forms of injustice.

Third, the river was itself a state facility.

#### Further Note

We said that manual work came first among the acceptable ways of earning a living.

They listed the forms of work they regarded as lawful and free of moral responsibility: gardening, sailing, tailoring, blacksmithing, cloth-cleaning, shoemaking, working as a copyist or bookseller, carrying goods, barbering, baking, fishing, spinning, and building houses—the last of which was Bishr al-Hafi’s trade.

They also included working in bathhouses, carpentry, and similar occupations.

### The Sufi Station

For the Sufis, opposition is a **station**.

A station is a quality that becomes firmly rooted in the Sufi through disciplined effort.

Most of them place the station above the **state**.

A state is temporary. It arrives and does not normally remain for long.

States include sorrow, exhilaration, contraction and expansion, longing, anxiety, and tranquillity.

Stations, besides opposition, include asceticism, scrupulousness, contentment, and patience.

Taken together, the stations form the conditions of opposition.

The Sufis gave many definitions of the station and the state.

In *The Life of Hearts*:

> Stations are the stopping places of those travelling toward God. States are passing conditions.

Stations are acquired.

States are gifts.

In other words, a person reaches a station through disciplined effort, whereas a state descends on the Sufi’s heart without preparation or effort.

They disagreed over whether states could last.

Some said that they could remain and become permanent. If they did not last, they were merely flashes and passing experiences.

Our own view is that the state is the same thing as the station, except that it comes without effort.

It is a gift.

One example of a lasting state is **awe**, or **majesty**, which arises from knowledge of divine grandeur.

This is not merely a passing condition. It effectively has the character of a station.

Others said that states are temporary, but can become stations through effort and discipline.

A person who is not naturally forbearing, for example, may train himself to act with forbearance.

To become truly forbearing, he has to struggle to behave that way until it becomes habitual.

The struggle is the state.

Once repeated and made habitual, it becomes a station.

On this account the state is something acquired, not something simply given.

Yet they also say that states come from the very source of generosity and are therefore gifts.

Tranquillity is one example.

It descends on the Sufi’s heart, takes him out of agitation and obsessive thought, and brings him toward a state of attraction.

They say that a person in a state is drawn out of his previous condition.

Tranquillity is therefore not chosen.

It descends.

But it does not remain for long, so it never becomes a quality attached permanently to the self. We do not normally say that a person is characterised by tranquillity.

A person may, however, be known for awe, asceticism, love, scrupulousness, or opposition—opposition being the station of stations.

He is not normally known for exhilaration, sorrow, contraction, or expansion, because these are flashes rather than settled qualities.

Three of the highest stations are **travel, estrangement, and witnessing**.

The traveller who wants to arrive must continue travelling. He does not settle.

This is permanent migration: movement from one state to another, or one station to another, as the self rises through the stages of space and time.

This is the intellectual migration called for by the chief of philosophers in *The Four Journeys*.

The station of estrangement accompanies the station of migration.

The migrant is a stranger.

But the Sufi’s estrangement is not what contemporary writers call **alienation**.

Estrangement is chosen and worked for.

Alienation is a condition into which a person falls without choice and without seeking it.

Estrangement is positive.

Alienation is negative.

The Sufi therefore does not rebel against his estrangement.

He lives it and bears it because he chose it and preferred it to remaining where he was.

In fact, estrangement can be a way of escaping the alienation found in the place one has left behind.

The Sufis were fond of a famous saying:

> Islam began as something strange, and it will become strange again. Blessed are the strangers.

Perhaps they invented it themselves.

In any case, it praises estrangement and the stranger because the stranger departs from the ordinary condition of people, who have become alienated from the essence of their own selves.

Estrangement makes it possible to recover that essence if it has been lost, or to protect it if it is threatened.

These three—travel, estrangement, and witnessing—are conditions for entering the station of opposition.

The first requires a complete break with the worldly powers.

The annihilation required by witnessing means forgetting the self so that a person gains the ability to do without those powers.

Someone who has disappeared from himself abandons his own demands.

He becomes neither sought nor seeking.

Such a person travels lightly.

Witnessing itself has three forms.

The first is **witnessing through the Real**: seeing things through the signs of unity.

The second is **witnessing the Real through things**.

The third is **witnessing the Real itself**, reaching certainty without doubt.

All three are found among Sufis, whether their path is one of knowledge or social action.

What unites them is the attempt to reach a level at which the knower becomes one with the essence of things, so that when he speaks it is as though the things themselves were speaking.

*The Life of Hearts* also says that states arise from stations.

Sorrow and contraction come from the station of fear.

Expansion comes from the station of hope.

Exhilaration comes from love, and love from witnessing beauty.

The state therefore follows from the station.

To my mind, this is more convincing than giving the state the qualities of a station.

To complete our discussion of state and station, we need to understand **contraction and expansion**.

These are among the most difficult paths because the same great master can pass back and forth between them.

Contraction is defined as a noble state of the knower, accompanied by reserve, in which he gives up even permitted pleasures and concessions in matters such as eating, drinking, and speaking.

While in contraction, the knower wants nothing except knowledge of the Creator.

This condition cannot last, because it collides with the needs of the body.

The knower therefore moves from contraction into expansion.

Expansion in the station of the heart is like hope in the station of the self.

It comes with a sign of acceptance, gentleness, mercy, and intimacy.

Contraction, however, is the foundation.

The Sufi does not expand for the sake of his own needs because his concern lies elsewhere.

Contraction and expansion belong together:

**contraction for oneself, expansion for others.**

Do you not see how the master denies himself chicken so that a widowed family can eat it?

The Sufi contracts himself against himself.

So contraction does not usually show on his face.

What shows is his warmth toward other people.

But contraction can never be absolute because the Sufi must take what is permitted in the amount required to keep himself alive.

That is why he eats and drinks.

Food and drink are not subject to total contraction.

Contraction applies only to taking more of them than nature requires.

It also applies to other things, such as marriage.

We will return to that in the section on Love, along with other needs that will appear as the discussion unfolds.

Opposition is the goal of all the stations.

Through opposition, the Sufi becomes a Sufi.

### The Communalism of Humanity and the Sufi

To reach freedom by giving up ownership is to enter **communalism**.

As I said earlier, the knower does not give up wealth in order to leave it in the hands of those who possess it.

He gives it up in order to take it from them and make it common.

The Arabic word *mushāʿ* means something made common: something circulated among people and not reserved for one person rather than another.

It comes from the verb *ashāʿa*, to make something common.

From the same root comes *shāʿa*: something spreads, becomes familiar, circulates, and becomes known to everyone.

Modern Arabic formed from this root the word *shuyūʿiyya*, communism.

This modern coinage has caused a great deal of harmful confusion. Many people have assumed that communism itself must therefore be a modern invention imported from Europe.

But the word may be new while the thing it names is old.

There is no necessary connection between the age of a name and the age of the thing named.

The name is accidental.

The thing itself is essential.

The essence remains while names change.

That is why languages contain so many synonyms: several names can refer to the same thing.

One of the older Arabic names for communism was **tasbīl**.

It comes from *sabīl*, the road.

To *tasbīl* wealth is to place it on the road, as it were, so that people can take it and divide it fairly among themselves.

Or it means putting wealth “in the path of God”—that is, distributing it among God’s servants.

The Sage of Maarra uses the term in his *Luzumiyyat*:

> Divide up all your abundant wealth,
> and make the land free for all.

A **public water fountain**, *māʾ al-sabīl*, is a place that provides drinking water to people free of charge. Such fountains are common throughout Arab lands.

Iraqis even use words from the same root as insults.

They may call a woman *mustaballa*—whether she deserves it or not—meaning a woman held in common by men.

This is the supposed “communism of women” that Europeans insist on talking about, and that some Easterners repeat after them.

*Tasbīl*, communism, and communalism are therefore different names for the same thing.

Economically speaking, communalism is the opposite of ownership.

What is common cannot be privately owned.

The history of the East—and by the East I mean Asia—is formed by the struggle between communalism, feudal ownership, and the commercial economy.

Most of the sages of the East were communalists.

Most of its politicians were feudal.

The sages exchanged knowledge with ordinary people.

The politicians exchanged favours with property owners.

Communalism succeeded in imposing itself not only in culture but in material life.

It prevented feudalism from becoming independent of the land and dominant over society as it became in the West.

Yet communal consciousness was the highest fruit of the struggle.

It became the unifying truth within wisdom, which is philosophy, and philosophy, which is wisdom.

The sages of China were communalists as a whole, apart from a small minority among the Legalists and conservative Confucians.

The sages of Islam were also communalists, if by sages we mean the Sufis rather than the philosophers.

The philosophers belonged intellectually to the Greeks—that is, to Western philosophy, which was governed by commercial logic.

The philosophers of Islam were philosophers, not sages.

Wisdom was found in Sufism and in exceptional thinkers such as the Sage of Maarra.

The Sufis traced their communal tradition back to Christ.

Christ spoke its language in a form close to the Tao. He expelled the rich from his kingdom and reserved it for the poor.

The Sufis, as we have already seen, live in the same spiritual time as their prophet.

What they took from the son of Mary became one of their great points of reference, especially in their teachings on renunciation and stripping oneself of possessions.

In this they followed his path more closely than that of any other prophet.

The son of Mary is our prophet of communalism.

His counterpart among the Chinese is Laozi.

The book of the Tao and the book of Jesus speak the same language.

But Laozi is a philosopher while Christ is a social fighter.

I say this to explain the difference between the tendency toward abstraction in Taoist speech and the simplicity of the words attributed to Jesus.

Christ does not belong to the class of cultural intellectuals.

He is an unlettered messenger, like the son of Amina.

But he did not break away from ordinary unlettered people.

He stayed among them.

That is the secret of his crucifixion and the survival of his successor.

I do not want the disciple to understand from this that everyone who stays with ordinary people is always destined for crucifixion.

I have no desire to multiply martyrs.

The Sufis of Islam followed the path of their first teacher and worked for **creation**—for ordinary people.

In the language of our own age, working for people means working in the economy, because people find relief when they possess enough to spare them exhaustion and grinding labour, and when they have food, clothing, shelter, dignity, and self-respect.

Dignity belongs first to the people as a whole, not to isolated individuals.

The individual is a part.

The people are the whole.

If dignity exists in the whole, it reaches each of the parts.

It cannot begin from the part because dignity confined to one part does not include everyone.

It has to begin from the whole and spread to the individual.

This is the collective spirit of the East.

Ordinary people understand it in everyday life better than the elite do.

An Umayyad caliph once stopped before a woman and asked what she needed.

She told him what the whole neighbourhood needed.

“I asked what *you* need,” he said. “Why are you asking me for the needs of the neighbourhood?”

She replied:

“I would hate to see the head of the valley flourishing while its far end lies barren.”

The phrase needs explanation. The reader can be forgiven: fourteen centuries stand between us and her language.

By **flourishing**, she meant growing green and prosperous.

By **barren**, she meant dry and empty.

Did I not tell you that we take wisdom from the people?

This unlettered woman spoke in the language of the collective, communal consciousness of the peoples of the East.

It is a difficult logic.

The elite do not understand it.

If you presented it to the intellectuals of our time, many of them would struggle with its meaning because it throws a certain shadow of confusion over the principles of scientific economics.

Our people had no detailed plans or programmes for putting communalist principles into practice.

Christ’s idea was to create communal groups inside the existing society.

The apostles tried to do this after his crucifixion. They practised it among themselves and among their small number of followers.

But they ran into the violence of the Roman Empire, and their hopes failed.

Paul had only recently become a Christian.

He took the idea from them and turned it into a religion and a church.

When Mazdak launched his movement, he could not overturn the Sasanian Empire.

He therefore turned to gradual absorption and succeeded for a time under King Qubad, who accepted some of his demands.

Then Qubad turned against him.

How could an emperor become a follower of Jesus?

Abu Dharr and his companion Salman wanted to communalise Islam.

Abu Dharr ended up in the desert of al-Rabadha.

Salman returned to the land of his birth and died there in despair.

Then came Hamdan’s turn in the Iraqi countryside.

He founded a community like the one the apostles had wanted, but with fuller and more developed elements.

Then he collided with the Abbasid Empire, which drowned his movement in blood.

Hamdan’s companions later found refuge on the Arab shores of the Persian Gulf, isolated by desert to the west and water to the east.

There they held out for nearly two centuries and established a fully communal system.

During this same period al-Hallaj was moving toward alliance with them.

He met the fate of Christ because he acted at the centre, and the centre is guarded.

Faced with these painful struggles, our people found another route.

They rooted the principles of opposition in the cultural sphere.

They succeeded to an extraordinary degree, stamping our culture with the mark of freedom from and hostility toward the state.

They could not move beyond that sphere in the face of the ruler’s power.

So they left the rest to those who would come after them.

The sages of China did the same.

Then the modern age arrived.

Christ, Mazdak, Hamdan, the people of the Tao, and the people of Sufism returned under new names.

Remember: names are accidents.

What they name is the essence.

In Russia—standing on the edge of the East and wrapped in its communal traditions—they were called **Bolsheviks**.

When one of them declared Christ a Bolshevik, he was simply expressing this Russian communal consciousness.

A new sun rose over the earth.

Trotsky called it:

**the government of the poor.**

The Chinese communists followed.

They came from the depths of their own communal wisdom and created a communism made from Marxist economics Sinicised through local communal traditions.

They provided food, clothing, shelter, and dignity to one fifth of humanity.

Then what happened, happened:

> What happened, happened. I will not tell.
> Think well of it, and do not ask for news.

Now we are back at zero.

But zero is the beginning of the beginning.

As we said before, beginnings do not emerge from emptiness.

Remember the saying of Huainan:

> A beginning has a beginning, and every beginning has a beginning. There is no beginning without a beginning.

We are walking across heaps of beginnings, all of them filled with things.

We can begin again, strongly and correctly, because we have tried and fallen, tried and fallen, tried and...

Perhaps next time we will try and not fall.

One of the masters was once asked:

“Who was your teacher?”

He replied:

“A beetle.

“I was sitting in my room with the lamp burning. A beetle came in and tried to climb it. I left it alone to see what it would do.

“It put its feet on the glass and fell.

“Then it tried again and fell.

“It kept trying and falling until I counted seven hundred attempts.

“Then I left the room to attend to something.

“When I came back, I found the beetle sitting on top of the lamp.”

I have nothing to propose for the new beginning.

But I do say that any beginning must rise from the accumulated experience of what came before.

The Russian model ignored that accumulated experience.

The Chinese model drew on it.

China did not fall as China in the way Russia fell as Russia.

Rather, its communist experiment was surrounded and contained through a collaboration between corrupt elements inside and capitalist forces outside.

Perhaps it is now on the road back to its origins.

Along that road it still possesses the accumulated experience of Chinese communism and a communist inheritance stretching back at least two thousand years.

I once saw a vision that moved me from responsibility for culture to responsibility for communalism.

I swore that I would give whatever years remained to me to reviving this station.

Communal work means building **grassroots communism**—the communism found in all the roots to which we return.

What our age produced was **top-down communism**, built by translators.

What the top builds belongs to the top.

And whoever translates merely speaks in someone else’s tongue.

The reader will find our communalist programme attached to the end of these orbits.

### Love

The Arabic words *maḥabba* and *ḥubb* can sometimes mean the same thing, and sometimes not.

*Ḥubb* usually means intense emotional love between a man and a woman, or a woman and a man, though the first usage is more common.

*Maḥabba* is more often ordinary affection.

If we say that there is *ḥubb* between a man and a woman, people understand that the two are joined by the powerful emotion that draws the sexes together.

If we say that there is *maḥabba* between two brothers, people understand that each loves the other with brotherly affection.

The Sufis preferred *maḥabba* to *ḥubb*, except in poetry, where metre or rhyme might require the other word.

They had many sayings about love.

The one I find most fitting is this:

> Love is a state a person finds in the heart that carries him gently beyond mere worship.

There is a dangerous secret in that sentence.

It releases love from the constraint of worship.

In other words, the lover is no longer a slave.

Earlier in these pages we said that a slave does not love his master.

If he comes to love him, the quality of slavery disappears and the two stand on one level.

Love is the first valley of annihilation, the pass through which the traveller enters the dwellings of effacement.

The theologians objected to the Sufis that love cannot properly be directed toward God.

How can we love something that resembles nothing, that nothing resembles, and that has nothing in common with our own nature?

Surely, they argued, we can love only something of our own kind.

The Sufis answered that every rational person feels love and attraction toward anyone described by qualities of perfection: knowledge, generosity, giving, goodness, mercy, compassion, and so on.

We do not love such a person merely because of his outward appearance.

We may hear of him without ever seeing him.

And even if we do see him, we may not find his appearance attractive.

Our love is therefore directed toward his inner qualities.

On this point, the Sufis are more logical than the theologians.

Love is not directed only toward visible individuals.

It can also be directed toward abstractions.

Al-Qushayri divided love into five levels:

**sensory, psychic, heartfelt, rational, and spiritual.**

Sensory love, in its true form, means obeying commands.

For them, this was the lowest degree of love.

The love of the self is attraction without attainment—that is, without receiving anything in return.

He seems to mean what we now call **unrequited love**.

I once heard a woman in an Egyptian film say that unrequited love is the highest form of love.

Heartfelt love is a fire in the heart that burns away everything except what the beloved wants.

Nothing remains in the lover’s heart except the beloved.

Everything else burns.

Rational love means keeping watch over the beloved both in presence and in absence.

That is, the lover remains faithful to what the beloved wants whether the beloved is present or not.

Ibn al-Dumayna—one of the great masters of complete love, the love that expects nothing in return—expressed it this way:

> I am so shy before you that it is as though
> even when you are absent, a watcher from you is over me.

Spiritual love means giving the spirit itself to the beloved.

The goal of sensory love is **benefit**: receiving what one wants from the beloved.

This is the lowest level and corresponds most closely to love in its usual European sense.

The goal of the soul’s love is **tawājud**, the stirring of ecstasy.

The goal of the heart’s love is **wajd**, ecstasy itself.

The goal of rational love is **wujūd**, being.

The goal of spiritual love is **encompassing**.

These degrees rise from the lower to the higher.

The stirring of ecstasy is the road to ecstasy itself.

A distance must be crossed between the soul and the heart because the soul still has its own claims and interests, which keep it close to the sensory level.

Once those are crossed, the lover enters ecstasy—that is, the heart.

He then reaches being at the rational level because reason is the perfection of a self that has moved beyond the constraint of pure emotion.

The spirit, finally, encompasses being.

Because it is so detached from material things, it can pass beyond what lies nearest to the senses and move toward the farther horizon.

Love has five objects.

The first is the existence of the self.

A human being loves his own existence, perfection, and survival.

The second is love for someone who benefits him in a way that helps preserve his existence and protect him from destruction.

This is really part of his love for himself.

The third is love for a person who does good to people in general even if he has never done anything for the lover personally.

This belongs to loving good for other people as one loves it for oneself.

A person of this kind loves both the one who benefits him and the one who benefits other people.

Such a lover has trained himself in the principles of Tao and Sufism until his love for other people becomes like his love for himself.

The fourth object is love for anything beautiful in itself, whether its beauty appears outwardly in visible form or inwardly in meaning.

This is love of beauty, whether embodied or abstract.

It includes loving a woman for her beauty and loving her for her perfection.

You may love her because she is beautiful.

You may love her because she is complete.

The highest beloved is one you love for both beauty and perfection.

For the Sufis, inner meanings are also forms of beauty.

Those meanings are qualities.

The fifth object is love for someone with whom one shares a true inward affinity.

If all these qualities came together in one person, love would inevitably multiply.

Imagine a child who is beautiful in appearance, good in character, complete in knowledge, sound in judgment, kind to other people, and kind to his parents.

Such a child would be loved to the highest degree.

But complete love, gathering every cause of love together, exists only in relation to God.

He is loved, and His perfection lies in His essence, His attributes, and His actions.

Our master Abu al-Ala does not accept this.

For him, deficiency reaches even the highest essence because of the vast amount of evil he sees in the world.

He regarded these evils as God’s doing.

On this point he disagreed with his teacher al-Razi, who regarded evil as the work of the soul and held that the Creator would not have wanted it had the soul’s will not overcome His own.

This is one of the disagreements between the Sage of Maarra and the Sufi masters.

He saw nothing worthy of love either in existence or in the one who brought existence into being.

I remain torn between him and them.

When I look at the abundance of evil, I agree with him.

But then I see things that are good and worthy of love, and I understand the Sufis too.

Even so, I think the Sage of Maarra has the wider excuse.

I do not agree with his absolute rejection of love.

He himself never put a thick curtain between himself and beauty.

Was he not the man who painted a picture of the beauty of night and the stars that no painter could equal?

One condition of love is that longing for everything except the beloved must come to an end.

Anyone who turns his eyes toward another remains veiled from the beloved.

A story is told of a man who saw a beautiful woman and became completely absorbed in her.

“All of me is occupied with all of you,” he told her.

“If all of you is truly occupied with all of me,” she replied, “then all of me is offered to all of you.

“But I have a sister. If you saw how beautiful she is, you would forget me.”

“Where is she?”

“Behind you.”

He turned around.

She slapped him across the face.

“Liar,” she said. “If what you said were true, you would never have turned to look at someone else.”

#### Aside

They said that ordinary love between people necessarily involves pleasure in the beloved.

Love of God, they said, is different because the stations of ultimate reality are stations of bewilderment and astonishment.

By “stations of ultimate reality” they meant witnessing, nearness, and union.

Anyone who reaches these states becomes absent from everything, even from himself and from his own perceptions.

He is shattered, left in bewilderment, and becomes absent from pleasure and pain alike.

I say that this also happens in love between human beings.

There is no good reason to define human love by pleasure alone.

Here we run into a linguistic problem.

Older Arabic uses the word *ladhdha*, pleasure, as the opposite of pain, whether physical or emotional.

Modern Arabic began distinguishing between the two by developing the word *mutʿa*, enjoyment.

Today *mutʿa* is used mainly for non-physical pleasure.

We say:

“an enjoyable book,”

not:

“a delicious book.”

And we say:

“delicious food,”

not:

“enjoyable food.”

We say that someone enjoys looking at paintings.

We rarely say that he takes physical pleasure in looking at paintings.

Umar ibn Abi Rabia says:

> I am a man obsessed with beauty and follow wherever it leads;
> my only share in it is the pleasure of looking.

This is an example of the old language being too narrow.

It would have been better for him to say **the enjoyment of looking**, because looking at beauty belongs not to bodily sensation but to the soul and spirit.

Ordinary love between human beings can of course contain physical pleasure.

That is bodily pleasure specifically, arising from physical union between the lover and the beloved.

Ordinarily this happens when the two agree to marry.

But the Arabs also knew the pleasure that comes from the suffering of love.

One poet says:

> You who find relief in seeing me tormented through him—
> for you, I find torment sweet.

This lover means what he says.

Among the old Arabs, as among many Eastern peoples, love did not necessarily require union.

That is why the Sufis could bring love of human beauty and love of divine beauty together.

Sadr al-Din al-Shirazi made love of women a path toward love of heaven.

In this kind of love too there are moments of bewilderment and astonishment in which the lover becomes absent from pleasure and pain.

Only the enjoyment of the spirit remains as it becomes absorbed in the beauty of the human beloved.

Abstraction then raises that beauty toward the meanings of divine love.

We will return to this in a later section.

#### Samnun the Lover

Samnun was one of those established in the station of love.

When he spoke about love, stone itself seemed ready to crack.

The lamps in the mosque would sway at his words.

Once, while he was speaking, the lamps shattered from sheer delight at what they heard.

The people listening to him would lose themselves and wander away without knowing where they were going.

Their minds were carried out of themselves.

Even birds were overcome by amazement and bewilderment when they heard him speak of love.

Once, while he was talking, a bird flew down beside him.

It walked until it reached his lap.

Then it left him, struck its beak against the stone floor, and blood came out.

It kept bleeding until it died.

One of the great men was asked:

“Why does Samnun’s speech about love affect people’s hearts in a way that the words of others do not?”

He replied:

“A mother mourning her own dead child is not the same as a woman hired to mourn someone else’s.”

Samnun lived in Baghdad in the time of al-Junayd and al-Saqati.

One of his sayings was:

> A thing can only be expressed through something more delicate than itself. Nothing is more delicate than love. So through what can love be expressed?

#### Glimmer

Love has three forms.

The first is love because of **kindness received**.

The second is love because of **qualities**.

The third is love of the **essence itself**.

The first is the love of ordinary believers.

They love God because He has been good to them.

This is the lowest degree of love.

The second is the love of the elect.

It is directed toward the qualities from which acts of kindness and all other actions proceed.

It therefore stands above the first.

The third is the love of the elect among the elect.

This is directed toward the essence itself.

It has no motive and no cause.

The heart has simply been made to drink it in.

The lover no longer pays attention to what he receives or loses, to benefit or harm.

He loves because of the perfection of the essence and the greatness of its qualities.

It is the love of beauty, perfection, and majesty together.

This degree of love is the difficult road leading to the dwellings of glory.

The lover is consumed within it.

Reward disappears from his awareness because his love is directed toward the essence itself, not toward what the essence does for him.

He approaches effacement.

The lover gives up his own demands for the sake of the beloved.

In that moment—a moment that becomes long and almost eternal—the lover loses sight of his own share and lives for the share of the beloved.

One of its greatest expressions is when the knower gives up what he wants for the sake of ordinary people.

The beloved at this level is the Absolute, appearing through a separate being who becomes spiritualised in the lover.

But in truth the beloved is not that separate being.

The separate being is the symbol around which love turns, not its final goal or resting place.

The final goal lies within the lover’s own heart and mind.

It merely appears to him in the form of the separate beloved.

This thing may be one of the great concerns carried by the knower—and the knower carries many concerns.

When it reveals itself to him, he is shaken, just as a lover is shaken at the moment of meeting the beloved.

Then he grows calm.

He moves along within it until he forgets his own claims and disappears.

Forgetting and effacement are conditions of arrival.

If the knower steps forward to fulfil his duty while still serving the claims of the self, he will never arrive.

This is the level reached by the fighter on the battlefield.

It is the highest level.

The great master reaches something similar when money falls into his hands and he distributes all of it among ordinary people without keeping anything for himself.

We can imagine it on an even larger scale.

Suppose Husayn ibn Mansur Abu al-Mughith—al-Hallaj—had succeeded in his revolution and taken power in Baghdad.

He would have seized the treasures of the caliphal palace and distributed them among the people of Baghdad without keeping anything for himself.

This is the effacement and annihilation our people mean when they make it the goal of witnessing.

The lover therefore enters completely into the attempt to satisfy the beloved.

This contains the happiness the lover is searching for.

He finds it in the effacement of annihilation.

Do not imagine that when the lover seizes the treasures of hoarders, distributes them to the poor, and keeps nothing for himself, he is acting like someone who has abandoned his own self and all its interests.

Do not imagine that he has done this without return or reward.

Far from it.

When the lover reaches this level, he reaches the highest happiness.

Sometimes that happiness becomes visible.

He dances.

Or laughs.

Or cries.

Others hold themselves to the manner of al-Junayd.

They remain like silent mountains while their hearts burst with the joy of meeting.

#### Completion

Unveiling is one of the fruits of love.

When the beloved knows that the lover is sincere in his love, he lifts the veil between them and reveals to him hidden knowledge and higher mysteries.

#### Postscript

*ʿIshq* is love carried to excess, and for that reason it is not a fitting word for the love of God. The saying, “Passionate love of women is a path to passionate love of heaven,” is mistaken. It would be better to say: “Love of women is a path to love of heaven.”

But this is only true when love of a woman becomes an abstraction rather than an attachment to the body. We will return to this later.

#### Dialogue with a Lover

Where have you come from?
From Him.

And where are you going?
To be near Him.

What is his food?
Remembering Him.

What does he drink?
Longing for Him.

And what does he wear?
His protection.

#### An Eastern Reminder

There are three kinds of Sufism: social Sufism, Sufism of knowledge, and rapturous, emotional Sufism. In exceptional Sufis, the three can overlap or separate. But I have found that they differ in the way they understand love.

We have the example of Shaykh Muhyi al-Din Ibn Arabi and his love for the wise young woman Nizam, of whom he said:

> Every name I mention is a name for her,
> and every home I mourn is her home.

On the surface, this looks like the love of a man for a woman. But it was not love of her body. The Shaykh did not marry Nizam. As far as we know, he met her only once, when she was with her Sufi father and the three of them were in Mecca for the pilgrimage.

Then they parted.

Nizam returned to her family and married. Muhyi al-Din continued his travels before eventually settling in Syria. We have no record of another meeting between them. Nor do we have anything from the Shaykh showing that he wanted to see her again.

He did not love her body or her individual person. Strictly speaking, then, he did not love her as a man ordinarily loves a woman, and therefore had no need to meet her. We have no report that he ever proposed marriage to her, and I suspect that even if she had proposed it to him, he would have refused.

Muhyi al-Din’s love belongs to the strange category of mixing fire with water—something impossible according to natural science, but possible for the Shaykh.

Its possibility for him does not mean that it literally happened in defiance of natural law. It points instead to the effect that would have been produced if it had happened.

And an effect can sometimes arise without its cause.

This belongs to the realm of mystical knowledge, not to science.

We can therefore say that Muhyi al-Din wanted to meet Nizam—but not as one person meeting another. What he wanted was the meeting’s effect: the effect that would have been produced if the meeting had actually taken place.

From the things he says about her, we can even say that he did meet her and speak with her after their meeting in Mecca. What tells us this is not the meeting itself but its effects. The meeting did not occur; its effects did.

This is what allowed Muhyi al-Din to unite his love of Nizam with his love of God.

The Kurdish pole Ahmad Khani spoke of the beloved disappearing into the folds of the soul. He warned the lover against betraying love, because the soul inhabited by the beloved would then disown him.

There is a hint here that love itself can disappear, leaving the beloved as a beloved without love.

This is Sufi love.

For this reason, I hesitate to classify Ibn al-Farid’s love as Sufi love. What I find in him is the love of a poet for a woman. Let us read from his famous poem in *tāʾ*:

> My soul tells me you will be the death of me.
> Let my soul be yours, whether you know it or not.
>
> You who deny me the sweetness of sleep
> and dress me in sickness and killing longing,
>
> have mercy on the little life still left in me,
> on what remains of my wasted body and ailing heart.
>
> I have nothing but my soul, and anyone who gives his soul
> for the one he loves cannot be called extravagant.
>
> If you accept it, you have granted me all I ask.
> What a wasted quest, if even this will not do.

How is this different from the poetry of al-Abbas ibn al-Ahnaf?

I am not questioning the truth of the feeling that burns through Ibn al-Farid’s poetry, nor the beauty of its rhythm or the delicacy of its language. Our subject is not love poetry as written by Ibn al-Farid and by other martyrs of love. What I question is whether this is specifically **Sufi** love.

It is the love of a man for a woman. It complains of separation and abandonment. It longs for meeting and union. The lover pleads and begs before his beloved, whether she is present or absent.

This is not the kind of love Abd al-Qadir al-Jilani meant when he said:

> When my eyes behold the faces of my beloveds,
> that is my prayer on the Nights of Wishes.
>
> Faces which, when they unveil their beauty,
> light all the worlds from every side.

Shibli resembles Ibn al-Farid in this respect.

We have evidence that Shibli was in love and that he concealed the name of his beloved. He himself declares this concealment in two magnificent lines quoted by al-Maʿarri in *The Epistle of Forgiveness*:

> Majnun of Amir proclaimed his passion,
> while I concealed mine and won my ecstasy.
>
> And when, on Judgment Day, they call,
> “Where are the people of passion?”
> I alone will step forward.

Al-Maarri went too far in criticizing him when he claimed that no one would grant Shibli this boast.

In truth, Shibli was proud of his secrecy in a society that demanded secrecy from a lover. But he had no right to boast over Majnun of Banu Amir, because Majnun had never been required to hide.

Among the Arabs, naming a woman—whether mother, wife, or beloved—had not originally been a source of shame. Poets openly named the women they loved, which is how we came to know Layla, Afra, Dumya, Lubna, Buthayna, Azza, and many others.

This continued until the end of the Umayyad period and the beginning of the Abbasid age, when Islamic society became more complicated and more restrictive, and women were increasingly shut away behind walls, veils, and prohibitions.

Shibli’s pride in concealing his beloved therefore makes sense by the standards of his own age, not by the standards of those who came before him.

Whatever the case, Shibli was a lover.

But his love was not Sufi, even though in other respects he was one of the great figures of Sufism of knowledge. His love poetry is the poetry of poets, not the poetry of mystics:

> When I reproach him, or others reproach him,
> he complains of what I have done and counts my faults.
>
> O you whose whole life with me is blame and anger,
> have I never once done well in all my days?

This is love poetry, not mystical ecstasy.

The same can be said of everything attributed to Shibli in this vein. I cannot turn his hidden beloved into a road to God, as I can with Muhyi al-Din’s beloved or al-Jilani’s beloveds.

Shibli wanted to meet her.

#### Illumination

Abu Yazid said:

> Whoever is slain by love, the blood-money for his death is vision.

Vision is the compensation owed to one killed by love.

Abu Yazid had reached a rank that gave him the right to claim this vision. He was a lover. We have no report that he loved a particular person, as Ibn Arabi did, but he spoke constantly of the path of passionate love in which the lover burns.

Notice the strange possibility of water itself burning.

The lover has merged into the Beloved, because for Abu Yazid union is absolute. His thoughts therefore move back and forth between his desire to see God and God’s desire to see him.

Who is seeing whom?

Does God want to see Abu Yazid, or does Abu Yazid want to see God?

Perhaps the question becomes meaningless once the two sides have become one: He and I—or He and He, I and I.

#### The Disappearance of Love

Abu Yaqub al-Susi said:

> Love is not complete until you pass from seeing love to seeing the Beloved, and knowledge of love disappears. The Beloved was already his in the unseen, and this was not through love. When the lover enters this relation, he becomes a lover without love.

When something reaches the furthest point of its movement, it turns back, breaks, and finally disappears.

The highest point of love is the disappearance of love and the survival of the lover.

To love without love, just as one can be drunk without drunkenness.

The Sufis do away with intermediaries in everything.

#### A Glimpse

Love cannot be confined to an individual form, because it is an act of the spirit. It arises when the lights of the higher world shine upon the spirit, burning away its individual forms and leaving it to flow through the seas of pure abstraction.

What this means is that one individual soul cannot love another except through the universal soul.

When love falls upon an individual soul, it removes that soul’s individuality and definite form and merges it into the universal soul.

This is the secret of love’s immortality. Philosophers had already reached the conclusion that immortality belongs to the universal soul, not to the individual one.

This also explains why love endures as a spiritual station rather than passing like a temporary state.

#### Warning

Some Sufis were accused of loving boys and keeping their company. Reports name particular individuals who did this and treat it as one of the ways Satan gained power over them.

I looked into the matter and found the reports to be as they were described. What became clear to me was that this belonged to Sufis of the lower ranks.

Sufism was a vast and sweeping movement, and every vast movement carries a great deal of foam along with it—foam that eventually disappears.

This is why I insist on distinguishing the **Sufism of the great poles**, the great Sufi masters, from Sufism as a mass movement.

I have removed from my orbits and letters the name of anyone to whom such behaviour was attributed. I have not quoted even acceptable things said by such people, because what is acceptable becomes stained when it passes through a tainted vessel. It is no longer acceptable.

#### Conclusion and Summary

The Sufis raised love to its universal level by stripping it of everything attached to it and restoring it to its simple unity.

What is simple can unite with what is simple. What is composite cannot.

Sufi love, as we have seen, is free of union and separation, complaint and reproach. There is no meeting or parting, no gathering or dispersal.

What it seeks is **vision**—not seeing with the physical organ of sight, but an act of inward perception.

The essential nature of Sufi love does not change with the object of love. Whether the beloved is God, a woman, a homeland, or humanity, the same principle applies: the lover is erased and annihilated in the being of the beloved.

In the case of women, Sufi love differs sharply from the love of poets, as we have seen directly.

For the Sufi, the woman is a spiritual and cosmic being. For the poet, she is part of a bodily relationship.

For the Sufi, a woman’s beauty is spiritualized. For the poet, it takes bodily form.

The poet wants to meet her. The Sufi wants annihilation.

The poet still possesses a complete self. The Sufi has passed beyond his self.

For this reason, the woman’s status and sanctity are far higher in the Sufi’s love than in the poet’s.

I have also found that the Sufis themselves differed in their schools of love.

Most of the great masters of social Sufism had no human beloveds. When they spoke of love, they meant merging into the universal spirit, symbolized by the divine name.

Human love appears much more often in rapturous Sufism,[^ms-v1-003] though there it tends to resemble the love of poets.

Its highest form appears in Sufism of knowledge, where Sufi love takes a human figure as its symbol in the fullest and most complete sense.

Human love appeared in Sufism of knowledge rather than social Sufism because the former contains an element of what might be called “intellectual luxury,” which gives the soul room to enjoy beauty.

The concerns of the Sufi of knowledge are lighter than those of the social Sufi. They are the concerns of cultural struggle rather than social struggle.

#### Digression — A Proposal for Married Love

Outside this world filled with Sufi ecstasy lies the ordinary world, crowded with people of every temperament, moral character, principle, and value.

Sufism, like science and culture, is a specialized field. Not everyone can enter it as easily as they enter a door open to the public.

Civilisation today, as in the past, rests on a division of labour, which means differences in professions, specialisms, and forms of knowledge.

Love in ordinary life presents a major problem.

Its harm may sometimes be greater than its benefit.

This is not the Sufi’s ecstasy. It is the love of poets and ordinary people.

Love often causes social ruin.

The best case is the love of an unmarried young man for an unmarried young woman, because this usually ends in marriage. It is safe love, with no destruction in it.

It should be left free to follow its course.

Society, the family, and institutions often interfere, making the lovers miserable and destroying their hope of marriage.

Yet love that ends in marriage often cools and weakens afterward.

This is natural.

Love is a state of emotional combustion. Meeting calms it; separation and absence set it blazing again.

If love is not to turn into its opposite after marriage, young people must understand something. I direct this especially to men, because they are the ones most likely to carry this passion and then empty it out: once two lovers have come together and built a family, the original flame cannot remain at the same intensity.

If a love wants to remain forever at full blaze, then the lovers would be better off not marrying.

But that is an unreasonable demand. A lover who is not a Sufi wants to meet his beloved. That is precisely what his love seeks.

The answer to the cooling of passion after marriage is to allow burning love to become **married love**.

Married love is the companionship that joins two people in a strong physical and spiritual bond.

The Quran contains a magnificent verse that explains the meaning of marriage:

> And among His signs is that He created for you spouses from among yourselves, so that you may find rest in them, and He placed between you affection and mercy.

Without question, this is the finest and most precise definition of marriage I have encountered in any literary or legal text.

Marriage is not simply a physical relationship mixed with burning passion. It is a spiritual relationship—a relationship of repose and refuge.

A man and a woman grow close to one another. He takes refuge in her and she in him from the hardships and exhaustion of life. Each becomes the other’s support against difficult people and difficult things.

This is the verse of marriage and married love: tranquillity, affection, and mercy becoming one.

Yet it does not ignore the physical needs of either partner. The Arabs even regarded fulfilment of these needs as one of the conditions for preserving affection within the family.

They described the ideal wife as a playful lover with her husband, while remaining chaste and properly adorned outside the home.

We should educate people in married love and make it the foundation for the family’s survival.

It must nevertheless be admitted that love outside marriage is widespread among men and has many female victims.

It is responsible for the destruction of many families, for ruining the lives of many innocent women, and for leaving their children displaced.

Two systems have allowed this behaviour to continue.

The first is the system of co-wives—polygamy—which allows a man to marry another woman in order to “renew his love.”

The second is the Western system, which permits illicit relationships outside marriage.

Both systems begin from the assumption that a man is too weak to resist a woman’s attraction, and that he should therefore have the right either to marry or to enter an illicit relationship with any woman he happens to like, with no need to observe moral principles or bring emotion under the control of the will.

Both the polygamous system and the Western one grant the same “rights” to an unmarried lover and a married lover.

They should not be treated alike.

An unmarried man marrying a woman he loves follows a natural and legitimate course.

But for a married man to marry another woman is an offence against his wife. It is almost a form of deliberate killing. Indeed, it resembles the occupation of someone else’s land, like the Jewish occupation of Palestine.

The same applies to entering an illicit relationship with another woman.

The law should prevent a man from doing this.

And a woman who allows herself to occupy another woman’s ground should be regarded as acting wrongly and as sharing responsibility for the man’s offence.

Just as the love of an unmarried person is natural and legitimate, the love of a married person should be restrained and treated as morally unacceptable.

Here the will must be given its proper role in controlling emotion.

Suppressing love is different from suppressing sexual desire.

Sexual suppression can cause psychological and physical disturbance. Marriage is the remedy for it.

Suppressing love, by contrast, turns it into a spiritual need and does not produce the same disorders.

Suppressed love is Sufi love.

This applies when a person has actually fallen in love.

What is more common among men, however, is simply the sexual desire for variety: a release from determination and moral discipline, a contest in masculinity.

The Chinese Communists banned this and punished it.

#### Supplement

The Sufi masters differed over whether a Sufi should marry.
