Some preferred celibacy and regarded marriage as “a descent from the summit of firm resolve to the low ground of permitted concessions.”

A **concession** here means something religious law allows a Muslim to enjoy without committing a sin.

Marriage, by this account, would be one such concession, although in reality it belongs to the strongly recommended practices.

Wearing soft, beautiful clothes is a concession. So is eating delicious food. If you choose not to wear such clothes or eat such food, you are not committing a sin. In fact, you may be rewarded for giving them up.

Marriage is different. A person who refuses it may be regarded as neglecting an important part of religion.

The concession is described as low ground because it stands opposite **firm resolve**: the force of will by which a person resists what he desires and rises above it.

Ibrahim ibn Adham, Abu Yazid al-Bistami, Bishr al-Hafi, and Maruf al-Karkhi did not marry.

Other masters said that marriage was necessary for a young disciple because it is difficult for a young man to resist a powerful sexual instinct.

They expressed this by saying:

> A young man’s discipline is not complete until he marries.

If he remains unmarried, his instinct remains unsatisfied. It may pull him into one deviation or another, disturb his condition, and prevent him from completing the path of discipleship.

For these Sufis, marriage meant one wife only. Very few of them had several wives.

Nor did they keep concubines. They did not buy enslaved women for sex or service, and they did not keep slaves.

Marriage, then, was a Sufi concession granted to the young man.

Problems could arise later, as a married Sufi advanced in knowledge and eventually reached the rank of a great pole, because at that stage he would normally cease to need a woman.

Yet a married Sufi kept his wife even after reaching this point, because these men had a strong sense of duty and responsibility. They did not abandon their partners simply because they no longer needed them.

We have no report of any of them divorcing his wife at this stage or abandoning his family and wandering off.

They believed in protecting the family, the woman, and the children.

Zhuangzi, however, went through a difficult experience on this path.

He reached the rank of wisdom while still relatively young and while his beautiful wife was still in the prime of her youth.

When he realised that he was no longer able to continue married life with her, he told her and offered her a divorce so she could marry another man and continue a normal life.

After some hesitation, she agreed.

She chose a man for herself.

Wanting to make sure of her future, Zhuangzi involved himself in the proposed marriage. He investigated the man and satisfied himself that he was a suitable husband for her.

The divorce went through. She left Zhuangzi’s home, and arrangements for the new marriage began.

But the marriage never took place.

She killed herself before the wedding.

It seems that she became trapped between her need for a husband and her loyalty to Zhuangzi, and resolved the contradiction through suicide.

Such precise calculations play a powerful role in the psychology of the Chinese individual and genuinely shape behaviour.

#### A Flash

They said:

> Whoever knows Beauty loves.
> Whoever knows Majesty stands in awe.

The attributes of God are divided between Beauty and Majesty.

Our Sufis favour the attributes of Beauty and give them precedence over the attributes of Majesty.

Beauty includes love, mercy, compassion, forgiveness, and pardon.

Majesty includes power, greatness, and grandeur.

Whoever knows God through Beauty loves Him.

Whoever knows Him through Majesty stands in awe of Him.

For the Sufis, awe is a rank beyond ordinary fear and reverent fear.

Ordinary fear belongs to the believer.

Reverent fear belongs to the learned person.

Awe belongs to the knower.

The knower stands in awe of God but does not fear Him.

Yet as long as awe remains, the veil remains. Divinity and servitude still stand apart.

This is why the Sufis take refuge in Beauty.

Love removes the distance between divinity and servitude. Lover and Beloved become equal, and the veil between them disappears.

I have already said that a slave cannot love his master.

If he loves him because he has seen beauty in him, he has already escaped the condition of slavery. The slave becomes master, and worship becomes a mutual duty:

> He praises me, and I praise Him.
> He worships me, and I worship Him.

Awe prevents love.

The attributes of Beauty are therefore needed to break it, because love is the foundation.

#### Farewell to the Orbit of Love

When the heart is perfected by the light of remembrance of the Essence and becomes a surging sea filled with the breezes of nearness, the purity of noble qualities flows through the channels of the soul’s character.

—*ʿAwārif al-ʿAwārif*

### Knowledge

At its origin, Sufism was both a social path and a path of knowledge.

Socially, it stood against the state and money.

In the realm of knowledge, it stood against religion.

Its social side was closely bound to the communalist tendency embodied by the *abdāl*, whose names we will give in the Orbit of Bāʾ.

Its intellectual side developed among the mystics who challenged religious authority and established a form of deism that did away with prophecy and approached heaven without intermediaries.

At this stage in the development of Sufi knowledge, both creed and religious law were surpassed.

Some Sufis even reached the point of what they called “abolishing works,” by which they meant the duties prescribed by religious law.

But this step became associated with moral and social dissolution and with unrestricted licence.

The great Sufi poles therefore purged themselves of it.

They wanted to strengthen the moral principles of Islamic society and add new ethical principles with communalist roots.

They continued to perform the prescribed acts of worship in their lawful form, but no longer for ordinary religious reasons.

They prayed in order to connect with the Absolute, not to enter paradise.

They fasted as spiritual discipline, not to earn reward.

They made the pilgrimage in order to return to the cradle and commune with the ancestors.

They also took part in war.

There were two main fronts: an eastern front against Indians and the Turks of Central Asia, and a western front against the Byzantines.

The eastern war was aggressive, and only a small number of Sufis outside the great poles took part in it.

The western war, by contrast, was defensive, fought against a historic enemy of the region and its people. Ibrahim ibn Adham and Amir al-Anbari therefore chose this front and did not go east, even though Ibrahim himself came from the East. As we noted earlier, he was an Afghan from Balkh.

War on the eastern front subsided as the Turks entered Islam, while the wars against the Byzantines and later the Crusaders continued.

What is striking here is the speed with which the peoples of the East entered Islam, compared with the refusal of the peoples of the West to do so. This supports Friedrich Engels’s statement that Islam was a religion suited to Eastern peoples, a remark found in his work on the history of early Christianity.

Sufism as a path of knowledge began to take clear shape with al-Bistami.

In his hands the ecstatic utterance reached its widest range, breaking through the veil of conventional beliefs and familiar habits.

Religion itself was shaken by the ecstatic sayings of Abu Yazid al-Bistami. They caused widespread confusion over doctrine and placed him at the centre of attack from both defenders of religious belief and those fascinated by ecstatic speech.

Al-Bistami underwent a profound spiritual experience that carried him beyond ordinary habits and made him speak as someone whose intoxication had overcome his sobriety.

His greatest hours were spent in this condition.

Ordinary people developed enormous faith in him. They even said that he could fly through the air.

A man once said to him:

“I have heard that you walk on water and through the air, and that when the call to prayer sounds you go to Mecca, bow in prayer, and return.”

Abu Yazid answered:

“The true believer is the one whom Mecca loves, so that Mecca itself circles around him and returns.”

He was pointing to the central place of the perfected human being in the universe.

On another occasion, according to al-Sahlaji, he said:

> I used to circle the House searching for Him. When I reached Him, I saw the House circling around me.

Ordinary people rarely develop this kind of faith in religious figures unless those figures have become spiritually alive, stayed close to the people, and worked for their welfare.

Stories of miracles gather mainly around the great Sufi poles and the Imams of the Prophet’s household.

The latter miracles are largely confined to Twelver Shiism. Miracles attributed to the Sufi poles, by contrast, spread widely among people of many different religious schools.

Religious authorities did not succeed in turning ordinary people against the Sufi poles by accusing them of heresy or apostasy.

The book by Ibn al-Jawzi mentioned earlier did little to change this.

But Sufi thought itself did not penetrate deeply among the public, because its language was difficult and highly symbolic.

The relationship between Sufi and public was built instead on the Sufi’s spirituality, his opposition to the state, and his love of people.

Sufi influence on the public therefore remained largely emotional and personal. It did not become an intellectual relationship.

In other words, the Sufis did not help shape general public consciousness in the way that Taoist, Mohist, and Confucian sayings shaped the Chinese personality.

This was partly because the language of the Chinese sages was easier to understand when they spoke about society and the state.

One must also remember that China had no class of religious clergy because China had no religion of the same kind. There was therefore no barrier standing between the Chinese public and the words of its sages.

Al-Bistami left no books.

What he left were sayings and ecstatic utterances recorded by his disciples and preserved throughout the Sufi sources, sometimes separately and sometimes in collections.

The best-known collection is titled *The Light from the Words of Abu Tayfur*. Abu Tayfur means Abu Yazid; his full name was Tayfur ibn Isa.

Abd al-Rahman Badawi edited the text and published it in three editions, the latest in 1978 through the Kuwait Publishing Agency under the title *Ecstatic Utterances of the Sufis*.

The compiler of *The Light* was al-Sahlaji, apparently a late author, as his Turkish surname suggests.

Yet many of the ecstatic utterances in the book also appear in early sources.

Perhaps they were taken from al-Junayd’s book explaining the ecstatic utterances of Abu Yazid al-Bistami. Al-Junayd belonged to the generation after al-Bistami.

For this reason, I describe al-Bistami as belonging to the **knowledge-oriented tendency** rather than to fully developed philosophical Sufism.

He did not plunge into the depths of Sufi philosophy. His concern was to draw the broad outlines of a Sufi approach to knowledge that broke away from traditional doctrine.

Al-Bistami did not yet speak explicitly of rejecting intermediaries, as al-Hallaj would do in the generation after him.

But he anticipated al-Hallaj in the themes of union and indwelling, and even went beyond him in declaring the end of the relationship of servitude and replacing it with complete equality.

The Sufi principle of freedom of belief and equality among religions can also be traced back to him.

He sided with minorities against majorities.

In *The Light*, it is said that he once passed a Jewish cemetery and said:

“They are excused.”

Then he passed a Muslim cemetery and said:

“They are deluded.”

In his biography in Sibt ibn al-Jawzi’s *Mirror of Time*, Abu Yazid tells this story about himself:

> I entered a monastery and saw people worshipping the cross. I looked at them and said, “Shame on you. Do you worship what can neither harm nor benefit you, and abandon the worship of the One who can benefit and does not harm?”
>
> Then a voice called to me: “We have no need of your advice. Go away. We have no use for you.”[^ms-v1-004]

A **voice** here means speech heard from an unseen source, usually attributed to a jinn, an angel, or some other spiritual being.

Modern Arabic later used the same word for the telephone, since one hears the speaker’s voice without seeing the person.

The story is Abu Yazid’s own construction, designed to express his doctrine of religious freedom.

Abu Yazid also reaffirmed a Sufi position that goes back to Ibrahim ibn Adham concerning paradise and the afterlife.

Since paradise is presented as a place of material and bodily happiness, Abu Yazid treated it as a divine trick for enslaving human beings through trade:

> In this world He deceives you with the marketplace, and in the next world He deceives you with the marketplace. You are forever a slave of the market.

—*The Devil’s Delusion*, p. 325

Abu Hamza al-Khurasani clarifies the point in his comment on the Quranic verse:

> Eat and drink with pleasure because of what you sent ahead in days gone by.

He says:

> He distracted them from Himself with food and drink.

—*The Devil’s Delusion*, p. 323

For al-Bistami, paradise is really two paradises: a paradise of pleasure and a paradise of knowledge.

The paradise of knowledge is eternal.

The paradise of pleasure is temporary.

—*The Light*, p. 114

This contradicts the Quranic statement that heavenly pleasure is eternal and moves instead toward the philosophers’ doctrine of a spiritual resurrection, which denies a bodily resurrection.

Goethe also held to the idea of spiritual survival. He argued that the wisdom of nature cannot be reconciled with the complete temporariness of human life, and that some form of continuation after death must therefore exist.

These are the dreams of emotionally driven thinkers whose hearts are full of love and free from the anxieties of the senses.

They do not, however, persuade philosophers of nature, who deal with the facts of matter alone.

Such an idea might have consoled al-Maʿarri in the face of death if he had been able to believe it.

But his poetry and prose suggest that he had no hope of return.

This despair was one of the sources of his criticism of a natural order that, in his view, lacked both reason and justice.

Those who believe in spiritual resurrection do not criticise nature or the Creator.

Nor do strict natural philosophers. They take the facts of nature as they are, within their systems and processes.

Al-Maarri was different.

His mind was open to the demands of the universal intellectual. He wanted to intervene in the processes of existence and refused simply to accept them as facts that could not be questioned.

Unlike Goethe, Abu al-Ala attacked both the wisdom of nature and the wisdom of the Creator, basing his argument on birth and death.

He saw them as the pointless activity of something without reason: creating and destroying as a child builds houses only to knock them down:

> You forbade deliberate killing of souls,
> yet You Yourself sent two angels to seize them?
>
> You claim that they will have a second return—
> how much better had they been spared both conditions.

With Abu Yazid, Sufi literature suddenly leaps into a highly concentrated symbolic eloquence.

He produced brief statements that listeners could repeat and remember because they were short and beautiful.

The symbolic language of Sufism goes beyond the limits of what seems reasonable or familiar. It overturns ordinary habits of speech and breaks out of conventional logic into an absolute dialectic.

This wild symbolism allowed the Sufi mind to move freely outside both doctrine and philosophy.

It also enriched Sufi literature with deep inward images that may later have influenced poets as they expanded their use of artistic imagery and learned to express ideas through images.

Some modern writers have called this symbolic excess in Sufism **the madness of language**.

The madness of language means breaking the conceptual rules of language in favour of free movement through the spaces of mind and feeling.

Read this statement from *The Light*:

> When I reached His oneness, and the first moment of unity came, I began travelling through understanding for ten years, until all my understanding was consumed.
>
> Then I became a bird whose body was made of oneness and whose wing was made of perpetuity.
>
> I continued flying through the air of the “how” for ten years, travelling a distance eight hundred million times greater than the distance from the Throne to the earth.
>
> I continued until I passed beyond perpetuity itself. Then I looked down upon unity in the disappearance of creation from the knower and the disappearance of the knower from creation.

—*Sufi Ecstatic Utterances*, p. 149

As you can see, this cannot be interpreted using ordinary methods or the usual rules of logic.

It might be read with a literary sense, as a work of art to be tasted rather than retold.

Yet the speaker was thinking about something as he spoke in the language of Sufi intoxication.

His meaning simply cannot be contained by a fixed rule.

And when such a text is presented to Sufis themselves, they disagree over what it means.

Unfortunately, al-Junayd’s book explaining Abu Yazid’s ecstatic utterances has not survived, so we cannot know how he interpreted this passage and others like it.

In any case, the text belongs to the Sufi principle that the person who realizes unity passes beyond his ordinary, visible self as his attributes and identity disappear into the attributes and being of the One he affirms.

Outside conventional religious monotheism, this is joined to an ecstatic flight through the spaces of perpetuity.

It is also what makes the Sufi recoil from the formula “There is no god but God” and refuse to repeat it even at death, in open defiance of religious custom.

The Sufi does not live inside familiar doctrine.

He has his own world, created by himself without a previous model.

Because al-Bistami did not explore philosophical questions at great length, he never completely departed from the demands of social Sufism despite his strong emphasis on the knowledge-oriented tendency.

He did not throw himself into social struggle in the way the great social Sufis did.

But in his ecstatic sayings he returned repeatedly to the rights of ordinary people and expressed concern for them in this world—and perhaps in the next as well.

One story about people’s rights in this world gives priority to helping the poor rather than spending money on pilgrimage.

Abu Yazid said, according to *The Light*:

> I set out for the pilgrimage. On the road I met a man who said, “Abu Yazid, where are you going?”
>
> I said, “To the pilgrimage.”
>
> He asked, “How much money do you have?”
>
> I said, “Two hundred dirhams.”
>
> He said, “Then circle around me seven times and give me the two hundred dirhams. I have a family to feed.”
>
> So I circled around him and gave him the two hundred dirhams.

—*The Light*, p. 164

The story is Abu Yazid’s own construction, meant to establish the principle that giving to the poor takes precedence over making the pilgrimage.

He tells it in Sufi language and Sufi logic.

The poor man becomes the Kaaba. Abu Yazid circles him seven times, gives him the money, and returns home considering this to have been his pilgrimage.

Perhaps Abu Yazid also had moments when his belief in the afterlife returned and he became afraid for humanity before the consequences of Judgment Day.

He said:

> The knower’s deepest wish in this world is that faith should remain. In the next world, it is that all creation should be pardoned.

—*The Light*, p. 168

The first wish is difficult, because according to this way of thinking a knower does not reach full knowledge while remaining inside ordinary faith.

The second wish—the pardon of everyone in the next world—is something the knower actively desires to bring about.

Abu Yazid also said:

> O God, if in Your prior knowledge You have decreed that You will punish any of Your creation in the Fire, then enlarge my body until there is room in it for no one but me.

Ibn al-Jawzi objected on three grounds.

First, Abu Yazid says, “if in Your prior knowledge,” even though, according to Ibn al-Jawzi, we know with certainty that some creatures must be punished in hell because God has explicitly named people such as Pharaoh and Abu Lahab.

How, after certainty has been established, can one say “if”?

Ibn al-Jawzi means that Abu Yazid is casting doubt on the Quran’s statements about hell.

Second, Abu Yazid says, “enlarge my body until there is room for no one but me.”

He does not say, “so that I may save the believers.”

He wants to save everyone, including unbelievers.

For Ibn al-Jawzi, this means trying to outdo God Himself in mercy.

Ibn al-Jawzi accepts the idea of Abu Yazid sacrificing himself to save people from punishment, provided the sacrifice is limited to Muslims.

Abu Yazid wants to sacrifice himself for everyone.

That is the difference between the jurist and the Sufi.

Third, Ibn al-Jawzi says that Abu Yazid must either be ignorant of the size of the Fire or excessively confident in his own ability to endure it.

Neither possibility can be accepted.

In other words, Abu Yazid does not understand the size of hell if he imagines that his body could ever be enlarged enough to fill it.

No human body could reach such a size.

And his confidence that he could endure the Fire is equally impossible.

—*The Devil’s Delusion*, p. 346

There is another doctrine concerning the Fire, expressed by Shibli:

> God has servants who, if they spat on hell, would put it out.

Abu Yazid never went this far in mocking the Fire.

Shibli belonged to the following generation.

Yet both agreed on the need to save human beings: from poverty and humiliation in this world, and from punishment in the next.

According to Ibn al-Jawzi, this amounts to trying to outdo both God’s mercy and His wisdom.

What is really happening is that the Sufis are proposing a different picture of justice from the one offered by religion.

Unequal provision among people in this world is, to them, neither just nor reasonable.

Punishing people in the next world also falls outside the demands of justice and reason.

Here we discover one of the hidden reasons behind the Sufi preference for the saint over the prophet.

The saint revises the truths of revelation in order to remove from the Sufi Absolute the roles of executioner and merchant:

the executioner who tortures people on Judgment Day,

and the merchant who divides them in this world between those who possess and those who are deprived.

In the person of Abu Yazid, the Sufi also possesses an extraordinarily precise, even excessive, sense of justice.

According to stories recorded in *Mirror of Time*, Abu Yazid once washed his robe outdoors while accompanied by a friend.

The friend said:

“Hang it on the wall around the vineyard.”

Abu Yazid replied:

“The owner has not given me permission.”

His friend said:

“Then hang it on a wild tree.”

Abu Yazid said:

“The branches might break and the tree would be damaged.”

His friend said:

“Then spread it over the *idhkhir* grass.”

Abu Yazid replied:

“That would spoil it. God made it as food for animals.”

So Abu Yazid turned his back to the sun, spread the shirt across his own back, head, and chest, and waited until it dried. Then he put it on.

*Idhkhir* is a wild grass.

Another story expresses the same extreme care over justice and avoidance of harm.

A man once took a handful of earth from a garden to his home.

Before using it, he thought:

“If everyone who passes this garden takes a handful, nothing will remain.”

So the next day he carried the earth back to the garden without having used it.

With al-Bistami, the knowledge-oriented tendency developed into philosophical Sufism in the following generation.

Al-Bistami’s thought was directed against established doctrine and concentrated especially on divinity.

He made the relationship between God and the human being one of equality rather than servitude.

The human being as a shared divine and human essence became one of the central themes of Sufi knowledge.

The Sufis greatly magnified the human being’s central place in the world and among created things, beginning from the Quranic statement:

> We have honoured the children of Adam.

Al-Bistami was the first Sufi to place the human being at the centre and grant him equality with the divine.

His knowledge stands against doctrine and works toward freeing humanity from the authority of dogma.

This teaching survives mostly in his ecstatic utterances. He himself did not subject it to systematic analysis or study.

Later generations of Sufis did that.

Perhaps al-Junayd’s lost book interpreting Abu Yazid’s ecstatic sayings was the earliest work of this kind.

After it came al-Hallaj’s *Tawasin*, which has survived.[^ms-v1-005]

The *Tawasin* develops a Sufi form of knowledge built around two themes: the rejection of intermediaries and divine indwelling.

The rejection of intermediaries gives Sufi form to the deist doctrine and complements the philosophical route by which al-Razi reached his own deism.

Al-Razi and al-Hallaj lived in the same century, and there is little doubt that they read one another.

Yet al-Razi was not influenced by al-Hallaj and remained distant from him, even though they agreed not only on this doctrine but also in their relationship to ordinary people.

The reason is that their methods were radically different even when they reached the same conclusion.

Philosophers cannot tolerate Sufi conduct or Sufi language, because that language breaks the rational mind apart and tries to move beyond it, accusing reason of being inadequate.

Al-Razi also lacked al-Hallaj’s courage in confronting the state.

His courage was limited to confronting religion, which was less costly.

The *Tawasin* presents, sometimes symbolically and sometimes openly, the doctrines of indwelling and union, developing and completing what had begun in al-Bistami’s ecstatic utterances.

The doctrine of indwelling is expressed through the unity of being.

In al-Hallaj, the idea existed only as a seed.

Ibn Arabi would later make it grow.

According to al-Sulami’s *Classes of the Sufis*, al-Hallaj said:

> Humanity is neither joined to Him nor separated from Him.

To deny joining is to deny absolute identity.

To deny separation is to reject the religious picture in which God stands as a ruling authority over creation, like a shepherd over a flock.

Humanity is connected to Him through its very existence, because that existence is the manifestation of the Real within it.

Yet humanity is also distinct from Him because human beings are many and varied, while He is one essence, absolute in unity.

Ibn Arabi would build his final doctrine of the unity of being on this foundation.

Al-Hallaj’s surviving cultural legacy consists of the *Tawasin*, his poems, and sayings scattered through historical and Sufi sources.

His writings strike ordinary consciousness and knock it off its usual track.

The result is usually one of two reactions.

Among religious people, they provoke rejection.

Outside that world, they can produce transformation.

One of al-Hallaj’s most startling moves was his identification with Iblis in Iblis’s refusal to bow before Adam, and his declaration of rebellion.

Later, Sadiq Jalal al-Azm summed up the implication by saying that Iblis was the first rebel in the universe and Adam the first opportunist, in his *Critique of Religious Thought*.

Al-Hallaj’s sympathy with Iblis has two sides.

The first is intellectual: rejection of intermediaries and direct contact with heaven.

Al-Hallaj reads Iblis’s refusal to acknowledge Adam as a refusal to believe in Adam’s prophethood.

Adam is the first prophet and the father of the prophets, not merely the father of humanity.

The second side concerns the essence of the human being, which must be protected from humiliation and degradation.

Al-Hallaj makes Iblis say:

> Even if He tortures me in His fire forever and ever, I will never bow before anyone, and I will never humble myself before a person or a body.

This is a social position.

The first was a position concerning knowledge.

Al-Hallaj is therefore a Sufi in both directions: in social, struggling Sufism and in philosophical Sufism.

He then joins Iblis to Pharaoh, who refused to acknowledge the prophethood of Moses.

Al-Hallaj also declares himself a student of Pharaoh, for the same reason.

This is deism expressed through a Sufi method, just as al-Razi’s deism was expressed through philosophy.

Here al-Hallaj clears Pharaoh of the charge of oppression.

He no longer treats “Pharaoh-like” rule as synonymous with tyranny.

Instead, he takes the biblical story, repeated in the Quran, and turns it into the story of one man who claims prophethood and another who refuses to believe him.

Pharaoh therefore moves from the class of tyrants into the class of free thinkers.[^ms-v1-006]

The language of al-Hallaj in the *Tawasin*, his poetry, and his ecstatic utterances is symbolic and dialectical, breaking ordinary rules of expression.

Taken according to formal Aristotelian logic, it is irrational.

Its force comes instead from a dialectical sensibility, though without becoming philosophical discourse.

For al-Hallaj and the Sufis generally, truth is spiritualized and touched by poetry.

This does not make them poets, because their language remains tied to Sufi intuition, Sufi knowledge, and a Sufi stance toward people, the self, and the universe.

It is a form of literature that both the Sufi and the literary reader can appreciate, even if they disagree about its meaning and the conditions that produced it.

They meet in its pure artistic beauty:

> I saw a bird among the birds of the Sufis. It had two wings and rejected my way because it still insisted on flying.
>
> It asked me about purity.
>
> I said: “Cut off your wing with the shears of annihilation. Otherwise do not follow me.”
>
> It said: “But with a wing I can fly.”
>
> I said: “Poor creature! Nothing is like Him. He is the Hearing, the Seeing.”
>
> That day it stopped in the sea of understanding and drowned.

And:

> O secret of a secret, so subtle
> it rises beyond the description of every living thing.
>
> Outward and inward, You appear
> in everything, through everything.
>
> O totality of all, You are not other than me.
> What apology, then, can I make to You?

Read al-Hallaj for knowledge, and he gives you knowledge in full.

Read him for revelation, and he reveals himself to you.

Read him as literature, and you find yourself absorbed into what you are reading.

He is thought in his meaning, Sufi in his revelation, literary in his beauty.

With him, Sufism reaches a stage of knowledge beyond al-Bistami’s earlier breakthroughs.

But this stage did not belong to al-Hallaj alone.

His generation also included Shibli, al-Junayd, al-Sari al-Saqati, Ibn Ata, and others.

They combined literature, poetry, and Sufi knowledge, although most of them belong primarily to the social tradition, under the leadership of al-Hallaj.

In truth, after Ibrahim ibn Adham, social Sufism became increasingly mixed with literature and knowledge, reflecting the development of both in that age.

Yet the distinction between the social Sufi and the philosophical Sufi never disappeared.

Different masters had different temperaments.

Some were dominated by philosophical concerns.

Others were dominated by questions of justice and equality among human beings.

Books began to appear on both sides.

In Shibli, knowledge glows through poetry, ecstatic utterance, and short pieces of prose.

There is no single book containing them. They are scattered through the sources of Sufism and history.

Shibli was a lover.

What revealed his condition to me was that he loved a human being and turned that love into a road toward the love of God.

We discussed this earlier in the section on love in the Orbit of Mīm.

He concealed the name of his beloved in obedience to the conventions of his age.

Perhaps Shibli’s love made him the most daring of his generation.

Perhaps this is what his friend al-Junayd meant when he said:

> Shibli’s sword drips with blood.

Here are several of the breakthroughs attributed to this great Baghdad Sufi.

He was born in Samarra to a father from Khurasan who had come from the village of Shibla in Central Asia.

His father served as a governor and chamberlain, and Shibli followed him into official work.

Then, at the age of forty, Shibli gave it all up and entered the Sufi path, where he matured and rose through the ranks.

The following passages are drawn from *al-Lumaʿ* and Badawi’s collection of ecstatic utterances.

> This self of mine asks me for nothing more than a piece of bread. But if I cast my secret toward the Throne and the Footstool, they would burn.

Here is the contrast between the need of the body, satisfied by a piece of bread, and the cosmic secret powerful enough to burn the Throne and Footstool above the seven heavens.

The great Sufi is irritated by nature itself.

Malik ibn Dinar used to say:

> I wish my sustenance were nothing but a pebble I could suck. I am tired of going to the latrine so often.

If creation had been left to them, they would have made another kind of creature.

Shibli also said:

> A thousand years gone are present in a thousand years still to come.
>
> This is time. Do not be deceived by appearances.

Elsewhere he said:

> Your times are cut into pieces. My time has no two ends.

For the Sufi, **time** means the instant in which the heart is fully present with God.

For Shibli, however, time becomes a station with neither beginning nor end.

A man once asked him about prayer.

Shibli replied:

> Cast your whole concern into the higher world, and from there into the lower world. Then burn into your heart the knowledge that there is nothing but God.

Prayer is their road to union with the Real, the Islamic Absolute.

As we have said, they do not perform it as an obligation in search of reward.

They perform it to reach this condition.

Shibli said:

> We never parted.
>
> How could we part, when the state of union never passed over us?

Separation can only come after union.

But if you were already with Him before union, how could you ever be separated from Him?

He also said:

> Creation loved You for Your blessings.
>
> I love You for Your affliction.

The Sufi does not seek comfort.

He is absorbed in the greater burden: the burden of creation and the burden of the Real.

When asked about love, Shibli said:

> It is a cup with a blaze in it. When it settles in the senses and takes up residence in the soul, inward selves disappear as souls themselves disappear.

Love is the erasure of the lover in the being of the Beloved.

One day, Shibli says, he whispered inwardly:

> Blaze for me.

The Arabic phrase *shub lī*—“blaze for me”—echoes his own name, Shibli.

He then said:

> He saw me and showed me the wonders of His tenderness.
> I was overwhelmed, and my heart melted into moaning.
>
> He is never absent, so I cannot console myself by remembering Him.
> Nor has He turned from me, so that I might disappear.

A man once came upon Shibli’s circle and asked him:

“Does true presence leave visible signs on those who experience it?”

Shibli answered:

“Yes. A light blooms beside the fires of longing, and its traces appear upon their bodies.”

He was asked why the Sufis were called by a name at all.

He replied:

> Because some remnant of their selves remained upon them. Had nothing remained, no name could have attached itself to them.

This resembles Laozi’s opening words in the *Tao*:

> The name that can be named
> is not the eternal name.

Eternity lies in the erasure of the name.

To break through the finite is to lose the name.

Because the Sufis had not yet reached complete erasure, a name still remained attached to them.

Shibli’s Sufism is love and refined literature.

His ecstatic speech raises the Sufi from the condition of servitude to equality.

Its subtle and difficult language jolts thought out of stillness and drives it toward reflection and wonder.

Wonder makes thought move.

It carries us down from the surface of appearances into deeper places, where truths are born from the labour of questioning.

Shibli never made a conspicuous turn toward social struggle on behalf of ordinary people.

He might well have done so, if only to free himself from the embarrassment of his earlier service to the state.

Instead, he confined himself to removing the motives of desire from his own soul and choosing poverty over wealth.

This was a posture of withdrawal.

Around the middle of the fourth Islamic century, Abu Talib al-Makki produced *The Nourishment of Hearts in the Treatment of the Beloved*.

He came from central Iraq, grew up and became known in Mecca, and was therefore called al-Makki.

He later travelled to Basra, where the Sunnis pursued him on accusations of Muʿtazilism.

He then moved to Baghdad, where he remained until his death in 386 AH.

In Baghdad he adopted Sunni doctrine and came to be counted among both the Sufi masters and Sunni scholars.

He therefore belongs to the line of Sunni Sufism.

This is clear in *The Nourishment of Hearts*.

He filled the book with religious preaching, and the language of religion dominates many of its chapters.

He also adopted the Sunni doctrine that obedience to the ruler is obligatory, contrary to the older Sufi position.

Even so, the book remains organised around Sufism and its concepts.

Al-Makki attacked the religious sciences and classified them as outward sciences pursued for worldly gain and used to associate with princes.

He said that knowledge of the heart is the knowledge behind all knowledge, and that inward knowledge is the true knowledge of the scholars.

He even counted hadith and jurisprudence among the sciences of this world, although both are normally classified as religious sciences.

(1/115)

He also grounded conduct on Sufi rather than juristic principles, citing a hadith of their own invention. The Sufis were among the people most willing to fabricate hadiths to suit their purposes.

One hadith says:

> “Righteousness is whatever puts the heart at ease and gives the soul peace, even if people give you ruling after ruling to the contrary.”

This opens the door to independent judgment in matters of religious law, based on what seems right rather than on the text itself. But the Sufis did not devote themselves to jurisprudence. Had they practised it according to this principle, the whole structure of religious law would have fallen away. Instead, they applied the principle to themselves: they became their own jurists.

That was a revolution within the Sharia. Intellectuals were never in a position to carry it through. The Ismailis and Qarmatians did. In modern times Muhammad Iqbal called for something similar but did not achieve it. Iqbal was perhaps the one true modern heir to Sufism in both its intellectual and social dimensions. He failed to carry this project through because he never plunged into the daily battles of politics and personally led a reform movement along Sufi lines.

In any case, *Qut al-Qulub* gives us an early example of someone breaking through the accepted assumptions of the religious sciences.

Abu Talib al-Makki placed direct spiritual knowledge above ordinary faith. He said that one of the three hundred *abdal* was worth three hundred ordinary believers. He cited a report saying that most of the people of paradise are simple-minded, while people of understanding are in *Illiyyun*. *Illiyyun* is the place of nearness to God, not the place of eating and drinking.

He even placed the Sufi above the angels.

He reports that one of the mystics once asked an *abdal* a question concerning the direct vision of certainty. The man turned to his left and said:

“What do you say, may God have mercy on you?”

Then he turned to his right and said:

“What do you say, may God have mercy on you?”

Then he lowered his head toward his chest and said:

“What do you say, may God have mercy on you?”

He was speaking to his own heart.

Then, the narrator says, “He gave me the strangest answer I had ever heard. I said to him: I saw you turn to your left, then to your right, and then toward your own chest. What were you doing?”

He replied:

“You asked me a question for which I had no answer ready. So I turned to the companion on my left and asked him, thinking he might know. He said: ‘I do not know.’ Then I asked the companion on my right, who knows more than the first, and he too said: ‘I do not know.’ So I looked into my heart and asked it. My heart told me what I then told you. It turned out to know more than either of them.”

That is, more than the two angels at his right and left. (1/120)

Al-Makki also absorbed the divine attributes into the divine essence itself. He pushed transcendence and abstraction further than the Asharis and the Sunni mainstream, following the Sufi tendency toward absolute unity. In their understanding, the divine essence is not separate from existence, and scriptural passages that seem to suggest otherwise have to be interpreted accordingly.

We have already seen that the God of religion has the attributes of an emperor. His essence is multiple rather than one. The God of the Sufis, by contrast, is an abstract essence. With Ibn Arabi this will eventually become the spirit of the cosmos: something that flows through existing things and has no independent existence apart from them.

The religious idea of God is closer to the philosophical one in the Peripatetic school, whose philosophers speak of a First Mover conceived as separate from the world, while regarding both the First Mover and the world as eternal.

Sufi thought prepares the way for a materialist understanding more readily than the Peripatetic system does. It is easier to merge an abstract divine essence into the world than to merge a First Mover into it, because the very concept of a First Mover implies separation and distinction.

Abu Talib did not arrive at this position in its full form, though parts of it had already appeared in al-Hallaj half a century earlier.

In his attack on the religious sciences, al-Makki said that the *abdal* withdrew to the far ends of the earth and hid themselves from public view because they could not bear to look at the religious scholars of their time or listen patiently to what they said.

He added that the ordinary people were easier for them to bear. Ordinary people did not posture as religious authorities, mislead believers, or claim to be scholars. They were therefore closer to mercy and further from what deserved hatred. (1/176)

In my book on Sadr al-Din al-Shirazi, I wrote that he did not leave his home and retire to a distant town because he was fleeing ordinary people. He was fleeing people of thought and culture.

What Abu Talib al-Makki says confirms my point.

Sufism, in its oppositional, ascetic, and popular form, took shape as the opposite of religious authority as represented by the “scholars of this age” in *Qut al-Qulub*. By the standards of our own time, it also appears as the opposite of the cultural class in general.

The intellectuals of the Islamic age shared the Sufi tendency toward opposition, asceticism, and closeness to ordinary people. The intellectuals of our own time are generally much closer to the way of poets than to the way of Sufis.

*Qut al-Qulub* is a book of Sufi knowledge, though rules of conduct dominate it. Al-Makki mixes the language of Sufism with the language of religion, which makes many chapters difficult to enjoy.

Most likely, Abu Talib al-Makki never settled firmly into one creed. He moved back and forth between Mu'tazilism, Sunnism, and Sufism. He lived in Baghdad and preached to gatherings there in a hybrid language drawn from all three.

Then came a moment of ecstatic excess in which he broke free of all of them.

He said:

> “Nothing is more harmful to the creature than the Creator.”

Perhaps what he meant was:

> Nothing is more harmful to people than religion.

He had already said something close to this in his remarks about the scholars of his time—the jurists, hadith scholars, and everyone else to whom the description “men of religion” applies.

His biographers say that after this, the people of Baghdad stopped attending his gatherings.

Ordinary people are generally tolerant about such matters, but this particular outburst left them little room for tolerance. Ordinary people do not turn against intellectuals who sympathise with them unless those intellectuals collide with their beliefs in such a violent way.

Even then, all they did was boycott his lectures.

Had they acted according to the instructions of the professional jurists, they would not have allowed him to remain alive. But ordinary people were rarely stirred into action by legal opinions alone, except in matters they themselves shared an interest in: defending society against outside aggression from the *dar al-harb*—mainly the Byzantines and later the Crusaders—or confronting an oppressive ruler.

In the same generation appeared *al-Ta'arruf li-Madhhab Ahl al-Tasawwuf* by Abu Bakr Muhammad ibn Ishaq al-Bukhari al-Kalabadhi, who died in 380 AH.

In a fairly concise form, he set out the basic principles and terminology of the Sufis. His approach was close to Sunni Sufism, and so the book contains few ecstatic excesses and was acceptable to the jurists.

It was published under the supervision of the Shaykh of al-Azhar, Abd al-Halim Mahmud, who found nothing in it that violated the foundations of Sunni doctrine.

*Al-Ta'arruf* is closer to a teaching manual than a work of investigation or analysis. In this respect it is inferior to *Qut al-Qulub*, and only about a quarter of its size.

Even so, some of the older writers treated it as a foundational reference. They said:

> “Were it not for *al-Ta'arruf*, Sufism would never have been known.”

A contemporary of al-Kalabadhi, Abu Nasr al-Sarraj al-Tusi, who died in 378 AH, produced *al-Luma'*. It contains far more of the actual substance of Sufi thought and is much more willing to make room for the Sufis’ ecstatic utterances.

Although *al-Luma'* is also usually counted as a work of Sunni Sufism, it can be read as a book of open Sufism, not tightly bound by Sunni doctrine.

Al-Sarraj accepted al-Hallaj rather than casting him out. He also accepted Abu Yazid al-Bistami and defended his ecstatic sayings. He entered into direct conflict with Ibn Salim, who had launched a campaign accusing al-Bistami of unbelief.

Al-Sarraj defined an ecstatic utterance, or *shath*, as an unusual expression produced by an overwhelming state of ecstasy: a feeling so powerful that it overflows, surges, and takes possession of the person experiencing it.

He said that such language can only be understood by people of inner knowledge and remains obscure to anyone who has not been trained in Sufi terminology.

This explanation is repeated whenever Sunni Sufis want to overlook their companions’ ecstatic sayings, or when a great Sufi master finds himself forced to hide what his own words really imply.

The conflict here is real. Jurists continually incited people against the authors of these utterances.

But the jurists knew perfectly well what the words meant. They understood their implications. It is simply not true that they were too ignorant to grasp them because this was supposedly a secret language for initiates.

In reality, the ecstatic utterances of the Sufis are no less clear in their intentions than the language of the so-called heretics.

They resemble the *Luzumiyyat* of al-Maʿarri. Al-Maarri himself wrote a book called *Zajr al-Nabih* in an attempt to reinterpret those poems and defend them. He did not succeed. Ibn Nubata said that the interpretations still did not rescue him from explicit unbelief.

When Abd al-Qadir al-Jilani says, for example:

> “O company of prophets, you were given what we were not given,”

the statement is perfectly clear.

This is a direct claim that his own spiritual rank is higher than that of the prophets and that he has known truths they did not know.

What interpretation can remove the plain meaning of words written in full daylight?

Abu Nasr al-Sarraj al-Tusi, in his attempts to interpret such ecstatic sayings, resembles al-Maʿarri interpreting the *Luzumiyyat*.

I do not think he was being entirely sincere.

That does not necessarily mean he personally accepted the conclusions implied by those sayings. He may have remained sincere in his own religious faith. But he was loyal to his own community and wanted to protect its members from accusation.

It is as though he wanted to reconcile believing Sufism with unbelieving Sufism so that he could preserve both his faith and his affiliation at the same time.

*Al-Luma'* contains the same basic subjects found in the great books of Sufism: the meaning of Sufism, its requirements, the Sufi path, and Sufi terminology.

On terminology, al-Tusi gives explanations of a large body of technical terms occupying more than forty pages in the printed edition. This makes his work the earliest surviving dictionary of Sufi terminology.

He also devoted chapters to criticising mistaken and deviant tendencies. In doing so, he anticipated al-Maʿarri’s later criticism of certain forms of Sufi conduct.

His criticism is extremely useful because it helps us distinguish serious Sufism from cheap, compromising, and opportunistic forms of it.

One example is the claim by some Sufis that wealth is better than poverty for an individual Sufi.

Anyone who says this is really encouraging the accumulation of money and denying that wealth conflicts with the conditions of Sufi life.

Al-Tusi attacked this position fiercely. He insisted on the view accepted by most Sufis: poverty is an essential quality of the Sufi.

The individual Sufi is called *faqir*, “poor,” and the Sufis collectively are called the “people of poverty” or simply “the poor.” The two terms become virtually interchangeable.

Al-Tusi also attacked affectation and imitation among minor Sufis who tried to behave like accomplished masters before gaining any real knowledge.

He explained that complete Sufi knowledge and conduct can be reached only by following the path of the masters, learning their manners, and beginning where they began, until one passes gradually toward their end: one state after another, one station after another.

He criticised vulgar behaviour during *sama'*, Sufi singing and music, and subjected it to the same standards.

He mocked immature enthusiasts who worked themselves into artificial ecstasy at musical gatherings and danced in the very manner that later disgusted al-Maʿarri and drew his arrows of criticism and anger.

This detailed criticism in *al-Luma'* shows us that the Sufi movement was never internally consistent in its principles.

Many minor Sufis tried to live off the reputation enjoyed by Sufism. They turned it into a livelihood. Joining the Sufi path became a way of making a living, until Sufism became a profession in much the same way as poetry.

In light of such corruption, we are justified in refusing to treat Sufism as one undivided movement and instead dealing with it through its great masters.

That is why I chose the term **qutb Sufism**—the Sufism of the great masters. It allows us to leave outside the category the wearers of patched robes, the dervishes, the professional dependants, and the corrupt.

In this respect, the great Sufi master resembles the Taoist philosopher.

He does not represent a school or an organised movement. He is an individual in whom wisdom has crystallised: someone who has fulfilled the conditions of wisdom and realised the capacities of the universal soul.

This gives him the spiritual power to affect the people around him and to move within a world governed by principles.

He may do this negatively, by obeying those principles through his own free personal choice.

Or he may do it positively, by launching an active assault on the sources of injustice and corruption in the state and society.

The words **Sufism** and **Tao** should be understood with these distinctions in mind.

Al-Tusi also criticised teachings associated with great Sufi masters that did not follow the path of religious faith.

As I have said, al-Tusi himself remained closer to believing Sufism.

He rejected the idea that a saint could rank above a prophet, using the story of Moses and Khidr as part of his argument.

This is a difficult question on which the Sufis themselves disagreed, although the great majority of major Sufi masters placed sainthood either on the level of prophecy or above it.

Al-Tusi also rejected *hulul*, the doctrine of divine indwelling.

He said that those who believed in indwelling fell into two groups.

One group said that God indwells only beautiful or pleasing things.

The other said that He indwells things we regard as ugly as well.

The second position is the doctrine of Zhuangzi that we discussed earlier. It belongs to complete unity of being, where the divine is understood as permeating all existing things.

Al-Tusi also rejected the claim that the spirit is eternal and uncreated.

That belongs to the more openly unbelieving levels of Sufism.

By rejecting it, al-Tusi confirms his own religious faith.

*Al-Luma'* is one of the foundational books.

It is superior to *al-Ta'arruf* in both breadth and depth and is therefore the better book to use as an introduction to Sufism.

Its author had a broad mind and a generous temperament. His own religious faith did not make him hostile or discriminatory in his treatment of the great Sufi masters.

One detail is especially striking.

Whenever he mentions al-Hallaj, he almost always adds:

> “May God have mercy on him.”

or:

> “May the mercy of God Most High be upon him.”

He rarely grants this courtesy to anyone else.

Was he deliberately taking a stand against the rejection al-Hallaj had suffered from some other Sufis?

The great leap in philosophical Sufism comes with Ibn Arabi: Abu Bakr Muhammad ibn Ali al-Ta'i, known as Muhyi al-Din, a descendant of Hatim al-Ta'i.

He grew up in Andalusia and received his early education there before entering the Sufi path.

He did not remain there long. He felt the pull of travel.

One of his first stops was Marrakesh. Around this time Ibn Rushd died, and Ibn Arabi joined the funeral procession that carried the philosopher’s body back to his native Cordoba.

He later stayed in Bejaia, in what is now Algeria. There he dreamed that he had married all the stars and planets.

Then the letters were added to them, and he married every letter as well.

From there he travelled to Egypt and then to the Hijaz and Iraq, where he met Shaykh Umar al-Suhrawardi.

Their meeting took place entirely in silence. Neither man spoke.

After they parted, al-Suhrawardi was asked what he thought of Ibn Arabi.

He replied:

> “An ocean of realities.”

Ibn Arabi, for his part, said of al-Suhrawardi:

> “He is filled with the Sunna from the top of his head to the soles of his feet.”

Perhaps the meeting between them took place on the level of Sufi feeling rather than Sufi thought, because intellectually they stood at opposite poles—as each man’s description of the other suggests.

Ibn Arabi had earlier travelled to Mosul, then later to Aleppo, and from there north into Anatolia.

There he married a woman named Laqut and raised her son in Sufism until the young man became deeply established in it. He became known as al-Qunawi, after Konya, where his teacher and stepfather had settled.

Ibn Arabi returned to Aleppo and met its ruler, al-Zahir Ghazi, son of Saladin.

Al-Zahir had earlier executed the philosopher Suhrawardi on his father’s orders after Saladin yielded to a legal ruling issued by the jurists of Aleppo.

We do not know whether Ibn Arabi raised the matter with him.

Al-Zahir himself apparently had not been pleased with Suhrawardi’s execution. Later, when the opportunity arose, he punished the jurists who had sent that disastrous report to his father.

In one of their conversations, al-Zahir revealed to Ibn Arabi certain things about the jurists. Ibn Arabi records the conversation in Chapter 310 of the *Futuhat al-Makkiyya*.

The ruler told him:

> “You condemn me for the evils and injustices committed in my country and kingdom. By God, I believe exactly as you do that all of these things are evil. But by God, my master, there is not a single one of them for which I do not possess a legal opinion from some jurist, written in his own hand, declaring it permissible.
>
> “One jurist”—and here he named the man he regarded as the finest religious and ascetic jurist in his country—“gave me a ruling that I was not required to fast this particular Ramadan. He said I only had to fast one month of the year and was free to choose whichever month I wished.”
>
> The ruler continued: “Something happened inside me when he said this, though I did not show him what I felt…”

Ibn Arabi had meanwhile presented the ruler with a list of requests brought to him by people in Aleppo, and the ruler granted them all.

In doing so, Ibn Arabi departed from the traditional Sufi principle of keeping one’s distance from political authority.

As we will see later, Ibn Arabi did not consistently follow the behavioural principles of Sufism.

Eventually the Shaykh laid down his traveller’s staff in Damascus. He remained there until his death in 638 AH.

His tomb stands in one of the city’s suburbs beneath Mount Qasioun, in a historic lane containing a number of schools dating from the seventh and eighth Islamic centuries, together with the Qaymari hospital, built in the sixth century.

It is the oldest surviving hospital in the world and remains in use as a health centre.

A mosque was built over Ibn Arabi’s tomb on the orders of Sultan Selim.

Sultan Selim understood nothing of what Ibn Arabi actually taught, but the Ottomans encouraged dervish practice in the name of Sufism.

The mosque of Shaykh Muhyi al-Din is still administered today by Ottoman-style dervishes.

Ibn Arabi wrote around four hundred books and treatises.

For earlier writers, a *risala*, or treatise, could be a medium-sized book or something closer to what we would now call a journal article. But Ibn Arabi produced dozens of works large enough to count as books in their own right.

His greatest work is the *Futuhat al-Makkiyya*, in which he gathered the insights that “opened” to him in Mecca.

It runs to several volumes, depending on the edition.

The most recent Lebanese edition appears in eight large volumes and includes a useful introductory study by Mahmud Matarji.

The earliest edition was printed in Egypt in 1910 at the recommendation of Emir Abd al-Qadir al-Jaza'iri.

Among Ibn Arabi’s finest works after the *Futuhat* is *Fusus al-Hikam*, a single-volume work printed many times, most recently in an edition prepared by Abu al-Ala Afifi with extensive commentary.

With Ibn Arabi, Sufi philosophy reaches its widest scope as an instrument of philosophical knowledge operating through a Sufi method.

The *Futuhat* and the *Fusus* contain his theories of:

* unity of being;
* unity of religions;
* prophecy and sainthood;
* religious law and belief;

together with the Sufi methods of **taste**, **unveiling**, and **direct witnessing**.

The *Futuhat* also branches into many fields of knowledge concerning the human being, life, and nature.

Ibn Arabi’s most important and distinctive theory is the **unity of being**.

Its origins can already be found in al-Hallaj, where it took the form of divine indwelling.

Ibn Arabi himself is not an advocate of indwelling.

He preserves a distinction between the Real and creation. He says that absolute annihilation cannot occur in this life as long as the servant still possesses a self.

A human being contains both a divine and a human dimension, while the Real is pure divinity. The two therefore cannot become literally identical.

Union takes place through spirits, not bodies.

From this one can conclude that Ibn Arabi did not believe in bodily resurrection but in a spiritual resurrection, as did the philosophers of Islam.

This also fits the broader Sufi understanding of paradise. Paradise is not physical pleasure. It is the bliss of nearness to the Real.

And the Sufi does not have to wait for the Day of Resurrection to attain this nearness.

He can reach it by spiritualising himself, breaking his attachment to matter except where material needs remain necessary to keep the body alive.

Then manifestation and direct witnessing occur through the inner eye.

As for the relation between the Real and existing things, Ibn Arabi describes it through the Real’s **manifestation** in the sensory forms of things.

This manifestation is also called **permeation**.

“Permeation” is actually the more precise term, because the idea is that the Real is the inner force flowing through existing things and giving them their sensory forms.

It is therefore not separate from them.

It does not come from an outside source, as the religions imagine.

The force is internal to things, and its manifestation is precisely its permeation through them.

This resembles the way the Tao exists within things in Zhuangzi’s thought, which we discussed earlier.

Sadr al-Din al-Shirazi often uses the language of permeation.

Ibn Arabi prefers manifestation.

The difference is largely the difference between the language of philosophy and the language of Sufism.

To solve the problem of the relationship between absolute existence and relative existence, Ibn Arabi uses two terms for the divine essence: **absolute oneness** (*ahadiyya*) and **manifest oneness** (*wahidiyya*).

Absolute oneness is God’s undivided unity. Nothing within it can be divided or broken into parts. It is stripped of names and attributes.

Manifest oneness is the divine essence as it possesses names and attributes.

It is this manifest oneness that appears in the forms of the fixed possibilities—the eternal archetypes of things that can exist.

We could therefore treat absolute oneness as equivalent to **absolute existence**, and manifest oneness as equivalent to **relative existence**.

Both expressions apply to the Real and to creation.

They apply to the Real through the streams of existence that flow from it and bring the archetypes of possible things into concrete determination, turning them into sensory beings.

Relative existence also belongs to those beings themselves, which receive existence from the Real.

Ibn Arabi does not explain the relationship between absolute oneness and manifest oneness—or between absolute existence and relative existence.

He never tells us how absolute oneness connects with manifest oneness in order to spread itself through manifestation, or how relative existence receives something from absolute existence so that existence can then flow into things.

There is a break here that never quite closes.

This was the difficulty that later puzzled al-Shirazi, who said that the manner in which this permeation occurs is unknown.

The same problem had confronted Neoplatonism in its attempt to explain how multiplicity could emerge from unity without any change taking place in the One itself.

The Neoplatonists therefore had to imagine a chain of emanations, beginning with the First Intellect. From the First Intellect comes one thing, the Soul, and from the Soul come the many existing things.

We should, however, be careful not to confuse the two theories.

Neoplatonic emanation does not contain a doctrine of unity of being.

It remains an ordinary process of creation issuing from a transcendent source that does not merge with the things it produces.

By distinguishing absolute oneness from manifest oneness, and absolute existence from relative existence, Ibn Arabi seems reluctant to sever his connection with the God of religion once and for all.

Why he felt this need is difficult for us to determine.

In any case, he repeatedly forgets these distinctions when he moves on to other questions connected with his doctrine of unity of being.

He then treats the God of religion as the one who manifests—not as manifest oneness but as absolute oneness; not as relative existence but as absolute existence.

This is revealed by his famous statement in the *Fusus*:

> “Glory to Him who created possible things and is their very essence.”

Ibn Taymiyya declared him an unbeliever for this, and he was right.

“Glory” can only be addressed to God Himself—that is, to absolute existence.

Yet Ibn Arabi makes this absolute existence the very essence of possible things: the essence of things themselves.

In doing so, he brings God down from His throne and merges Him with determined relative existence—that is, with the material world.

Ibn Taymiyya was unusually quick to accuse people of unbelief, unlike most of the great jurists.

But he had a strong command of both Sufi and philosophical language and understood its implications very well.

Ibn Arabi’s other statements on unity of being follow the same pattern.

Within existing things he distinguishes two stages:

the **fixed archetypes of possible things**, and the **actual entities of existing things**.

Manifestation is directed toward the fixed archetypes, and through it the sensory forms of existing things appear.

The fixed archetypes are abstract essences or spiritual substances capable of receiving sensory form through manifestation. Once they receive it, life flows through them.

Before their concrete existence, they remain in a state of relative non-being.

Absolute nothingness is impossible in Islamic philosophy.

Relative non-being is therefore not true nothingness. It is the state of something not yet concretely composed and present in the sensory world.

When these archetypes acquire form, they pass into the world of the senses.

Ibn Arabi then returns to the Ashari doctrine of continuous creation and the passing away of accidents.

He speaks of continuous bringing-into-being—that is, continuous manifestation—together with the continuous receptivity of possible things.

Formation therefore goes on without interruption, continually renewed.

The emergence of things through emanation or manifestation resembles words emerging from the speaker’s mouth.

The breath spreads itself through individual letters, combining them into words.

In the same way, the Real “breathes,” and possible things are clothed in existence.

This is a very physical image. It implies movement within the essence of the Real of the same kind that the religions attribute to Him.

Al-Shirazi avoided this problem and left the manner of permeation unknown.

Neither al-Shirazi nor Muhyi al-Din normally uses the verb **to create** for this process.

For Sadr al-Din it is **permeation**.

For Muhyi al-Din it is **appearance** or **manifestation**.

For Ibn Arabi, the creation of things is simply the appearance of the Real within them. It does not involve a command of “Be!”

Existence itself becomes a relationship of mutual dependence between the Real and creation.

The Real exists through creation, and creation exists through the Real.

Ibn Arabi says in the *Fusus*:

> “It is through our being His worshippers that we make Him God.”

That is: He becomes God through us.

Without our existence, He would not exist.

He makes the point explicitly in verse:

> Were it not for Him,
> and were it not for us,
> what came to be
> would never have been.
>
> The matter has therefore been divided
> between Him and us.

He also says that our existence is His nourishment, meaning that He does not exist except through our existence.

So existence is something we share with Him.

Does this mean what some atheists in our own time mean when they say that divinity is a product of our own thought—that we ourselves created God and fashioned His image?

Ibn Arabi does not put the matter in such a simple form.

But he does state the same central truth these modern atheists are trying to express: the Real would not exist without our existence.

If we look at the sequence of things, we know that human beings did not always exist in the world. Humanity itself came into being.

There were therefore periods in the history of the world when the Creator was unknown.

The religious books say that stones and other non-rational beings glorify Him, but that is an assertion without proof. It belongs to the realm of metaphor, and interpreters have returned such statements to their symbolic origins.

The Creator remained unknown even after the human being emerged from his ape-like ancestor, according to the evolutionists.

When humanity first appeared and began thinking about the world, it did not immediately discover a Creator.

Instead, people represented divine power through sensory objects. This is why idolatry came before the heavenly religions.

Everything therefore depended on the development of human consciousness.

The Creator did not appear until a comparatively late stage—not much more than three thousand years ago.

That is a million years after the human being emerged from the *nasnas*.

The Shaykh appears to agree with the modern atheists that God is one of our own products.

But unlike them, he still affirms God’s real existence.

We did not create a mere illusion or hypothesis. We discovered a real being.

Its reality does not come simply from the fact that we produced the idea of it. It depends on the existence of a cosmic spirit with which the great Sufi master can connect.

The deity we ourselves have brought into being exists in the secret of the cosmos.

We discover that it permeates the world, but without assigning to it the act of creation.

Perhaps it resembles the god of Mozi, which performs a function outside the simple relationship between Creator and created.

Ibn Arabi could not accept a picture of the universe as a kingdom ruled by a man sitting on a throne and surrounded by servants who carry out his orders.

But neither could he tolerate a universe without intelligence.

This is especially true because, in his eyes, the universe is beautiful.

He says in the *Futuhat*:

> “Nothing more beautiful, more wonderful, or better than this world is possible, because the world has received divine beauty and splendour, and they have appeared through it.” (3/449)

There is little doubt that he would have rejected al-Maʿarri’s criticism of the world.

Perhaps he would have dismissed it as one of the interpretations available to poets—even when the poet was al-Maʿarri.

Behind Ibn Arabi’s doctrine we should look for a psychological secret rather than a purely intellectual one.

The great Sufi master does not see his own existence except in the mirror of absolute existence.

The limits of earthly life drive him to search for a cosmic extension in which the spirit can continue its permanent existence.

He also needs to spiritualise his earthly life so that he can escape the narrow circle of a life ruled by sensory needs: eating, drinking, and sex.

The desire to escape this circle is felt by many major intellectuals, especially in the East.

It can also be joined to a personal ambition to expand one’s social or political presence—or one’s presence in any other field—in such a way that the ambitious person feels he has risen above the circle of eating, drinking, and sex and become something larger and higher.

Al-Mutanabbi’s ambition for political power grew from this kind of feeling.

He expressed it when he said:

> Some people are content
> with whatever life makes easy for them:
> their own legs for a mount,
> whatever little travel gives them.
>
> But inside my chest is a heart
> that knows no limit
> to how far it would carry me
> toward what it desires.

And again:

> Do not imagine glory
> is a wineskin and a singing girl.
>
> Glory is the sword
> and the first fierce blow;
>
> striking the necks of kings,
> even when black armies
> and banners surround you;
>
> leaving behind you in this world
> a roar so loud
> it seems to pass
> from ear to ear
> like fingers counting.

In literary language, these are called **great souls**.

They are not the souls of ordinary people greedy for political power.

Al-Mutanabbi was an intellectual who saw power in the hands of people who understood nothing beyond the pleasures and privileges it gave them.

He wanted the power of an intellectual: someone who felt within himself desires higher than the petty appetites of ordinary rulers.

That is what “great soul” means.

The Sufi, however, reaches for something different.

Al-Mutanabbi is useful as a contrast with the ordinary individual or the ordinary ruler. He is not the model for the great Sufi master or the **cosmic intellectual**.

The cosmic intellectual wants to extend himself into the spirit of the cosmos and become part of its substance.

From there he wants to acquire a spiritual energy that gives him influence in the world.

It is from this energy that the cosmic intellectual acquires the power to challenge the three visible authorities:

**the authority of the state, the authority of religion, and the authority of money.**

The Sufi confronts them in two ways.

First, he makes the ruler small in his own eyes and therefore stops fearing him.

Second, he rises above petty concerns by escaping the obsession with possession.

His confrontation with the religious man and the religious institution follows naturally from this.

The religious man belongs to the outside powers. He is governed by sensory desires on one side and by the narrowness of his thought on the other.

Ibn Arabi’s need for spiritualisation, and the same need among his companions, is therefore what separates pure materialism from philosophical-Sufi materialism.

The latter depends on the existence of the philosopher-sage: someone who extends himself into the cosmic spirit and finds in it either the highest spiritual happiness or a source of power with which to influence the world.

It is unlikely that Ibn Arabi believed in the sensory existence of a Creator.

But he felt the Creator’s presence in the world by imagining a spirit flowing through it.

This spirit is merged into matter.

It is a product of matter, not the cause of matter.

Ibn Arabi denies an independent act of creation and makes existence a condition shared by the Real and creation, neither of which can do without the other.

The Real retains one special quality: absolute existence, which does not change.

This is necessary for the order of the world.

Behind all transformations there must be something stable that does not disappear but merely moves from one form to another.

This is connected to the idea that matter does not perish—that its total quantity in the universe remains constant.

Ibn Arabi states this principle in the *Fusus*:

> “Nature neither increases by what appears from it nor decreases by what does not appear.”

We can therefore give the idea of absolute existence a physical interpretation by comparing it with the principle that the total quantity of matter remains constant.

But this is a spiritualised physics of the kind Goethe sought in his own writings on nature.

Here the merging of the Real into creation is absolute, and all duality disappears.

Ibn Arabi therefore asks:

If everything is the same thing, what can God’s judgment and decree over things possibly mean?

He replies:

> “Nothing judges us except through us. Indeed, we judge ourselves through ourselves—but in Him.”

Duality disappears.

What remains is a complete and solid unity of being.

This confirms what I said about his rejection of the sensory existence of a Creator and his neglect of an independent act of creation within his system of existence.

He could therefore have dispensed with the Real altogether, since we ourselves are the ones who judge ourselves.

But as I have explained, the issue is not one of pure philosophical truth alone.

It is also an attempt to imagine existence in a way that answers the demands of the human spiritual essence as it rebels against the limits of ordinary life.

Ibn Arabi wanted to create a world like the one Goethe would later seek: not this world as assembled according to the principles of pure physics.

Here thought runs wild into an act of creation of its own.

It reshapes the world according to the needs of the cosmic intellectual, remaking it into the most beautiful, wonderful, and perfect world that Muhyi al-Din could imagine from within rather than from without.

#### The Law-Governed World

If the Real is merged into creation, there can be no room for free—or arbitrary—divine will, as Sadr al-Din al-Shirazi called it.

For Ibn Arabi, the world is governed by an internal law rather than by a force outside it.

Things move and change according to their own natures.

God cannot act in a way that contradicts wisdom—that is, the law of nature.

This is why Ibn Arabi denied the usefulness of prayer.

What a person seeks depends on his readiness to receive it: on the capacity already present in his nature.

In the *Fusus* he says:

> “Readiness is the most hidden form of asking.”

Abu al-Ala Afifi explains this by saying that this is the kind of request to which the Real truly responds.

If something has been eternally determined to exist in a particular way, and the nature of that thing asks for what has already been determined for it, what it asks is immediately realised. (2/22)

Afifi regarded this as a form of determinism.

The Sufi submits to divine and natural laws, through which the fate of the world is determined at every moment. The practical result, Afifi says, is the Sufi’s acceptance of God’s decree and destiny.

But Ibn Arabi distinguishes the laws of nature from the law governing human action.

In nature, things occur only according to their laws.

This has to be stated because Ibn Arabi wants to follow natural law and reject the religious Sufi habit of explaining the facts of things through arbitrary divine will.

For him, violating the nature of existence would violate wisdom. It would add nothing to divine perfection.

As I have said, the God of religion is not the God of unity of being.

In religion He is an emperor.

Here He is a power permeating existing things, which move according to fixed eternal laws.

These laws govern nature, including the human being.

But they are not the religious decrees written on a Preserved Tablet.

Ibn Arabi denies that God intervenes in the movement of things.

He says in the *Fusus*:

> “Nothing comes to possible things from the Real except what their own natures give them in their different states.”

Concerning the human being, he says:

> “What happens to the servant happens according to what he himself is. No one other than himself gives him good, and no one other than himself gives him the opposite of good. He is the one who blesses himself and torments himself. So let him blame no one but himself and praise no one but himself.”

This is in the eighth *fass*.

In the eleventh he says:

> “Nothing affected them except themselves, just as their formation came from none but themselves.”

These are explicit statements rejecting divine decree and destiny where human action is concerned.
