The determinism here is the determinism of the world: the fixed eternal laws governing it.

The human being moves within those laws, but differs from other things because he is conscious of himself.

He therefore has a capacity to direct the affairs of his own life that inanimate objects do not possess.

He brings good and evil upon himself. No one else does.

The human being therefore both acts and receives action.

Things merely receive action.

The eleventh *fass* contains several statements along these lines concerning human responsibility.

In Ibn Arabi’s interpretation of the command “Be, and it is,” he says in effect:

If the thing coming into existence did not already possess within itself the power to form itself in response to the command, it would never form.

What brings the thing into existence after its previous non-existence is therefore the thing itself.

Formation belongs to the thing, not to the Real.

The Real contributes only the command.

It is like a master telling his servant:

> “Stand.”

The servant stands in obedience to the command.

The master’s only role in the servant’s standing is the command itself.

The act of standing belongs to the servant, not to the master.

Ibn Arabi concludes:

> “Whoever understands this wisdom, establishes it within himself, and comes to witness it directly will free himself from dependence on anyone else. He will know that no good and no evil reaches him except through himself.”

After such direct and explicit words, what possible meaning remains in Afifi’s claim that the Sufi surrenders himself to divine decree and destiny?

I have to ask:

How many people have researchers led astray by skimming over texts from above?

May God forgive them.

Ibn Arabi’s denial of the usefulness of prayer begins with Epicurus, who denied that the gods interfere in human affairs.

A later Chinese philosopher, Wang Chong, also rejected prayer.

In denying it, Wang said that prayer was nonsense because heaven is extremely far away from us and cannot hear our voices.

Besides, heaven does not know Chinese.

How could it understand what we are asking?

The Chinese pray to heaven even though they have no religion particularly skilled in the art of prayer.

#### Consequences of the Unity of Being

We have already quoted Ibn Arabi’s claim that:

> “Formation came from none but themselves.”

This again confirms his denial of an independent act of creation and therefore of the idea of a Creator in the ordinary sense.

From here he moves toward the term **the Real Existence**: that which permeates existing things, or manifests itself to them, and through which they come to exist.

Its permeation comes from inside things because the Real Existence is not separate from them.

But, as we have seen, rejecting a Creator does not necessarily lead Ibn Arabi to pure materialism.

The idea of a Real Existence that manifests itself to things gives the universe the vitality of a cosmic spirit and prevents it from becoming a dead mass of purely sensory matter.

A distance therefore remains between physics and unity of being, because each operates in a different sphere.

Ibn Arabi, Goethe, and Spinoza all remained committed to scientific truth.

They did not oppose physics.

What they opposed was a universe emptied of spirit.

Without spirit, human relationships themselves risk becoming mechanical.

The human being then becomes an isolated natural phenomenon, like a tree that grows and bears fruit for itself, and when it grows old is cut down so that people can use its wood and plant another tree in its place.

But the human being is a social being, not merely a natural one.

Scientific materialism itself says this.

On this point it agrees with Sufism and with Goethe: the human being is a higher being who emerged from nature, but emerged in order to complete nature rather than simply remain subject to it.

Human beings transform nature’s harsh and rigid laws into social relationships infused with spirit.

Even the Soviet system of the Brezhnev era, materialist through and through in all its methods, found itself compelled to use the expression **spiritual culture** as the opposite of reducing the human being to a purely material object.

It raised the slogan:

> “Man does not live by bread alone.”

But the slogan was raised too early.

First comes the bread.

The hungry must be fed, clothed, and housed before we begin demanding that they spiritualise themselves.

The people always in the greatest hurry to raise this slogan are politicians and intellectuals—the people who were well fed from the beginning.

For the Sufis, bread itself is one of the necessary conditions of spiritualisation.

Their programmes begin with feeding the hungry.

In Sufi unity of being, the human being becomes divine and therefore becomes the object of the highest good and absolute justice.

For that reason, a human being must not be killed.

Ibn Arabi says in the Jonah chapter of the *Fusus*:

> “The right to destroy this human structure belongs to God alone. It is not permitted to human beings.”

This is the earliest opposition to the death penalty.

It takes away from the human being the authority to kill another human being and makes death the exclusive right of God, who causes each person to die at the appointed time.

Ibn Arabi supports his position with a story about David.

David wanted to build the Temple in Jerusalem. Each time he finished building it, it collapsed.

He complained to God.

God revealed to him:

> “This house of Mine cannot be established by the hand of someone who has shed blood.”

David replied:

> “Lord, was that blood not shed in Your cause?”

God answered:

> “Yes. But were they not also My servants?”

Ibn Arabi also cites the verse:

> “If they incline toward peace, then incline toward it.”

He takes this to mean that peace comes before war.

And he cites:

> “The repayment of an evil is an evil equal to it.”

From this he concludes that retaliation itself is an evil.

For Ibn Arabi, killing prevents the purpose for which the human being was created from being fulfilled.

The human being was created in order to become complete.

Throughout the stages of his life, until his appointed end, he remains capable of acquiring the perfection for which he was created.

Anyone who destroys him therefore prevents him from reaching the purpose of his creation.

Within the same argument, Ibn Arabi makes **existence** synonymous with **mercy**, and the **existent** synonymous with the **recipient of mercy**.

He says that mercy embraces everything.

Punishment, meanwhile, is not punishment as literalists understand it.

It too is mercy.

Indeed, he even derives *adhab*, punishment, from *udhuba*, sweetness.

He uses this to interpret the punishment of the inhabitants of hell.

Happiness belongs to everyone, though it takes different forms.

The people of hell enjoy a form of bliss but suffer from the pain of being veiled.

That is what distinguishes them from the people of paradise, who enjoy nearness to the Real and the vision of Him.

The people of hell are veiled from Him.

Their punishment is therefore spiritual, not physical.

To reach this conclusion, Muhyi al-Din commits astonishing violence against Quranic texts, at times almost treating them with contempt.

Look at his interpretation of the wind that destroyed the people of Ad, in the tenth *fass*:

> “‘He drives the criminals’—that is, those who deserved the station toward which the west wind that destroyed them drove them away from their own selves. He takes them by their forelocks, and the wind drives them. The wind itself is the desires they followed. It drives them toward hell, which is the distance they imagined.
>
> “But once they are brought to that place, they find themselves in the very essence of nearness. Distance disappears from them, and with it what they called hell.
>
> “They therefore attain the bliss of nearness by right, because they are criminals. This delicious station of direct experience was not given to them as a gift. They obtained it through what the realities of their own actions deserved…
>
> “And in this wind is *adhab*—that is, something they find sweet when they taste it, though it pains them because it separates them from what they were accustomed to.”

The Shaykh seems unwilling to recognise punishment, whatever the crime.

Instead, he rewards the criminals with bliss—the very “punishment” they discover to be sweet.

He also opposes punishment in this world.

But the earthly punishment he explicitly rejects is killing.

He does not discuss lesser punishments such as imprisonment.[^ms-v1-007]

His arguments against killing are new in the history of human thought, and they possess both depth and originality.

But the Shaykh soon gets himself into trouble.

He is carried into it by intuitive chains of association that are not governed by any clear logic, and he begins speaking in a way marked by intoxication.

A drunk man can be right and wrong at the same time.

Perhaps Ibn Arabi never went back over the *Fusus* after completing it.

Or perhaps he remained drunk, since he seems to have spent most of his time in that condition.

Either way, his contradictions reached us exactly as they were.

The great disease of writing is failure to revise.

Had I lived in his time and worked beside him, I would have brought him out of his intoxication by pointing out where he contradicted himself.

In the Seth chapter of the *Fusus*, while discussing return to God and the way things vary in the eye of the observer according to the observer’s temperament—or the observer’s temperament varies according to the manifestation—Ibn Arabi says:

> “If the dead person and the murdered person—whoever the dead person may be and whoever the murdered person may be—did not return to God after death or killing, God would never have decreed anyone’s death or legislated killing…”

Return to God is the hope for the immortality of the spirit that has occupied humanity ever since we first became conscious of death.

It is also the hope of the Sufi when he becomes unable to tolerate the small circle of earthly life.

The murdered person shares in this return.

But here the Shaykh attributes the legislation of killing to God.

He makes killing part of divine law after previously rejecting it and saying that the destruction of the human structure belongs to God alone.

Clearly, when he says that God “legislated killing,” he is referring to the Sharia rules governing retaliation and similar punishments.

Yet earlier he rejected retaliation and, citing the Quran, called it an evil like criminal killing itself.

He should have slowed down and thought more carefully before overturning what he had said about destroying the human structure.

But then again, the drunk are not held to account.

Another consequence of unity of being is the disappearance of revelation from outside the self.

Ibn Arabi says in the *Fusus*:

> “No revelation comes to you from another, and you do not cast it toward another.”

That is because you are Him, and He is in you.

Your revelation comes from within yourself.

The philosophers of Islam and the great masters of philosophical Sufism agree on this interpretation of prophecy.

They also agree in rejecting the religious picture of God as a ruler sitting on a throne, surrounded by servants who carry out His orders and whom He sends out on missions.

Gabriel is a symbol, not a courier carrying a message.

The angels exist, but they do not take on physical bodies.

One of the Mu'tazilites had already denied such embodiment.

The philosophers accept Gabriel only as a symbol.

In the Joseph chapter of the *Fusus*, Ibn Arabi is explicit about Gabriel.

Read his words and take them as he intends them:

> “When revelation came to the Prophet—peace be upon him—he was taken away from the ordinary world of the senses. He was covered and became absent from those around him. When the state passed, he returned.
>
> “What he perceived, therefore, he perceived only in the presence of imagination, though he is not described as sleeping.
>
> “Likewise, when the angel appeared to him in the form of a man, that too belonged to the presence of imagination.”

Prophecy is imagination.

This idea had circulated in Islamic philosophy since al-Farabi.

But prophetic imagination is not the imagination of poets.

The prophet is a genius united with heaven, drawing close to it and accepting a mission that he carries to his people or to humanity.

Confucius belongs in this category.

He was certain that *Shang*, the Chinese heaven, approved of his mission.

Had his contemporaries not called him a prophet, we would have done so, because he deserves the title more than most.

Muslim philosophers also spoke of the prophecy of Socrates.

He was not far from it. He too had his connection with Delphi.

No one, however, attributed prophecy to poets—except Abu al-Tayyib al-Mutanabbi, who claimed it for himself.

People therefore called him **Mutanabbi**, “the would-be prophet,” not **Nabi**, prophet.

He might have borne the title had he not descended to the level to which poets descend when they praise the outside powers.

Prophets do not approve of praise of that kind.

Al-Suhrawardi of Baghdad wrote about the Prophet Muhammad thinking of suicide whenever revelation stopped coming to him.

A prophet cannot do without revelation and remain a prophet.

If prophecy disappears, the meaning of his continued life disappears with it.

Hence the repeated desire to throw himself from the top of a mountain.

Al-Suhrawardi mentions this in *Awarif al-Ma'arif*.

There is support for the idea in the fifteen-day interruption of revelation after Quraysh challenged Muhammad to tell them the story of the People of the Cave.

The answer did not come to him, and for those fifteen days he remained cut off from them.

The laws of artistic creation work in much the same way.

Al-Farazdaq once said that there were times when pulling one of his own teeth was easier for him than composing a single line of poetry.

The prophet is sincere in his imagination.

The charlatan deliberately lies.

The prophet’s relationship with heaven is like a one-sided love: wholly sincere and wholly exalted.

#### Unity of Being and Unity of Witnessing

Ibn Arabi often says that the Real is the only true existent and that all other existing things are merely His shadow. They possess no real existence in themselves.

This may belong to the state of intoxication.

As Abu al-Ala Afifi explains it, the Sufi in such a state feels the unity of the Real and creation. He loses awareness of himself and of everything other than God and recognises only the Real as truly existing.

This is the state of union with the Real.

It is called **unity of witnessing**.

Some have called it **unity of the existent**.

Sadr al-Din al-Shirazi, the chief of the philosophers, also entered this state because of the Sufi-Illuminationist element in his spiritual formation, not because of his philosophy.

Al-Shirazi was first and foremost a philosopher.

But his teacher al-Damad, the leading figure among the Shi'i mystics, gave him flashes of Illuminationist experience.

They were what drove him to make the pilgrimage seven times.

Philosophers do not make the pilgrimage seven times.

On his seventh pilgrimage he died in Basra, our sweet city.

I searched everywhere there for some trace of his grave and found nothing.

Al-Shirazi followed this path in a state of ecstasy, living in a strange condition suspended between the sobriety of philosophers and the intoxication of the Sufi poor.

Sobriety, however, remained stronger in him.

Sadr al-Din al-Shirazi received the doctrine of unity of being from Ibn Arabi, then carried it east and west and built from it his theory of the ascent of beings and his other theories concerning substantial motion.

Unity of being is therefore the mother and origin of the system.

Unity of witnessing is not.

Nor is unity of the existent.

When the Sufi wakes from intoxication, the actual entities of things appear to him again in their true reality and identity.

He sees them with the physical eye and the inner eye together.

It is on this sober perception that the doctrine is built as a philosophical structure.

Sufi intoxication can do to a person what it did to al-Hallaj, or what it can do in other states of manifestation and confrontation with authority.

It may reveal things that cannot be revealed in sobriety.

But sometimes the intoxication comes from the hangover rather than the wine.

Dreams also differ in this way.

Our people use the word **vision** for a noble dream and reserve ordinary **dream** for its opposite.

A vision is when you see the Lord of Majesty, your shaykh, or your cosmic beloved.

A mere dream is when you see the caliph or the governor.

Intoxication works in the same way.

It can make Ibn Arabi legitimise the death penalty in one drunken state after rejecting it in another.

Or it can lead him into unity of witnessing, when sobriety would have served him better.

Sobriety, visionary intoxication, and the true vision itself nevertheless dominate the Sufi experience.

It is from them that we derive the real concepts of Sufism in their overall consistency and intelligibility.

But this does not require us to excuse their excesses.

What man is ever completely free of fault?

We will return to this kind of issue under the **Essence of Reproach**, where I will discuss it in greater detail.

#### Prophecy and Sainthood

In Chapter 73 of the *Futuhat al-Makkiyya*, Ibn Arabi divides prophecy into two forms:

**the prophecy of sainthood** and **the prophecy of legislation**.

The saint is therefore a prophet, but one without a religious law of his own.

The qualification is necessary so that the saint cannot reach the point of abolishing the Sharia.

Ibn Arabi opposed its abolition, for reasons we will discuss later.

But the prophet who brings a law can learn from the prophet of sainthood.

The saint therefore becomes the prophet’s teacher.

Ibn Arabi bases this on the story of Moses and Khidr.

Moses is a prophet.

Khidr is a saint.

And the Quran explicitly presents Khidr’s knowledge as higher than Moses’:

> “That which you have not encompassed in knowledge…”

In the *Fusus*, Ibn Arabi makes an extraordinary move from the question of the caliphate.

Among Muslims other than the Shi'a, the generally accepted view is that the Prophet Muhammad did not explicitly name anyone as his successor.

Ibn Arabi uses this to explain the continuation of prophecy.

In the David chapter he says:

> “The Messenger of God died without explicitly appointing anyone as his successor or naming a particular person, because he knew that within his community there would be those who received the caliphate directly from their Lord and thus became caliphs from God, while remaining in agreement with the revealed law.
>
> “Knowing this, the Prophet did not restrict the matter.”

Being a caliph from God is not necessarily identical with prophecy.

Elsewhere Ibn Arabi quotes the hadith:

> “There is no prophet after me.”

He says that this hadith narrowed the outward path available to the saints of God, because it blocked the road to prophecy.

Ibn Sabʿin would later draw the same conclusion from the hadith.

Ibn Arabi nevertheless tries to open the road again by distinguishing between the prophecy of sainthood and the prophecy of legislation in the way we have described.

Then, at this point, ambition gets the better of him.

He says:

> “God was gentle with His servants. He left them the prophecy that contains no new legislation. He also left them legislation through independent reasoning in determining legal rulings. And He left them inheritance in legislation, for the Prophet said: ‘The scholars are the heirs of the prophets.’
>
> “There is therefore no inheritance except in those rulings they reach by independent reasoning and then legislate.”

The saint can therefore inherit even legislative prophecy.

The general conclusion is that the Sufis wanted prophecy because prophecy allowed them to escape the confinement of religious doctrine.

They paid much less attention to the problem of religious law.

That was the concern of the Ismailis, who argued that the Sharia could be abolished and actually abolished it in territories under their control: the Qarmatian state in al-Ahsa and the Ismaili strongholds in Syria and Iran.

Ibn Arabi took a different position on abolition.

He believed that the oppression people suffered under rulers came from failure to observe the Sharia, not from the Sharia itself.

We have already seen his conversation with the ruler of Aleppo and what the ruler revealed to him about the corruption of the jurists and their willingness to issue legal rulings permitting injustice and wrongdoing.

Ibn Arabi therefore concluded that the way out of political oppression was a return to the rule of the Sharia, not its abolition.

In Chapter 310 of the *Futuhat*, he explains that jurists attached to the established schools often gave rulings without returning to the schools’ actual foundations.

This led him to reject personal opinion in legal rulings because, in this setting, personal opinion meant bending law to appetite and desire.

Originally, *ra'y*, reasoned opinion, had been the method associated with Abu Hanifa for extending legal rulings to new questions.

It had been one of the foundations of the development of jurisprudence.

Ibn Arabi would probably never have rejected it if the jurists of his own age had not abused it and put it at the service of their patrons, the rulers.

The reality of the question of abolition is this:

The Sharia was necessary as a means of controlling rulers and restraining them from injustice—confiscating property, killing people arbitrarily, following their desires, and spreading corruption.

In this respect it served the same function that a constitution serves in a modern state, limiting political action through law.

But if we want to move further toward social liberation and solve the everyday economic problems of the masses, then the Sharia must eventually be superseded.

This is what happened in the Qarmatian state in eastern Arabia.

The Qarmatians went beyond the legal rules of zakat. They took control of public and private wealth and reorganised the economy in a way intended to guarantee people equal rights within it.

Alongside this new economic order, they abolished other rules that did not suit their community.

Among them was polygynous marriage, which they replaced with the single-partner family together with the abolition of concubinage.

The Sufis too abolished some rules of the Sharia in practice, even if they never turned the abolition into an ideology.

Most refused to marry more than one woman, avoided concubinage, and did not own slaves.

They also moved beyond the level of wealth accumulation permitted by the Sharia.

Sometimes they theorised the invalidity of a practice while continuing to perform it.

Pilgrimage is one example.

They were close to unanimous in regarding pilgrimage as spiritually invalid in its literal form.

Ibn Arabi says in the *Tarjuman al-Ashwaq*:

> She circles my heart
> hour after hour,
> in longing and anguish,
> kissing my pillars,
>
> just as the best of messengers
> circled the Kaaba—
> though reason itself
> can point to its deficiency—
>
> and kissed its stones
> though he was a speaking being.
> What rank can the House possess
> beside the worth of a human being?

Yet Ibn Arabi himself performed the pilgrimage twice.

The Sufis carried out an even greater abolition when they called for **feeding the hungry of the whole world**.

That effectively cancels the limits of zakat and greatly expands the prohibition on hoarding wealth.

It would require fundamental changes to the laws governing earnings and property, much as the Qarmatians introduced.

Had the Sufis ever taken political power, we might have discovered what they would actually have done in order to realise this programme within a state.

#### Unity of Religions and Freedom of Belief

One principle runs through the great Sufi masters, whether their concern is social or philosophical:

opposition to repression, persecution, and physical or psychological torture.

Psychological torture means humiliation: an assault on human dignity.

Dignity was one of their firmest principles and was closely connected to the older Arab idea of personal honour.

Their rejection of cruelty in this world was matched by their thinking about the next.

They rejected the idea of endless punishment in the afterlife, just as they rejected a crudely material picture of paradise and understood its pleasures in spiritual terms.

There were two main positions.

The first held that punishment is real but temporary.

A person should not be punished for longer than the offence itself warrants.

Since a person’s wrongdoing is limited in time—it can only take place during a finite human life—the punishment cannot be eternal.

The earliest person I know to have said this was al-Jahiz, according to a report quoted by al-Ghazali in *al-Mustasfa*.

Ibn Arabi states it openly in both the *Fusus* and the *Futuhat*.

In the *Futuhat* he says that mystical unveiling had not shown him exactly how long the punishment lasts. He invites anyone to whom the Real reveals the answer to add it at that point in his book.

No one added anything after him.

In any case, the period is finite and broadly corresponds to the finite span of earthly life.

After that, they remain in the Fire—but their punishment becomes pleasurable.

The second view was also put forward by Ibn Arabi.

He played on the Arabic relationship between *adhab*, punishment, and *udhuba*, sweetness, and argued that what is called punishment is not really punishment in the ordinary sense even from the beginning.

We have already seen the forced interpretation he gave to the punishment of the people of Ad.

In the standard theological account, punishment in the next world falls on unbelievers—that is, non-Muslims:

pagans; Jews after the coming of Christ; and Christians after the coming of Muhammad.

Jews and Christians belonging to the earlier periods enter paradise.

Sinful Muslims can also be punished.

For the Sufis, the removal of punishment extends to all of them.

The same principle applies in this world.

People must not be harmed because of their religion.

As for Muslims, sinners among them also fall within the scope of mercy and should not be harmed.

The great Sufi masters did not directly attack the legal punishments laid down by religious law.

Their teachings on good conduct and tolerance seem instead to have been addressed to people in their dealings with one another.

There is little doubt that they found those punishments repellent because of their harshness.

But I have not found anyone among them who openly dared to reject them.

What they rejected was punishment as a general moral principle.

The phrase **“enforcing the prescribed penalties,”** so common among the jurists, is largely absent from Sufi literature.

Their language turns instead toward mercy for **creation**.

When speaking about mercy and tolerance, they rarely use the narrower word **Muslims**.

They prefer **creation**, because it includes Muslims and everyone else.

In Ibn al-Mulaqqin’s *Tabaqat*, Ishaq, the son of the great Sufi master Ibrahim ibn Shayban, once asked his father:

“How do I reach true scrupulousness?”

His father replied:

“By eating what is lawfully earned and serving the poor.”

“Who are the poor?” Ishaq asked.

His father answered:

“Everyone.”

This is my translation from the *Tabaqat*.

He made “everyone”—all creation—equivalent to “the poor,” meaning ordinary people as a whole.[^ms-v1-008]

His answer was deliberately universal so that his son would not imagine he meant only poor Muslims.

Ibn Arabi makes similar statements in both the *Futuhat* and the *Fusus*.

In the *Futuhat*, in the section on the alms paid at the end of Ramadan within Chapter 70 on the mysteries of almsgiving, he comments on the legal ruling concerning a Muslim who has a Jewish or Christian relative for whom support is due.

He says that the Muslim, Jew, and Christian stand on equal ground in belief.

By this he means the unity and closeness of the three heavenly religions.

But even if we accept the argument that the Jews fell into associationism by saying that Ezra was the son of God, and the Christians by saying that Christ was the son of God, the life of a person holding such a belief still has exactly the same right to protection as the life of a believer.

From here Ibn Arabi makes a much bolder leap.

He treats religious beliefs as conventions constructed by those who hold them.

God, in these beliefs, becomes something fashioned from inside the human self:

> “They saw nothing but themselves and what they had placed within themselves.”

From this he reaches the following advice:

> “Never bind yourself to one particular belief and reject all the rest…
>
> Make your own self capable of receiving every form of belief.”

(*Fusus*, 10)

In the twelfth *fass* he speaks of:

> “people of different beliefs who call one another unbelievers and curse one another, and who have no one to come to their aid.”

He means the conflict among religions.

The gods of beliefs differ.

The god of one belief has no authority over the god of another.

God does not give victory to one believer in isolation over the others. He encompasses all people of belief together.

In other words, God does not possess one particular religion.

This directly challenges the principle:

> “The religion with God is Islam.”

It also challenges anyone who claims that his own religion, uniquely and absolutely, is God’s religion.

Ibn Arabi here rejects the idea that any one doctrine possesses absolute truth.

Conflict among religions had occupied Muslim thinkers long before him, beginning with al-Razi, then al-Maʿarri, and later the great Sufi masters.

They had a great deal to say about the question.

We have discussed their statements, as have others, in many different places.

Ibn Arabi’s position on religion is connected with his doctrine of unity of being, just as most of his other doctrines branch out from that central theory.

In the *Futuhat* he says:

> “All is witnessed and witnessing. All is superior and inferior. If one says ‘I,’ the other says ‘I.’ If one says ‘you,’ the other says ‘you.’”

This applies to the relationship between the Real and creation.

It also applies among created beings themselves.

#### The God of Religion and the Absolute God

We have already discussed Ibn Arabi’s claim that people of different beliefs are equal, and that the god of each belief is true for the believer who holds it.

It therefore makes no sense to call that believer an unbeliever, because the god of every belief is that believer’s true god and cannot be disputed away.

Ibn Arabi returns to this subject in the final, Muhammadan chapter of the *Fusus*, where he approaches it from another angle and develops a theory distinguishing the **God of religion** from the **Creator—or Absolute God**.

The deity, he says, is the particular form of the Real that the servant creates in his own heart, either through his own thought or through imitation of others.

This deity varies according to the inner readiness of the place in which it appears—that is, according to the believer.

Ibn Arabi cites a saying of al-Junayd.

Someone asked al-Junayd about knowledge of God and the person who knows God.

He replied:

> “The colour of the water is the colour of its vessel.”

Ibn Arabi says this was an unsurpassed answer because it described the matter exactly as it is.

This deity—the god created by believers—is the deity mentioned in the Quran as “praying upon” or blessing us:

> “He is the one who blesses you, together with His angels…”

This god appears only after humanity appears.

That is, he exists later than the human being.

The implication is that the human being himself produced him.

As usual, Ibn Arabi takes a Quranic verse and interprets it according to his own consistent method.

The Quran says:

> “There is nothing that does not glorify Him with praise…”

Ibn Arabi refers the singular pronoun back to the **thing**, rather than to God.

He argues that praising the deity is really the worshipper praising himself.

Why?

Because this deity is the worshipper’s own creation.

When a person praises his god, he is therefore praising something he himself produced.

Ibn Arabi adds that because the believer created his own deity, he finds fault with the deities of others.

Every person loves what belongs to him and dislikes what belongs to someone else.

This, Ibn Arabi says, is ignorance.

The believer should instead return to al-Junayd’s saying:

> “The colour of the water is the colour of its vessel.”

Then he would understand that the relationship between his own god and himself is identical to the relationship between another person and that other person’s god.

The difference between one god and another simply reflects the difference between one believer and another.

In the end, all of them are believers, since the god in each case originates in the believer himself.

The god worshipped by a believer is therefore a limited god.

That is why the believer’s heart is large enough to contain him.

The same is true of every god believed in by people belonging to the various religions.

We are speaking here of the gods of religion, whether pagan or heavenly.

But this god is not the same as the **Absolute God**, whom nothing can contain because He is both the essence of all things and His own essence.

Because the Absolute God is unlimited, He cannot enter the believer’s heart.

The unlimited cannot be contained by the limited.

He is therefore unknown to religious believers.

He is neither limited nor personified nor constructed.

We are left, then, with two gods.

The first is the limited, manufactured God of religion, who enters the hearts of believers.

The second is the Absolute God, who brings existing things into being through manifestation—that is, by merging with them.

This Absolute God is absolute existence, or the Real Existence, or whatever other name we choose to give it.

This God has no religion.

Nor are there believers who worship Him.

Believers worship the particular god they themselves know, the god who can fit inside their hearts because he is limited.

The Absolute God, by contrast, is a hidden reality through which the existential philosopher explains the emergence of the world.

Here a creative imagination has produced an extraordinary distinction between two ideas:

the idea of a Creator as connected with natural existence, and the idea of a god as connected with religion.

The implication is that the God of religions is not the true creator of the world.

#### The Beautiful Names

Among the Shi'a they number sixty-one.

There are also one hundred names of the Prophet and ninety-nine names of Ali.

For the rest of the Muslims, the Beautiful Names number one hundred and belong exclusively to the Creator.

For Ibn Arabi, however, all the Beautiful Names are human attributes just as much as divine ones.

We are His attributes because we are His manifestation.

This follows directly from unity of being, where the Real and creation become identified with one another.

Muhyi al-Din does not reserve a single attribute exclusively for the Real rather than creation.

He does not even reserve the power of bringing-into-being.

As we have already seen, he makes that reciprocal:

> He brings into being, and I bring into being.
> He brought me into being so that I might bring Him into being.

Al-Sarraj al-Tusi, however, makes two exceptions in *al-Luma'*:

**al-Rahman**, the All-Merciful, and **al-Samad**, the Self-Sufficient.

The quality of *al-Rahman* is something we attach ourselves to, not something we ourselves embody.

And *al-Samad* is beyond all comprehension or containment.

It is infinite, while the human being is finite.

The word *Rahman* originally comes from mercy.

But the Arabs already used it before Islam as another name for the divine, and Muslim Arabs continued that usage.

They therefore did not permit it to be used as a description of anyone other than God.

Apart from this, a human being can be described with almost every description applied to God:

powerful, knowing, majestic, great, merciful, forgiving, self-sustaining.

Al-Sarraj does not mention **al-Bari'**, the Originator, or say whether it too can be applied to a human being.

But his silence suggests that he includes it within the general rule.

Religious people allow only some divine qualities to be used of human beings, such as merciful or seeing.

They are horrified by others: great, majestic, forgiving, self-sustaining, originator.

Likewise, they do not permit people to be given the Beautiful Names as personal names unless they are joined to **Abd**, servant:

Abd al-Azim,
Abd al-Jalil,
Abd al-Ghafur,
Abd al-Bari'.

The Sufis allow the names to be used not merely as personal names but as real descriptions of human beings, because they accept the unity of the attributes of Creator and created.

#### Dialectic

I apologise to my dear readers for using this worn-out word.

Ordinary people are not the ones who wore it out.

If the word had any place in their lives, they would never wear it out except by using it for something useful.

I apologise because intellectuals and politicians are the ones who have ruined it.

And these people never wear out anything that is actually useful to ordinary people.

In their hands, “dialectic” has become part of the school curriculum.

A student is asked about it in an examination and is judged brilliant or stupid, passed or failed, depending on the teacher’s opinion.

But I have found no substitute for the word.

Dialectic is a subject, not a particular individual thing.

Subjects do not disappear when the individual things that carry them change. The individual things come and go while the subject remains.

So I have no choice but to apologise once more and then continue discussing dialectic as the Sufis used it.

Its clearest use appears in their statements about unity of being.

This is a unity made of opposites, in which the Real and creation exchange positions.

The Real becomes creation.

Creation becomes the Real.

The Shaykh has countless scenes of this kind, in prose and poetry, filling his major works—especially the *Futuhat* and the *Fusus*, the crown of his writings.

A short journey through them will be useful:

> Whoever He makes universal,
> He also makes particular;
>
> whoever He makes particular,
> He also makes universal.
>
> No essence is other than essence;
> the light of its eye
> is its darkness.

The pronoun refers to **the Real** in its relation to creation. That relation is one of unity and distinction at the same time. The Real is creation itself, and creation is the Real itself; yet the Real remains the Real and creation remains creation. They are one contradictory whole, made up of two distinct sides that are joined even as they differ.

Let us read some of this delirium:

> We are His, as surely as our humanity is real—
> and yet we are not.
>
> He has no being apart from my being;
> we are His as we are ourselves.
>
> I have two faces: He and I,
> yet He has no “I” apart from me.
>
> And in His manifestation,
> we are to Him like water in a vessel.

The two sides overlap and run into one another. Neither appears without immediately becoming the other. The pronoun “He” enters the pronoun “I”; what belongs to Him becomes ours; the two faces become one.

Yet they remain distinct, like water and the vessel that holds it. Water is not the vessel, and the two do not become one substance. They become one in colour. The relation works in both directions: the water enters the vessel and takes on its colour, while the vessel gives its colour to the water—that is, it becomes visible through it. The water becomes the vessel’s image.

So too the Real has no colour of its own. It takes its colour from whatever it appears through, just as water takes the colour of its vessel.

Contradictions are unavoidable if we are to accept existence as it actually is. Existence needs beings, and beings need existence. The Real brought beings into existence so that it might exist through them. They feed on it, and it feeds on them. Each depends on the other for its existence. The existing thing is therefore also what brings into existence; the created is also the creator; what is general contains the particular, and what is particular contains the general.

The same applies to the relation between servant and Lord. The servant is Lord and the Lord is servant; the worshipper is the worshipped, and the worshipped is the worshipper:

> He praises me, and I praise Him.
> He worships me, and I worship Him.
>
> For this the Real brought me into being:
> that I might know Him—and so bring Him into being.

None of this works according to formal logic, where every term must have an identity independent of every other term, and names and things must not be confused.

This is why Ibn Arabi insists that philosophical reason is incapable of understanding such matters: it sees things in separation, not in connection.

What he probably has in mind is the Peripatetic school—the Aristotelian tradition that dominated large parts of philosophy in the Islamic world. Its logic rests on two laws governing the consistency and distinction of things: the **law of non-contradiction** and the **law of the excluded middle**. Added to these is another principle: that a substance cannot contain opposites.

These are Aristotelian laws. Aristotle dominated philosophical study both in the Islamic age and in medieval Europe. He placed philosophy behind a screen that prevented it from moving further, and left it satisfied with the truths he had established—truths that were, in themselves, immense and valuable.

Islamic thought tried to move beyond Aristotle. In some areas it succeeded, and in others it failed.

One success was al-Razi’s establishment of intellectual independence in philosophy. Another was the Sufis’ treatment of contradiction, where they simply set aside Aristotle’s three laws.

The great master of Islamic dialectics is Ibn Arabi, followed later by Sadr al-Din al-Shirazi.

Ibn Arabi’s treatment of opposites puts the human mind through severe trials. It forces the mind to leave what it is used to and try to absorb truths that initially look irrational and illogical.

He gives free rein to his visions and often falls into delirium. Reason rejects them at first sight, then at second sight, and then for many sightings after that. But gradually it can train itself to live with them. It moves from the quiet comfort of harmony into the restless movement of collision and contradiction.

Ibn Arabi used this experience as a way of accusing reason itself of weakness, replacing it with unveiling and revelation.

But it is Aristotelian reason that should stand accused here, not philosophical reason as such.

The instruments of unveiling and revelation are intuition and contemplation outside the rules of Aristotelian logic. They lead the mind to deal with things through their oppositions and double nature, rather than through harmony and separate identities.

In Ibn Arabi’s thought, nothing exists without also containing its opposite.

Contradiction is universal and complete. It is not limited to one kind of thing and excluded from another.

This differs from the philosophers who inherited from their First Teacher, Aristotle, the idea that contradiction applies to accidents but not substances—to qualities and quantities, but not to the inner nature of a thing.

Ibn Arabi’s extension of contradiction to everything is what later led Sadr al-Din al-Shirazi to place contradiction within substance itself, and from there to build his great theory of **substantial motion**.

Dialectics has earlier roots in the philosophy of the Greek Heraclitus, who came before Aristotle, and before Heraclitus in Chinese Taoist philosophy.

Taoist contradiction, like Sufi contradiction, appears in everything. Nothing among human beings or in nature exists without carrying its opposite within it. Even our hidden feelings—grief, pain, misery, happiness—pass into one another and overlap.

Ibn Arabi went even deeper into these reciprocal relations when he argued that cause and effect are bound together.

In doing so, he attacked what he saw as the limits of philosophical reason, which rules that a cause cannot itself be caused by the thing it causes.

By contrast, he said that the “knowledge of revelation” shows us that a cause can indeed become the effect of its own effect.

He says this in the *Fusus*.

The reciprocal relationship between cause and effect is also one of the questions taken up by modern dialectics. Marxism places particular emphasis on it because it enters directly into its method for interpreting both nature and history.

Through it, the limits imposed on understanding by formal logic can be broken. It also corrects a weakness in induction, which tends to remain confined to relations between particulars and avoids broader generalisation.

Generalisation requires a dialectical understanding of relationships.

In this respect Marxism is doing work very similar to what earlier masters of dialectics had attempted, among them Muhyi al-Din Ibn Arabi and Sadr al-Din al-Shirazi.

Marxism is not responsible for the way our own boys have vulgarised dialectics.[^ms-v1-009]

Dialectics is a complicated business. It is not easy.

In fact, it may be harder to **use** than formal logic, though not harder to explain.

Formal logic is complicated in its explanations and details, but easy to use.

Dialectical logic is easy enough to explain and state in general terms, but difficult to use properly.

Sadr al-Din noticed this difficulty himself. In his *Four Journeys*, his major philosophical work, he says that it requires a penetrating insight able to see, by the light of its own eye, how the thing that passes away is also the thing that remains.

It is easy enough to understand a formal syllogism:

“All human beings die.
This is a human being.
Therefore this person will die.”

It is not nearly as easy to understand how a cause can itself be caused by its own effect.

So fear God when you talk about dialectics, and stop blackening our faces with it.

#### The Shaykh and the Woman

Women occupy a better position among the great Sufi masters than they do among religious authorities.

Reading the great Sufis, you do not come across the same things that shock you in the writings of religious men—statements that attack a woman’s basic humanity. For them she is a complete human being, just as a man is. They do not repeat the things male chauvinists say about her.

The great Sufi master does not use a masculine language of superiority in matters concerning women.

As we have already seen, many of them preferred marriage to one woman and avoided concubinage. I am speaking here of the major masters.

And although they were wary of sexual temptation, they did not place the burden of that temptation on women or treat them as little more than traps laid by male desire.

Sufi principles allow a woman to enter the path. Her guide can be a male shaykh; there is no requirement that her guide be a woman. There are male and female disciples, male and female travellers on the path, male and female gnostics.

A woman on the path may also be less strict about veiling and leave her face uncovered.

A wife may become a Sufi while her husband does not, or both may follow the path.

We will return to all of this in much greater detail in the Orbit of Habāʾ.

In the *Futuhat*, Ibn Arabi devotes an entire chapter to women—chapter 324.

He begins by repeating the common sayings of his time: that women are deficient in reason, that woman was created from a man’s rib, and so on.

This is often his habit when he is preparing to state a personal opinion of his own.

Ibn Arabi’s position on women is close to that of Ibn Rushd, though he develops it more fully. He may well have known Ibn Rushd’s view. They came from the same country, lived at the same time, and Ibn Arabi was the younger man.

The Shaykh says that men and women can share every spiritual rank, including the highest rank of all, that of the **Pole**.

He then comes up against the hadith, “A people who entrust their affairs to a woman will not prosper.”

His answer is that he is speaking about **God appointing someone**, not people appointing someone.

Unlike his normal habit, he does not spin off into a series of interpretations designed to make the hadith fit his position. He simply separates appointment by people from appointment by God, and assumes that the hadith applies only to the first.

In any case, the jurists did not forbid women from studying law, religious sciences, or other branches of knowledge. Nor is there anything in the teachings of religious law that prevents a woman from becoming a saint. The Quran itself gives us holy women, among them Mary.

Ibn Arabi says that every station, rank, and quality open to a man can also be given by God to any woman He chooses.

He even produces a playful formal proof to give women a degree above men, balancing the Quranic phrase that “men have a degree over them.”

The Arabic word *imraʾa*, woman, contains more letters than *marʾ*, man, because of the additional *tāʾ*.

The same is true in English, where **woman** has more letters than **man**.

But the two are equal in Chinese!

He performs another reversal with the Quranic verse that makes the testimony of two women equal to that of one man.

The verse explains this by saying that if one of the women forgets, the other can remind her.

Ibn Arabi turns the meaning around.

For the woman, he says, forgetfulness is only partial. For the man it is total, because the Quran tells us of Adam: “He forgot, and We found in him no firmness of purpose.”

The verse therefore shows, on Ibn Arabi’s reading, that a man may forget his testimony altogether and never recover it, while one of the two women must remember, because the verse itself says so.

And if the second woman remembers without forgetting, then she has been given a divine attribute: freedom from forgetfulness, as in Moses’ statement, “My Lord neither errs nor forgets.”

This is certainly the talk of a drunkard.

The verse explicitly attributes forgetfulness to one of the two women and says nothing of the kind about the one man.

In more serious reflections, the Shaykh says that femininity is honoured by the fact that the word **essence** (*dhāt*) is grammatically feminine when applied to God, as is the word **attribute** (*ṣifa*).

We say “the essence of God,” “the divine essence,” and “the divine attributes.”

This particular “honour” is accidental as far as God is concerned. But it is real in the structure of Arabic itself, where masculine and feminine forms are spread throughout the vocabulary, with a noticeable tendency toward the feminine in the names of major natural and social phenomena and in many collective nouns.

The Shaykh goes further, using his familiar method of strained interpretation to prove the greatness and sacredness of the feminine.

God, he says, forbade us to think about the **essence** of God, but did not forbid us to speak about **divine unity**.

Since “essence” is feminine, this supposedly proves the holiness of the feminine, which must not be subjected to thought.[^ms-v1-010]

“Unity,” on the other hand, is grammatically masculine and therefore less sacred, leaving us free to discuss it.

In the final chapter of the *Fusus* he displays the same bias in favour of the feminine.

Choose whatever philosophical doctrine you like, he says, and you will still find the feminine coming first.

Even those who speak of a first cause and say that the Real is the cause of the world must use the feminine noun *ʿilla*, “cause.”

And if you prefer to say “power,” *qudra*, that too is feminine.

In the same chapter of the *Futuhat*, Ibn Arabi compares woman in relation to man with **nature** in relation to the divine command.

This belongs to the doctrine of unity of being, not to ordinary theology.

Woman is the place in which the concrete forms of children come into existence, just as nature is the place in which the divine command gives visible form to physical bodies.

Bodies are formed in nature and appear from it.

A command without nature produces nothing, and nature without the command produces nothing.

The universe depends on both.

This comparison concerns the role of each side in preserving the species.

The woman receives the man’s seed and from it produces a human being, just as nature receives the divine command and from it brings material beings into existence.

In both cases the process is one of union.

The Real manifests itself in nature through a union, and nature produces.

Man unites with woman, and she produces.

In this analogy the man can seem to occupy the place of God.

The Shaykh does not stop to consider that danger. His purpose is equality between the two sides.

The comparison is strengthened by the fact that both nature and woman occupy the receptive side of the process.

But Ibn Arabi does not want receptivity to mean inferiority or second place.

Her role in the process of production is equal.

The universe depends on both sides.

#### A Reproach

With Muhyi al-Din Ibn Arabi, philosophical Sufism comes close to its highest point.

In this sense he is a Sufi philosopher.

In fact, philosophy may suit him better than Sufism. He is usually counted among the Sufis because of his Sufi method and his rejection of Aristotelian logic.

He treated questions of existence and natural life as philosophical subjects, but approached them through intuition, which he called **unveiling**.

Unveiling is intuition set free from logical rules. The philosopher moves through the hidden interiors of things without being bound by a syllogism or a prior assumption.

This method brought Ibn Arabi into dialectical contradiction. It shook the mind loose from its restraints and released it into a wider field of movement.

His complete neglect of logic, however, prevented him from developing and branching out into the full range of philosophical questions in the way we find in the works of the philosophers.

Still, he is unquestionably a philosopher, with discoveries and theories that we do not find among them.

His study should therefore be moved out of the Sufism section alone and into philosophy as well.

Ibn Arabi should be studied beside the philosophers of Islam just as he is studied beside the Sufis.

To ignore Ibn Arabi the philosopher is to diminish our understanding of what Islamic philosophy achieved.

At the same time, the dominance of philosophical and intellectual concerns in him seems to have deprived him of part of the spiritual formation expected of a great Sufi master.

Here the comparison with the masters of social Sufism no longer helps him.

He departed from them on essential points.

He maintained relations with the state and sat with sultans, although he did not use those connections for personal gain. He also tried to help people and lighten their burdens.

But he did not turn political opposition into an ethical doctrine.

Nor is he known for any extreme commitment to asceticism or for living in severe austerity.

He gave little attention to the problems of poverty and hunger.

His concern lay instead with injustices inflicted on people as a whole: the oppression of rulers and their lack of restraint.

These injustices affect rich and poor alike. They were also common concerns among Islamic intellectuals, including jurists and theologians.

Whenever the word **justice** appears in the writings of such groups, it generally refers to these injustices and the need to remove them. It is not necessarily connected to the problems of the poor and oppressed.

Umar ibn al-Khattab and al-Maʾmun, for example, were both described as just in this sense.

For the masters of social Sufism, by contrast, the central problem is always the poor and the hungry.

When Ibn Arabi discusses the prohibition on hoarding wealth in the *Futuhat*, he gives no distinctive opinion of his own. He follows the commentators who held that the prohibition had been superseded by the institution of almsgiving.

He says explicitly:

> “That was before almsgiving was made obligatory... When God imposed almsgiving on His believing servants, He purified their wealth through it.”

He simply repeats the position taken by the wealthy Companions, who rejoiced that the Quranic verse forbidding hoarding had been superseded by the law of almsgiving, “which God made a purification for their wealth.”

In other words, after paying the required alms, the rest of their wealth became lawful for them.

(*Futuhat*, chapter 70)

Ibn Arabi also violated his own doctrine of the equality of beliefs and the prohibition against attacking them when he advised a ruler in Anatolia to impose restrictions on the Christians of his province.

The editor of the Lebanese edition of the *Futuhat* excuses him by appealing to the circumstances of the Crusades.

It is more likely that the reason was Anatolia’s proximity to the Byzantines. Ibn Arabi issued no similar recommendation elsewhere. The Christians of Anatolia mixed closely with the Byzantines, and many were themselves of Byzantine origin.

But none of this excuses a recommendation that contradicts his own principles.

In such circumstances, the reasonable measure would be to keep watch over people who genuinely aroused suspicion—not to subject an entire population to discriminatory rules.

In fact, Ibn Arabi’s proposal would make matters worse.

It puts loyal members of that community in a difficult position by forcing them to live under restrictions imposed for supposedly security-related reasons.

Ibn Arabi was an intellectual.

He was not good at politics.

He also contradicted his rejection of punishment when he told a story about a stubborn enemy of his who was struck on the head by a stone sent from heaven and killed.

That is risky.

Heaven might have got its aim wrong and dropped the stone on Nizam’s head instead of the stubborn enemy’s.

The Shaykh also did himself no favours by reproducing in the *Meccan Revelations* some verses he had written in his youth about a beautiful boy.

There was no reason to include them except that he tied them to one of his fanciful stories about miracles that had happened to him.

He would have done better to invent another story that did not require those verses.

I wish he had kept the *Futuhat*—and especially the *Fusus*—free of such nonsense.

These things belong to the same appetite that seized him after his marriage to the stars.

I will confess to my dear readers that when I reached this story I was genuinely shocked.

I even considered ending my visits to him.

Ever since I came to Damascus I have visited him at the mosque Sultan Selim built around his tomb.

I do not actually enter the mosque, because I do not want to see the faces of the dervishes surrounding him.

Instead I apologise to him, stand at the gate, greet him, and offer him a Confucian blessing.

But I gave up the idea of ending my visits.

It would not be right to punish him so severely for one lapse.

As the old writers used to say, even the intelligent man of letters sometimes slips.

In any case, our Shaykh Muhyi al-Din Ibn Arabi is not spiritually complete when measured against the great masters of social Sufism.

His life resembles that of a philosopher more than that of a Sufi.

The philosophical life stands midway between the life of the poet and the life of the Sufi: moderation without asceticism or harsh self-denial; relations with the state without praise or flattery; and little concern with the everyday affairs of ordinary people.

This is where pure culture ends up:

the culture of the mind when it has not become one with the culture of the heart.

---

The second great Sufi philosopher after Ibn Arabi is **Abd al-Haqq Ibn Sabʿin**.

Like him, he was an Andalusian from the Islamic West who later went east.

Driven out of the Maghreb, he went to Egypt, preceded by a message from those who expelled him warning the Egyptians not to receive him because he said:

“I am He, and He is I.”

Unable to remain in Egypt, he went on to Mecca, where he was received and lived under protection until his death.

According to al-Dhahabi, he killed himself by opening the veins in his hands and allowing himself to bleed to death.

It is also reported that he considered going to India in search of a wider space for his thought.

Had he thought of China instead, he might have found what he was looking for.

China had no single dominant religion.

India was a continent of competing religions.

One of the best-known events in Ibn Sabʿin’s life was his response to the philosophical questions of Frederick II.

Frederick, emperor of Sicily, was passionately interested in science, rather like al-Maʾmun before him.

But he carried out the same project in reverse: he sponsored the translation of Islamic philosophy into Latin.

Frederick sent his philosophical questions with envoys to Egypt, Syria, Iraq, Yemen, and Central Asia.

The answers he received did not satisfy him.

He then asked about scholars in the Maghreb and al-Andalus and was told about Ibn Sabʿin.

An envoy was sent by sea carrying the questions together with money.

Ibn Sabʿin answered the questions but refused the money.

The emperor admired the answers and sent him a valuable gift.

Ibn Sabʿin refused that as well.

His flight to Mecca contains one of history’s ironies.

He had been expelled from the Maghreb for saying:

> “The son of Amina made something broad unnecessarily narrow when he said, ‘There is no prophet after me.’”

That is how *Fawat al-Wafayat* reports it.

And so the city of the son of Amina received him and gave him safety.

We have already seen Ibn Arabi’s comment on the hadith “There is no prophet after me”: he said that it breaks the backs of the friends of God because it prevents them from declaring their own prophethood.

The principal source for Ibn Sabʿin’s Sufi philosophy is his collection of letters.

They received a scholarly edition from Abd al-Rahman Badawi in 1965.

He also wrote a work called *The Beginning of the Gnostic*, which survives in manuscript and which I have not seen directly. But I have read many excerpts from it quoted by Muhammad Yasir Sharaf in *The Philosophy of Sabʿinian Sufism*.

It is most likely an early work.

His biographers say that he wrote it at the age of fifteen, or at the latest in his twenties if we follow Badawi’s view that it was composed while he was still in the Maghreb.

It should not be used as the main basis for judging his philosophy.

Its language and ideas are immature and show the fever of youth.

Ibn Sabʿin’s philosophy is the philosophy of **unity of being**, as it is in Ibn Arabi.

In some of his letters he speaks almost in the voice of Zhuangzi.

In advice to his disciples he says:

> “In everything there is a secret from His secret.
> It hardens in minerals,
> appears in plants,
> moves in animals,
> and declares itself in the human being.”

(*Letters*, p. 315)

In this way he makes the divine run through all things: pleasant and unpleasant alike, much as Zhuangzi does.

Like Ibn Arabi before him, he distinguishes between **absolute existence** and **conditioned**, or relative, existence.

Conditioned existence is not truly real.

Absolute existence alone is fully real.

This does not mean denying the visible and sensible world. It means that conditioned existence passes away, while absolute existence remains.

Unlike Ibn Arabi, however, Ibn Sabʿin speaks in a very strong language of divine indwelling.

He repeatedly says:

> If there is nothing but me, then there is nothing but Him.
> And if there is nothing but Him, then there is nothing but me.
>
> He is the First, and I am the first.
> He is the Last, and I am the last.
> He is the Manifest, and I am the manifest.
> He is the Hidden, and I am the hidden.

(*God’s Speech in the Language of His Light*, Letters, p. 245)

He also unites knowledge with existence.

It is not enough, he says, to say:

“We know existence and comprehend existing things.”

Rather:

“We **find** existence and act within existing things.”

This is the Sufi way of knowing.

Knowledge becomes part of the movement of things themselves.

What is known is not separated from the knower.

To know something is to become united with it.

Following Ibn Arabi, Ibn Sabʿin also denies that reason can reach true knowledge by itself.

Reason breaks the object of knowledge into parts and confines it.

Only the imaginative power—intuition—can surround the object as a whole, because it moves and changes beyond the limits imposed by rational categories.

(*Letter on Comprehension*, p. 133)

For the same reason he did not believe Aristotelian logic could reach hidden truths.

He restricted it to outward and superficial matters.

Like Ibn Arabi, he dealt with phenomena dialectically.

He delighted in bringing opposites together.

At times he sounds as if a Taoist were speaking:

> “The cause of pain is itself the cause of pleasure,
> and within suffering itself lies well-being.”

Then, in the *Letter on Comprehension*, he gives the familiar cascade of opposites:

> “Within comprehension, the even mixes with the odd;
> the stars become one with dung;
> fullness agrees with the single...
>
> In short: Saturday is Sunday.
> The one who affirms unity is the very One.
> The day of duty is the day of debt.
> The time that has gone is the present.
> The first in visible appearance is the last.
> What is hidden in imagination is the manifest...
>
> These are truths of wisdom, not imaginary inventions.”

He plays with minds by shocking them with paradoxes.

He pulls them out of stability and habit, destroys the pillars of ordinary logic, mixes things together, and at times reaches the edge of delirium.

Like other Sufis, Ibn Sabʿin writes while drunk.

Sometimes the intoxication takes hold of him in the middle of a passage. His rational powers fade, leaving the field open to intuition and an imagination set loose without restraint:

> Do not glorify while affirming unity.
> Do not learn while you already know.
> Do not love while you still possess a heart.
> Do not speak while fully present.
> Do not argue while you are the one speaking.
> Do not worship while still desiring.
> Do not bargain while still holding on.

Thinking through opposites is the only road that allows absolute existence and relative existence to be brought together.

It lets the absolute run through the relative, so that existing things become its own reality—the forms through which it appears.

This is the kind of speech whose individual words can be understood while their combinations cannot, as Ibn Daqiq al-Id once said.

But when those combinations touch a mind already trained in opposites, the mind can become one with them.

It may not “understand” them in the usual rational sense, because they are not rational in that sense.

But it can unite with them and so grasp them.

They become part of the mind’s own conceptual structure.

In the modern age, dialectics was refined through Hegel and Marx, familiar names in modern philosophy.

Its earliest pioneers, however, were Taoists and Sufis.

They did not master it in a systematic way.

They practised it.

They used it as a method for reaching the reality of things.

Ibn Sabʿin discusses the usual instruments of the Sufi method: unveiling, inner prompting, sudden intuitions, and inspiration.

But he places above them the instruments used by what he calls the **realised knower**, whom he ranks above the ordinary Sufi.

He speaks of existential propositions, inner reports arising in his own balanced consciousness, the dazzling spirit issuing from his own essence, encompassing quiddity, the absolute word, pure identity, perceptions beyond time, and a rank that establishes realities outside place...

Here the tools of the method become mixed up with the conclusions produced by the method.

Ibn Sabʿin’s chains of association have no limit except the end of the page.

Still, he did not deny the role of reason.

He confined reason to its proper field and made it an instrument for regulating and weighing the sciences.

This is his sober state, when he sees scientific truths.

The Sufi, in sobriety, witnesses knowledge.

Reason belongs to the scholar. Without it, no science can be properly established or weighed with precision.

Reason and science therefore enter the Sufi method, but they do not rule the Sufi completely, nor does the Sufi rule them completely.

(Letters, p. 195)

This is where scholar and Sufi part company within the field of reason.

Ibn Sabʿin gives reason and science their due in the scholar and does not deny them.

But for himself he opens another horizon beyond the fences of reason and science.

There he takes refuge in dialectics, or unites himself with the objects of intuition, in order to reach the highest realities of existence beyond the partial natural world studied by the scientist.

This is not only the task of the Sufi.

It is also the task of the philosopher.

The philosopher generalises from the particular findings of the sciences and builds from them a theory of existence.

Ibn Sabʿin, and Ibn Arabi before him, treated philosophical reason unfairly because they looked at it only while it was imprisoned within formal Aristotelian logic.

In reality, the philosopher’s mind can open onto a much wider horizon in proportion to the breadth of its knowledge and foundations.

Strict adherence to Aristotelian logic certainly narrowed the horizons of philosophers who built their methods entirely upon it.

But other philosophers moved beyond the Aristotelian school and produced philosophies equal to, or more advanced than, Sufi philosophy.

We can point, for example, to the independent philosophy of al-Razi and to that of Sadr al-Din al-Shirazi, which is far more advanced than any Sufi philosophy.

Even the philosophers who remained within Aristotelian logic produced systems of enormous value and contributed greatly to the development of philosophical thought.

Ibn Sabʿin was not fair to al-Farabi, Ibn Sina, or Ibn Rushd.

In truth he surpasses them only in his dialectical method.

As philosophers, they are much larger figures than he is.

Ibn Sabʿin’s objection to the hadith “There is no prophet after me” reveals a soul longing for prophecy.

But this is the prophecy of the intellectual, not the prophecy of the prophet.

Ibn Sabʿin and others like him among the Sufis found within themselves such depth and power of thought, reaching so far into things, that revealed books could begin to seem small beside it.

Their broad culture cannot easily be compared with the modest culture of the prophets.

Prophets are not philosophers, scientists, or even intellectuals in the technical sense.

They belong to other kinds of activity.

Jesus was a social struggler.

Moses and Muhammad were founders of states and civilisations.

Buddha carried an ethical message.

These are not qualities that automatically belong to Sufis.

Sufis are intellectuals in a more restricted sense, and the intellectual cannot simply perform the work of a prophet.

The prophethood they longed for was intellectual transcendence of the doctrines brought by earlier prophets.

The scale of their minds made it difficult for them simply to accept inherited doctrine as it came.

In this they shared something with the philosophers.

But philosophers did not usually dream of becoming prophets.

Prophecy is an emotional and unseen aspiration. It rises from a spiritualised soul that identifies with intuition and inspiration rather than pure reason.

It is also tied very strongly to a message carried by the prophet to a country or a people in order to move them from one condition to another in their social, political, and ideological life.

In that sense, prophecy lies closer to the field of Sufi activity because it contains a social and reforming element.

This is far removed from the philosopher whose principal concern is thought itself.

On this basis we can regard Sufis such as al-Hallaj, al-Bistami, al-Jili, Ibn Arabi, and Ibn Sabʿin as prophets.

The same can be said of the great Taoist philosophers.

And the same can even be said of some modern philosophers, such as Karl Marx and Lenin.

They are prophets in the sense that they carry messages—social messages more than cultural ones.

Ibn Sabʿin certainly seems to have had a powerful prophetic impulse.

He expressed it in both his words and his behaviour.

He gathered many disciples and followers around him. As Lisan al-Din Ibn al-Khatib tells us, they carried his doctrine east and west.

Some of his letters even seem to imitate the Quran.

He wrote pieces of rhymed prose broken into short units resembling Meccan verses, though the effort often feels forced.

Ibn al-Khatib said of the writings spread by his disciples that almost none were free from falsehoods, and that they were closer to the obsessions of the deranged and the delirium of the fevered than to the methods of serious scholars. (*al-Ihata fi Akhbar Gharnata*, biographical notice, p. 294)

Perhaps Ibn al-Khatib—the poet, artist, and man of delicate feeling—had read things like this:

> “...and he renounces that because the limit confined him,
> and he renounces that because it is the sign of satisfaction,
> and he renounces that for the sake of that,
> and in that it is that,
> and likewise after that,
> at the time of the existence of that!”

Or:

> “Yes, only.
> Yes, the creed is greater than its mother.
> Yes, from Ali.
> Yes, would that I knew.
> Yes...”

Not everything Ibn Sabʿin wrote is like this.

Most of these outbursts come only after a sequence of connected, coherent ideas with clear meaning.

Then intoxication overtakes him and he collapses into one of these attacks.

Seen this way, they express an intellectual or psychological crisis discharged through language.

They are not pure nonsense.

Ibn Sabʿin’s Sufism also contains a humanising social attitude, though he never went far enough to join the line of the great masters of social Sufism.

In his personal life he followed a path of simplicity and renunciation.

He interpreted “leaving the self” as giving up wealth, and interpreted the annihilation of the self in the same way.

But he did not devote himself deeply to the suffering of the poor, nor did he insist on cutting ties with the rich and powerful.

In this he was closer to Ibn Arabi than to al-Hallaj or al-Jili.

He upheld tolerance, though he did not speak in the same explicit way about the unity of religions.

His tolerance was broader and more general: it extended to people who were mistaken without singling out the followers of particular religions.

At the end of the *Letter on Comprehension* he writes:

> “Peace upon the one who rejects and the one who accepts, the one who prevails and the one who is mistaken...”

Abd al-Rahman Badawi read the word “accepts” here as “Muslim,” deriving it from Islam.

The better reading is simply “one who assents,” because it stands opposite “one who rejects,” not opposite “unbeliever.”

Ibn Sabʿin means those who reject his views and those who accept them.

We can call this the **democracy of a free thinker**.

He also called for adherence to the ethical teachings of religious law and made them a road toward taking on the qualities expressed in the divine names, following the normal Sufi method.

A person who takes on the divine names until they become part of his inner substance makes those names his own essence and spirit.

The names are attributes of God.

God’s attributes are not something added externally to His essence.

Therefore the person who takes on these names is no longer external to the divine essence.

(*The Book of Wisdom and Admonitions*, commentary, p. 81 of the Letters)

In other words, the person who fully takes on religious ethics acquires divine qualities and clothes himself in the divine essence.

Sufis, whether believers or atheists, separate **belief** from **religious law**.

As we saw earlier, they treated law as a means of regulating the behaviour of rulers and ordinary people.

The demand that rulers apply religious law remained part of Islamic reform movements because it placed limits on rulers’ freedom of action.

But the desire to move beyond that law emerged elsewhere, for two reasons:

to escape burdensome rituals and acts of worship, and to establish an economic order that went beyond the system of compulsory almsgiving.

Ibn Sabʿin also distinguished the sin of the ordinary person from the sin of the ruler.

The latter, he said, is more dangerous and more harmful.

For this reason he wrote:

> “A king who persists knowingly in sin commits more major sins in a single day than another person commits a thousand times over.”

(*al-Ridwaniyya*, p. 319)

By *one who persists*, he means someone who sins deliberately and knowingly.

Apart from this, mercy extends to all who err, just as it extends among ordinary people to believer and unbeliever alike.

He opposed war.

And when he spoke of it he sounded again like the Taoists, describing the evils produced by hostility in both victory and defeat.

Laozi said that victory itself is a defeat, and that celebrating victory is like celebrating a funeral.

Ibn Sabʿin says:

> “The victor is carried away from the station of contentment and cut off from unity, because he remains caught in awareness of the other and in conflict with opposites through the affliction of victory.”

(Letters, p. 99)

Is it not astonishing that Ibn Sabʿin and Laozi could speak almost the same language without either knowing the other, hearing of the other, or reading the other?

Perhaps it is the memory of a single East.

Ibn Sabʿin also opposed religious wars.

He called for spreading Islam by persuasion.

He writes:

> “Drawing the enemy through kindness, wisdom, and good policy is more praiseworthy in religious law, more fitting, and more beloved to God. In this way you remove his hostility and draw him toward Islam. You become merciful and generous and follow the Prophet’s example, for he drew people by kindness, as he did with those whose hearts were to be reconciled. He also drew them through forbearance, forgiving those who harmed him and asking forgiveness for them.”

(Letters, p. 99)

Along the same line of thought, Ibn Sabʿin speaks of the unity of humanity.

Humanity is one species.

People differ in value only through their actions and their virtues.

Knowledge is a condition for both.

(p. 95)

Once human beings are understood in this essential way, superiority based on religious belief disappears.

Distinction rests instead on knowledge and action.

No creed possesses an automatic advantage over another, because knowledge and practical virtue are open to every human being whatever that person believes.

In this profound human concern, Ibn Sabʿin continues both sides of Sufism: the social and the philosophical.

The masters of both schools agreed that people should not be ranked by religious belief and that tolerance should prevail among followers of different religions, since all of them seek the Real, each by a path of their own.
