Like many people of thought and culture, Shaykh Abd al-Haqq imagined that the basic cause of hostility among human beings was error in knowledge.

Once knowledge settled a question and exposed the mistake, people would agree.

May God forgive him.

He made things so simple and so delicate that they almost broke.

Human conflict has many sources.

There are religious differences.

National and tribal conflict.

Psychological complexes and pressures.

And above all there is conflict over the sources of livelihood and survival—what the German Shaykh calls **class struggle**.

This struggle divided Muslims and gave rise to civil wars in Islamic history.

Had Ibn Sabʿin known Islamic history as well as Umar ibn Abd al-Aziz did, for example, he might have thought much longer before issuing such a quick judgment.

#### Words of Ibn Sabʿin

In some notes he wrote on *The Beginning of the Gnostic*, Ibn Sabʿin says:

> “Whoever reaches pure truth will need few means, and the journey will become short.”

But how can any human being reach pure truth?

Did he really believe he had reached it when he said this?

Was the story of his suicide true because he had reached that final point and felt that nothing remained to be done with life?

I cannot settle any of this.

But I tasted these words of his and drank them.

Then I built upon them the poem “The Final Clause,” which the reader will find near the end of this book.

I do not know whether I wrote it as an explanation of his words or in obedience to his command.

Elsewhere he says:

> “I seek refuge in God from an ambition that comes to a stop, and from a mind that becomes satisfied.”

This is one of the marks of people of thought.

The mind is never satisfied because it is always asking questions.

Questions about existence never end because existence itself is endless.

So questioning too is endless.

Only the believer’s mind can finally become satisfied, because the believer’s world is finite and the boundaries of existence are already known.

The universe challenges the universal intellectual and keeps him searching and asking forever.

But the universal intellectual cannot remain lost forever in the seas of the unseen.

At some point he must stop at a shore where his feet touch the earth.

That is his task among people.

For this reason Ibn Sabʿin says:

> “True solitude does not mean withdrawing from one’s family. It means separating the self from what is ugly and destroys it. The true, intelligent knower does not fall under the ordinary division of the species: he is a species of his own, yet he remains among people, one person among people...”

To this I add:

The knower withdraws from the powers that dominate him.

He does not withdraw from people.

And in the *Letter on Comprehension* Ibn Sabʿin says:

> “Do not fear your madness at this moment. It is a more complete form of knowledge.”

Madness is part of the completion of reason.

When a person’s reason becomes mature and full, saturated with knowledge, and when he also has a heart accompanying him in sobriety and intoxication, he must break some of reason’s sharpness and give the heart room to move and wander so that it too can perform its task.

Ibn Sabʿin expresses the same wisdom as the old Arabs: a person made entirely of reason cannot move.

The perfectly calculating person measures everything too carefully. He becomes excessively cautious and afraid.

He needs a space of madness in which to move himself and move the things around him.

Madness is Sufi.

The outside powers reject it because they are always sober.

Reason is useful and harmful at the same time.

The human mind opens roads toward many forms of evil that are unknown to creatures without reason.

The evils produced by speaking, rational human beings are greater than those produced by animals without speech.

For the human being to become truly human, the feeling for justice must therefore deepen within him.

That means he must possess a heart.

And the person of heart lives in a compound state of sobriety and intoxication.

Sobriety draws the road of action and lights it so that he can walk without stumbling.

Intoxication frees him from the powers outside him. Fear falls away. Everything becomes passable.

Then he advances.

He storms forward.

He may even strike.

All of this happens because he touches the earth and carries the burden of other people.

That burden lives in the heart, not in reason.

Ibn al-Khatib preserves the following poem by Ibn Sabʿin in his *Ihata*:

> How long will you hide behind the two valleys and the landmark,
> when the matter is clearer than fire upon a hill?
>
> How long will you speak of Salʿ and Kazima,
> of Zarud and the neighbours of Dhu Salam?
>
> You keep asking about Najd while standing in Najd,
> and about Tihama—what can this be but a lover lost in longing?
>
> Is there anyone in the camp but Layla,
> and still you ask the camp where Layla is?

The secret beside you is very near.

Only a thin veil keeps it from you.

Yet he remains wandering and bewildered, asking questions as he circles the landmarks of the cradle: the two valleys, the marker, Salʿ, Kazima.

There, where hearts make their home, they settle and become safe.

Near the edges of the cradle, you cannot become lost.

Your nurses and mothers are with you, feeding you light scented with the wild flowers and garden hills.

Still, he does not stop asking.

He knows that Layla is in the camp.

There is no one in the camp but Layla.

And still he asks the camp where Layla is.

Why ask for Layla when there is no one there but Layla?

Questions can come from reason.

They can also come from the heart.

A question from the heart comes mixed with water and blood, and with something of fire to protect it from the coldness of reason.

Perhaps this is the secret of asking about Layla.

I think it is.

And so the question about Layla goes on speaking, bewildered and unending, because it comes from the heart.

The heart begins asking when reason falls silent.

In fact, it begins exactly where reason ends.

### Abd al-Karim al-Jili

Abd al-Karim al-Jili was a descendant of Abd al-Qadir al-Jilani.

He appeared late in the Islamic age, and I believe he was the **Seal of the Saints**, because no one after him came close to matching him.

After him, the sun of Islam set.

Among Muslims there was no one left asking about Layla.

His principal book is *The Perfect Human Being*.

It was printed by Mustafa al-Babi al-Halabi, the great Egyptian publisher of classical works, early in this century.

The edition dates to 1334 AH.

I read it inside the Chinese Islamic Institute founded by the Chinese Communists in 1955.

Because the edition is so old, copies are now rare, except in such places—institutions that guard their treasures and do not hand them out to just anyone.

Abd al-Karim al-Jili belongs to philosophical, knowledge-centred Sufism.

He did not share the same social concerns as his ancestor.

He also belongs to the more openly heterodox side of Sufism.

In matters of doctrine he seems even further from orthodox belief than Ibn Sabʿin, almost as if he had studied directly under Ibn Arabi.

His appearance at that late and dangerous turning point in Islamic history, with such rich and daring Sufi breakthroughs, can be compared to the appearance of Ibn Khaldun in his own field—though al-Jili entered a field already well travelled, unlike Ibn Khaldun.

Abd al-Karim al-Jili devoted himself to explaining unity of being in the manner of Ibn Arabi.

He speaks of the Real running through existing things.

But he denies that this means God physically enters them or touches them, because the Real is not something separate from existing things in the first place.

Its existence is in its beings.

Its beings are its manifestations.

The Real is one and cannot be divided.

Its manifestation is identical with its hiddenness.

Its firstness is identical with its lastness.

Its before is identical with its after.

The Real sees creation only through itself, because creation is not other than it.

Beyond the ordinary idea of divinity, Real and creation interpenetrate.

Existence and non-existence stand as opposites, and the sphere of divinity surrounds them both.

Divinity gathers opposites together:

the eternal and the created,
the Real and creation,
existence and non-existence.

Within it, what is necessary can appear impossible after first appearing necessary.

What is impossible can appear necessary after first appearing impossible.

The Real appears in the form of creation, and creation appears in the form of the Real.

Divinity therefore becomes something like the spirit of the universe: surrounding existing things, taking form in them and through them.

In the Real it appears absolutely.

In creation it appears partially, because creation appears within divinity according to all the changing possibilities proper to created beings: their differences, transformations, disappearance, and existence.

Creation therefore contains a share of divinity derived from the Absolute.

This picture of the relation between the Real and creation leaves behind the ordinary religious model of creator and created.

There is no separate act of manufacture.

There are instead manifestations of one reality through many existing things.

Al-Jili seems to want to begin from the philosophical idea of the simple One, from which the many arise.

The One itself remains indivisible even though the many come from it.

But al-Jili does not need the Neoplatonic chain of emanations in order to protect the One from division and multiplicity.

Unity of being joins the Real to creation through **manifestation**, not through emanation.

Nor does it imagine a solid and motionless One like that of Parmenides.

It is a One that becomes many through its existing things, which are itself insofar as they are its manifestations.

It does not stand apart from them like the Neoplatonic First Intellect.

It runs through them.

It is the force of their existence.

At this point, the relation between Real and creation in the doctrine of unity of being comes surprisingly close to a physical understanding of primary matter.

Primary matter becomes many through compounds.

The matter itself remains constant and absolute in its atomic and nuclear composition, while its compounds change, come into being, and pass away.

Unity of being, however, preserves a spiritual element within the world by speaking of divinity.

Here divinity has left behind the mechanical image of ordinary religion:

a maker and things made,
a creator and creatures,
joined through the industrial command “Be!” and it is.

Instead, divinity becomes the inner force of existence itself.

In materialist language, it is almost like the active power of matter.

Yet ultimately it returns to the idea of a spirit of the universe.

As we have said before, unity of being can be seen as a material explanation of creation and coming-to-be that recoils from pure materialism and adds a spiritual element drawn from the mystery and grandeur of the universe, swallowed up in infinity.

Through this spiritual element added to existence, the world takes on beauty.

Created things become images of divine beauty.

Everything is therefore beautiful.

There is no absolute ugliness in the world.

There is absolute beauty, while ugliness exists only by comparison and circumstance.

A good word, for example, may become ugly in a particular situation even though it is good in itself.

A foul smell is foul only in relation to a creature whose nature rejects it—such as a human being.

Other creatures, such as beetles, may love the same smell.

Al-Jili also contains traces of a doctrine of divine indwelling.

He says that when God manifests Himself to one of His servants through one of the divine names, that servant is overwhelmed by the lights of that name.

If you then call on the Real by that name, the servant answers you, because the name has fallen upon him.

At the highest degree of manifestation, God erases the servant’s name and establishes the divine name in its place.

Then if you say:

“O God!”

that servant answers:

“At your service.”

This is an extremely strong form of divine indwelling, stronger even than al-Hallaj’s and not something normally found in Ibn Arabi.

From here al-Jili moves to affirm the saint’s miracles through annihilation in the Real.

These occur in states of intoxication and absence.

Al-Jili too has his delirious passages, like Ibn Arabi.

He describes meetings with angels and prophets, including one gathering in the Yemeni city of Zabid in Rabi al-Awwal of 800 AH.

He also says that he heard all existing things praising God literally, not metaphorically.

Sufi intoxication begins by breaking the familiar order.

The intoxicated Sufi escapes the ordinary laws of things and acts through a different faculty—a spiritual instrument of thought.

Nature has not yet managed to manufacture such an instrument.

The Sufi simply gets ahead of nature and deals with things outside the five senses.

This is why they say that in intoxication the Sufi sees **the state**, while in sobriety he sees **knowledge**.

Sobriety belongs to the reasoning mind, to pure intellect.

Intoxication belongs to the faculty of action—the heart, or what we might cautiously call practical reason.

I use the phrase “practical reason” with hesitation only because action enters into it.

Strictly speaking, it is the heart that takes control of the Sufi in intoxication.

Still, the delirium of Ibn Arabi and Abd al-Karim al-Jili had unfortunate consequences.

Its worst results appeared later in the age of the dervishes.

That age follows al-Jili directly, under Ottoman rule, when Sufism was transformed into dervish practice.

The dervishes took advantage of the delirious passages of Ibn Arabi, al-Jili, and other great Sufis and used them to develop forms of fraud and superstition.

The Sufi symbol collapsed into priestcraft.

Ibn Arabi and al-Jili needed stricter control over their intoxicated states so that the symbol would remain within the suggestive realm of spiritual experience and not become a tool in the hands of charlatans.

Abd al-Karim al-Jili speaks of Iblis in the manner of the heterodox Sufi.

He explains the name of Iblis through the idea of confusion: the matter became confused for him, because he believed that if he bowed to Adam he would be worshipping someone other than God.

Iblis, al-Jili says, knew the etiquette of the divine presence better than any other creature and understood perfectly how to answer a question.

God did not ask him **why** he refused.

He asked him what prevented him.

Iblis therefore spoke of the hidden truth of the matter:

“I was created from fire.”

That is, he was superior.

This is the outward meaning of his answer.

But beneath it lies another truth.

Iblis refuses to bow before anyone except God.

He is carrying out the Sufi command never to become a servant of another human being, and in doing so he protects the essence of the human being as a free creature.

The same command appears in the words of their great ancestor, who told his son Hasan:

> “Do not become the slave of another when God created you free.”

Heavenly Islamic theology falls into contradiction here, because it orders Iblis to bow to Adam—that is, to commit what looks like an act of associating another with God.

For this reason the Sufis defended Iblis.

They argued that his refusal follows knowledge, not the command.

And the command itself must finally follow knowledge.

What God knows eternally is that Iblis will bow to no one but God.

Iblis therefore protects human dignity from being destroyed.

By refusing to bow to Adam, he gives Adam and his descendants a lesson:

Do not bow to anyone but God.

In their interpretation of Iblis, the Sufis place divine commands themselves within the realm of argument and counterargument.

They even subject divine thinking—the mind of God—to a dispute in which Iblis ultimately wins.

For, according to al-Jili, Iblis will eventually return to the divine presence after the Day of Judgment.

No curse remains.

Only nearness.

The Real therefore ends up on the side of Iblis.

God becomes the one who was mistaken.

And once that happens, what remains of the divine commands embodied in religious law?

What remains of belief itself when revelation becomes open to argument?

Abd al-Karim al-Jili also tried to defend the Christian Trinity by drawing on Ibn Arabi’s distinction between the fixed divine essence as absolute existence and its changing forms in existing things.

The three persons of the Trinity, he says, are not the absolute essence itself.

They are forms in which the essence appears through the Son and the Holy Spirit.

He also draws on Ibn Arabi’s combination of divine incomparability and resemblance in order to defend the divinity of Christ through the appearance of the One in the many.

And he appeals to unity of being to say that Christ is the very reality and essence of God, while God is the very reality and essence of Christ.

Al-Jili’s obvious departure here from Islamic belief does not seem intended as a statement of one universal dogmatic truth.

Its real purpose is to serve the Sufi position on religions and religious beliefs.

As we have already seen, the Sufis do not recognise one religious truth possessed by one community alone.

They see the Real distributed among the followers of all religions.

No one therefore has the right to dismiss or condemn another.

Abd al-Karim al-Jili uses deliberately strained interpretation to defend freedom of religion, equality among religions in their relation to truth, and even the freedom of **no religion at all**—atheism—and its equality with religious belief.

He writes, in effect:

The unbelievers worshipped Him in His essence because the Real is the reality of all existence, and unbelievers are part of existence. He is therefore their own reality. They imagined themselves to have a Lord, but in truth they have no Lord apart from the Absolute Lord Himself. They worshipped Him according to what their own natures required, for He is the very reality of those natures.

Those among them who worshipped idols understood His complete existence to be present—without physical indwelling or mixture—in every particle of existence. He was therefore the reality of the idols they worshipped. So in worshipping them, they worshipped nothing but God.

They followed the Real within themselves, because their hearts testified that goodness lay in what they believed.

Their beliefs settled upon that inner truth.

And He is according to His servant’s good opinion of Him.

As for those who say that everything is ruled by Time, they say that Time itself is unknown in its divine aspect and that there is nothing but wombs bringing forth and earth swallowing up.

They therefore worshipped Him through identity.

For the Prophet said:

> “Do not curse Time, for God is Time.”

Strained interpretation and verbal manoeuvring are essential parts of this Sufi argument for freedom of religion and freedom from religion.

Its aim is to defend every person’s right to believe in any religion, or in none, and to place unbeliever, Muslim, and atheist on equal ground in the search for truth.

Through this destructive logic, al-Jili approaches something genuine.

He shifts questions of belief toward the testimony of the heart that goodness lies in the thing a person sincerely believes.

He rests the whole matter on the good intentions of human beings in their beliefs.

Even in our own time, enlightened people are still struggling for freedom of belief.

The Sufis were among the founders of that struggle.

For them it also connects with the denial of endless punishment and with their reinterpretation of punishment in the next world.

For al-Jili, hellfire is not an original or permanent part of existence.

It must therefore disappear.

It is not eternal.

Mercy is the original principle because mercy is an essential divine attribute.

Anger is not.

God is called **the Compassionate, the Merciful**.

He is not called “the Furious” or “the Wrathful.”

By the same reasoning, al-Jili gives priority to the attributes of beauty and treats them as original, while the attributes of majesty come later.

The attributes of beauty include names such as the Knowing, the Merciful, the Holy, and the Gentle.

The attributes of majesty include the Great, the Compeller, the Proud, and others like them.

The majestic names apply to some beings and not others.

The names of beauty, by contrast, spread throughout existence.

The influence of Jesus on Islamic Sufism is clear here.

We have already seen how Abd al-Karim al-Jili defends the Trinity and interprets the divinity of Christ through his dialectic of unity of being.

In any case, this concern belongs especially to the masters of philosophical Sufism.

They find in the peaceful heart of Christ something that draws them toward freedom of belief and equality among religions in relation to truth.

The masters of social Sufism find something else in the heart of Christ:

his communal spirit and his closeness to ordinary people.

#### An Astronomical Note

Abd al-Karim al-Jili also discusses astronomy while treating questions of existence.

He says that the sky we see is not the lowest heaven itself.

What we see is vapour rising naturally from the dryness of the earth and the moisture of water, lifted by the sun’s heat into the air until it fills the empty space between the earth and the lowest heaven.

The light we see depends on the way illumination falls through these vapours.

A hadith says that the distance between the earth and the lowest heaven is a journey of five hundred years.

Everyone agrees, al-Jili says, that human sight cannot cover a journey of five hundred years.

If the rays of the stars did not reach the earth, we would not see them.

And how many shining stars exist in the heavens whose light never reaches the earth?

We do not see them because they are too distant and too subtle.

These correct scientific ideas go back to al-Kindi, who devoted several treatises to natural and astronomical phenomena and thereby earned his leading place in ancient astronomy and natural science.

Al-Jili’s purpose in bringing them up seems to have been to deny that heaven can be assigned a simple spatial direction and to undermine the ordinary religious picture of the sky.

#### Flashes from al-Jili

> I am the son whose father is his son,
> and the wine whose vineyard is its jar.
>
> I am the branch that produced its own root,
> and the arrow whose blade became its bow.
>
> I met the mothers who gave birth to me
> and asked to marry them,
> and they gave me themselves in marriage.
>
> When I travelled happily through the outward forms of the roots,
> I tied together the image of the fruit.
>
> I veiled myself within myself,
> turning through my own sensation.
>
> I carried the trusts of primal matter
> and perfected the presence described as first.
>
> Then I found myself
> father of all,
> and mother of the grown and the infant.

---

When the spirit takes on the form of the body, it also takes on the body’s qualities: heaviness, confinement, weakness, and similar limitations.

The spirit then loses the freedom, subtlety, and movement that originally belonged to it—not by becoming separated from itself, but through its connection with the body.

Its original qualities remain, but only as possibilities rather than active powers.

If the person uses angelic qualities, the spirit grows stronger and gradually frees itself from the rule of heaviness.

This can continue until the body itself becomes like spirit.

Then the person walks on water and flies through the air.

We have already mentioned the road toward this condition:

continuous right reflection,
little food,
little sleep,
little speech,
and giving up the demands of ordinary bodily life.

The physical frame then acquires spiritual subtlety.

These teachings can be summed up in one idea:

through prolonged Sufi discipline and the completion of spiritual formation, the human being acquires extraordinary strength.

Obstacles fall away.

The difficult becomes easy.

The high becomes level.

Fear disappears from the heart as the person sinks into the spirit of the universe and into the vast divinity surrounding everything.

State and army lose their power over him.

He can then repeat with Shao Yong:

> He is the one whom a great salary cannot tempt,
> and a great army cannot frighten.

His final annihilation in both the Real and creation becomes an annihilation of the three powers:

**the state, religion, and money.**

#### Farewell to the Orbit of Mīm

We must distinguish between **spiritualist thought** and **spiritual conduct**.

Spiritualist thought is otherworldly and hostile to change.[^ms-v1-011] It lies close to religion.

It gives spirit priority over matter, joins religion in its war against physics, denies causality, and accepts fate and predestination.

Spiritual conduct is something else.

It is the conduct of the Sufis as we have described it in this book:

the conduct of a socially rooted human being formed by noble values and higher ideals, the kind of values an intellectual needs if he is to defend ordinary people.

The Sufis may use spiritual language when they speak about creation and existence.

But behind that language lie physical truths contained in the doctrine of unity of being.

There is also a rejection of fate and predestination, and an affirmation of causality through the relationship between cause and effect and through the reciprocal relations among things.

The Sufis—like Goethe after them—avoid the language of hard-line materialists because they want to **spiritualise human relationships**.

At the same time, and for the same reason, they dream of reshaping the order of nature itself so that nature becomes a friend to the human being.

That is a utopian ambition.

Physics does not agree with it.

But if it could ever be realised, it would be a legitimate and just one.

## The Orbit of Bāʾ (ب)

### The Dot beneath the Letter Bāʾ

Some shaykhs claim the dot beneath the letter *bāʾ* for themselves. The Shiites give it to Ali ibn Abi Talib.

The Najafi poet Muhammad Ali al-Yaqubi once attacked a Shiite jurist who had ruled that Ali’s name should be removed from the call to prayer. Shiites invoke Ali after Muhammad, saying: “I bear witness that Ali is the friend of God.”

Al-Yaqubi wrote:

> He erased the dot beneath the *bāʾ* from the call to prayer,
> so that we might accept him as the Fourth Point.

The dot beneath the *bāʾ* is not just any dot. It is the dot beneath the *bāʾ* of the *basmala*: **In the name of God, the Compassionate, the Merciful.** It occupies a meaningful and essential place. Remove it, and the whole structure is disturbed.

This is what al-Yaqubi means when he says that the jurist erased the dot beneath the *bāʾ* from the call to prayer. Just as the *basmala* is thrown out of order when its *bāʾ* is lost, the call to prayer is thrown out of order when Ali is removed from it.

This amounts to placing Ali ahead of the Prophet. Some Shiite groups, such as the Nusayris and the Ghurabiyya, stated this openly. The more moderate Shiites—the Twelvers—did not say so openly, though they believed it.

For the Sufis, Ali is the great ancestor from whom the Sufi lineage descends.

But that adds another dot to the *bāʾ*. A great many movements, sects, and organisations have traced themselves back to Ali. If we set the Kharijites aside, there is scarcely a political or social movement that did not invent a genealogy connecting itself to him.

Even *futuwwa*, the tradition of chivalric brotherhood, acquired such a lineage. When the Abbasid caliph al-Nasir li-Din Allah tried to organise the *futuwwa* bands and turn them into a striking force under his own control, he gave them a genealogy. This was not difficult. Gabriel had supposedly proclaimed at Badr:

> There is no sword but Dhu al-Fiqar,
> and no young hero but Ali.

So al-Nasir could present himself as a follower rather than an innovator.

It is also well known that the history of opposition begins with Ali. He was said to be “the first to teach fighting against the people of the qibla”—in other words, the first to establish the principles of civil war among Muslims.

This is precisely what Yusuf ibn Umar, the Umayyad governor of Iraq, meant when he gave a speech after crushing the uprising of Zayd ibn Ali:

> “The first man to open the door of civil strife and bloodshed upon this community was Ali and his black companion.”[^ms-v1-012]

The Umayyad governor was not simply making things up.

But Ali was not a Sufi. What he did establish was the principle of ascetic conduct among rulers.

A figure with a closer claim to being an ancestor of Sufism may have been Amir al-Anbari. We have written about him elsewhere in these pages. Al-Anbari is a common ancestor of both the Sufis and al-Maʿarri.

This spiritual fatherhood should not, however, be extended to Abu Dharr or Salman, despite what some modern writers—and some old Sufi pretenders—have claimed. Both were professional political actors and social militants working through the politics of everyday life. They do not belong to the class of intellectuals to which the Sufis generally belong.

### Fatherhood

In the actual history of Sufism, the first Sufi was not Ali, the dot beneath the *bāʾ*. It was Ibrahim ibn Adham of Balkh, the Afghan.

His position as the father of Sufism may even preserve traces of contact with Chinese Taoism, given the relative closeness of Afghanistan to China.

I am not suggesting that Ibrahim himself read Taoist works. He almost certainly knew no Chinese. But Chinese civilisation exerted a powerful influence in those regions adjoining China, which were always open to Central Asia.

It is difficult to imagine that Sufism simply dropped into Ibrahim’s heart from nowhere. There must have been some education behind it and some intellectual sources from which he drew.

Ibrahim’s personality is very close to the descriptions of the sage found in Taoist books.

It has nothing to do with Indian mysticism, despite what some Orientalists have believed. Indian mysticism is thoroughly religious and heavily mixed with superstition and ritual. The path taken by Ibrahim ibn Adham and his successors is much closer to the Taoists: relatively free of ritual, in tension with fixed doctrine, and directly concerned with the problems of ordinary people.

These Orientalists, followed by their Arab pupils, repeatedly insisted on the legend that gave Ibrahim a Buddhist ancestry simply because he was said to have been born to royalty, like the Buddha, and to have abandoned kingship for the spiritual life.

That proves no genealogy at all.

Orientalists often fail to distinguish one shade from another. They lack a precise understanding of texts and a sound grasp of the movement of history and ideas.

If Ibrahim ibn Adham really is the father of Islamic Sufism, then the question of this kinship between Sufism and Taoism has to be raised.

What is certain, at any rate, is the striking closeness between Sufi and Taoist language, attitudes, and general principles. This may be the result of some connection between their origins, or it may be the result of similar conditions of life and social development across Asia.

The strongest resemblance, in fact, may be between Chinese and Islamic civilisation.

This similarity to Taoism extends to the Gospels. Their attitude toward money and the rich reflects a tendency so closely resembling Taoism that at times it looks as if Jesus had read the Taoist classics before announcing his mission.

Ibrahim was the one who established manual labour as a Sufi principle. He worked in farming and in watchkeeping.

What is certain is that he inherited a considerable fortune from his father—not a king, but a rich man—then distributed it and continued to earn his own living with his hands.

Ibrahim also took part in defending the Syrian frontier against the Byzantines. But he would not accept army rations. During pauses in the fighting, he went off to work so that he could provide his own food.

He also avoided mixing with the soldiers.

His hostility toward the military appeared early.

In Abu Nuaym’s *Adornment of the Saints*, one of Ibrahim’s companions, a man named Harun, tells this story:

> We were with Ibrahim on a military expedition, working at the harvest.
>
> He said to me, “Harun, let us move away from this place.”
>
> I asked, “Why?”
>
> He said, “I have heard that a military force is being sent to Africa.”
>
> I said, “What has that got to do with us?”
>
> He replied, “The road they will take passes close by here. We cannot be sure that one of them will not come over and ask us how to reach such-and-such a place. Should we show him the way? Better for us to move so far away that we neither see them nor they see us.”

Another story in the *Adornment* says that Ibrahim once worked drawing water somewhere in Palestine, perhaps in Gaza itself. Whenever an army passed him on its way toward Egypt, he would cut loose his bucket and throw it into the water rather than give the soldiers a drink.

This hostility toward soldiers is deeply rooted in the Taoist tradition.

The Sufi attitude toward paradise can also be traced back to Ibrahim.

The *Adornment of the Saints* records this prayer of his:

> “God, You know that paradise is worth less to me than the wing of a mosquito. If You give me intimacy through remembering You, grant me Your love, and make obedience to You easy for me, then give paradise to whomever You please.”

Love makes paradise unnecessary.

Nothing like this is recorded before Ibrahim. Nor do we find it among the earlier ascetics whom the biographical collections later classified as Sufis.

Those men were ascetics, not Sufis.

Asceticism is one element of Sufism, but it is not Sufism itself. Their asceticism remained religious, closer to Indian asceticism and far removed from the Taoist and Sufi kind.

The *Adornment of the Saints* also tells us that one of Ibrahim’s companions was asked:

“Did Ibrahim pray a great deal?”

He answered:

“No. But he was a man of reflection. He would sit through the night thinking.”

Something similar was said of Abu Dharr: that most of his worship consisted in reflection.

Ibn Asakir also quotes Ibn al-Mubarak as saying that Ibrahim was a man of hidden inward states, and that he never saw him make a public display of reciting praises to God. (2/175)

Ibrahim ibn Adham belongs above all to **social Sufism**. His thought and his way of life were built around it.

Yet, as we have seen, he also spoke about love, and perhaps entered states of ecstasy.

One story tells that he heard a man reciting:

> Everything you do is forgiven,
> except turning away from me.

Ibrahim supposedly fell unconscious.

He was stronger than that, surely. Perhaps the person who invented the story had been influenced by Ibrahim’s language of love and built the tale around it.

Whatever the case, Ibrahim ibn Adham was the true founder of Sufism.

There is no one before him who really deserves the name.

There were ascetics, as we have said, and asceticism is one pillar of Sufism. But asceticism by itself is not Sufism.

Al-Qushayri says that the name “Sufism” appeared before the year 200 AH—that is, around the time of Ibn Adham or a few decades after him.

#### A Play on the Dot beneath the Letter Bāʾ

In the Najafi poet’s line about the dot beneath the *bāʾ*, there is a pun on the **Fourth Point**.

The Fourth Point meant American aid.

After the Second World War, the Americans created an aid programme through which they hoped to gain access to countries emerging from British and French colonial rule. The fourth clause of that programme dealt with this assistance, and so it became known as **Point Four**.

Nationalists understood what lay behind it, and Point Four became a target of the national struggle.

Eventually the term itself became an insult.

I remember sitting in a lecture hall at the College of Economics in 1952. Beside me was a fellow student named Abd al-Qadir, fiercely patriotic and far more politically aware than I was.

The professor came in. He was a supporter of the monarchy.

My friend began muttering under his breath:

“Agent. Spy. Point Four!”

That is what the Najafi poet had in mind. He was accusing the jurist he attacked of being an American agent.

Still, the pun between the dot beneath the *bāʾ* and Point Four forces a connection where none is really needed. The Americans do not care about the wording of the call to prayer. They can work perfectly well with it in pursuit of their interests whether Ali’s “dot” is present or not.

### The Gate

The **gate** is the caller.

It is also said to mean Ali ibn Abi Talib, according to al-Hifni’s dictionary of Sufi terminology.

The *dāʿī*, or caller, is an Ismaili term. Sufis use Ismaili vocabulary in some areas because they share the Ismailis’ taste for inward interpretation. They, too, are esotericists.

The identification of Ali with the gate comes from the saying attributed to the Prophet:

> “I am the city of knowledge, and Ali is its gate.”

The hadith is fabricated.

Al-Shawkani explicitly classifies it as such in *The Collected Spurious Traditions*, on the grounds that no sound chain of transmission reaches the Prophet.

I would also judge it fabricated on the basis of the wording itself.

This is not the language of Prophetic hadith. Nor is its subject one of the familiar questions on which the Prophet normally spoke. It belongs to the language and concerns of a later period, after the first Islamic century.

For the Shiites, this hadith is one of the texts used to support Ali’s right to the caliphate.

The Sufis, who generally avoided the quarrels dividing sects and denominations, took instead the mystical and esoteric flavour that rises from it.

It is a saying that excites the imagination. Whoever invented it must have been a skilled writer with a firm command of the language of esotericism and Sufism.

Another thing that may have appealed to the Sufis is the image itself.

A city without a gate is exposed to intrusion. Its affairs fall into disorder. The gate, therefore, governs access to the city.

Read in this way, the saying places the saint above the prophet.

#### Flash

A **flash** is a glimmer that comes from the highest and holiest Unknown, then quickly goes out.

It belongs to the first beginnings of unveiling.

It is like the spark that appears before the fire catches.

#### Sudden Visitations

These are things that descend suddenly from the unseen upon the heart, producing either contraction or expansion.

The singular form means something that comes without deliberation or thought, rather like an immediate intuition.

But this visitation is a feeling rather than an act of understanding. It may belong to the psyche rather than the intellect.

### Weeping

Al-Hifni’s dictionary says:

> “Weeping is one of the remaining traces of existence. Those who weep while listening to music experience different states. Some weep from fear, some from longing, and some from joy. Weeping from inward feeling is the highest form.”

But weeping does not arise only from listening to music.

Nor is weeping from fear really characteristic of the Sufis, because the Sufi does not fear. He longs.

He does not rejoice in the ordinary sense either. He experiences a quieter gladness, a state that comes over him when, in contemplation, he beholds the unseen kingdom.

As for the statement that **weeping from inward feeling is the highest form**, that is the furthest point Sufi weeping can reach.

One example of this kind of weeping is a story told about the shaykh of Maarra, Abu al-Ala al-Maʿarri.

Someone entered upon him without al-Maʿarri noticing and heard him reciting:

> Surely in this is a sign for anyone who fears the punishment of the world to come.
> That is a day for which all people will be gathered, a day that will be witnessed.
> We delay it only until an appointed time.
> When it comes, no soul will speak except by His permission. Some will be wretched, and some blessed...

Al-Maarri began to cry, saying:

“Glory to the One who said this in the beginning. Glory to the One who said this in eternity.”

Al-Maarri needs to weep.

His tears are tears of inward feeling, not of joy and not of fear. Al-Maarri neither fears nor rejoices.

What moves him to tears are great passages of poetry and the Quran, passages that penetrate him more deeply than they do other people because of the depth of his artistic sensibility.

Al-Ghazali, however, devoted an entire chapter of *The Revival of the Religious Sciences* to the question:

**Why do Sufis prefer music to the Quran?**

He explains that Sufis enter states of ecstasy while listening to music and begin to weep because music contains kinds of language, imagined meanings, and emotional meanings different from those found in the Quran, whose contents are more serious and severe.

Al-Maarri differs from the Sufis here.

He did not listen to music. In fact, he accused them of turning worship into dancing.

Not all Sufis dance, of course. Some do not listen to music at all.

They may weep in solitude instead. A single line of poetry can throw them into ecstasy. So can a Quranic verse that carries them, on the wings of great eloquence, toward the cosmic Absolute.

Weeping from sadness is also a form of weeping from inward feeling.

For the Sufis, sadness is normally a **state**: something that descends upon the soul but does not remain permanently.

Some of them, however, called sadness **the alms-tax of the intellect**.

In other words, it is a price that becomes due when a person’s awareness deepens and his mind expands enough to take in more of the world and penetrate more deeply into reality.

The idea already appears in Ecclesiastes:

> “In much wisdom is much grief, and whoever increases knowledge increases sorrow.”

Abu al-Darda put the same idea more simply:

> “Whoever grows in knowledge grows in pain.”

Understood in this way, sadness becomes a lasting quality. It is no longer a passing state or sudden flash.

This supports those who believed that spiritual states can become permanent.

But I would say that sadness of this kind is a **station**, not a state, because it is one of the necessary consequences of knowledge.

The knower may laugh, although his laughter is usually only a smile.

But he weeps more often than he laughs.

Why?

Because he has given up the claims of the ego. He no longer desperately wants things, and things no longer make him rejoice.

Nor does he grieve over something he himself has lost.

His grief comes from seeing the victory of evil and the vast reach of injustice.

Weeping loosens tension and enriches the inner life.

Sometimes it comes suddenly in response to an event or a scene that arouses pity.

This was the kind of weeping that overtook Muhammad ibn Tabataba when he saw a woman picking up dates that had fallen from the loads he was carrying. She and her daughters had nothing else to eat.

He wept.

And then his tears were followed by a decision to revolt.

Abd al-Qadir al-Jilani once gave this instruction:

> “Beware for Him. Beware of Him. Beware over Him.”

He left no direction of weeping untouched.

Weeping can also come in a wild movement in which a person loses control of himself, tears his clothes, or wanders out alone into the wilderness.

This happened in the gatherings of al-Jilani.

Words would descend upon him all at once and he would lose control of them. He might burst into ecstatic speech, or plunge so deeply into inward feeling that phrases suddenly spilled from his tongue and stopped the ordinary rational mind in its tracks.

Sometimes fires seemed to blaze inside him.

The knower, because of his fierce concern for creation, weeps over people.

He cannot bear the sight of the hungry, the sick, and the beggar.

What he sees overwhelms him with grief.

If he cannot actually come to their aid, he tries at least to relieve his own personal crisis through some individual act of help. And in doing so, states of happiness may descend upon him and lift his heart.

It has occurred to me that the intellectuals of our own time do not weep.

Few of them will stop before a beggar or a shoeshine boy, groan, and pour curses on those who threw such people onto the roadside and robbed them of everything they deserved.

Perhaps the intellectual lacks the determination that seized Ibn Tabataba.

Even so, knowing perfectly well that he is powerless, he can still groan.

And to ease his own distress, he can put his hand into his pocket, pull out a five-thousand note, and give it to the beggar.

Or he can suddenly discover that his shoes desperately need polishing, take them off, hand them to the shoeshine boy, then pay him twice the usual amount.

That can become a kind of soothing drink for sorrow.

But our people of culture have no station in weeping.

No state.

No flash.

No sudden visitation.

Their hearts have hardened until they are like stones, or harder still.

Why?

Because they have placed the claims of their own egos before the claims of other people.

And because they depend completely on imported thought: calculating European thought.

However many books they write, their condition resembles that of a certain sage whom our companions once told us about.

Abu Talib al-Makki relates the story in *The Nourishment of Hearts*.

There was once, in the time of the Israelites, a man who wrote three hundred and sixty books on wisdom. People came to call him “the Sage.”

God revealed to the prophet of his people:

“Tell so-and-so: you have filled the earth with hypocrisy, and none of it has brought you any closer to Me. I accept nothing from your hypocrisy.”

The man was devastated.

He grieved.

Then he abandoned all of that, mixed with ordinary people, and walked through the markets.

God then revealed to the prophet:

“Tell him: now you have found what pleases Me.”

Three hundred and sixty books had failed to bring him divine favour.

But when he abandoned culture, went down among people, lived with them, and shared their worries, he became human and godly at once.

Then he attained that state of acceptance of which the pole al-Jilani said:

> I went on roaming through the fields of acceptance
> until I was given a rank that cannot be given.

The fields of acceptance are the fields of **creation**—of human beings.

I am now convinced that weeping does not come from books.

It comes from seeing and mixing with people.

#### A Question

Can one of the knowers ever reach a point where his tears dry up completely?

The answer is yes.

When they reach the truths of nearness and taste the sweetness of arrival in God’s goodness, weeping leaves them. They weep only while travelling toward God.

Once they arrive, the tears are over.

I found this question and answer in a few handwritten pages given to me by a man from Karkh. He told me that they had belonged to his father.

I could not identify the author. But I saw him mention al-Shadhili, so I knew the text belonged to a later period.

What it says about tears drying up is puzzling.

Suppose we really do reach the truths of nearness and taste the sweetness of arrival.

Does that mean there is no longer a hungry person on our earth?

No oppressor?

No condition left that calls for tears?

Does our entire existence turn into uninterrupted gladness?

Permanent happiness weakens determination.

Perhaps this is one reason our companions rejected paradise.

Paradise is absolute joy and absolute rest. Everything there stops moving, and the purpose of existence disappears.

Existence itself is built on opposites, as the Taoist masters taught. Opposition is the secret of its survival.

For that reason, I do not think we will ever reach a state in which our tears dry up.

The author of those little pages seems to have been in too much of a hurry.

### The Abdāl

*Lisan al-Arab* defines the **abdāl** as:

> “A group of righteous people through whom God keeps the earth standing: forty in Syria and thirty in the other lands. Whenever one of them dies, another takes his place, and for this reason they are called *abdāl*—substitutes.”

Ibn Shumayl says:

> “The abdāl are the best among the best.”

Ibn al-Sikkit says:

> “Those distinguished in righteousness were called *abdāl* because they replaced the righteous people who came before them.”

That is, they took their predecessors’ place after they were gone.

The *abdāl* are also described as saints and worshippers. Each time one dies, another is substituted for him.

The *Taj al-Arus* gives another account:

> “The abdāl are seven. Their number neither increases nor decreases. When one dies, another takes his place. Through them God protects the seven regions, each *badal* having one region under his guardianship. One follows in the footsteps of Abraham, the second in those of Moses, the third Aaron, the fourth Idris, the fifth Joseph, the sixth Jesus, and the seventh Adam.
>
> They know the secrets, movements, stations, and other matters God has placed in the wandering stars. They bear names drawn from the divine attributes, and each receives according to the scope and reach given by the reality of the divine name he bears.”

Al-Zabidi also quotes one of his teachers as saying:

> “A sign of the abdāl is that they have no children.”

They said that Hammad ibn Salama was one of them. He married seventy women and had no child.

In al-Fasi’s commentary on *Dalaʾil al-Khayrat*, we are told in the biography of its author that he left no male child. Some annotators took this as evidence that he had been one of the abdāl.

Izz ibn Abd al-Salam even wrote a treatise attacking the belief that such figures exist, strongly rejecting the claim that “through them God protects the earth.”

Let us pause at one detail:

**They have no male children.**

This was the condition of Jesus, who never married at all.

It was also the condition of Muhammad, who left no surviving son.

The same is true of most of the great Sufi poles.

Among modern figures, Karl Marx had no son who survived him, and Lenin had no children.

But some poles had many sons and daughters: Abd al-Qadir al-Jilani, Ibn Arabi, and al-Hallaj, who had at least one son. Tolstoy had both sons and daughters.

So was the detail recorded in the *Taj al-Arus* simply a coincidence?

Izz ibn Abd al-Salam himself was accused of leaning toward Sufism. He was brought to trial over it and acquitted.

He believed in the sainthood of his contemporary Ibn Arabi.

He was also one of those jurists who cared deeply about ordinary people and their rights.

That concern came to him from Sufism, not from jurisprudence.

Yet he rejected the story of the abdāl in the form we have just seen.

Let us look at some other accounts before deciding what seems sound.

Al-Jurjani’s *Definitions* gives the *badal* an openly supernatural character.

According to him, the abdāl are seven men. Whenever one of them travels away from a place, he leaves behind a body in his own image, apparently carrying on his usual actions, so that no one realises he has gone.

The implication is that the *badal* never truly dies. He simply moves through bodies and forms that preserve his appearance.

Others put their number at forty and do not restrict them to Syria.

Another hierarchy says that forty follow the character of Abraham, seven the character of Moses, three the character of Jesus, and one the character of Muhammad.

According to their rank, they are the masters of creation.

The best of these definitions is still the one in *Lisan al-Arab*:

They are righteous people through whom God keeps the earth standing, and they are called *abdāl* because whenever one dies, another takes his place.

Calling them “worshippers” adds something unnecessary.

Al-Munawi, in his commentary on *al-Jami al-Saghir*, attributes this statement to Ali ibn Abi Talib:

> “They did not surpass other people through great quantities of prayer, fasting, or praise of God, but through good character, true scrupulousness, good intentions, and sound hearts.”

Al-Shaarani’s *Warning to the Deluded* attributes another saying to the Prophet:

> “They did not enter paradise through great quantities of fasting and prayer, but through generosity of spirit and sincere concern for the community.”

Both sayings, whether attributed to Muhammad or Ali, were invented by the Sufis.

But they remove worship as the defining quality.

What makes someone a *badal* becomes righteousness and sincere concern for the community.

As for entering paradise, if we take these figures on their own Sufi terms, they do not enter it at all in the sense of making paradise their goal. They do not depend upon it.

The *abdāl* are a Sufi concept and must therefore be understood according to Sufi conditions and demands.

It seems that Izz ibn Abd al-Salam could accept the idea of the *badal* understood as the **pole and helper**, the person who has reached the highest level of knowledge and acquired mastery over things, so that he directs them rather than being directed by them.

He has the same relation to the state.

He is stronger than it.

The state fears him, while he does not fear the state.

And he has the same relation to money.

He wants no share of it, large or small. Instead he becomes, in the Christian sense, an enemy of its masters.

Izz was right to reject al-Jurjani’s definition, because al-Jurjani gives the *badal* the qualities of a god or an angel—qualities that go beyond those of a knower.

But when Izz rejected the statement that God preserves the earth through the abdāl, he was reading it from the standpoint of jurisprudence rather than Sufism.

Taken symbolically, the statement means something different.

The existence of the abdāl provides a counterforce to the destructive power of the state, religious authorities, and the rich.

The role of the *badal* lies somewhere between mobilising human forces against that destruction and reducing the damage it inflicts on the earth and its people.

In any case, saying that God keeps the earth standing through them gives us another name for the abdāl:

**the awtād—the stakes or pegs.**

The image comes from the Quranic picture of the cosmos, in which mountains are described as pegs holding the earth steady and preventing it from tilting.

The term was borrowed for the abdāl because their presence upon the earth prevents its people from collapsing into corruption and keeps them in some measure of righteousness and balance.

An Iraqi saying expresses the same idea:

> “If it were emptied, it would overturn.”

Meaning: if the earth were emptied of righteous people, it would turn upside down.

Alongside **abdāl** and **awtād** we find another name:

**God’s cherished ones.**

The Arabic term comes from a root meaning extreme care, possessiveness, or reluctance to give up something precious.

God’s cherished ones are those whom God guards against corruption and protects from moral contamination.

The same root can describe a person unwilling to part with something valuable.

These people are precious beings who occupy a special place with God and therefore receive special care.

Not care that preserves their lives.

Care that preserves their souls.

Their lives may be short. They may endure severe trials and disasters. But their inner selves retain their strength and integrity, and their influence remains deep.

“God’s cherished ones” is the broadest category. It gathers the abdāl and the awtād under its wings.

Now we are entering their sea.

We will try, as far as possible, to identify them.

I have drawn their names from many different nations and religions, because the concept of the *badal* is universal.

These people are the stakes of the whole earth, not of one nation alone.

#### Laozi

The first of the abdāl in history.

He lived in the sixth century BCE, toward the end of the Zhou dynasty, during the Spring and Autumn period in which Chinese philosophy flourished.

He founded the Taoist school of philosophy.

His teachings were collected in the *Tao Te Ching*. Historians disagree over whether he himself wrote the book or whether his followers compiled it a century later.

The *Tao Te Ching* is the foundational source of **social Taoism**—militant Taoism—and, by extension, of social Sufism in Islam.

Laozi called for the state to be made simpler so that its power could be reduced.

He called for wealth to be shared rather than hoarded and monopolised by a small class of rich people and state officials.

He opposed excessive civilisation when it came at the expense of the human essence, which in his view should remain as free as possible from the additions imposed by civilisation, apart from what is genuinely necessary.

He opposed war and called for swords to be turned into ploughs.

He also laid out the path an intellectual should follow toward the state:

**boycott it and refuse dealings with it.**

He challenged the emperor directly:

> When the emperor is crowned,
> or the three ministers appointed,
> do not send a gift of jade
> or a team of four horses.
>
> Remain where you are
> and reveal the Tao.

He raised the banner of the Tao against the banner of the state.

The Tao is the Chinese Absolute.

The Taoist unites with it in much the same way that the Sufi unites with the Islamic Absolute.

Union with the Absolute becomes the alternative to union with the state.

Five centuries after Laozi, his name was used to establish a religion called Taoism.

The religion and the philosophy continued alongside one another without either fully replacing the other.

The religion did retain some positive elements from the philosophy’s principle of negation.

It became a school of strength in which Chinese people trained themselves to resist foreign penetration of their country.

They developed forms of physical exercise designed to produce strong citizens.

But the religion also became heavily burdened with superstition.

It turned into a school of empty negation, misreading the philosophical principle of negation in the Tao.

Some governments used it for their own purposes, which took it directly away from the master’s teaching of boycotting the state.

For that reason Mao Zedong attacked it.

Unfortunately, he failed to distinguish between the religion and the philosophy. His attack swept Laozi up with the rest, almost as though Laozi himself were responsible for founding the later religion.

This was one expression of Mao’s vulgar materialism—the same vulgar materialism that produced the disaster of the Cultural Revolution.[^ms-v1-013]

Laozi’s ideas are communalist in their general structure.

They are one of the roots of modern Chinese communism, which Chinese communists built and developed by mixing Marxism with Chinese wisdom.

This Chinese form of Marxism achieved the communal transformation of Chinese society and fulfilled the dream of the Taoist sages by feeding the hungry.

In the time of the Taoists, China had fewer than fifty million people.

Today it has more than a billion, all of whom have been fed, so that none remain hungry.

Meanwhile India, with its seven hundred million people, has continued to endure the hunger of past ages under an Indian philosophy saturated with religion and an Indian mysticism overloaded with ritual.

The communists of India never discovered the path taken by the communists of China.

And so they never produced the same miracle.

#### Epicurus

In Arabic his name is usually pronounced *Abiqur*. The original consonant is a **p**, as in “Paris,” and the original initial vowel is closer to *i* than to the Arabic pronunciation that became standard.

He was a Greek philosopher of the second half of the fourth and the beginning of the third century BCE.

He developed the atomic materialism of Democritus and brought it close to atheism—or perhaps to a form of deism.

He accepted the existence of the gods but denied that they interfered in the affairs of the world.

He left them in their world, far away from ours.

This is close to Chinese ideas about heaven, where some thinkers imagined a heavenly empire that had no direct relation to human life on earth.

Epicurus fought against religious superstition, especially beliefs that filled people with terror of the gods.

He denied the effectiveness of prayer.

The Chinese thinker Wang Chong did the same.

Epicurus said, in effect:

If the gods listened to human prayers, humanity would quickly destroy itself, because people are constantly praying for evil and destruction to fall upon one another.

He called for a human life free from fear and grief.

His ideas were badly misread, just as Laozi’s were.

People turned them into a philosophy of pleasure-seeking.

A later school calling itself Epicurean arose and taught enjoyment of life without proper regard for moral values and principles.

Epicurus himself was innocent of this.

What moved him was love for human beings and pity for the terror religion inflicted upon them.

#### Mozi

Mozi was a philosopher of the fifth century BCE.

He worked on logic and the human sciences.

He may have been the first major critic of Confucianism.

He accused it of impoverishing people by burdening them and occupying their lives with ritual.

He also criticised Confucianism for denying spirits.

Confucians did not recognise beings existing separately from the visible world.

Mozi, however, believed such beings were necessary to restrain people and to strengthen goodness and love within them.

In his emphasis on love he resembles Jesus.

He made love the basis of the good life.

Mozi spoke of one great God who wanted human beings to love one another and who judged their actions, rewarding those who did good and punishing those who did evil.

For Mozi, God was necessary because belief in Him encouraged virtue.

He did not, however, make this God the creator.

Chinese thought in general never devoted much attention to the question of creator and creation.

Mozi’s God was there to watch and guide.

For that reason Mozi did not establish a religion.

Modern historians who describe him as the only Chinese thinker to have founded a religion are mistaken.

Talking about God does not necessarily amount to religion.

Mozi rejected both ritual and fatalism, and those two things are part of religion’s warp and woof.

Mozi lived during the Warring States period and therefore thought deeply about war.

He rejected aggressive war but accepted defensive war, much as al-Maʿarri would later do.

He also tried to imagine a system for regulating conflicts between states.

He called for the creation of a common force that could defend small states against aggression by larger ones.

That proposal qualifies him for the title:

**father of the United Nations.**

Mozi loved human beings and pitied them, as Epicurus would after him.

But, like Epicurus, he did not give the problem of hunger and poverty the same attention.

That was the particular mission of the Taoists.

#### Mencius

His original name was Mengzi.

“Mencius” is the Latinised form given to his name by the Italian Jesuits who came to China in the seventeenth century.

He was the second great master of Confucianism and lived in the fourth century BCE.

He accepted the general doctrine of his teacher but differed from him in politics and society.

Mencius stood with the people against rulers.

He recognised the people’s right to revolt when rulers violated their rights.

He possessed an intense sensitivity toward poverty and hunger: the things suffered by ordinary people while kings and the privileged classes lived in fantastic luxury.

He personally went to rulers and confronted them over the theft of the people’s rights and over their own enjoyment at the people’s expense.

His language was direct, forceful, and openly defiant.

Mencius also adopted the **nine-square system** of agricultural reform.

Modern historians often attribute the scheme to him, but in reality it came from the legacy of the Zhou dynasty in the first millennium BCE.

It remained alive in the memory of the philosophers.

The Taoists adopted it, and so did Mencius, until it became one of the classic utopian projects of Chinese philosophy.

The scheme divided an area irrigated by a single water source into nine plots.

Eight plots were distributed among eight families.

The ninth belonged to the state and was cultivated collectively by those eight families on the state’s behalf.

This ninth plot replaced taxation.

The farmers paid nothing on their own eight plots apart from contributing their labour to the ninth.

Agriculture within the squares was directed and planned in advance.

Alongside crops, families were required to raise animals for meat and silkworms for clothing.

A family was free either to consume these products or sell them.

But first it was required to provide adequate food and clothing for its young people and its elderly.

The scheme disappeared after the fall of the Zhou dynasty during the third century BCE.

Philosophy was flourishing at precisely this time, and the philosophers adopted the old system because they viewed the Zhou realm as a model of the good society.

They did so because of the communal character of its economy.

Some historians have tried to understand Mencius through a simple opposition between progressive and reactionary, left and right.

They have said that Mencius was right-wing in ideology but left-wing in politics.

This is hardly unusual in the history of ideas.

We repeatedly find progressive thinkers who are remote from the concerns of ordinary people, just as we find conservative or religious thinkers who stand close to them.

Religious thought, of course, generally sides with the property-owning classes.

Mencius was not a religious thinker. He was a conservative thinker within the Confucian school.

Confucianism itself is not a religion.

The contradiction between the progressive and the aristocratic runs throughout history.

Xunzi, one of the fathers of Legalism, was a progressive thinker who remained distant from ordinary people and their concerns.

The same was true of most Islamic philosophers, with the exception of al-Razi.

Al-Razi’s distinctiveness came from the fact that he had absorbed something of Eastern spirituality.

He escaped the narrow world of the Peripatetics and their rigid, purely formal logic and moved toward a humanistic deism.

The same is true of the social Sufis beginning with Ibrahim ibn Adham.

As we saw earlier, the great masters of philosophical or intellectual Sufism did not join the communalist social struggle.

In this respect, they stood closer to the philosophers of Islam than to the social Sufis.

#### Sayings of Mencius

> Whoever lacks compassion is not human.
> Whoever lacks shame and generosity is not human.
> Whoever lacks humility and reverence is not human.
> Whoever lacks a sense of right and wrong is not human.

Humanity, then, is a philosophical achievement, not merely a natural fact.

The conditions for becoming fully human are moral.

A creature’s physical development from a proto-human form into the shape of a modern human being does not by itself turn that creature into a human being in the full sense.

This idea is characteristic of Chinese philosophy.

It has no real equivalent in Greek philosophy or in the Islamic philosophy that grew from it.

Its clearest parallels appear among the Sufis.

Mencius’s conditions for becoming human fit closely with the common Sufi principle that what truly distinguishes the human being from the animal is not intelligence alone, but the **sense of justice**.

Some animals possess forms of intelligence.

Al-Maarri even observed that the silkworm surpasses human beings in certain kinds of skill.

Intelligence can also be used for aggression and destruction, as it so often is even today.

Torture appeared among human beings, not among animals, because the animal does not possess the kind of intelligence that allows it to invent increasingly elaborate ways of killing.

Whole systems of corrupt and vicious behaviour exist among humans that do not exist among animals.

So the passage from the proto-human to the human is completed only through a sense of justice.

#### Zhuangzi

Zhuangzi lived between the fourth and third centuries BCE.

He was the second great philosopher of Taoism.

He continued Laozi’s teachings, developed them, and deepened them.

Unlike his master, Zhuangzi did not work in government.

Laozi had served in the imperial archive, effectively as a librarian.

Zhuangzi chose isolation.

As we mentioned earlier in the Orbit of Mīm, in the discussion of love and marriage, he also left his wife during this period of withdrawal.

When a delegation from a king came to invite him to court, he drove them away and mocked them:

> “Do not defile me. I want to enjoy my own free will.”

This follows exactly the Sufi attitude toward the state: boycott it and regard contact with it as contamination.

Zhuangzi’s social philosophy centred on the idea of a return to nature.

He was one of the earliest philosophers we might call a **naturalist**.

From this position he opposed the excessive expansion of urban life, seeing it as something that clouds and corrupts the human essence.

His teacher Laozi, however, was more concerned with poverty and hunger.

Laozi did not devote as much attention to naturalism. He promoted instead his teaching of non-action and tied it to meeting people’s material needs.

At times Laozi sounds almost exactly like the Sufis when they criticise excessive cultivation of the intellect at the expense of people—much like the story we have already seen about the Israelite sage.

Laozi called for:

> **“Emptying the mind and filling the stomach.”**

I take this to mean that excessive preoccupation with culture makes a person forget the rights and concerns of ordinary people.

It is as though filling the mind comes at the price of emptying the stomach.

This is an early indication of the conflict between purely intellectual work—pure reason—and social work.

We saw the same thing in Islamic culture.

The Islamic philosophers and the intellectual Sufis—the philosophers of Sufism—remained distant from ordinary people and their concerns.

The great figures of social Sufism came closer to those concerns.

They paid less attention to philosophical propositions.

Less attention—not less knowledge.

The most important and distinctive thing about Zhuangzi is that he was one of the first thinkers to draw a clear line of opposition and boycott between the free intellectual and the state.

He made this separation a condition of free culture.

In his own conduct, he also met the Sufi conditions for the sage.

When he became firmly established in the Tao, he left his young wife.

But he did not simply abandon her to her fate.

As I have repeatedly said, God’s cherished ones are guided by inward feeling even in the smallest details of daily life.

Zhuangzi was not responsible for his wife’s suicide.

He wanted her to find happiness away from him after he had become unable to give her happiness with him.

Zhuangzi also taught the idea of the **Great Society**, which corresponds closely to the Sufi principle of **the well-being of creation**.

In such a society, the state and its officials exist to serve the people.

The people do not exist to serve the state, as they continue to do even today.

#### Jesus of Nazareth

Immanuel, son of Mary by her lawful husband Joseph son of David.

“Jesus” was his title. The original name was Joshua, later altered in Hebrew into the form rendered in Arabic as Isa.

He was born in 6 BCE and lived for thirty-six years.

Among God’s cherished ones, he had the shortest life.

The Romans executed him with the cooperation of Jewish agents after recognising the dangerous implications of his movement.

Some modern historians have questioned whether he ever existed.

Most of those who raised such doubts were Western Jews.

Their arguments were later taken up by an Egyptian atheist writer, Issam al-Din Hafni Nasif, in a book devoted to the subject titled *Christ in a Contemporary Understanding*.

These doubts are arbitrary.

Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels rejected them and paid them little attention.

The Gospel accounts of Jesus are clear enough. Apart from passages shaped by doctrinal language, there is nothing inherently mythical in them.

The first chapter of Matthew says:

> The birth of Jesus Christ happened in this way. His mother Mary was engaged to Joseph. Before they had come together, she was found to be pregnant by the Holy Spirit.
>
> Joseph, her husband, was a righteous man and did not want to expose her publicly, so he decided to leave her quietly.
>
> While he was thinking about this, an angel of the Lord appeared to him in a dream and said:
>
> “Joseph son of David, do not be afraid to take Mary as your wife. What has been conceived in her is from the Holy Spirit.”
>
> When Joseph woke, he did what the angel had commanded and took Mary as his wife. He did not know her sexually until she gave birth to her firstborn son, whom he called Jesus.

In the second chapter of Luke, an angel comes to Mary and tells her that she will become pregnant and give birth to a son whom she will call Jesus.

She answers:

“How can this happen when I have known no man?”

The angel replies:

“The Holy Spirit will come upon you...”

The same chapter then describes the birth of Jesus and Mary and Joseph caring for the child.

If we set aside the religious embellishment, what remains is a perfectly natural birth.

A child named Immanuel was born.

His mother may have become pregnant during the period of engagement, before the marriage was consummated. That is what the two Gospel accounts seem to suggest to me.

This event was then used as the basis for the story of the Holy Spirit and of a birth without defilement—that is, without sexual intercourse.

Eastern peoples have often regarded sex itself as something sinful, even within marriage.

Abu Dhuayb al-Hudhali once praised the mother of one of his heroes by saying that she had conceived him, as it were, through her clothing:

> Born of women who conceived while their waistbands were tied,
> so he grew without taint.

The meaning is that no direct bodily contact supposedly took place between her and her husband.

It is strange that Muslims missed this particular opportunity and never inserted anything similar into the biography of Muhammad’s birth.

Islamic doctrine is severe regarding the impurity associated with the sexual act, which is why it requires the full ritual washing after intercourse.

The Arabic word *janāba* comes from a Semitic root associated with being put aside and with forms of sin or impurity.

The washing of *janāba* is therefore understood as a purification from that condition.

I once discussed Christ with a doctor from Burj.

He said to me:

“I know he was not a prophet. He was a philosopher.”

I replied:

“It would be more accurate to call him a sage.”

In fact, perhaps even “sage” is not quite right.

Jesus was above all a **social militant**.

He did not work in the field of culture or scholarship, so it is difficult to classify him as an intellectual in that sense.

Yet this creates a problem for us.

Our condition for inclusion in this list of abdāl and cherished ones is that they should belong broadly to the world of culture rather than to ordinary politics.

Jesus, however, was not a politician in that conventional sense.

He was a prophet.

Prophecy is an independent phenomenon. It belongs neither to culture nor to politics, though it may contain elements of both and may act within both.

Perhaps Jesus deserves the title of prophet more fully than the earlier Israelite prophets.

Those men were kings, leaders, and military commanders—or, at best, lawmakers and judges.

The same was generally true of those who came after Jesus.

Muhammad forms a partial exception because of the particular nature of his leadership and government.

His rule differed from the politics of kings, emperors, and military men because it was constrained by Arab principles of **futuwwa**, the older ethic of chivalric and anti-aristocratic leadership.

That is why early Arabs and Muslims placed prophecy in opposition to kingship, and the prophet in opposition to the king.

This was a specifically Arab understanding of prophecy, rooted in the Arab rejection of despotism and hereditary rule.

The prophecy of Jesus was something different.

It was a distinctive blend of wisdom, knowledge, and social Sufism, with a taste entirely its own.

Nothing else is quite like it.

The movement Jesus set in motion shares features with the earlier revolt of Spartacus and the later movements of al-Hallaj and the leader of the Zanj rebellion.

But his strategy differed from theirs.

The difference came from his assessment of the balance of forces and from what he learned from the failure of Spartacus.

Here we should correct the famous statement attributed to Karl Marx that **the defeat of Spartacus prepared the way for the victory of Christ**.

It should instead read:

**the defeat of Spartacus prepared the way for the victory of Christianity.**

Unfortunately I have never found the original text of Marx’s remark and depend on translators. Perhaps Marx himself meant Christianity rather than Christ.

Otherwise, Christ did not win.

His fate was the same as that of Spartacus, al-Hallaj, and the leader of the Zanj.

All of them were martyrs.

And if we count Paul’s victory as a victory for Christ, then we might as well count the victory of the British Labour Party as a victory for Karl Marx.

As I have said, Jesus combines Sufi and Taoist qualities.

But those qualities appear in him through a form of active, leading prophecy: a prophecy that incites and organises without submitting itself to the calculations of everyday politics, unlike the Israelite prophets or Muhammad.

In his dreams, longings, and personal conduct, Christ resembles the Sufi and the Taoist.

In his movement, he resembles al-Hallaj rather than Zhuangzi or al-Bistami, because he entered the field of direct struggle.

In truth, it is difficult to find any real equivalent to Christ.

Not among prophets.

Not among Sufis.

Not among Taoists.

He is something of his own, called **Christ**.

In the same way, it is difficult to find an equivalent to al-Maʿarri among poets, Sufis, or Taoists.

Within the world of culture, al-Maʿarri is something of his own, called **al-Maʿarri**.

Both men share features with the people to whom we compare them, yet both resist being reduced to those comparisons.

Jesus moved among the masses of slaves and poor people.

His relationship with them followed the same path laid down by Taoism and Sufism.

So did his break with the rich and his exclusion of them from his kingdom.

He prohibited the hoarding of wealth, using language strikingly similar to that found in the *Tao Te Ching*.

Jesus is one of the fathers of Islamic Sufism.

Among the prophets, he is the one closest to the Sufis.

He also stood very close to al-Maʿarri.

Of all the prophets, Jesus is the clearest and most complete prophet of communalism.

He tried to present his movement as a purely religious movement with no interest in political power.

This was a way of misleading the Romans until the movement could grow stronger and spread more widely among the masses.

In reality, he was trying to establish a communal fellowship inside Palestinian society under Roman rule.
