We do not know what else he may have intended beyond that.

The fellowship he sought resembles the Qarmatian community later established by Hamdan ibn al-Ashath in the Iraqi countryside.

His disciples tried to continue his mission after him.

But after seeing what had happened to their prophet, they did not dare proclaim his ideas openly.

The movement therefore remained almost frozen until Paul took hold of it and set it on the road that eventually turned it into a religion called Christianity—a religion of which Christ himself knew nothing.

Prophecy is Christ, and Christ is prophecy.

Muhammad’s prophecy, as we have seen, represented the Arab conception of chivalric leadership and authority: the prophet set against the king, in keeping with an Arab consciousness hostile to despotism and hereditary government.

It belongs, in that sense, to the history of democracy.

The prophecy of Jesus, by contrast, had a flavour entirely its own.

Its elements were Taoism, Sufism, and communalism.

The combination does not resemble any of those elements taken separately, yet it contains them all.

That is what places Christ among God’s cherished ones, the abdāl without whom the earth cannot continue its eternal movement.

Religious tradition gave Jesus the usual miracles of the prophets: knowledge of the unseen and raising the dead.

The Sufi imagination added the ability to walk on water.

It explained this through the lightness of his body, supposedly produced by extreme asceticism.

His body had become so thin and light that it could float.

Sufi miracles often follow this equation.

Some Sufis were even given powers beyond those attributed to Christ and were said to fly through the air.

These miracles are Sufi exaggerations built around stories of extreme asceticism and severe reduction of material needs.

The idea is simple:

if the body contains almost no food and becomes thin enough, it will eventually grow lighter than water or even air and therefore become capable of floating or flying.

As physics, the equation sounds straightforward.

Medically, it is impossible.

Long before a human body reached that degree of lightness, it would die from lack of nutrition.

Yet such stories do express something real about the Sufi struggle against nature and the attempt to challenge its demands through iron will and absolute determination.

Of course, the Sufis could not win that contest.

They compensated for their defeat by turning to miracles outside the laws of medicine.

Still, they did succeed in reducing their needs to the minimum compatible with bodily survival.

They did this with food and drink.

They went even further with clothing.

They did not seek elegance, colour, or display.

They wore what covered them and protected them from the cold.

They were strongest of all in resisting the demands of sex—but generally only after passing through youth.

The ordinary pattern was for a disciple to marry.

As he grew older and advanced toward the rank of a pole, he became capable of living without women.

For that reason it is rare to find one of them marrying after the age of forty.

Perhaps this also explains why so many of them lived longer than rulers and rich men: their bodies had not been consumed by appetite.

Westerners understood Paul’s letters better than they understood the Gospels.

The Christianity taught by Paul was Greek rather than Palestinian.

Yet because the Gospels and Acts remained within the New Testament, they did affect receptive European hearts.

From time to time, social movements with communalist tendencies appeared in different parts of Europe.

The greatest of these was the German Peasants’ War of the sixteenth century.

It was a Christian movement.

Friedrich Engels wrote an entire book about it, *The Peasant War in Germany*. He brushed away the dust that had covered it and placed it at the centre of European history.

One striking feature of the founders of Marxism is that they did not allow doctrine or ideology to blind them when they dealt with historical events.

They examined historical movements through their social and class content.

This is why Engels recognised the German Peasants’ War, despite its Christian ideology, as a genuine and fully developed form of class struggle.

He approached Christ in much the same way, outside the boundaries of religious belief, as can be seen in his work *On the History of Early Christianity*.

#### Mazdak

Mazdak was a Persian of the fifth century CE.

He developed a Gnostic doctrine drawing on the teachings of Mani and Zoroaster.

He led a vast communalist movement, larger in scale than the movement of Jesus.

Like Jesus, he called for wealth to be held in common.

His enemies accused him of also calling for women to be held in common.

Nothing of this kind appeared in the history of the East, where communal ownership of wealth repeatedly existed alongside strict moral discipline.

Mazdak’s movement produced enormous upheaval within Sasanian society and became a major pressure upon the empire.

King Kavad adopted the movement, though he never implemented its principles completely.

His heir, Khosrow Anushirvan, later launched a war of extermination against the Mazdakites, destroying the movement and scattering those who remained.

Among those displaced Mazdakites, we have suggested the possibility of including Rozbeh of Isfahan, who later became known in Islam as Salman the Persian. We will return to him.

Mazdakism also left traces among some Arab tribes, including Tamim.

Abu Dharr al-Ghifari may also have been influenced by it.

But in truth there is no need to explain one communalist movement in the East simply by tracing its influence upon another.

The communal struggle never really stopped.

It formed a continuous chain stretching into modern times, when it finally merged with modern communist movements.

Mazdak’s personality combined features of the Jesus tradition, Taoism, and Sufism.

We see this in his asceticism, restraint, purity, even cleanliness of dress.

We see it even more clearly in his relationship with ordinary people.

For Mazdak, the people had the first right to wealth and resources.

These things should be distributed among them rather than monopolised by state officials and the rich.

#### Abu Dharr al-Ghifari

Jundab ibn Junada, from the Arab tribe of Ghifar in the desert.

His family belonged to the tribe’s poor.

Stories about his life before Islam differ.

Some describe him as a highwayman.

Others describe him as a religious seeker.

The two may not actually contradict one another if we imagine his highway robbery on the model of Urwa ibn al-Ward.

In any case, highway robbery appears in the early lives of several Sufis, including Bishr al-Hafi and al-Fudayl ibn Iyad.

Sufyan al-Thawri went so far as to say that a jurist who begins life as a highwayman is better than one who enters the path of jurisprudence directly.

His reason was that **futuwwa**, which was often associated with forms of banditry practised by the *ayyarun*, gave the future jurist a more open personality.

It made him better able to deal with people and less rigid and fanatical.

We find something of the same spirit in the pole al-Jilani’s call to:

> “Plunder the wealth of God and distribute it among the servants of God.”

Abu Dharr accepted Islam during the earliest phase of Muhammad’s preaching in Mecca.

He did not, however, join the Prophet permanently until after the migration to Medina.

At first he played no distinctive role among the Companions.

He was like any one of them, carrying out the general duties of war and peace.

He took part in fighting, but no exceptional feats of arms are attributed to him like those associated with famous Muslim warriors such as Ali ibn Abi Talib or al-Miqdad.

From the beginning, however, Abu Dharr stood out for his personal asceticism and his closeness to the poor.

Some sources count him among the **People of the Suffa**.

These were poor Companions who had no houses of their own and lived under a covered area attached to the mosque, surviving on support from the people of Medina.

Perhaps Abu Dharr was counted among them simply because he spent time with them out of sympathy for their poverty, rather than because he actually lived there.

His first clear political role came in the dispute over the succession after the Prophet.

Abu Dharr stood with Ali.

He belonged to the minority that supported Ali’s claim and sided with him against the group that had gathered at Saqifa.

His name is also listed among the seven or ten people said to have attended the burial of Fatima.

She had asked to be buried secretly so that Abu Bakr would not pray over her body.

After Fatima’s death, matters gradually returned to normal.

She had been the one preventing Ali from giving allegiance to Abu Bakr.

The conflict therefore diminished.

Abu Dharr nevertheless remained close to Ali.

He took part in the wars of conquest under the first two caliphs.

Then, during the caliphate of Uthman, he began his struggle against the newly emerging Qurayshi and Islamic aristocracy.

By this time, a clear division had appeared between rich and poor within the community of the Companions.

Abu Dharr became active within the opposition camp led by Ali.

But he followed his own rhythm of struggle.

He was more forceful and less restrained than his leader.

His personal choices also went further.

Abu Dharr refused to own anything beyond his annual military stipend of five thousand dirhams.

He lived on that and distributed whatever remained to the poor.

He reduced the needs of himself and his family to the bare minimum.

In doing so, however, he treated his daughters unfairly.

They suffered deprivation because of his severe restrictions.

To me, injustice against daughters is no different from injustice against any other poor person.

If I had a daughter, I would allow her to wear beautiful clothes and decorate her hair in a reasonable and acceptable way.

I would let her eat the sweets other girls eat.

Without extravagance.

Without waste.

Abu Dharr also took only one wife and had no concubines.

He owned no slave women and no male slaves.

In other words, he personally abolished slavery for himself, just as he abolished polygamy.

Rozbeh of Isfahan—Salman the Persian—did the same.

If we add Amir al-Anbari, Salman’s disciple, who never married at all and also owned no slaves, then these three men can be regarded as the great early teachers of communalism in Islam.

They provided the model from which the masters of social Sufism took their own practice of marrying only one woman and refusing to own slave women or slaves.

Abu Dharr appears in the lists of saints.

Sufi biographical works include him among their own figures.

He can therefore be regarded as one of the fathers of Sufism.

Yet neither he nor Salman really belonged to the class of intellectuals.

They lived before Islamic intellectual culture had properly developed.

That culture arose only in the generation of the Successors.

Maabad al-Juhani, a disciple of Abu Dharr, may in fact have been the first genuine thinker in Islam.

Abu Dharr, like Salman, was a social militant in the manner of Jesus.

His movement came from class awareness and human feeling rather than from any developed intellectual system.

In their time Muslims knew no book apart from the Quran.

The Quran is a book of belief, politics, and law.

It is not a book of philosophy or science.

I have nevertheless included Abu Dharr here, and will include Salman after him, despite my rule that the abdāl should generally be people of thought.

Their way of life belongs to the same world as Taoism and Sufism, not conventional politics.

The description **social militant** suits both of them better than **political leader**.

And these orbits are concerned with Sufism primarily as a cultural current.

Abu Dharr held firmly to the Quranic verse condemning the hoarding of wealth, even though, according to the accepted Muslim legal understanding, its original force had been superseded.

He used it in his struggle against the wealthy Muslims and called for them to be stripped of wealth beyond what they actually needed.

His story is too long to tell fully in this short list of God’s cherished ones.

In the end, he was exiled to the desert settlement of al-Rabadha with his wife and daughters.

He died there.

Al-Zirikli says in *al-Aʿlam* that Abu Dharr was:

> “the first socialist persecuted by governments.”

This is an understandable attempt to claim him for Islam, but there had been many persecuted figures before him.

Let us complete this short account with the words Ali spoke when Abu Dharr left for al-Rabadha in exile:

> Abu Dharr, you became angry for the sake of God, so place your hope in the One for whose sake you became angry.
>
> These people fear you because of their worldly possessions, while you fear them because of your faith.
>
> Leave in their hands what they fear you may take from them, and escape from them with what you fear they may take from you.
>
> How badly they need the thing you tried to deny them, and how little you need the thing they denied you.
>
> Tomorrow you will know who has truly gained, and who is most to be envied.

Ali remained optimistic even in the bitterest days of defeat.

It was he who said:

> “The day of the oppressed against the oppressor will be harder than the day of the oppressor against the oppressed.”

So when he told Abu Dharr:

> “Tomorrow you will know who has truly gained, and who is most to be envied,”

he was surely not talking about Judgment Day.

Ali was fighting for possession of this world.

Before leaving Abu Dharr, we should mention this fabricated hadith:

> “May God have mercy on Abu Dharr. He walks alone, dies alone, and will be raised alone.”

The saying is attached to the story of the expedition to Tabuk.

Abu Dharr had fallen behind the army, and his enemies began spreading rumours about him.

Then a lone figure appeared in the distance, hurrying along the road.

The Prophet saw him and said:

“Let it be Abu Dharr.”

And it was Abu Dharr.

When he drew close enough to be recognised, the Prophet supposedly spoke the words above.

The report was probably invented by the Sufis.

It carries the unmistakable flavour of **estrangement**, a theme that runs through all their lives.

Abu Dharr’s estrangement is made perfect:

he walks alone;

he dies alone, exiled in the desert;

and he is raised alone.

On the Day of Resurrection he will come walking by himself, with no companion beside him.

A stranger on earth.

A stranger in heaven.

And the strangest thing of all is that his estrangement came at the very height of Islam.

#### Salman — Ruzbeh al-Isfahani

Ruzbeh was most likely his original name; Salman was the name he took after becoming Muslim. He left his home in Isfahan and headed west during the persecution of the Mazdakites. He became a Christian in Syria and worked in the church serving the poor. He also spent some time in Mosul for the same purpose.

But the churches did not satisfy his hopes for justice. He found too much corruption and too few trustworthy people among them. After hearing that a prophet had appeared in the Hijaz, he set out for Arabia. Perhaps he had already learned something of this prophet’s secrets and of his relations with figures connected to Christianity, such as Waraqa ibn Nawfal and his cousin Khadija, while he himself was still searching Christianity for people faithful to Christ and his principles.

Ruzbeh joined Muhammad in Yathrib. Muhammad received him warmly, and the two began holding long conversations at night. Around this time, the deeply controversial and ambiguous verse prohibiting the hoarding of wealth was revealed.

Salman seems to have worked from behind the scenes. He spoke little, and Arabic did not come easily to him, so what he did mattered more than what he said.

When the Muslims split at the Saqifa, Salman sided with the minority that supported Ali’s claim to the caliphate. Even so, he cooperated with Abu Bakr and Umar, just as his own imam cooperated with them.

He took part in the conquest of al-Mada’in, the capital of his ancestors, and accepted Umar’s appointment as its governor.

There Salman created the kind of authority sought by Taoists and Sufis alike: **a ruler who leads without ruling**.

He guided people while standing behind them, because he did not want them standing behind him and did not want to burden them with his authority. He did not move into the governor’s palace but lived in an ordinary house. He had no court, no servants, no wives, and no slave women.

His historians went so far in describing his simplicity and contempt for the trappings of power that they claimed he walked barefoot. Perhaps he did, at least at times.

Something similar happened under the Abbasids when Ali al-Rida, the eighth Shi‘i imam, was heir apparent to al-Ma’mun. On one occasion al-Ma’mun asked him to lead the people in Friday prayer. The heir apparent came out barefoot and on foot, at the head of a procession of worshippers rather than courtiers and attendants. The people were captivated. They gathered around him from the far ends of the city.

The intelligence officials hurried reports to the caliph warning him to act before matters went any further. Al-Ma’mun sent someone to stop al-Rida halfway there and bring him back.

When he returned, al-Ma’mun told him:

“We asked too much of you, Abu al-Hasan.”

Umar, however, was not pleased by Salman’s extreme disregard for the outward signs of authority. He began putting pressure on him and criticizing him, while Salman stubbornly continued as before.

Then the reports about him stop.

Did he remain in al-Mada’in and die there? Was he removed from office? Did he resign and leave?

In earlier writings on Salman, I argued that he withdrew and returned to his native Isfahan.

He came back disappointed by Islam, just as he had earlier been disappointed by Christianity.

Salman was not a man of ritual devotion. He performed the required duties, but rejected excessive ritualism.

He was sparing in food and clothing even before becoming governor. His conduct, like that of his companion Abu Dharr, suggests that they regarded asceticism as an obligation both for people in power and for those outside it.

Ali ibn Abi Talib held a different view. For him, asceticism was required specifically of those who held power.

Salman’s crisis was not spiritual or intellectual, as Salman Rushdie and his commentator Sadiq al-Azm tried to portray it. It was a **human crisis**: the crisis of a Mazdakite activist searching for a solution to the suffering of the deprived and the poor.

Salman does not belong among the intellectuals.

He was a social activist.

Salman preferred to live from the work of his own hands. Whenever he received his salary, he distributed it and supported himself in his spare time by weaving palm leaves.

In this way he followed the Sufi way of life without himself being a Sufi, since Sufism had not yet appeared in his time.

As we have seen, Sufism is a cultural phenomenon connected to a deep current of heterodoxy in the person who embraces it. Salman was not equipped for that dimension. He came from a civilisation that had shown relatively little interest in philosophical culture.[^ms-v1-014]

Salman the Persian had a student from the Banu Tamim who followed much the same path.

#### Amir al-Anbari

He was also known as Ibn Abd Qays. He belonged to the Banu al-Anbar, one of the branches of Tamim.

He lived in Basra, a city strongly associated with Tamim. His resolve became visible during the caliphate of Uthman, when corruption was already spreading through the early Islamic state. Amir was one of the first to confront it.

He has been treated as one of the learned religious men, in a line that brings him close to figures such as Ahmad ibn Hanbal and Bishr al-Hafi.

But he was a fabric of his own, unique in both warp and weft.

He lived like the Sufis without being counted among them, because he appeared before Sufism itself had emerged. He followed disciplines later adopted by al-Maʿarri, though without possessing the immense learning from which al-Maʿarri built his towering intellectual world.

He was an opponent of authority, but he did not join the organised opposition that erupted at the time against the caliph’s corruption and ultimately ended in Uthman’s death.

His conduct resembles that of an intellectual dissident who does not know how to involve himself in politics. Yet he cannot really be classified among the intellectuals either, because Islamic culture in his time was still an unformed embryo.

Nor did he undertake anything like the intellectual work later attempted by Ma‘bad al-Juhani, who helped open the door to speculative theology.

Amir al-Anbari was accused of a number of things he shared with al-Maʿarri and with the Sufis, and he never seriously denied them:

1. He did not marry.
2. He did not eat meat.
3. He did not attend Friday prayer.
4. He defended a non-Muslim subject against a Muslim.
5. He said that he was better than the family of Abraham.
6. He boycotted the governor and refused to sit with him.

And that is indeed how Amir lived.

More than that, he would step forward to defend slaves.

The stories people later wove around him say that his wife in paradise would be an oppressed slave girl whom he had tried to rescue from cruel masters, but who died before he could reach her.

Amir was expelled from Basra and sent to the Hijaz, where the caliph Uthman questioned him. He was then exiled to Syria, where Muawiya, the governor, questioned him in turn.

Muawiya ordered that he remain in exile in Syria and forbade him from returning home to Basra.

He eventually died in Palestine and was buried in Jerusalem.

Amir al-Anbari emerged from an early form of heresy. Later writers said that he resembled the *people of heterodox views*—that is, people who had strayed from the straight path of doctrine.

His heresy was tied to vegetarianism, celibacy, defending the oppressed, and boycotting those in power in the manner later associated with the Sufis.

Al-Anbari is therefore a predecessor of al-Maʿarri and an early reference point for the militant social current within Sufism.

Why is the defence of ordinary people so often accompanied by religious rebellion?

Whatever the answer, Amir did pray—but alone, like al-Maʿarri and the Sufis. He fasted beyond Ramadan and went out with the fighters on the Byzantine frontier, but he refused to eat the army’s food and survived instead on wild plants.

The people rewarded him for his hunger, asceticism, and hatred of rulers by giving him miracles of his own.

They said that when he was on the frontier in winter and wanted to perform his ablutions, he would take freezing water in his hands. The moment it touched him, it became warm and began to boil.

When he stood to pray, dangerous creatures were said to fear him and stay away.

He exposed himself to wild animals because he preferred to pray alone, far from other people, avoiding congregational prayer.

The people have no finer reward to give their friends than miracles:

> You have no horses to give, and no wealth.
> Then let words bring joy
> when circumstances cannot.

#### Ibrahim ibn Adham

He came from Balkh, in what is now Afghanistan.

He grew up in a wealthy family and studied literature and religion in Balkh. He later travelled to Baghdad, moved through different parts of Iraq and the Hijaz, and eventually settled in Syria.

He spent a long period on the military frontiers fighting the Byzantines.

While he was stationed there, one of his father’s slaves came to him carrying ten thousand dirhams that Ibrahim had inherited. Ibrahim freed the slave and gave him the entire ten thousand.

He earned his living through harvesting, tending animals, carrying loads, and grinding grain. He was physically strong and capable of extraordinary endurance in milling, harvesting, and heavy labour.

We do not know with certainty how his life ended. The author of *al-A‘lam* considers it most likely that he died on the frontier near the fortress of Sufnin and was buried there.

In Jableh, near Latakia, there is a large mosque attributed to him. I visited it in 1988 and saw an inscription recording its renovation in 994 AH.

In Lebanon there is also a famous fish called **Sultan Ibrahim**.

“Sultan” was a title the people gave Ibrahim ibn Adham. It is used figuratively: he had become a sultan through his spiritual station and dignity, rising above actual sultans.

The people also wrote his life into a popular story called *The Life of Sultan Ibrahim*. It follows the style of folk romances and epics and contains almost nothing from his real history. What it records instead is the people’s impression of him.

Even the fish that bears his name comes from one of the stories woven around him.

As we established in an earlier orbit, Ibrahim was the true founder of Sufism: the first person to live as a Sufi in the senses the tradition would later give the term.

He had been educated in religious and literary culture to such a level that the great scholar Sufyan al-Thawri, whenever Ibrahim attended one of his gatherings, would become cautious about speaking too freely in case Ibrahim caught him in a mistake.

Beyond that, little has come down to us about Ibrahim’s education.

There is little doubt that he had some grounding in speculative theology, which was the main platform for philosophical thought in his age, before philosophy in its Greek logical form had fully flourished.

The sayings attributed to Ibrahim are few, and they revolve around the stations and values later associated with Sufism.

Ibn Kathir reports this saying in *The Beginning and the End*:

> Every ruler who is not just is no better than a thief.

That is democracy in the sense in which the Russian communist Zinoviev used the term, as Adnan Jamus translated it from him.

Ibrahim continued:

> Every scholar who is not morally scrupulous is no better than a wolf.

He means the opportunistic intellectual: grasping, acquisitive, and greedy.

And:

> Everyone who serves anyone other than God is no better than a dog.

By this he means the person who serves the ruler.

To serve God, in their language, is to serve **creation**—God’s creatures.

We have already seen that Ibrahim was not a ritual devotee. His worship was thought itself. Sufyan al-Thawri reports that Ibrahim did not make a display of glorifying God or similar outward practices.

Ibrahim ibn Adham laid down the basic outlines of Sufi conduct:

to live from the work of one’s own hands; to boycott political power; to have no need of paradise; not to marry, or to marry only one woman; not to own slave women or slaves; to dislike military men; and yet to fight the Byzantines.

The Sufis opposed the Byzantines, but they did not oppose religions as such. They called for equality among people despite differences in religion and belief.

We have already discussed this.

Ibrahim’s Sufism was social. He did not enter into the philosophical propositions later explored by the masters of intellectual and metaphysical Sufism.

For him, Sufism was more a **way of living and a position toward the world** than a philosophy.

#### Al-Hallaj

Al-Husayn ibn Mansur, Abu al-Mughith.

Al-Baghdadi calls him Mansur al-Hallaj.

He was originally from the city of al-Bayda in southern Iran and grew up either in Wasit or Tustar—the first in central Iraq, the second in southern Iran.

He travelled through many countries before settling in Baghdad, where he associated with some of the city’s major Sufi figures, including al-Junayd and Abu al-Husayn al-Nuri.

He emerged in the generation after al-Bistami, the great master of Sufi ecstatic utterance.

Al-Bistami had taken the spiritual experience of Sufism to its furthest limit. He laid down the foundations of union, indwelling, and annihilation in the Divine Essence through a complex equation in which the human becomes divine and the divine becomes human.

The atmosphere was still filled with al-Bistami’s ecstatic sayings and enormous spiritual experiment when al-Hallaj appeared with his own ecstatic speech and a new spiritual experience.

But al-Hallaj did not stop where al-Bistami had stopped.

He moved into a much wider arena and tried to establish the **state of Truth**—something Abu Yazid had never attempted.

Al-Dhahabi writes in *al-Ibar*. I quote the passage in full because of its importance. The text comes from British Museum manuscript Or. 3006, folio 124:

> This man travelled through Khurasan and Transoxiana. People in India wrote to him addressing him as “al-Mughith?” and those in the lands of the Turks as “al-Mughith,” because those regions were far from the faith. In nearer lands they wrote to him from Khurasan as “Abu Abd Allah the Ascetic,” and from Khuzistan as “the Shaykh, Carder of Secrets.” His followers in Baghdad called him “al-Mustalam,” and in Basra “al-Mukhbir?”

In al-Maqrizi’s *Lessons for the Thoughtful*, p. 227 of the printed edition, while discussing the Ismaili and Qarmatian mission, we find:

> As for Khurasan, Abu Abd Allah al-Khadim advanced there with the mission. It first appeared in Nishapur... and many people there joined it, including leading men and men of arms... al-Ray through a man known as al-Hallaj, who carded cotton and became known by that name...

The printed text is confused.

What can be taken from it is that al-Hallaj had some connection with the movement, assuming that he is in fact the person meant in this report.

Still, it is difficult to believe that al-Maqrizi would have been ignorant of al-Hallaj and referred to him in such an obscure way.

The meaning of the passage becomes clearer, in any case, when we compare it with Ibn al-Nadim’s statement that al-Hallaj:

> At the beginning of his career called people to rally behind a member of the Family of Muhammad. He was informed on, arrested in the mountain region, and flogged.

(*al-Fihrist*, p. 283)

Ibn al-Nadim’s statement is reinforced by what al-Khwansari reports in *Rawdat al-Jannat*, quoting *Majalis al-Mu’minin*:

> This man originally belonged to the Imami Shi‘a. He called on people to support the Prophet’s Household, announced the coming relief and the appearance of the Master from Taliqan, and turned ordinary people away from following the Abbasids. They therefore accused him of heresy and leaving the religion so that they could kill him under that pretext.

(p. 227 of the Iranian lithograph edition)

Al-Tanukhi, in *Nishwar al-Muhadara*, gives further support to this claim.

He reports, through one of al-Hallaj’s followers among the state secretaries, that al-Hallaj once sent a written instruction to one of his missionaries. The secretary Ali read it and remembered these words:

> The time has come for the radiant state, the resplendent Fatimid state, surrounded by the people of earth and heaven. Permission has been given to the manifest band, despite the power of its weakness, to advance into Khurasan, so that Truth may remove its veil and Justice stretch out its arm.

This belongs to the final stage of al-Hallaj’s activity and therefore corrects Ibn al-Nadim’s claim that such activity belonged only to the beginning of his career.

It is evidence that he had entered the Ismaili-Qarmatian mission.

We cannot know for certain whether his movement operated inside the formal organisation of the mission or whether he conducted an independent campaign within its wider field.

The second possibility seems more likely.

A person such as al-Hallaj would not easily place himself inside a constraining organisation that closed tightly around him.

Al-Hallaj, then, was risking everything in an attempt to establish the communalist state that his Qarmatian companions had created in eastern Arabia and the agricultural lands around Kufa.

He wanted something larger: a state covering the Islamic world.

That is the ambition of a Sufi master unconstrained by the limits that bind ordinary politicians and soldiers.

The reports we have examined suggest that he was directing a broad movement stretching across India, Central Asia, Iran, and Iraq.

His decision to settle in Baghdad makes sense if Baghdad was the centre from which he hoped to launch a revolution capable of reaching everywhere.

The Hanbali population of Baghdad sympathized with his activities.

The Baghdadi Sufi master Muhammad ibn Khafif also supported him, and Ibn Khafif followed the Hanbali school in law.

The Hanbalis combined a popular form of religious belief with a spontaneous social instinct, something found among ordinary Hanbali working people more strongly than among followers of the three other legal schools.

Later, Ibn Aqil was accused of supporting al-Hallaj. Ibn Aqil was one of the leading Hanbalis and was forced into hiding when some jurists of his own school pursued him with accusations of unbelief.

The Hanbalis also demonstrated against Hamid ibn al-Abbas, the minister who oversaw al-Hallaj’s trial and execution.

Hamid was an enemy of both al-Hallaj and the Hanbalis.

His mismanagement of Baghdad’s markets had contributed to high prices and shortages.

He was an ignorant man who could neither read nor write.

Al-Muqtadir had appointed him minister in place of Ali ibn Isa, who was an able administrator. Eventually he had to bring Ibn Isa back into government, giving him the actual management of affairs while leaving Hamid with the title.

One Baghdadi mocked the arrangement:

> Tell Ibn Isa these words—
> Ibn Mujahid himself would approve:
>
> You are the minister.
> Hamid’s beard is all they have made a joke of.
>
> Whenever you have doubts, just ask him:
> How much is one times one?

Al-Muqtadir had appointed Hamid because he needed a policeman capable of suppressing the opposition of the people of Baghdad and the entrenched Turkish military establishment.

No one other than Hamid ibn al-Abbas would have dared take the risk of killing al-Hallaj.

The ordinary people of Baghdad were not unaware of who his killer was.

Ibn Kathir reports in *The Beginning and the End* that the Baghdadi Sufi master Ibn Ata once said to Hamid while being questioned alongside al-Hallaj:

“What business do you have with this? Go back to what you appointed yourself to do: taking people’s money, oppressing them, and killing them. What do you have to do with the words of these masters and saints?”

Hamid ordered that Ibn Ata be struck across the face, that his sandals be pulled off, and that he be beaten over the head with them until blood ran from his nose.

He then ordered him imprisoned.

Hamid’s advisers told him that the public would be disturbed by this and would not accept it, so Ibn Ata was taken to his home instead of prison.

He died seven days later.

He had prayed against Hamid:

“O God, kill him and cut off his hands and feet.”

Not very long afterward, Hamid himself was killed brutally. His hands and feet were cut off and his house was burned.

Ibn Kathir writes:

> The common people believed this had happened because of Ibn Ata’s prayer against him, according to their usual way of telling stories about anyone who harmed someone for whom they had affection.[^ms-v1-015]

Al-Hallaj’s supporters were not limited to ordinary people.

He also had allies among administrators and secretaries of the government bureaus.

Among the Baghdadi Sufi masters who supported him were Muhammad ibn Khafif, Abu al-Abbas ibn Ata, Abu Muhammad al-Hariri, and Ibrahim ibn Muhammad al-Nasrabadi.

Al-Nasrabadi used to say:

> If, after the prophets and the truthful saints, there was ever a true monotheist, it was al-Hallaj.

Abu Bakr al-Shibli said:

> Husayn ibn Mansur and I were one thing. The difference was that he revealed, while I concealed.

And when he saw him crucified, he said:

> Did I not warn you about the worlds?

Al-Junayd opposed him—perhaps from fear, perhaps because of some personal motive.

It cannot seriously have been because of al-Hallaj’s ecstatic utterances. Al-Junayd had defended the ecstatic sayings of Abu Yazid al-Bistami and even written a book interpreting them.

Al-Bistami’s ecstatic speech and al-Hallaj’s came from the same spring.

It has seemed to me that al-Junayd never completely broke with the worldly powers of wealth, state, and religion.

Most of the major Sunni Sufi figures also disowned al-Hallaj.

Yet al-Umawi, in *Life of Hearts*, records a middle position:

> Someone was asked about al-Hallaj and replied: “He was a man in a sound spiritual state, but ecstasy and spiritual experience overwhelmed him until they burst out in speech, and he did not know what he was saying. The words of a drunk man should be folded away, not repeated. The man who was killed was a martyr, and the man who killed him was a fighter in the path of God.”

(2/274)

This is classic Sunni reconciliation.

They had previously said much the same about the battles of the Camel and Siffin: the killer and the killed are both in paradise.

Still, the statement shows some sympathy with al-Hallaj and refuses to declare him an unbeliever.

Abd al-Qadir al-Jilani also spoke about al-Hallaj, though his words are evasive.

As al-Yafi‘i reports in *Qala’id al-Jawahir*, he said:

> A wing grew too long in its claim, so the shears of the religious law were set upon it and clipped it.

He should have said this about Abu Yazid al-Bistami, whose wing was considerably longer than al-Hallaj’s.

Yet whenever he mentions Abu Yazid, he says:

“My brother Abu Yazid.”

They were not brothers by blood.

They were brothers in doctrine.

There is no direct evidence that al-Jilani rejected the ecstatic sayings of this brother.

Elsewhere al-Jilani said:

> Husayn al-Hallaj stumbled, and in his time there was no one to take him by the hand. Had I lived in his time, I would have taken him by the hand.

Al-Tadifi, p. 17

But he does not tell us what the stumble was.

Was it al-Hallaj’s confrontation with the state?

Perhaps.

Maybe al-Jilani saw it as a badly calculated adventure.

He himself stood in a similar position. The ordinary people of Baghdad, as usual, were with him. But he did not risk leading them in an assault on the caliphal palace, for reasons he understood and we do not.

If the “stumble” was al-Hallaj’s ecstatic speech, however, then it was exactly the same stumble made by his brother Abu Yazid.

Al-Jilani also left a bewildering text preserved in an appendix to Massignon’s edition of *al-Tawasin*, taken from a manuscript in the Bibliothèque Nationale in Paris:

> A bird flew forth, taking the form of one of the knowers, from the nest in the tree of his own image. It rose into heaven, piercing the ranks of the angels.
>
> It was a falcon from among the King’s falcons. Its eyes were sewn shut with the thread of: “Man was created weak.”
>
> In heaven it did not find the prey it sought.
>
> Then the quarry “I saw my Lord” appeared to it, and its bewilderment increased before the words of the One it sought: “Wherever you turn, there is the face of God.”
>
> It descended again to the enclosure of the earth, seeking something more precious than fire in the depths of the seas.
>
> It looked with the eye of reason and saw nothing but traces.
>
> It thought, but found in the two worlds nothing it desired except its Beloved.
>
> It rejoiced and said with the tongue of its drunken heart: “I am the Truth.”
>
> It sang a melody unknown among human beings, chirping in the garden of existence with a song that brought about its death.
>
> And in its secret it was called:
>
> “O Hallaj, did you think your strength and power belonged to you?
>
> Now speak on behalf of all the knowers. Let the One suffice for the individuals of the One.
>
> Say: O Muhammad, you are the sultan of Reality. You are the human being at the very eye of existence. At the threshold of the door of your knowledge, the necks of the knowers bow. Within the sanctuary of your majesty the life of all creation is made clear.”

I have not understood what our Shaykh al-Jilani intended by this long address.

And I swear by all the orbits that he did not understand what he meant either.

Peace be upon them all.

Still, it seems to me that he is repeating the story of Ibn Babak, which al-Dhahabi reports in *al-Ibar* and which Ahmad ibn al-Hallaj transmitted:

> I heard Ahmad ibn Babak, my father’s student, say:
>
> “Three days after my father was killed, I saw the Lord of Glory in a dream.
>
> I asked: ‘Lord, what did You do with Husayn ibn Mansur?’
>
> He replied: ‘I disclosed My doctrine to him, but he called creation to himself, so I brought upon him what you saw.’”

That is an accusation in need of witnesses.

Husayn ibn Mansur had no self left for him to call anyone toward.

When he said, “I am the Truth,” he was practising the station of annihilation in the Truth. He was not setting himself up as a claimant and calling people to himself.

This collusion between the Lord of Glory and the disciple is one of the consequences of misunderstanding.

It seems that neither of them had properly studied the stations of the master in question so as to see them directly through mystical witnessing.

Ibn Abi al-Hadid, in his *Commentary on the Nahj al-Balagha*, transmits verses by al-Hallaj complaining of being misunderstood. In them he draws upon an earlier complaint attributed to Ali ibn Abi Talib:

> O Lord, I carry hidden knowledge—
> if I revealed it,
> they would say:
> “You are one of those who worship idols.”
>
> Good and righteous men
> would declare my blood lawful,
> believing the ugliest thing they did
> to be good.

Here the heretics manufacture yet another genealogy for Abu al-Husayn.

If Ali could be made the father of Shi‘ism, the Mu‘tazila, chivalric brotherhoods, Sufism, and political opposition, why should he not also become the father of the heretics?

I later found the same verses attributed in a Shi‘i source to Ali ibn al-Husayn, Zayn al-Abidin.

In truth, the poem belongs to neither of them.

But as a complaint from al-Hallaj, it is a bitter one.

Why was al-Hallaj misunderstood when al-Bistami was not?

Let us return to Ibn al-Nadim:

> He was bold before rulers, committed enormous acts, and sought the overthrow of states.

(p. 283)

That statement simply confirms the evidence already presented in these sources, which in both its broad outline and its detail explains why one form of “heresy” was treated differently from another.

I have argued elsewhere at greater length what I will say here briefly.

**Heresy as abstract thought was not what drove the state to attack thinkers.**

The Islamic state patronized philosophy and speculative theology. It even protected both from the religious mob.

It would naturally have protected Sufism as well—if Sufism had remained abstract thought.

Abu Bakr al-Razi could write about the tricks of prophets in the very same city that sentenced al-Hallaj to death. Instead of executing al-Razi, that city appointed him head of its largest hospitals.

No public religious prosecution was brought against al-Bistami, even though he attacked doctrine at its roots and said things al-Hallaj never said.

Anyone who reads Ibn al-Jawzi’s *The Devil’s Delusion* is astonished that such language could have appeared under the shadow of the Quran and its great empire.

Ibn al-Jawzi was not joking when he wrote it, nor was he some detached author.

But he too was incapable of doing more than writing a book.

Sufism survived him, just as philosophy and speculative theology survived.

After al-Hallaj, no one was executed except those whom the Turks managed to seize within their own sphere of power.

The Sufis had learned from that bloody adventure. They understood that the space available to them would not stretch far enough to accommodate what had destroyed their companion.

The great Baghdadi masters of al-Hallaj’s own generation defended him because Baghdad as a whole was in a state of ferment, ready to descend upon the palaces of the caliphate.

Once the crisis passed and the Abbasids and their Turkish masters regained control, everyone returned to his own private calculations—to his own “rationality.”

That happens after every failed revolution.

The case of al-Hallaj is not primarily a case of ideas or intellectual Sufism.

It is a **social and political event**.

So why this endless insistence that he was killed by the sword of religious law?

Ignorance of the actual circumstances of a historical event—especially within heavily standardised European methods—places a veil between the historian and history. History becomes a register of assumptions rather than a laboratory for disciplined knowledge.

No field has suffered as much aggression, arbitrariness, and bad faith as history.

The reason is that history is the study of social and political movement, and those movements carry warnings of disaster for people who live comfortably inside fixed assumptions: the settled minority who enjoy the privileges of the state, culture, religion, and wealth.

These people have no history.

History belongs to those who seek justice because they need it: the hungry and deprived among creation.

One of the most satisfying things for an intellectual studying a historical event is to explain it through ideas.

Why?

Because then the intellectual can make the event reflect his own privileged position in culture and imagine himself as the oppressed party.

“The state does not repress ordinary people. On the contrary, the state conspires with ordinary people against intellectuals.”

That is what cultural history tells us when it falsifies the true shape of events.

Historical conflict is always, in the final analysis, an expression of class conflict—not between intellectuals and the state, but between **the state and its subjects**.

Intellectuals are followers. They stand at the margins of one side or the other.

Some fight with the state.

A few fight with the people.

In either case they are followers, not the makers of the historical event and not its original victims.

We need to correct this lopsided understanding of historical relations.

The real historical conflict is not between state and intellectual.

It is between **state and subjects**.

Human tragedy does not lie in the equation:

**illiterate person / intellectual**

but in the equations:

**state / subjects**
**oppressor / oppressed**
**owner / deprived**

Nor should science and culture be confused.

Science is neutral.

The state benefits from it, and the people benefit from it. Science has no independent place in history and does not make history. It produces the material conditions of civil life and helps all sides maintain life and struggle.

Both predatory unjust states and just states have supported science.

Culture is different.

Culture stands at the margins.

It may stand with the state against the people, or with the people against the state.

The Eastern state did not wage war on culture.

It waged war on **the intellectual who sided with the people**.

Whenever you find a persecuted intellectual, therefore, look for the cause of that persecution in politics rather than in pure thought.

The real victim is not the intellectual.

It is the people.

#### Proof of Innocence?

Al-Munawi says in *al-Kawakib al-Durriyya* that when al-Hallaj’s blood fell to the ground, it formed itself into the word:

**God. God.**

This was taken as a sign of his belief in divine unity.

Al-Munawi then asked:

Why did the blood of Husayn not write the same thing?

His answer was that Husayn had no need to prove his innocence, while al-Hallaj did.

Al-Munawi was one of al-Hallaj’s defenders and reports that the majority regarded him as a saint.

By “the majority” he means the majority among the Sufis.

The story he reports about al-Hallaj’s blood repeatedly writing “God, God” on the ground was used to defend him and clear him of the charge of heresy.

Such a miracle was needed because he had been executed as a heretic, so his blood wrote the name of God as proof of his faith.

But if one asks why Husayn’s blood did not write the word “God” after it poured onto the soil of Karbala, the answer is that Husayn needed no such acquittal.

His killers had no possible way to accuse him of unbelief.

He was the Prophet’s direct grandson, while the Umayyads themselves were accused of hostility to Islam, having resisted it until the conquest of Mecca and converted only under compulsion.

These were facts people knew in Husayn’s own time and afterward.

There was therefore no need for the people to invent a miracle clearing him of unbelief, as they did for al-Hallaj.

#### Al-Niffari

Muhammad ibn Abd al-Jabbar came from the ancient Sumerian city of Nippur.

Its name was altered in Arabic to **Niffar**, with a short *i* and a doubled *f*.

The change is not far from the original pronunciation: the first vowel was already short, while the original *p* sound became *f*, as Persian *p* often does when adapted into Semitic languages.

The ruins of the city lie near modern Diwaniya in central Iraq.

Al-Niffari was one of the great Sufi masters of the fourth Islamic century.

He developed a unique style of Sufi writing in his *Addresses* and *Stations*.

It relies on inner association, on phrases flowing into one another and circling back upon themselves, somewhat like the form of *band* poetry that flourished in nineteenth-century Iraq.

Hamza Abboud, the editor of *The Addresses* and *The Stations*, gives a brief but revealing analysis of the style, and the reader can consult it there.

In al-Niffari’s writings, symbolic literature and extraordinary verbal craft come together with the deep esoteric content of Sufi culture.

If you surrender yourself to reading them, you move from sobriety into intoxication. The world around you disappears.

You only wake again when the sentence stops circling and you stop with it to catch your breath.

Reading *The Stations* and *The Addresses* in the final part of the night makes this absorption easier than reading them at any other hour.

In fact, they should not be read in daylight.

They do not open themselves to the reader then.

For all the depth of his culture, al-Niffari carried the concerns of ordinary people. For this reason he belongs within the river of social Sufism.

In the following passage from *The Stations*, al-Niffari sets the communalist state into motion.

It makes one wonder whether he was merely thinking—or whether he was preparing for a movement that would seize power and establish the awaited state.

His project remains suspended in the moment of waiting.

It will remain the task of the universal intellectual who carries deep within himself the pulse of Tao, Jesus, and Sufism, and who takes the finished elements of his movement from Karl Marx and his closest successors.

Yes.

The time has come for al-Niffari to announce his appearance.

##### The Awaited Dawn of Communalism

> My time has come.
>
> The hour has come for Me to uncover My face and reveal My glory.
>
> My light will join the horizons and what lies beyond them.
>
> Eyes and hearts will look upon Me.
>
> You will see My enemy love Me,
> because I do not treat him as he treats Me.
>
> You will see My friends rule.
>
> I will raise thrones for them.
>
> They will send out fire and it will not return.
>
> I will restore My ruined houses,
> and they will be adorned with the true adornment.
>
> You will see how My justice destroys everything opposed to it.
>
> I will bring all people together in ease,
> and they will no longer be divided,
> because they will be equal in wealth.
>
> They will no longer be humiliated,
> because they will need no one.
>
> I will bring out My treasure
> and distribute it among them.
>
> What I concealed My news and promise for will be fulfilled.
>
> My rising is near.
>
> I will rise.
>
> The stars will gather around Me.
>
> I will bring the sun and moon together.
>
> I will enter every house.
>
> They will greet Me,
> and I will greet them.

#### Abu al-Ala al-Maʿarri

Ahmad ibn Abd Allah ibn Sulayman al-Tanukhi came from a family of jurists and judges.

He lost his sight at the age of four.

From an early age he showed a strong desire to learn and began frequenting libraries. He composed poetry when he was eleven.

He later immersed himself in language, law, literature, history, and philosophy.

He mastered poetry so completely that he bent it to his will and stood among its greatest figures.

He kept his reputation as a great poet until he reached forty. At that point he increasingly turned toward philosophy.

He did not abandon poetry, however. Instead he put it to work for his philosophy and chose the *Luzumiyyat* as the vehicle for his philosophical views.

He did not follow a strict philosophical method like the professional philosophers.

His literary temperament therefore continued to shape his philosophical treatment of questions, producing contradictions and inconsistencies.

The *Luzumiyyat* is an exhibition of opinions, many of them sharply at odds with one another because of shifts in poetic mood—or because of concealment and accommodation.

I extracted roughly a thousand verses from the *Luzumiyyat* that express what I take to be his philosophical position: his rejection of prophecy and his criticism of the order of nature and society.

I assumed that this body of verse most faithfully expresses his true views, while the rest was written for purposes of caution.

Al-Ma’arri showed personal weakness when confronted directly by his critics. Face to face, he tended to accommodate them.

When alone, however, his thought ranged and flowed freely, restrained by no consideration.

It is fair to say that al-Maʿarri remained a believer, even though his immersion in philosophy revealed to him the simplicity and illogic of ordinary religious consciousness.

But this did not turn him into a pure materialist.

He moved instead within a deep river of nonreligious Eastern spirituality.

That river begins with Laozi, continues through the Taoist philosophers and the Neo-Confucians, passes through individual philosophers belonging to no single school, and appears again in the great masters of Islamic social Sufism.

Experience shows that pure materialism is necessary in the exact sciences.

It is not necessary in philosophy or the social sciences.

Over the long term, in fact, it can become harmful there.

The difference can be seen in the contrast between Stalin’s pure materialism and the Tao-inflected materialism of the Chinese.

After the Bolshevik generation, Soviet communism became a harsh, rigid politics of state power.

Chinese communism before the Cultural Revolution was a social politics that tried to merge with the spirit of the people and with their humanized communal tradition.

Before the Cultural Revolution, Chinese communism possessed a humane heart that Soviet communism lacked after the Bolshevik generation.

The spirituality chosen by Abu al-Ala and the Sufis is not religion.

Abu al-Ala reveals a truth still largely unknown, and ignorance of it continues to unite most intellectuals:

**religion has no necessary relation to spirituality.**

Religion, in his reading, is a sensory, pleasure-seeking, authoritarian tendency.

Spirituality is a nonreligious abstraction.

An atheist or a deist can enter it and rise above petty concerns, elevating the life of the spirit at the expense of excessive bodily appetites.

While ascetics are rare among religious officials, asceticism stands at the very heart of atheist Sufism.

Religious teachings also preach asceticism, but in practice that preaching has failed.

Religious officials did not live by it.

Ordinary people, meanwhile, are ascetics by force of circumstance because they are usually poor and deprived. They have nothing available to renounce.

Asceticism instead became a general way of life among sages and leaders of social revolutions: Jesus, Mazdak, al-Niffari, and the Qarmatians.

It was also the way of modern revolutionaries such as the Chinese communists and the Russian Bolsheviks.

To treat asceticism as essentially a religious value is a grave mistake and dangerous to social awareness.

Perhaps it is one of the intellectuals’ tricks for exempting themselves from practising it.

Al-Ma’arri was a Sufi in conduct, not in doctrine.

His language is far removed from Sufi language.

Indeed, his criticism included the Sufis along with prophets, rulers, and religions.

He was unfair in his criticism of them because he generalized instead of distinguishing.

He failed to separate the great masters from ordinary Sufis.

I have not found him mentioning al-Hallaj at all.

But in conduct he was like them.

He was ascetic and lived on very little.

He boycotted the state.

He opposed religion and its representatives, wealth and its owners.

He remained close to ordinary people, defended their rights, and called for wealth to be placed in common use.

Like al-Bistami and Ibn Adham, he never married.

Unlike them, he adopted vegetarianism. They did not forbid themselves meat, though they ate little of it.

Like them, he leaned toward solitude and contemplation.

He wept in seclusion and prayed in order to connect with the Absolute, not to fulfil religious obligations. For that reason he did not attend Friday prayer.

He differed from them by refusing the pilgrimage, which they performed.

He agreed with them in fasting during Ramadan and outside it, because fasting was one of his spiritual disciplines.

He had a project for establishing a communalist state, but he lacked the courage to pursue it as al-Hallaj had done.

He disagreed with the Qarmatians of Arabia and attacked them because he disliked their policies.

Perhaps this was due to a difference in cultural level. The Arabian Qarmatians were rough Bedouins, while al-Maʿarri was a philosopher.

They went too far in shedding blood, whereas he objected even to shedding the blood of animals.

Yet he expressed no opinion on the Qarmatians of Iraq.

They were educated and urban.

He neither praised nor condemned them, though he might have been expected to celebrate them just as the Taoists celebrated the communalism of the Zhou dynasty.

His failure to mention either them or al-Hallaj is strange.

He agreed with the Sufis on equality between religions and on freedom of belief.

Al-Ma’arri was a nation unto himself.

He cannot be repeated.

But something of the **Ma’arrian spirit** can appear in part within the hearts of sages and universal intellectuals, because his way forms a pattern toward which the cultivated spirit can aspire.

In al-Maʿarri, the authority of the universal intellectual reaches its furthest limit, corresponding to what the Sufis call annihilation.

Al-Ma’arri’s annihilation of self made rulers fear his authority.

With that authority he once saved his town from a military assault that neither an ordinary poet nor an ordinary jurist could have stopped.

#### Abd al-Qadir al-Jilani

He was descended from Hasan ibn Ali.

His name refers to Gilan in northern Iran along the Caspian Sea. He is also known as al-Kilani, with different Persian pronunciations of the initial consonant.

He may have been the last of the great masters of social Sufism.

He was born in Gilan and moved to Baghdad in his early youth.

There he educated himself, entered the Sufi path, and pursued literature extensively until he became skilled both in poetry and public speaking.

He began teaching publicly in Baghdad and remained there until his death in 561 AH.

He wrote many works.

Some collect his sermons; others were composed as books. Among them are *The Opening of the Lord* and *Revelations of the Unseen*.

He studied law within the Hanbali school and became an authority qualified to issue legal opinions.

He therefore combined the roles of jurist and Sufi.

This dual identity affected his intellectual personality, where Sunni orthodoxy and Sufism overlap.

In his social personality, however, he was a Sufi completely, standing firmly within the militant social current of Sufism.

He lived to nearly ninety.[^ms-v1-016]

The great Sufi masters live long because their bodies are not worn out by pleasures like the bodies of the rich, rulers, and poets.

Al-Jilani lived through the caliphates of the period of independence, after Baghdad had succeeded in driving out the Seljuks.

The caliphs of this period—the final Abbasid age—were less tyrannical than their predecessors and exercised less direct domination.

Their real power extended little beyond Baghdad and its immediate surroundings.

They were almost rulers of a city-state.

Al-Jilani’s relations with them shifted between opposition and agitation on the one hand and accommodation with some of them on the other.

Al-Tadifi writes in *Qala’id al-Jawahir* that al-Jilani accepted robes of honour from some caliphs and then distributed them among the poor.

His relations with al-Muqtafi were bad.

This caliph had helped drive out the Seljuks and defeat them. Once he recovered effective authority over the caliphate, however, he began to grow more authoritarian.

Al-Jilani would threaten him indirectly.

During one gathering he addressed a palm tree and said:

“O palm tree, do not act unjustly, or I will cut off your head.”

Al-Jilani’s influence was stronger than the caliph’s because the ordinary people of Baghdad followed him and moved when he gave the word.

The caliph therefore did not dare provoke him.

Al-Jilani’s headquarters were in Bab al-Azaj, one of the southeastern districts of Baghdad.

Today it is called Bab al-Shaykh after him. His tomb and great mosque stand there, and palm trees are still planted in its grounds.

Al-Jilani made the function of Sufism explicit:

**to serve creation—to bring people relief.**

And the first form of relief is providing food to the hungry.

He used to say, as reported in *Qala’id al-Jawahir* and elsewhere:

> I examined every good deed and found nothing better than feeding people.
>
> I wish the whole world were in my hands so that I could feed it to the hungry.

This means turning the wealth of the earth into common property for the hungry.

But al-Jilani found that the world was larger than anything he could possess.

He could not even possess Baghdad in order to distribute it among its people.

The wealth of the world remains far from the possession of the hungry.

When the Russian Bolsheviks tried to turn the world upside down for the sake of the poor, a calamity arose from among them and turned their story into a lie.

The hungry are still waiting.

This is the ordeal of ordinary people and the ordeal of the **abdāl**, the substitute saints.

It is not the ordeal of politicians or intellectuals.

Politicians and intellectuals make the earth revolve around culture.

The abdāl make it revolve around the contradiction between poverty and wealth.

One reason the earth remains in the hands of worldly powers is that the abdāl have not yet moved to take it into their own hands and distribute it to the hungry.

One day—on a day already counted—we will have to take it.

Al-Jilani thought about this constantly.

His thought, combined with his awareness of his own powerlessness, led him to issue a strange and obscure call for wealth to be seized and distributed among the people.

He did not explain how this seizure should take place.

Was he thinking of the method of Urwa ibn al-Ward and the pre-Islamic outlaw-poets?

It is a bewildering call, and it reveals the bewilderment of a man who sees people starving and poor but cannot do anything for them.

Al-Jilani believed that political authority, whatever its form, was the authority of thieves.

A story expresses this view.

The caliph al-Mustanjid once came to visit him carrying ten bags of money, each carried by a servant.

Al-Jilani refused to accept them.

When the caliph persisted, he took one bag in his right hand and another in his left—the two finest bags—and squeezed them.

Blood poured from them.

He said to al-Mustanjid:

“O Abu al-Muzaffar, are you not ashamed before God? You take the blood of the people and then bring it to me as a gift?”

Something like this probably happened.

Popular memory then enlarged the story until the bags themselves bled in his hands.

The point was to confirm what opposition figures had been saying since the caliphate of Uthman:

**rule means theft.**

Al-Jilani also contributed to intellectual Sufism, which is one reason he wrote so much.

His books discuss the purpose of Sufism, its requirements, the qualities of the saint, and what the saint owes other people.

In *Revelations of the Unseen*, article 75, he codifies eight qualities of Sufism:

generosity, patience, the sign, estrangement, wool, wandering, and poverty.

He traces **the sign** to Zechariah, from the Quranic passage:

> He said: “My Lord, give me a sign.”
>
> He said: “Your sign is that you will not speak to people for three nights, though you are sound.”
>
> Then he came out to his people from the sanctuary and signalled to them that they should glorify God morning and evening.

Zechariah’s speech here is the gesture or sign.

Estrangement belongs to John.

The estrangement of John the Baptist is the estrangement of the Sufi whom the ignorant people of state and wealth fail to understand.

The end of John’s estrangement was his murder.

Wandering belongs to Jesus.

Christ wandered in order to preach his message.

The Sufi also moves through journeys, physically or spiritually.

Poverty he assigns to Muhammad.

Stories of prophetic poverty are common among Muslims.

Al-Buhturi describes his own condition in the line:

> Poverty like the poverty of prophets, and estrangement...

In reality, however, no prophet was truly poor except Christ.

Following the path of his fellow Sufis, al-Jilani tried to shape the character of the individual—and first of all the Sufi—through a set of values broadly shared by the tradition.

As we have seen, these include generosity and patience alongside other qualities specific to the Sufi path.

He tells the Sufi to hold himself high before the rich and humble himself before the poor.

Not merely to be modest.

To humble himself.

For the poor, in the eyes of the Sufis, are the masters of the world.

Al-Jilani gave poverty and wealth moral meanings.

Poverty means not making yourself dependent on someone like yourself.

Wealth means becoming independent of someone like yourself.

That is the basis of personal dignity.

Part of dignity is refusing to rely upon anyone.

Reliance upon another person is a form of idolatry.

One should rely on God so as not to need anyone else.

At times, however, al-Jilani goes too far in his preaching.

He belittles earning and the effort to secure one’s livelihood on the grounds that sustenance is guaranteed by God.

This is one effect of Sunni thought on him.

The Sufis themselves broadly agreed on the need for manual work.

A person must work so that he does not become dependent on anyone, beginning with the state and continuing downward.

When promoting this principle, they also emphasise trust in God.

Some of them take that principle too far, as they often take their other teachings to extremes, wanting firm absolute rules that admit no compromise.

But the dominant position among them—whether Sunni or heterodox and deist—is that work is an obligation.

Reliance upon God comes **after** work.

It consoles a person when his efforts do not succeed.

Here fatalism enters the ethical system.

Islamic moral teaching uses it to help people endure the blows of life with patience and resilience.

When someone loses a loved one, he consoles himself with the thought that death is God’s will.

When someone is afflicted by illness, he bears it because it comes from God.

When a person fails in his work, he need not despair because sustenance has been decreed by God.

These are religious devices that can bring people psychological relief.

But within Sufi values a person does **not** patiently endure humiliation and oppression.

He must resist them in kind.

Nor should he stretch out his hand asking anyone for help, because this too is counted as humiliation.

Al-Jilani says:

> No one has ever reached his hand into another man’s food without becoming humbled before him.

This belongs to the ethical inheritance of pre-Islamic Arabia that the Sufis later absorbed.

It is specific to Islamic Sufism and has no equivalent in Chinese Taoism, which inherited a different cultural tradition.

As usual, the Sufis take the principle to extremes.

Some refused even to attend banquets, explaining:

> Waiting for the gravy is humiliation.

Al-Jilani summarizes the general rule of human dignity in *Revelations of the Unseen*:

> Be the servant of the Lord, not the servant of creation.

Servitude to the Lord is the alternative to servitude to creation at the ordinary level of human existence.

But this no longer applies at the level of the supreme Sufi master, where union reaches such a degree that the relation between Lord and servant disappears and the two become one.

Just as the supreme Sufi escapes servitude to another creature, he also escapes servitude to the Creator.

The Sufis freed the spirit from the humiliation of creaturehood, because creaturehood itself implies deprivation.

They also speak of the **upper hand** and the **lower hand**.

The lower hand is not only the hand stretched out to receive from another.

It is also the person whose very being is subjected to the action of another.

These are ideas found specifically among Muslim Sufis, rather than among Taoists or Indian and European mystics.

To regard creaturehood itself as humiliation is an extreme elevation of human dignity.

It turns atheism into a companion of dignity, not merely a philosophical doctrine.

A free personality also requires the disciple to separate from his shaykh once he has become firmly established on the path.

Al-Jilani writes in *Revelations of the Unseen*:

> When the disciple reaches the state of his shaykh, he is separated from the shaykh and cut off from him. The Truth then takes him into His care and cuts him off from creation...
>
> There is no suckling after the age of two.

(p. 32)

Receiving instruction from a shaykh is necessary for the disciple according to the ordinary method of teaching.

The Sufis said:

> Whoever has no shaykh, his shaykh is Satan.

But this applies only during the stage of discipleship.

The Frenchman Renan fell into compounded ignorance when he used this principle as evidence that the “Semitic mind” was constrained.

He had never encountered the complementary teaching that the disciple must separate from his shaykh once his education is complete.

Many Western judgments about Eastern peoples arise from incomplete reading, even if we put questions of motive aside.

Alongside independence of personality, self-respect, and the extreme defence of human dignity comes humility, patience, refusal of arrogance, and refusal to look down upon others.

Consider this teaching from article 78 of *Revelations of the Unseen*.

Al-Jilani took it from al-Ghazali’s *Beginning of Guidance* and expanded it:

> You should never meet anyone without regarding that person as having some superiority over you.
>
> Say to yourself:
>
> “Perhaps this person is better than me in God’s sight and stands at a higher degree.”
>
> If he is younger, say:
>
> “This person has not disobeyed God as much as I have. There can be no doubt that he is better than me.”
>
> If he is older, say:
>
> “This person worshipped God before I did.”
>
> If he is learned, say:
>
> “This person has attained what I have not attained and knows what I do not know.”
>
> If he is ignorant, say:
>
> “This person disobeyed God through ignorance, while I disobeyed Him knowingly.”
>
> If he is an unbeliever, say:
>
> “I do not know. Perhaps he will become Muslim and his life will end well, while perhaps I will fall into unbelief and my own life will end badly.”

In this way, no one possesses any superiority over another that would justify arrogance, humiliation, or repression.

Al-Jilani’s Taoist tendencies have parallels among the Taoists themselves.

Consider this important passage from Zhuangzi:

> Suppose you and I argue.
>
> If you defeat me instead of my defeating you, does that necessarily make me wrong?
>
> If I defeat you instead of your defeating me, does that necessarily make me right?
>
> Is one of us right and the other wrong?
>
> Or are both of us right, or both wrong?
>
> Neither you nor I know.
>
> Nor does anyone else.
>
> Whom should we ask to give the correct judgment?
>
> We could ask someone who agrees with you—but if he already agrees with you, how can he make the judgment?
>
> We could ask someone who agrees with me—but if he already agrees with me, how can he make the judgment?

These teachings from al-Jilani and Zhuangzi are exercises against repression and against the repressive personality.

They run alongside, and complete, the exercises in refusing humiliation and enduring hardship in order to preserve dignity.

The complete human being neither submits to humiliation nor humiliates anyone else.

The complete human being is also the person who owns nothing and is owned by nothing.

Abu Yazid al-Bistami says this.

Abd al-Qadir al-Jilani adds that whoever owns a thing is himself owned by that thing.

Property, then, should not belong to individuals.

It should belong to everyone, so that no one can rise above anyone else.

The Taoist and the Sufi both look toward a society containing no owner and no deprived person, no oppressor and no oppressed person.

Such a society can only be the communal society that takes shape within the conscience of the Eastern sages.

Al-Jilani also moved toward clearing Iblis of guilt, though more cautiously than al-Hallaj.

He writes in *Revelations of the Unseen*:

> I saw the accursed Iblis in a dream and intended to kill him.
>
> He said to me—may God curse him:
>
> “What is my crime if destiny has decreed evil for me and I have no power to turn it into good?”
>
> Then he smiled at me with a timid, frightened smile.
>
> This took place on the night of Sunday, the twelfth of Dhu al-Hijja, in the year 516.

What is this smile in the face of the Imam of Bab al-Shaykh?

Does it not suggest the possibility of collusion?

Abd al-Qadir al-Jilani also wrote beautiful poetry.

Verse gave him room to express things his prose perhaps could not carry, because poetry can come close to intoxication.

One of his finest poems is this one, rhyming in *b*:

> There is no sweet spring of longing
> from which I have not taken the finest share.
>
> There is no special station in union
> where my place is not nearer and more dear.
>
> The days gave me the brightness of their purity;
> their springs became sweet, and sweet my drinking.
>
> Every noble prize now seeks my hand,
> though even the clever lose their way in reaching it.
>
> I am one of men whose companions fear
> neither the treachery of time nor anything terrifying.
>
> A people whose place in every glory is high,
> and whose procession moves with every army.
>
> I am the nightingale of spirits,
> filling their garden with song,
> and in the heights
> a grey falcon.
>
> The armies of love now move beneath my mount,
> obedient to me; whatever I seek does not escape.
>
> I have no hope now, no wish,
> no promised thing for which I wait.
>
> I wandered through the fields of contentment
> until I was given a station that cannot be given.
>
> The suns of those who came before have set,
> but our sun upon the highest sphere
> never sets.

Al-Jilani refined divine love until the armies of love itself became obedient to him.

His place in union was unique, and his station was the nearest and most precious.

His sense of closeness and union was direct, without an intermediary.

There was no strongly human erotic element in him through which earthly love might lead to heavenly love.

Al-Jilani’s ordinary life contained no such excess.

He married, had many children, and left descendants through both sons and daughters.

His emotional intensity remained controlled.

We can see this in the coherence of his speech and the relative rarity of ecstatic utterances.

This came from combining legal scholarship with Sufism.

He spoke out of a sense of strength and of the power to break through obstacles:

> Every noble prize now seeks my hand.

Here the “noble prize” is the difficult position, especially in war.

He belongs among men who do not fear.

They hold a place in every glory, and a procession moves with them in every army.

He has moved beyond pure withdrawal into action.

There can be little doubt that he was thinking of the people of Baghdad around him, ready to move at a signal from him.

They were the army of the Sufi master who did not believe it was enough merely to cut himself off from the ruler.

The ruler had to be reminded that he was present, active, and ready.

So al-Jilani was the **grey falcon**, just as he was the nightingale filling the garden of spirits with song.

The people of Baghdad gave him the title “the Grey Falcon” because of this poem.

This is the power of the Sufi who fears nothing, hopes for nothing, and waits for no promise.

He possesses everything precisely because he has stripped himself of everything.

He roamed the fields of contentment until he reached a station no one else could be given.

The suns of the earlier ones set.

His remained.

I do not think that by “those who came before” he meant anyone other than the prophets.

He believed he had reached a station they themselves had not reached.

The conclusion appears in one of the ecstatic sayings reported by al-Tadifi in *Qala’id al-Jawahir*:

> Assembly of prophets: you were given the title. We were given what you were not given.

Ibn Arabi therefore placed him among the knowers who abolished intermediaries and approached heaven directly.

Al-Jilani has another poem in *Qala’id al-Jawahir* that moves in a different direction.

It speaks of the secret that must not be revealed, because revealing it makes a person’s blood lawful.

But the possessor of the secret cannot always keep it.

The secret does not remain permanently sealed within the chest.

The lover drinks from the wine of reverence, a wine no one tastes except the person who truly loves.

The moment he tastes it, he becomes drunk.

And once drunk, he can no longer remain silent:

> When we came to Midian’s water to draw,
> thirsting for the spring of whispered communion,
>
> a fire appeared to us in the distance, burning,
> and there we found the one we love and desire.
>
> He gave us drink, greeted us, brought us to life;
> our souls came alive,
> and we were made drunk
> on the wine of His majesty.
>
> A wine bound by a covenant:
> none shall taste it
> except the lover sincere in love,
> emptied of every claim.
>
> We mixed it with reverence
> so our souls might grow strong.
>
> Who has ever seen a wine
> mixed with reverence?
>
> We drank.
>
> We spoke out.
>
> And so our blood was made lawful.
>
> Is blood really made lawful
> merely because someone revealed
> the secret of the one he loves?
>
> Among free people
> the secret is a trust.
>
> But once the wine grows fine and flows—
> who still has strength enough to keep silent?

What secret is this, whose disclosure gets a person killed?

It can only be the secret revealed by al-Hallaj and al-Bistami.

Al-Hallaj was killed for revealing it.

Al-Bistami survived.

Al-Jilani remained suspended between the two.

At one moment he blames al-Hallaj for speaking openly.

At another he repeats al-Bistami.
