And between them he sometimes reveals and sometimes conceals.

We see him advance toward the caliphal palace with the people of Baghdad behind him, then turn back without storming it.

To justify his hesitation, he blames al-Hallaj.

We see him become one with the secret, abolish intermediaries, leave this world and the next to ordinary believers, and hurry forward until the prophets themselves are left behind him.

Then suddenly he returns to a tightly disciplined law, firmly governed by its principles.

Ask him about Abu Yazid, who rejected legalism and collapsed the distinction between Creator and creature, and he will answer:

“He is my brother. The only thing in which I surpass him is that I married and he did not, and I possessed things while he possessed nothing.”

He would hardly have claimed such a superiority—which is no superiority at all—except that he wanted to save a Baghdadi family from breaking apart.

The head of that family had sworn an oath of divorce that al-Jilani was superior to al-Bistami.

“My horse is saddled, my spear is set, my sword is drawn, and my bow is strung.”

That was how he proclaimed himself in Baghdad during a brief moment of mystical intoxication.

There were two caliphs in Baghdad. One was the Abbasid caliph; the other was Abd al-Qadir. Abd al-Qadir was the caliph of the **people** of Baghdad. The Abbasid was merely the caliph of Baghdad.

But Abd al-Qadir did not arrive at that position overnight. He told the story of his life, and of how he reached it, in a tangled sequence of Sufi recollections:

> “At the beginning I endured every kind of terror. There was no ordeal I did not go through.
>
> “I pretended to be mute and mad, and I was taken to the bimaristan, the hospital for the insane. I wandered aimlessly, whether I was in the desert or among people.
>
> “The ordeals became so severe that people thought I had died. They brought a shroud and laid me out on the washing slab. Then the distress lifted from me, and I got up.
>
> “After that I decided to leave Baghdad because of all the turmoil there. I went out as far as Bab al-Halba. A voice said to me, ‘Where are you going?’ Then I was struck so hard that I fell to the ground.
>
> “The voice said, ‘Go back. People will benefit from you.’
>
> “I said, ‘I want my religion to remain safe.’”
>
> By “my religion” he means his conduct, not his creed: a sound way of living.
>
> “The voice said, ‘You shall have that.’
>
> “I never saw who spoke.
>
> “Later the ordeals came over me again, and I longed for someone who could explain them to me. One day I passed through al-Qantara, a district in northern Baghdad. A man opened the door of his house and said:
>
> “‘Abd al-Qadir, what was it you asked for yesterday?’
>
> “I had forgotten, so I said nothing. He became angry and slammed the door in my face.
>
> “I walked on. Then I remembered. I went back looking for the door, but I could not find it.
>
> “It had disappeared.
>
> “The man was Hammad al-Dabbas. I came to know him later, and he explained everything that had been troubling me.
>
> “Whenever I had been away from him seeking knowledge and then returned, he would say:
>
> “‘What brings you to us? You are a jurist. Go to the jurists.’
>
> “I would say nothing.
>
> “One Friday I went out with the group in bitter cold. He shoved me and threw me into the water.
>
> “I said:
>
> “‘The Friday bath, in God’s name.’
>
> “I was wearing a woollen cloak, and in my sleeves were sections of the Quran. I held my sleeves up so that the pages would not be ruined.
>
> “They left me there and walked away.
>
> “I wrung out the cloak and followed them. I suffered terribly from the cold.
>
> “The shaykh used to hurt me and strike me. When I came to him he would say:
>
> “‘We had plenty of bread today, and sweet pudding too. We ate it all and saved you nothing. Bad luck.’
>
> “Seeing how he treated me, his companions began taking liberties with me too.
>
> “They would say:
>
> “‘You are a jurist. What are you doing with us?’
>
> “When he saw them mistreating me, he became protective of me and said:
>
> “‘You dogs! Why are you hurting him? By God, not one of you is like him. I only hurt him to test him, and I find him a mountain that does not move.’
>
> “Some time later a man from Hamadan arrived. His name was Yusuf al-Hamadhani, and people said that he was the qutb, the spiritual pole of his age.
>
> “He stayed in a Sufi lodge. I went to see him.
>
> “He sat me down, described everything that had happened to me, and cleared up what I still did not understand.
>
> “Then he said:
>
> “‘Speak to the people.’
>
> “I said:
>
> “‘My master, I am a rough foreigner, practically tongue-tied. How can I speak before the eloquent people of Baghdad?’
>
> “He said:
>
> “‘You have learned jurisprudence and its principles, the disputes among the schools, grammar, language, and Quranic interpretation. And you think you are unfit to speak?
>
> “‘Climb onto the chair and speak. I see a root in you that will one day become a palm tree.’
>
> “Commands and prohibitions came to me both asleep and awake.”
>
> Prophecy.
>
> “Words would overwhelm me. They crowded into my heart, and if I did not speak them I felt as though I would choke. I could not remain silent.
>
> “At first two or three men would sit with me. Then word spread. The crowds grew until around seventy thousand people were attending my gatherings.
>
> “I examined every kind of good deed and found nothing better than feeding people.
>
> “I wish the whole world were in my hands so that I could feed it to the hungry.
>
> “My hand has a hole in it. It cannot hold on to anything. If a thousand dinars came to me, I would not let them spend the night in my possession.
>
> “I wish I could live again in the deserts and open country as I did at the beginning, seeing no one and seen by no one.
>
> “Burdens descend on me that would shatter mountains if they were laid upon them. I lie down on the ground and say:
>
> “‘With hardship comes ease.’
>
> “Then I lift my head, and the weight has gone.”

One of his companions said:

> “I used to visit the shaykh in the depths of winter, in the bitterest cold. He would be wearing a single shirt. Someone would be standing beside him fanning him, while sweat poured from his body.”

The power of spirit, joined to the power of resolve, can make sweat and blood erupt.

With that same force he stood against fate itself.

Ibn Taymiyya quotes him in *Servitude*:

> “Many men, when they reach the question of divine decree and destiny, fall silent.
>
> “I do not.
>
> “A window was opened for me onto this matter, and I struggled against the decrees of the Real by means of the Real, for the sake of the Real.
>
> “A true man is one who struggles against destiny, not one who simply submits to it.”

This is an on-the-ground reading of fate and divine decree.

There is no fatalism here. There is a will that challenges prohibitions and barriers and breaks through walls.

This is the Sufi power to break through.

#### Shao Yong

Shao Yong was one of the philosophers of the Taoist tradition that modern writers call Neo-Confucianism. He emerged in the first half of the eleventh century, during the Northern Song dynasty.

He concerned himself with metaphysical questions[^ms-v1-017] and developed a theory of the origin of existence and its transformations.

He believed that the substance of the world is one, and that the world has a single living spirit. Birth and death, beginnings and endings, are simply transformations of the world’s substance and its vital force.

He also sensed the dialectical interplay within existence. The limited, he said, comes from the unlimited, and the finite from the infinite.

A plant produces a seed, and the seed produces another plant. The second plant is not the first plant, yet its vital spirit is the same.

By this he meant the power of life that produces both plants. Their changing matter differs, but the life within them is one.

He believed that the vital spirit of heaven comes from the sun, while the vital spirit of a human being comes from the heart when awake and from the reproductive organs during sleep.

Freud would have been delighted if he had known about that.

But Shao Yong did not preach the sexualisation of everything. He did not reduce every human movement and every moment of stillness to sex.

His philosophical rationalism led him to reject the myths and beliefs about heaven that had circulated among the ancient Chinese. He said:

> Heaven has never spoken.
>
> It does not live in the blue vault above us.
>
> It is neither high nor far away.
>
> Those are images human beings create in their own hearts.

He therefore gave heaven a physical interpretation, treating what we see as the reflection of the oceans into space.

A similar thought appears in Ibn Rushd’s *Decisive Treatise*:

> “Whoever says that God is in heaven is not among the people of demonstration.”

This follows the doctrine that denies direction: God cannot be said to occupy one direction rather than another. In other words, God cannot be located in space. This also means rejecting a literal throne, a literal sitting upon it, and every attempt to give God a physical location.

In his philosophical life, Shao Yong fulfilled the conditions of both Taoism and social Sufism.

He lived apart from emperors. He chose poverty over wealth.

And in a philosophical poem called **The Song of Happiness**, he set out the conduct expected of a truly universal intellectual.

Here is the poem in full:

> No one knows the name
> of the Master of Happiness.
>
> For thirty years
> he has lived beside the Luo River.
>
> His feelings are those
> of wind and moon.
>
> His spirit rests
> on river and lake.
>
> He sees no great difference
> between high office and low,
>
> between poverty and wealth.
>
> He does not run after things
> or lose himself among them.
>
> He has no chains
> and no prohibitions.
>
> He is poor
> but does not regret it.
>
> He drinks
> but does not get drunk.
>
> He gathers the springtime
> of the whole world into his mind.
>
> He has a small pond
> beside which he reads poetry,
>
> a little window
> beneath which he sleeps,
>
> a little carriage
> in which he amuses himself,
>
> and a mighty brush
> with which he carries out his will.
>
> Sometimes he wears
> a sun hat.
>
> Sometimes
> a sleeveless shirt.
>
> Sometimes he walks
> along the riverbank.
>
> He delights in seeing good people
> and hearing their stories
> of good conduct.
>
> His pleasure lies
> in carrying out a good intention.
>
> He does not praise
> the masters of spirits,
>
> nor flatter
> the practitioners of magic.
>
> Though he hardly leaves his home,
>
> he is alone
> with heaven and earth.
>
> No great army
> can frighten him.
>
> No great salary
> can tempt him.
>
> And so he has been
> a happy man
>
> for sixty-five years.

He wrote this poem a year before he died.

It gives a remarkably precise account of Sufi conduct: poverty; separation from the state and the power of money; Taoist forgetting and Sufi annihilation; freedom from fear; refusal of temptation; love of people; serving them through good intentions and learning from them; speaking the truth and carrying one’s will into action; solitude combined with union with humanity.

Shao Yong differs slightly from strict Sufi renunciation in allowing himself two modest pleasures.

He owned a small carriage, which he drove around his retreat for the pleasure of wandering among riverbanks, forests, and gardens.

And he had a small pond beside which he read poetry.

Zhuangzi or Ibrahim ibn Adham might not have approved of such luxuries.

Maruf al-Karkhi and Abd al-Qadir al-Kilani probably would have.

Even so, Shao Yong was not far removed from the Sufis in general. Many of them considered travel necessary to knowledge.

One of them used to say when spring arrived:

> “Sufis, the earth is beautiful now. Go wandering.”

The great spiritual poles rarely wandered without a purpose. They might travel, for example, to visit another qutb simply to see him.

For them, seeing someone could complete nearness.

Sufis were not all the same. There were ordinary Sufis and there were people of the rank of the qutb.

Shao Yong’s possession of a small pond and a little carriage might therefore remain open to debate.

But the people of his town had no such doubts.

They knew his relationship with them. They knew that he preferred them to the emperor. They trusted his piety, his integrity, and the seriousness with which he pursued the life of wisdom.

Shao Yong became, in effect, an inner moral restraint upon the people of the town.

They would warn one another not to commit wrongs because the sage Shao would hear of them and become angry.

What king, emperor, or sultan ever achieved an authority like that?

##### A Gleam

Shao Yong’s line, “He drinks but does not get drunk,” establishes an important meaning of drinking among the sages.

The same idea appears in the words of Christ, in the position of first-century Muslim jurists who permitted drinking wine, and in Abu Hanifa’s view that a drinker should not be punished unless he actually became drunk.

Drunkenness is not the same as exhilaration. Drunkenness means losing control of the rational faculties.

Our Sufis spoke of divine wine.

It is possible that some of them drank ordinary wine to help themselves enter contemplation.

But we have no direct evidence that they did.

#### Goethe

Johann von Goethe was a German who lived between 1749 and 1832.

He was first and foremost a poet. But he went beyond poetry into wisdom.

He was therefore a poet-sage, or a sage-poet.

He possessed the heightened sensitivity of any great poet—al-Mutanabbi, Li Bai, Shelley—but also the personality of a sage such as Laozi, Epicurus, or Ibn Arabi.

His poetic sensitivity gave him an instinct for love that overwhelmed him, much as love overwhelmed Ibn al-Farid.

He lived his life between two women: his wife and his beloved.

With his wife he had a loving marriage undisturbed by anything else. With his beloved he had a spiritualised, almost Sufi love.

Yet the anguish of the lover burned in him as it did in Ibn al-Farid.

His language about Charlotte—Meryana—in *The Sorrows of Werther* is the language of Ibn al-Farid in his poems built around the rhymes jīm, mīm, and yāʾ.

But Goethe’s love was universal.

It overflowed beyond the beloved until it embraced all people.

His love crossed the borders of Germany into Europe, and then crossed Europe itself toward the hated East, toward the even more hated Islam, and toward the Arabs, who aroused still greater disgust and hostility in Europe.

Goethe loved the Arabs in pre-Islamic poetry and elsewhere.

And he loved Islam for its call to good works and justice among people.

All the medieval and modern prejudices surrounding Arabs and Islam were transformed in him into love.

That love was so deep and so sincere that the people of his own continent could barely tolerate it. They obscured it and tried to cut it away from his legacy, so that it emerged only gradually, in fragments, and with great hesitation.

The wonders of humanity never end.

And what could be more astonishing than the appearance of such a universal intellectual in a society washed through with racialism and savage tribal feeling?

This was a country that produced some of the greatest philosophers of the modern age. Yet, with one exception—and he was an outsider among them—they failed to escape the complexes of racial pride and chauvinism.

They produced a philosophy of purity for the European and murderous coldness toward the rest of humanity.

In Goethe the sage, we see universal truth becoming spiritual.

It appears as attraction among things and as a bond of love among human beings.

Physicists weighed heavily on his heart.

He rejected the natural sciences when he saw them turning the world into numbers, while he wanted the world to be filled with spirit.

He developed a theory of nature and existence based on calculations of beauty, treating beauty as a great relationship among existing things.

It was a poet’s attempt to breathe spirit back into matter.

Physicists studied the theory, found it charming, and published it. Germany’s leading physicist at the beginning of this century included it alongside his own book, *The Philosophy of Physics*.

Goethe’s theory was never going to compete with physics.

Its purpose was to correct the one-sidedness of pure materialism.

Physics deals with pure matter.

It has to.

Nature, in itself, presents material facts. Without treating them materially, physics could do nothing with them.

This physical way of dealing with matter is what allowed science to rise to its present heights, and industry to acquire the enormous power that moved humanity from one condition into another.

If the people of the first condition had known what the second would become, they would have died of envy.

But while pure science and pure matter brought humanity to this extraordinary point, they also left human beings trapped inside a cage of material relationships.

Material relationships belong between **things**, not between **people**.

Between people, relationships must be spiritual.

Just as the natural world advances by understanding material relations, human beings must remain independent of them.

And just as spiritual relationships fail when we try to use them to govern nature, material relationships fail when we try to use them to govern human beings.

The numerical minds of scientists must not rule people.

Human beings need to be governed by human hearts.

Economists, using the same numerical minds, have helped increase the wealth of society.

But they have done almost nothing for human beings themselves.

The condition of humanity remains much as it was in any earlier age.

Hunger in the age of aircraft, computers, and space travel is the same hunger people knew in the age of donkeys, camels, and mules.

Indeed, people in those earlier ages often found solutions to hunger and poverty that the people of our own age have failed to find.

The amount of injustice that has accompanied scientific physics and scientific economics is greater than its equivalent in any previous age.

This is where Goethe’s rebellion against physics comes from.

Goethe is one of the abdal the earth needs. Without people like them, the earth would tilt and those living upon it would stagger like drunkards.

Goethe’s theory of nature is not an alternative to physics.

It is the remedy sages seek for the distortions produced by pure materialism.

Goethe wanted spiritual relationships—the relationships of love, beauty, and goodness—to have a place in nature.

No doubt he understood that nature itself does not understand such relationships.

They belong to human beings.

His physical theory is therefore really a social theory: an attempt to humanise science, so that science serves people instead of punishing them for possessing a humanity that cannot be confined within pure matter.

Goethe is one of God’s treasured ones.

God’s treasured ones possess human hearts capable of balancing the hardness and dryness of numerical minds without preventing those minds from doing their work.

Leave the physicist in his laboratory.

Let him explain and analyse.

Let him give the manufacturer what is needed to improve the material conditions of human life.

But after that, the final word belongs to God’s treasured ones: the friends of humanity.

That was what Goethe wanted.

That was how he spoke.

And this explains why his fellow German Karl Marx loved him so much, even though Marx was a philosopher of class and Goethe a sage of humanity.

According to pure materialism, Goethe would have to be dismissed as a petty-bourgeois idealist and a collaborator.

But Marx was never a pure materialist.

Accounts of Goethe’s life describe a relationship with the poor that has a strongly Taoist-Sufi character.

Klinger says that Goethe was loved by everyone and that he was a blessing upon his town, Weimar.

Merck says that Goethe was everything to everyone, that he directed everything, and that people were pleased with him because he benefited the majority and harmed no one.

Lenz says that Goethe helped many poor families in Weimar and was a man with a living conscience.

Wendt says that people saw Goethe as a good-hearted, well-intentioned man who enjoyed everyone’s respect and affection.

N. Voss says:

> “Yesterday a woman told me that Goethe is a blessing upon Weimar. He sets everything in motion and does good to everyone in need.”

—Katharina Mommsen, *Goethe and the Arab World*, trans. Adnan Abbas Ali, p. 384.

This is exactly the Sufi path as Abd al-Qadir al-Jili defined it:

**to bring ease to people.**

Goethe did, however, depart from the Sufi principle of keeping away from the state, since he enjoyed the patronage of the ruler of Weimar.

There can be little doubt that he used this relationship to help the people of the town rather than to enrich himself.

Sufis differed over whether a relationship with political power could be justified in order to serve people.

Most of them did not approve.

#### Karl Marx

Karl Marx came from a prosperous Jewish family that converted to Christianity.

He lived in Germany between 1813 and 1883 and died at the age of seventy.

This is around the average lifespan of God’s treasured ones, who tend to live longer than rulers and the rich.

In Islamic history, the average lifespan of rulers and wealthy men is around fifty years. It is not much higher in Europe or China.

There is little point introducing Karl Marx.

He was a philosopher, economist, politician, and social activist.

What his historians and followers have generally failed to say is that he was also a **sage-philosopher**.

Philosophy and wisdom are not the same thing.

They can come together in one person and separate in another.

One person can be a philosopher without being a sage.

Another can be a sage without being a philosopher.

Most European philosophers since the age of the Greeks belong to the first type.

They have been philosophers of nature, logic, metaphysics, politics, and ethics.

Their philosophy is strict and logical.

It reflects the mind of the merchant: abstract, calculating, numerical.

They expanded our knowledge of nature, prepared the way for humanity to dominate it, and widened the horizons of reason until the mind succeeded in possessing the universe.

Yet during the Greek and Roman periods, they did not help develop technology in proportion to this expansion of the mind.

The more advanced technologies were found in Chinese civilisation, which invented paper, printing, the compass, and gunpowder, and developed steelmaking, shipbuilding, and navigation.

Wisdom living beside technology?

The sage-philosopher is, broadly speaking, characteristic of the philosophers of China.

They combined the study of nature and the cosmos with social struggle.

Few Chinese philosophers devoted themselves entirely to abstract questions of nature.

They developed dialectical logic and did not know formal logic.

At the same time, they went deeply into natural philosophy, cosmology, and metaphysics.

They would have needed the Greek experience to advance further in the strict calculations of pure logic.

Yet there is an irony here.

As we have just said, China advanced much further than Greece and Rome in industry and technology.

Modern industrial civilisation grew from Chinese inventions, not from Greek or Roman ones.

The Greeks and Romans contributed little along that road.

The intermediaries were the Muslims, who developed Chinese inventions—with the exception of printing—and passed them on to Europe in usable form.

A sage does not necessarily have to be a philosopher.

That was true of the great masters of our own social Sufism.

Ibrahim ibn Adham, al-Bistami, al-Hallaj, al-Jili, and others like them were sages rather than philosophers.

The sage-philosophers among us were people such as al-Maʿarri, Ibn Arabi, and Ibn Sabʿin.

There were fewer of them than there were sages proper.

Nor does the title of sage-philosopher fit the group usually called “the philosophers of Islam,” from al-Kindi to al-Shirazi.

They were philosophers, nothing more.

Al-Razi is the exception.

He possessed a form of wisdom bound up with the spirituality that brought him close both to the Absolute, which he addressed directly, and to ordinary people, whom he defended and served through his medicine.

Karl Marx was a philosopher in the strict sense of modern European philosophy.

His philosophy had two branches.

The first was dialectical materialism, which is a philosophy of nature and metaphysics.

The second was historical materialism, which is a philosophy of history.

Dialectical materialism is pure materialism because, as we have said, the facts of nature are purely material.

Nature and existence as such are matter.

Dialectical materialism has no necessary connection with Marx’s communist programme.

Joining the two together is artificial.

Communism, as we have said many times, is a social position that can be grafted onto any philosophy or creed.

A person can be a dialectical materialist without being a communist.

The reverse is also possible.

Historical materialism, however, is a philosophy of the human being.

A human being is a mixture of material and spiritual relationships and cannot be reduced entirely to matter.

In Marx’s writings we do not find detailed expositions of historical materialism beyond its great root: economic labour as a force driving history.

But Marx did not make the economic factor the sole force moving history.

That is not part of his philosophy.

It belongs to his **method**.

And Marx’s method is different from his philosophy.

Here his thinking and judgment are governed by a broad reason capable of taking in more than one single factor.

A genius can use reason to go beyond himself and beyond his own fixed logical assumptions.

Reason has repeatedly widened the horizons of thinkers and carried them beyond the boundaries of schools and doctrines toward a universality broad enough to contain many different methods, ideas, and positions.

When you read Karl Marx, you are reading a writer who does not ignore any phenomenon that has real influence upon the event he is studying, whether economic, social, material, psychological, or spiritual.

If Marx had known the theories of Ibn Khaldun, Freud, Toynbee, or the neo-Darwinians, he would have used them.

For Marx, Marxism was a **method of methods**.

It was not a narrow, rigid method like those of many of his Western colleagues, whose methods repeatedly harden into creeds.

Freudianism ceased to be merely a philosophy and became a creed.

The same happened to structuralism.

Toynbee likewise never moved beyond the assumptions he placed at the root of history, neglecting theories and explanations that did not fit them.

All serious methods—not frivolous theological ones—contain some truth, as our masters say.

But none contains the whole truth.

Perhaps Marx alone consistently went beyond his own theory whenever he wrote and thought, under the influence of his universal reason.

Marxists are not like Marx.

Karl Marx’s understanding of nature was therefore materialist, precise, and strict.

His understanding of history was not.

He did not see the human being as an animal, but as a material and spiritual being.

Yet human beings had been stripped of their material needs by the power of the state and the power of money.

Marx therefore concentrated first on those needs.

This is what he shares with all of God’s treasured ones: concern with people who have been deprived of material necessities, and struggle to satisfy those necessities.

For that reason, such sages do not give first place to the spiritual needs of ordinary people.

They fear that doing so will confuse the proper order of human needs.

Religious authorities do exactly the opposite.

They neglect people’s material needs and concentrate on spiritual ones.

Society is then divided between poor people who are told to worship, and an elite who are paid for providing religious and spiritual services.

Meanwhile the state and the rich are left to enjoy material abundance in obedience, supposedly, to the judgment of God:

**asceticism for ordinary people, luxury for the elite.**

The spiritual teachings we find among Sufis and Taoists point in the opposite direction.

They are addressed to the elite, not to ordinary people—to creation as a whole—because ordinary people are already busy struggling to satisfy their material needs.

Our concern is to bring ease to people.

To feed the hungry.

Asceticism is required of those who are capable of luxury, not of those already deprived of it.

God’s treasured ones are all people who could have enjoyed comfort.

Ordinary people often cannot.

Asceticism is therefore required of the first, not the second.

It is also the first group who need spiritual discipline most, precisely because their material needs are already satisfied or can easily be satisfied.

This is where Karl Marx’s wisdom lies:

**working to bring ease to people.**

In his own time, these people were the workers of Europe.

They were, and remain, among the largest groups and classes in society, making up the great majority.

When Marx speaks about the working class, he is therefore speaking about the mass of the people, not some small numerical category.

A class can be large or small.

The large class is usually the deprived one.

Under feudalism the peasants were the mass of the people, while the feudal lords were a minority.

In our own phase, capitalists are a minority and workers a majority in Europe.

Among us, however, peasants remain the mass of the people because capitalism has not yet fully entered our societies.

This has produced confusion in our descriptions of “the people” and “the working masses.”

We must return to reality itself when we describe and classify them.

We must not simply copy and translate categories.

Such categories are particular when they arise from concrete reality.

They are not universal simply because they have become theoretical or conceptual terms.

Karl Marx’s writing about the condition of workers in his own time comes from a Christlike heart, not from a numerical mind.

Some of it produces both pain and anger:

pain for the workers,

anger at the capitalists.

In such passages Marx writes as a sage, almost as a Sufi, beyond the world of numbers.

His enthusiasm for the Paris Commune, which was not Marxist, confirms this Sufi-like wisdom: a wisdom grounded in feeling, not reason alone.

The same current runs through the preparatory writings for *Capital* published under the title *Grundrisse*.

They drift toward the future of the human dream, looking toward a world ruled by justice, prosperity, and freedom.

When I read them in Cyprus in 1991, I felt that Karl Marx was walking along the straight road of the abdal—a road that remains straight even when it never quite arrives.

When I finished the book, I wrote inside it these lines by al-Maʿarri:

> How many preachers have warned us,
> how many prophets risen on earth.
>
> Then they departed
> and suffering remained,
>
> while our sickness
> remained exhaustion itself.

It was a passing dose of despair.

The density of the missiles falling on Baghdad at the time made it stronger.

Marx lived a moderate personal life, neither austere nor luxurious.

He had little money and depended for much of his livelihood on his friend Friedrich Engels.

His standard of living was closer to Shao Yong than to Zhuangzi.

He remained untouched by the luxury of the rich and the powerful without deliberately depriving himself.

He did not withdraw from society because he had a political project.

Seclusion belongs to passive opposition: to the sage who sets down teachings for life without entering directly into political struggle.

That is why al-Hallaj did not live in seclusion either.

Marx was emotionally sensitive, like the other sages.

This can be seen in the intensity of his love for his wife, Jenny.

His heart burned with love for her.

Perhaps their frequent periods of separation, caused by his many obligations, intensified it.

Separation feeds love.

He had no bodily or mystical relationship with another woman, unlike Goethe, who combined marital love with spiritual love.

Perhaps Marx’s strict philosophical formation kept him away from the additional beloveds of the poet-sage.

His heart, in every other respect, was full of feeling and burdened by the suffering of the destitute.

He did not weep like the people of Tao and Sufism.

But neither was he one of the cheerful.

Weeping is an Eastern profession.

Despite his distant Eastern ancestry, Marx carried many elements of Western formation simply because he was born and lived in the West.

Yet these elements never surrendered him to the Western numerical mind.

His philosophy remained mixed with wisdom.

His scientific mind remained in dialogue with a heart full of feeling.

A sage’s heart.

Karl Marx stands apart from the other abdal because his communalist project was complete at the intellectual, economic, and political levels.

He launched the great movement that gathered millions of hungry people across continents.

For a hundred years it established states with socialist economies and, in some countries, forced demands upon the state and its ruling classes that radically improved the lives of working people.

After the failure of the states that adopted his doctrine, stories began to circulate about the failure of Karl Marx’s project.

But the movement he launched has not come to a final end.

It may never recover its old form in Europe or North America.

Its prospects for revival may also be limited in sub-Saharan Africa, with its weak civilisational inheritance.

But the movement has a broad future in Asia and Latin America.

In Asia it joins with the continent’s own communal heritage, as happened in the experiences of China and North Vietnam.

Karl Marx’s arrival on that vast continent was no surprise.

He came at the head of a long line of communalist sages who had filled Asian history with opposition to feudalism, trade, and private ownership.

When the Chinese communists achieved their distinctive synthesis between Marx’s doctrine and China’s communalist wisdom, they performed miracles.

The communists of North Vietnam, led by Ho Chi Minh, did the same.

They are not responsible for the failures that may be traced to the political system they took over from the Soviets.

After the Bolsheviks, the Soviets lost the road.

They created a political system that contradicted their economic system.

The political system crippled the economic one and made it unable to bring ease to the people.

So the people rejected it and drifted away.

But they did not turn away from the economic system.

They turned away from the authority that administered it.

The sages, especially the Sufis, do not accept any separation between human freedom and feeding the hungry.

A state that assumes responsibility for feeding people must also guarantee their freedom and dignity.

The political system borrowed by the Chinese and Vietnamese, and later by the Cubans, from the post-Bolshevik Soviet Union did not guarantee the citizen’s freedom or dignity.

It made the citizen subordinate to a state that took on the character of an alien power and separated itself from the people.

Yet the sages, ending with Karl Marx, never arrived at a form of government capable of matching their communal economy.

This was the compounded weakness in communalist wisdom.

The alien powers of wealth and those who stand beside them exploited this weakness to declare communism an absolute failure.

They then began searching for an alternative in Western democracy.

But these people seek comfort for themselves.

The comfort of the people does not concern them.

The writer of these lines, who graduated from the schools of Tao and mystical knowledge, has no alternative to propose either to the Soviet system or to Western democracy.

But he does support the project of communal organisation in society.

And he struggles alongside his companions to impose it upon the state, whatever colour that state may have.

This does not mean that he believes it can be imposed upon just any state.

The savage states that rule our Arab countries resist every communalist movement, even one that raises the slogan of giving Caesar what belongs to Caesar.

The solution is to support the socialists in reaching power, and then force them to accept communal organisation.

The socialists in the form currently familiar to us are led and controlled by effendis: intellectuals and petty-bourgeois politicians who learned socialism through translation.

They therefore turned their backs on grassroots communism—the communalism of ordinary people.

Yet communalists have more room to struggle under a state run by distorted socialists than they do under a state ruled by the most extreme capitalists.

They must therefore fight capitalism alongside these distorted socialists.

After that, they must impose themselves upon reality by awakening the communal instinct of the people, so that the people stand against the distorted socialists and rescue the project of communal organisation for themselves.

I believe the German sage and his predecessors among the sages of Tao and Islam would agree with me on this difficult equation.

I could persuade them if I told them that the instincts of aggression and evil do not exist only among feudal lords, merchants, and capitalists.

They also exist among the intermediate groups who own little or nothing.

They even exist among people who embrace socialist beliefs, and among communists themselves.

I could give them evidence that can be seen, touched, heard, smelled, and even tasted of the corruption of socialists once they acquire a share of power.

We therefore cannot guarantee that even a communalist state would remain faithful to its honourable course or prevent corruption from entering its leadership.

The only guarantee lies in communal organisation carried out by the people themselves and defended by them through an active mechanism of struggle directed even against the state.

My companions and I would therefore rely upon the creation of a **Ministry of Communal Organisation in Iraq**, together with communal committees in every neighbourhood and village.

The people of every neighbourhood and village would then be organised to defend their popular markets, public restaurants, and free hospitals even against the state itself.

This would come after working with the distorted socialists to bring them to power in Iraq.

But we would remain distinct from them as an independent force too strong to be erased by an official decision.

Nor would we retreat like little schoolchildren in obedience to such a decision, as a hundred members of the Soviet party leadership once withdrew before the decree of a single man who had seized power...

#### Lenin

Nikolai was Russian. “Lenin” was the name by which he became known.

It is worth remembering that many of humanity’s great figures are known by titles or adopted names rather than their birth names.

Hammurabi’s original name is unknown.

Christ’s name was Immanuel.

Muhammad’s was Qutham.

Salah al-Din’s was Yusuf.

Lenin lived between 1870 and 1924.

Among the abdal, he was one of the shortest-lived.

This was not because he wore out his body through pleasures, as people of state and wealth often do.

He wore it out through extraordinary labour until he burned himself up from within.

Lenin was the fourth of the four original pillars of Marxism:

Karl Marx,

Friedrich Engels,

Plekhanov,

and Lenin.

That is true in terms of establishing and developing Marxism in its different elements.

As a philosopher, Lenin stood on roughly the same level as Plekhanov.

Plekhanov, however, devoted more attention to theory. His writings are philosophical in direction and academic in form.

Lenin’s writings are devoted mainly to political and organisational work.

His only major philosophical book, *Materialism and Empirio-Criticism*, was written as a response to theoretical deviations produced by new discoveries in physics.

It is therefore a polemical and militant book.

Yet Lenin possessed the ability to reconcile the demands of controversy with philosophical truth.

The book kept its full philosophical value despite the sharpness of its polemical language.

No one should imitate him in this respect.

Some lesser writers among our own people tried to do so.

They produced a kind of thought that was neither militant nor philosophical.

Philosophers are there to be drawn upon, not copied.

When Lenin applied Marxism to revolutionary politics and society, he developed it into a basis for the Russian Revolution.

In other words, he **Russified** Marxism, just as the Chinese communists later **Sinicised** it.

For that reason Russian Marxism came to be called Marxism-Leninism.

Marxism as a social doctrine adapts itself to circumstances and traditions.

Marxism as a philosophical doctrine adapts itself to physics.

The two paths are separate, as we have already explained.

Social Marxism is communism.

Philosophical Marxism is a doctrine for explaining nature.

A person can adopt the second without being a communist, just as someone may follow Aristotle, Ibn Rushd, al-Shirazi, or Kant.

Adapting Marxism to circumstances means mixing it with inherited and living national values.

The Chinese communists did this better than anyone.

They Sinicised Marxism by joining it to the Chinese communalist heritage and shifted its social base from the working class to the peasants.

Lenin was both a social philosopher and a philosopher of nature.

He knew neither Tao nor Sufism.

Yet his personal formation combined principles found in Taoism, Sufism, and the proletarian character.

He lived simply and knew nothing of material luxury.

He married one woman and had no children?

Some Sufis, as we noted earlier, say that the abdal do not father children.

Perhaps they drew this idea from Christ, Ibrahim ibn Adham, Abu Yazid al-Bistami, Amir al-Anbari, and Abu al-Ala al-Maʿarri before them.

Lenin provides another example.

This is not a law of nature.

It is a choice.

The abdal are so occupied by the concerns of other people that they have little time or energy left for forming a family of their own.

Lenin’s position among God’s treasured ones parallels Karl Marx’s.

He set in motion the same movement Marx had set in motion before him.

In its later phase, however, the movement also came to include the national revolutions of colonised peoples.

The question of those revolutions had not been settled by Marx.

Under Lenin, it was.

Because Lenin became immersed in this movement, his heart became a mixture of the Sufi and the politician.

His chief concern was to create the economic foundation of a communal society.

That required the people to exhaust themselves in the struggle for production and to bear sacrifices that were extremely difficult.

He had little choice.

Russia at the time did not possess the resources needed to give the poor immediate relief.

This gave rise to the theory that production must first be developed before the communal system could be established:

the theory of abundance.

China, with its enormous agricultural resources, did not follow this path.

The Chinese communes carried out two tasks at once:

they increased agricultural production,

and they distributed its returns among the peasants.

In this way hunger was eliminated across the Chinese continent in less than twenty years.

Russia, whose population was only around a third of China’s, remained unable to guarantee food security for its people until the experiment ended in the familiar way.

I should add that the leaders of modern communalism lacked wisdom and good judgment in managing their disagreements with one another.

China and Russia could have complemented one another.

China could have supplied its surplus agricultural production to Russia and solved Russia’s food problem.

Russia could have supplied China with manufactured goods and spared it the burden of industrial projects undertaken when its own means of industrialisation were weak.

This was not Lenin’s responsibility.

He was known for his judgment and wisdom.

The responsibility belongs to those who inherited power in Russia.

It also belongs personally to Mao Zedong, whose later conduct showed a considerable lack of wisdom and sound judgment.

Contradictions of this kind leave me uncertain about the future of our communal history.

But they do not discourage me or my companions to the point of paralysing our ability to continue with our project.

At the personal level, we have already seen how many qualities of Taoists and Sufis Lenin possessed.

In this respect we can genuinely compare him to Christ.

Even in youth he enjoyed none of the small allowances Shao Yong and, later, Karl Marx permitted themselves.

He turned himself into a continuous stream of spiritual energy devoted entirely to other people.

He put his bodily needs aside.

His resistance weakened, and he did not live long.

He destroyed himself for the poor.

This is one of the rarities of history.

Usually a ruler destroys himself for himself and dies quickly by the sword of pleasure.

This Russian mystic possessed Russia and its dependencies—an enormous empire.

Yet he took nothing from its resources for himself.

He chose poverty, and even hunger, and died early.

That is one degree of Sufi annihilation in humanity.

#### Tolstoy

Tolstoy was Russia’s greatest writer.

He lived between 1828 and 1910.

He came from a feudal family that owned serfs.

He spent his youth in amusement and dissipation, then began moving from hardness toward clarity after the age of twenty.

He joined the Tsarist army and took part in campaigns to suppress uprisings among peoples incorporated into the Russian Empire, including a campaign in Chechnya.

The battles he witnessed awakened him to the tragedy and disgrace of war.

From then on he took the same position toward war and soldiers as the Taoists and Sufis.

Later, witnessing an execution by guillotine in Paris led him to call for the abolition of the death penalty.

On the estate he had inherited, he worked to reduce the suffering of the serfs even before his thinking matured enough for him to conclude that they should be freed altogether.

He founded schools for their children.

He repeatedly intervened to help victims of Tsarist repression, making use of his position as the heir to an aristocratic family and as a famous writer.

He organised relief campaigns for people suffering from the famines that struck Russia during his lifetime.

And, to confirm his identity as an intellectual, he personally worked the land.

Eventually he began thinking about freeing the serfs and abolishing feudal ownership.

He submitted memoranda on these questions to the Tsar and his prime minister.

His efforts remind us of Mencius trying to persuade the kings of his own time to return to the nine-square system.

In 1856, when Tolstoy was twenty-eight, he made an unsuccessful attempt to free his own serfs.

His efforts continued until his death.

They eventually brought him into conflict with his wife, producing a bitter struggle within the family.

In the end he left home and died at a railway station after passing the age of eighty-two.

Unfortunately, a wife can often have a destructive effect upon a husband who is engaged in social struggle.

What will our women say to that?

As Tolstoy approached sixty, he stopped eating meat.

Three years later he made the choice final and became a complete vegetarian.

He was therefore the third person on our list to do so, after Amir al-Anbari and Abu al-Ala al-Maʿarri.

Tolstoy sided with ordinary people.

He understood their virtues in contrast to those of the elite.

He wrote:

> “Common people are superior to us through their life of labour and their independence, to such an extent that people like us should be ashamed to search for their faults, expose them, and describe them.”[^ms-v1-018]

He defended the virtues of peasants against those of aristocrats.

He even made peasants wiser in the way they died.

The peasant, a child of nature, dies when nature itself reaches the season of death.

The arrogant aristocrat reverses nature in death, dying just as nature is opening and coming back to life.

That is the reasoning his historians draw from his stories and writings.

Perhaps the aristocrats of Russia really did die in spring and its peasants in winter?

Or perhaps aristocrats died in spring because the season opened new opportunities for pleasure and indulgence, exhausting the bodies of the old until they died just as nature was being born again...

Tolstoy’s philosophy came close to deism.

He explains it in the following words:

> “I believe in one God, beyond the reach of thought and conjecture, whom the spirit knows as eternal, and in the eternal part of our deeds.
>
> “I do not understand the mysteries of the Holy Trinity or the birth of a Son of God, but I respect the faith of my fathers and do not deny it.”

—*Christian*, p. 31.

He therefore separated religion from divinity.

He declared that he did not believe in doctrine while continuing to respect the faith of his fathers.

He stood with Christ the human being, not Christ the God.

Here he was entirely consistent with his deism and with what he understood to be the true nature of Christ.

Maxim Gorky treated him unfairly.

He misunderstood Tolstoy’s beliefs and, without relying on any decisive passage from Tolstoy’s writings, simply assumed that Tolstoy spoke about the human Christ without enthusiasm or tenderness.

Gorky merely **supposed** that Tolstoy saw Christ as simple and naïve, almost an object of pity, and that he rarely loved him.

Gorky went further into contempt.

He claimed that Tolstoy seemed almost afraid that, if Christ returned and entered a Russian village, the local girls would laugh at him.

Gorky continued belittling Tolstoy and Tolstoy’s Christ, accusing Tolstoy of arrogance and domination.

He claimed that Tolstoy preserved the outward appearance of an aristocrat despite despising aristocratic class privileges, and that perhaps he consoled himself with his soft boots and the comfortable slippers made to fit them.[^ms-v1-019]

I do not know when Gorky wrote these harsh words about Russia’s great writer.

My guess is that they belong to the excesses of his youth.

Gorky sounds here like an adolescent atheist looking down upon any symbol touched by faith.

He throws it a contemptuous glance, as a newcomer sometimes does when first admitted to the open feast of knowledge.

At the same time, he seems to have wanted to place himself in the position of the literary critic empowered to issue final judgments upon the giants of his age, stripping them of virtue and accusing them of falseness.

Later in life, Gorky himself became troubled by an obsession with religious faith so strong that Lenin had to send him on leave to Italy to forget his anxieties.

The faith troubling Gorky was not Tolstoy’s faith in one God from whom he drew spiritual energy.

It was church faith.

Because Gorky’s cultural background was limited—literature without philosophy—he was always liable to move between denial and belief:

between crude atheism and religious faith.

Atheism is the child of philosophy.

Someone who becomes an atheist through philosophy does not abandon that atheism, because once philosophy truly enters the human mind it separates that mind from religion permanently.

No philosopher therefore moves back and forth between faith and atheism.

That belongs to writers and artists.

Tolstoy was a social activist.

Gorky was a political activist.

Tolstoy’s relationship with the people was closer than Gorky’s because it was governed by the communal instinct of the East.

That instinct produced the alliance between Tolstoy and the human Christ.

Because Gorky lacked a philosophical background, he did not understand the difference between Christ and Paul.

Tolstoy did.

A political activist is always vulnerable to separation from the people, even when he begins from a class struggle.

Politics subjects him to two pressures.

The first is the nature of political work itself, which does not necessarily obey principles.

The second is the politician’s ability to turn his positions and causes into ideology.

Ideology is often created at the expense of utopia—that is, at the expense of the direct principles that establish the rights of the people.

The social activist, by contrast, remains in his place among the people and gives little weight to political calculations.

But his struggle does need political support.

That support can come from a state founded upon the same principles.

Yet precisely because it is a state, it cannot remain fully inside the people.

That is the task of the social activist.

Perhaps this explains why Tolstoy never joined the communist movement.

The movement required, first of all, a political activist.

Tolstoy was not suited to that kind of struggle.

Yet the principles of the communist movement were the same principles Tolstoy embraced:

abolition of private property,

liberation of the peasants,

and the establishment of a shared economic system in which a small group could no longer seize its returns for itself.

Tolstoy went further than the communist movement in opposing the death penalty, which communists did not oppose except in China.

For Tolstoy, the source of this opposition was Christ.

In China, it was Taoism.

Communism is not a creed.

It is a social, ethical, and class position that can be adopted by people holding many different beliefs.

Turning it into a creed helped corrupt and dissolve it.

Once it became a creed, it turned into a system of rituals that a communist was required to perform whether or not those rituals remained connected to the original principles.

A creed becomes a veil over both reason and justice.

The believer measures things according to the creed and its rituals instead of according to the demands of reason and the principles of justice.

This explains why we see so many members of communist parties remaining loyal to their party regardless of its policies.

As long as the party embodies, carries, and protects the creed, loyalty to the party becomes a religious duty.

That is the nature of creeds and religions in general.

After Stalin, Soviet communism turned the privileges of its leaders into ideology.

Those privileges became part of the structure of the creed itself.

A good communist now became someone who defended the privileges of the leaders because, by doing so, he demonstrated loyalty to the party and its doctrine.

Communism is built upon direct social struggle.

Upon the communal heart, which gives no sacred status to either state or party, even though it needs state and party in order to take power away from governments founded upon private ownership.

The relationship is complicated.

A state based on communist principles must first be established and led by one communist party, or by several.

That is one step.

Then comes the struggle of the communalists against the state and the party, preventing utopia from turning into ideology.

This stage requires social activists rather than political activists.

And these social activists need their own popular organisation outside both party and state.

Its purpose is to mobilise ordinary people to impose their direct demands upon society, the state, and the party, and to confront their class enemies in all three arenas.

In this field of struggle, the teachers are Tolstoy, Goethe, al-Hallaj, Christ, and others like them among the abdal.

They are not party leaders, party writers, heads of state, or state officials.

##### A Side Note

1. When news of Tolstoy’s death reached Egypt in 1910, Ahmad Shawqi wrote a poem mourning him.

It was not really an elegy.

It was a celebration in which Shawqi listed Tolstoy’s human principles and defended him against the Church’s accusation that he was an unbeliever:

> Can one who wrote such books
> be called an unbeliever in the Gospel?
>
> They are themselves gospels,
> bearing warning and good news.

Shawqi’s use of the word “gospels” refers to ethics, not sacred status.

2. Tolstoy wrote a small book called *The Wisdom of the Prophet Muhammad*, in which he translated fifty-six Prophetic sayings.

When Shaykh Muhammad Abduh heard about the book, he wrote Tolstoy a letter thanking him and praising his kind initiative toward Islam.

Tolstoy replied with a letter that is difficult to understand and rather ambiguous.

I suspect he did not understand why the shaykh was thanking him.

Tolstoy had not translated those sayings in order to defend Islam.

He translated them because he admired them.

Like the other abdal, he did not draw rigid distinctions among religions and had no problem with any of them.

His philosophically trained mind, his communal conscience, and his complete universal humanity freed him from national, racial, and religious complexes.

#### Ivar Lo-Johansson

Ivar Lo-Johansson was a modern Swedish writer.

He was born near the beginning of the century and died near its end, in 1990.

He possessed no Taoist, Sufi, or Marxist culture.

He simply had a heart like theirs.

That heart compelled the Lord to make him one of His treasured ones.

Johansson was born and raised in a family of **statare**, agricultural labourers who lived in conditions little better than serfdom.

Swedish barons employed them on their farms and estates without wages.

Instead, each family was crowded into a single room.

There they cooked.

There they ate.

There they slept.

To avoid losing the room, every able member of the family had to perform whatever work the baron assigned.

A person was born in that room, raised there, prepared there for a lifetime of labour, and finally died while still working.

During this entire life, the worker scarcely knew money.

His wage was the room in which he lived, the food he ate inside it, and the place where he slept.

With very little schooling, the young Ivar became aware of his own tragedy and that of his family.

He began looking for a solution.

He turned first to history.

He read it, became fascinated by the lives of heroes and stories of heroic action, and gradually expanded his reading until he educated himself and rose above his circumstances.

His culture was literary, and it pushed him toward the short story and the novel.

Before the age of thirty he had thrown himself fully into writing.

When he reached that age, he found himself capable of doing something important about the tragedy of his family and the statare as a class.

He wrote a novel called *Escape*, treating the statare question through the story of his own life.

It was the first and last literary work to deal with this human tragedy.

The novel appeared in 1933 and is now counted among the great works of Swedish literature.

But Johansson did not simply say his piece and walk away.

He continued walking **with** the words he had written.

When he published the novel, he was still too young to launch the larger campaign he had in mind.

He waited several years.

Then he began a nationwide campaign to save these wretched people.

The campaign succeeded in bringing their tragedy to a final end.

Their labour contracts were abolished.

They were moved into decent homes.

The state was forced to give them decent work.

They became like the rest of their countrymen:

free,

dignified,

and able to live happily.

This happened in 1945, by which time Ivar had passed the age of prophecy.

Johansson then turned his attention to the elderly, another miserable group in its own way.

He devoted enormous effort to helping them.

He did not succeed because he could find no one willing to support his campaign.

He then worked to guarantee a livelihood for older people.

I do not know how far he personally succeeded.

But Sweden is now known as one of the world’s advanced welfare states.

There can be little doubt that it owes something of this to Johansson and his companions.

Among those companions was the poet Gullberg, a social activist in the manner of the Taoists and Sufis, although, like Johansson, he was neither Taoist, Sufi, nor Marxist.

Gullberg used to say:

> “The poet”—he should have said **the intellectual**—“is a soldier in God’s secret army.
>
> “He is required to struggle for the poor and the weak and against the instinct of greed and destruction among the people of state and money.
>
> “Anyone who fights this struggle is rewarded by seeing Christ appear to him in the whirlwind of streets and public squares.”

I wonder how many times Christ appeared to Johansson.

Johansson also tried to create a group of writers and intellectuals from proletarian backgrounds.

He wanted them to break the monopoly held over culture by the bourgeoisie and their children.

We do, however, have one criticism of Johansson.

He became involved in advocating sexual freedom between men and women.

Perhaps some of what happens in Sweden today is an echo of that campaign, although Westerners hardly need anyone to provide them with an ideology for such behaviour.

We did not remain silent about it, because even the English did not remain silent about it.

The English translator of Johansson’s *Escape* called the matter controversial.

If even the English say that, why should we be expected to say otherwise?

One of the qualities shared by the abdal is modesty and sexual restraint.

I do not assume that Johansson practised what he preached in his personal life.

His biographers do not describe any sexual dissipation of the kind associated with some poets.

For him it seems to have been a theory.

He believed it would free young people from psychological complexes and repression.

It is now known that it increased those complexes and repressions rather than freeing them.

Johansson’s position had an earlier precedent in al-Ghazali, who advised men with strong bodies to take many women.

Yet al-Ghazali himself married only one woman and owned no concubines.

#### Postscript

We have many groups among us who resemble the statare.

There are the shoeshiners, whom people in Greater Syria call **boyajiyya**, from *boya*, meaning polish.

There are the porters.

And in Iraq there are especially the Faili Kurds, with the first *i* pronounced obliquely:

One of them carries a thousand kilograms on his back for a single dirham, then goes home to eat bread and tea for lunch or dinner. And do not ask what kind of home he returns to. I have already described the homes of the *statare*. We have people in Egypt who live among the tombs too... and many others besides.

But the hearts of our intellectuals died seven centuries ago. They have consoled themselves by writing about the outward signs of poverty on the principle of: say your piece and move on. It has served them well. They acquired the title of “committed writer” without losing any of their own comforts.

One of the tricks of culture—unknown to ordinary people, who do not know how to use it to get what they need...

## The Orbit of Habāʾ

### Romantic Passion and Romantic Sufism

The word I use here, *ihtiyāmī*, is what modern people call “romantic,” from the Latin *ROMANICE*, meaning a poetic or prose tale, a love story, a song, and so on. The *Nibrās* dictionary rendered it as *ihtiyām*—passionate infatuation—and did very well. Both the word and its meaning are pleasing and well chosen.

Romantic Sufism revolves around love and beauty, whether divine or human. It differs from ordinary romantic poetry—*ghazal*, or love poetry—in that it abstracts love from the body and brings together love for that human creature called woman with love of heaven.

As I see it, romantic Sufism is a third current running alongside philosophical Sufism and social Sufism. A great Sufi master may belong particularly to this current, or masters from either of the other two currents may also practise it. In truth, all the great masters are lovers. For them, love is the foundation of existence and the force that draws everything together.

The mystic disappears beyond sensation and touch as he speaks of his love. Then the face of his beloved breaks upon him like the sun. His intoxication continues; the two delights overwhelm him; and the cups of love and knowledge circle around him. As the poet says:

> He kept drinking from her, while love drank down his mind,
> and his spirit announced that it was ready to depart,
> until, drunk, he sank down with his right arm for a pillow
> and surrendered his spirit to the wine.

When separation has had its fill of the mystic, he observes the courtesy of concealment. Even if something escapes him, or the scent of what is within him gives him away, we still cannot say that he has betrayed the secret. The lights of the Divine Presence appear to him; he becomes lost in wonder when he sees the face of its beauty in all its transformations and states.

When Muhyi al-Din Ibn Arabi searched for a single religion that could unite people and put an end to their hostility and hatred, he found it in love. Love alone is the religion within whose circle the fire-temple, the monks’ monastery, and the Kaaba of the Hijaz can all become one. In this the Shaykh takes as his model the fatherly tenderness of Christ toward humanity.

Here love has its broad meaning. It includes affection, tenderness, and loving-kindness. It does not include passionate erotic love, because that belongs to the relation between one individual soul and another. Affection and loving-kindness are more general.

Ibn Sida defined *wudd*—affection—as love wherever goodness enters into it. In other words, *wudd* expresses a desire for something good. *Hubb*, love, can be directed toward good or bad alike. This is why the English say “make love” to mean having sex. Arabic gives us a way around this. Love can contain good and bad, but when we mean only the good we can return to *wudd*, affection.

That is why the lover chose it in this verse:

> Deep within heart and breast, there will remain for her
> a secret tenderness on the day all secrets are laid bare.

He does not say “a secret love,” because love can suggest a bodily relationship, while he is moving toward abstraction.

His reference to “the day all secrets are laid bare” is to the Day of Resurrection: the day when secrets are tested and exposed, their hidden contents revealed, and the human being called to account for them. The lover is saying that somewhere deep inside him there is a hidden tenderness toward her, unknown and unrevealed because it cannot be seen or physically sensed.

Only on the Day of Judgment will his breast be opened and that secret become visible—the secret no human eye could uncover, but which now lies exposed before the divine eye.

And once it is revealed, he will not be called to account for it, because it is a secret of tenderness, not a secret of bodily love.

I have already said that our Sufis chose *mahabba*, loving affection, to express what they meant in this area, though in poetry they might use the simpler word *hubb*, love, when metre forced them to. What they really meant was *mahabba*.

They moved away from the ordinary word “love” because poets had taken possession of it for their own purposes. With very few exceptions, poets see love in the body of the beloved. For them the body cannot be separated from love, so their use of the word comes close to the English expression “make love.”

The Sufis had their own opinion of poetry and poets, and placed them among the forces of the outside world. This is why Shaqiq al-Balkhi said:

> “I used to be a poet. Then God blessed me with repentance.”

In the same category as the poet they placed the ruler, the cleric, the rich, and even the neighbours of the rich. All are veiled by sensory pleasure. Their relations with things are governed by usefulness: something is loved because it is useful, as the saying goes, “I love you, my useful one.”

The people of true love, by contrast, may even love the one who torments them. For them, as Shaykh Muhyi al-Din says, even *ʿadhāb*—torment—can be heard as related to *ʿudhūba*—sweetness.

This is why one of them stood at the Kaaba and addressed God:

> If You call me to account for my sins, I will call You to account for Your generosity.
> If You examine me closely in the reckoning, I will examine You closely in Your forgiveness.
> And if You imprison me in the Fire, I will tell the people of the Fire how much I love You.

Here “my love” has the force of an act, not merely a noun.

This applies only to the Beloved. The Sufi does not love the ruler, the rich man, or the cleric, because they belong to the alien powers of the world. Their torment is torment, with no sweetness in it.

Love is a bridge stretching between woman and heaven. On that bridge the woman becomes spirit. Her body disappears and only the letters of her name remain.

This is why the Sufi does not write ordinary love poetry. He speaks instead of absolute beauty and of longing to see it with the inner eye. He does not use the five senses in his relationship with the one he loves.

I said a great deal about love in the Orbit of Mīm, so the reader can return there. What I want to do here is make the difference between the poets’ love and the Sufis’ love clearer.

For the poet, woman is the body: a bodily being. This is why the poet complains of separation and longs for union.

But even “union” changes its meaning from one age to another.

An Abbasid sage once said:

> “There was a time when a young man would love a girl and circle her house for an entire year, delighted if he merely caught sight of her. If he was lucky enough to sit with her, they would complain of their suffering to one another and recite poetry together. Today, when the two meet, they complain of no love and recite no poetry. He simply goes for her as though Abu Hurayra and his companions had already witnessed their marriage contract.”

He was speaking of love in the pre-Islamic age and the earliest period of Islam, up to the end of the first Islamic century: what later came to be called **Udhri love**, after the Banu Udhra, the tribe famous for lovers of this kind.

Not all love at the time was like this. Al-Farazdaq, Umar ibn Abi Rabi'a, al-Akhtal, and many others were thoroughly bodily in their love. But Udhri love was a major phenomenon, with its heroes and martyrs.

It enriched Arabic literature with romantic poetry and popular love stories that remain alive to this day. Some were even turned into legends. Ordinary people said that Layla and Qays had been lifted into heaven and transformed into two neighbouring stars called Layla and Majnun. People would point them out on summer nights and show one another where they stood in the sky.

Before Islam, the Arabs also permitted friendship between a man and a woman outside marriage, provided that the woman was unmarried. The same restriction did not apply to the man.

A married woman who took such a male friend was called a *dāmida*, and the relationship was called *ḍamd*. Another condition was that she must not have more than one such friend; if she had several, she was called a *ḍanūṭ*. And a third condition was that there be no bodily union between them.

Most of these relationships were for conversation, as the sage’s story makes clear.

The change he describes was a change in urban love.

A Bedouin woman was once told how city people loved, in much the same terms as the sage above. She replied:

“If this is what an enemy and stranger does, what does a loving friend do?”

This was one stage through which Arab romantic passion passed. It later retreated before bodily love as urban civilisation advanced and the money economy developed.

Europeans passed through a similar stage in the eighteenth century and part of the nineteenth, producing the great poets of romantic love: Goethe, Lamartine, Shelley, and others.

European romanticism then ended where Arab romanticism had ended. With the advance of urban civilisation it gave way to a cultured form of bodily love, then broke free of restraint altogether. Among Muslims it ended in concubinage, sex between men, and sex between women; among Westerners, in sexual permissiveness and homosexuality, crowned in recent years by AIDS—the disease of Western love.

To understand what poets after early Islam meant by love, consider this strange verse:

> She came when death’s veil already stood between us,
> and offered union when union could no longer help.

The poet tells us that his beloved came to him when she heard that he was dying. They had never met before, and he could see no point in her coming at such a moment.

That is because, for the poet, union means bodily union, love means bodily love, and woman means a bodily being.

But she understood the matter differently. She believed that his love for her had a romantic, spiritual meaning. By going to him in the hour of his death, she would make death easier for him. She would let him look at her as he left this world, so that hers would be the final face his eyes rested on before he went.

This shows that she possessed a Sufi sensibility the poet himself did not understand. He dismissed her and made light of what she had done.

Woman is closer than man to this sensibility. A man surpasses her in it only when he becomes a Sufi and spiritualises himself. Otherwise, woman seeks sex less insistently than man and is satisfied with one man, apart from those who depart toward the European pattern. Such women are few among Eastern women and were few in the West before the capitalist age.

Woman also reaches a point at menopause when her need for man comes to an end. Man has no such point.

In woman the purpose of the union of male and female therefore becomes visible: the preservation of the species.

Had Islamic society in its golden age not been so intensely male-dominated, we would have found more women Sufis than men. I have counted more than a hundred of them.

Some reached the highest levels of Sufism: Rabi'a al-Adawiyya; Maymuna bint Shaqula; Fatima al-Naysaburiyya, who lived in the time of Dhu al-Nun al-Misri and whom he called his teacher; and Umm Harun, whose sainthood was said to be so complete that if she encountered a lion she would speak to it:

“There is food for you in me. Eat.”

And the lion would turn around and leave.

There was also Rabi'a bint Isma'il, who used to tell her husband:

“I do not love you with the love of spouses, but with the love of brothers.”

It was said that if the houris of Paradise saw her, they would hide themselves from her.

And there was the wife of Riyah al-Qaysi, whose name I have not been able to find. After performing the evening prayer, she would beautify herself, put on fine clothes, and ask her husband:

“Do you need anything?”

If he said no, she would remove her ornaments and fine clothes and return to prayer.

She did this out of loyalty to the Prophetic custom, under which she was required to respect her husband’s right to her body.

Rabi'a bint Isma'il, however, preferred that her husband marry another woman, because she was too occupied to attend to him. She even gave him money for the purpose.

She was repeating the experience of the Taoist Zhuangzi, but in reverse. We have already seen that he offered to divorce his wife so that she could marry someone else because he was too occupied to give her what she needed.

A woman who becomes a Sufi is more capable than a man of doing without.

The biographical collections also place Aisha bint Ja'far al-Sadiq among the earliest women saints. She used to threaten God:

> “If You put me in the Fire, I will take my belief in Your oneness in my hand, walk among the people of Hell with it, and say to them: I proclaimed Him One, and He punished me.”

What a scandal...

The greatest woman saint was Rabi'a al-Adawiyya. She was the first to speak of divine love, and the verses attributed to her travelled like sunlight until people near and far had heard them:

> I love You with two loves:
> a love of longing,
> and a love because You are worthy of love.
>
> In the first, remembering You
> keeps me from everything else.
>
> In the second, You lift the veils
> and let me see You.
>
> No credit is mine in either love.
> In both, all grace is Yours.

By “the love of longing,” she means passionate love: she loves Him simply for love’s sake.

The Arabic word *hawā* is not an ideal word for love, because it can also suggest a loose, shifting whim, something unstable and not properly ordered within the feelings. Sufis are sometimes forced to use it in poetry because the metre demands it. They use it there and almost nowhere else, because outside poetry they have no metre to satisfy.

Rabi'a proves that she loves Him for love’s sake by saying that she thinks of no one else.

In a love that fills the whole heart, everything other than the beloved disappears. It is absolute unity, with neither multiplicity nor division.

Yet He also remains worthy of her love.

Love simply for love’s sake does not require worthiness. It needs no explanation. It is a state wrapped in ecstasy and drowned in intoxication.

But when Rabi'a says that He is *worthy* of her love, she is speaking from sobriety. Worthiness requires a reason and an argument.

The reason He deserves her love is that He does not hide from her. Whenever she wants Him, He lifts the veil from His face and lets her see Him.

When He reveals His face, He draws her into His Presence. The lights of the Presence seize her, and she disappears into Him.

Then the Presence moves her as it wishes.

At that point remembering itself comes to an end. She no longer remembers Him; He is the one who remembers her.

This is the station the Sufis meant when they said that love itself finally disappears, until love, lover, and Beloved become one.

Rabi'a was the first teacher of divine love. She loved God directly, with no intermediary.

This is the love of the woman saint. A saint may sometimes love Him through an intermediary.

Rabi'a had no desire for men. She resisted her suitors and their generous offers and sent them away humiliated.

She may also have founded what we might call the doctrine of **neither world**: neither this world nor the next, in contrast to the religious ideal of happiness in both worlds.

She told God that she did not worship Him because she feared His fire or desired His paradise, but because He was worthy of her worship.

The Sufis called this **the worship of free people**.

Its opposite is the worship of slaves: worship motivated by fear of the Fire.

And there is also the worship of merchants: worship motivated by the hope of Paradise.

I say that Rabi'a *may* have been the first, rather than claiming it with certainty, because there is disagreement over her generation. Some say she lived until 135 AH, others until 185. On the first dating she would precede Ibrahim ibn Adham; on the second she would come after him.

Rabi'a’s love occupied her completely, leaving room for nothing else, as she herself said.

But what was this “everything else”?

We have already heard the story about the ruler’s torches. Rabi'a once used the light from those torches to sew a tear in her shirt. For some time afterward she lost her heart.
