Eventually she understood the reason. She tore open the stitching she had sewn by the ruler’s light, and her heart returned to her.

Whoever loves heaven hates the state.

Do not object that many people who claim to love heaven also love the state. Love and institutional religion do not meet, just as love and the state do not meet.

Religion and the state, by contrast, meet very easily. They form one structure, built on aggression against people and the theft of their rights.

People of the state and people of religion often claim to love.

In reality they love nothing in heaven or on earth except what they think will bring them happiness in both worlds: bodily pleasure in eating, drinking, sex, prestige, domination, and display.

That is what the powers of the outside world love, in the other meaning of the word “love,” not the pure tenderness Ibn Sida described.

Abu Abd Allah al-Isfahani, a student of Umar al-Suhrawardi, expressed it in this verse:

> So many people now lay claim to love
> that the captive of passion and the one free of it seem the same.

No love can enter hearts sealed shut with fat.

Love is like wisdom. As Dhu al-Nun al-Misri tells us, it does not enter a house already stuffed with food.

Rabi'a al-Adawiyya belongs to romantic Sufism rather than philosophical Sufism, which developed after her lifetime. Her temperament does not fit the hard foundation of philosophy, even philosophy softened by Sufi fluidity.

Nor does she seem to me to belong to social Sufism. She did not concern herself greatly with the affairs of ordinary people. She lived for love.

Her distance from the state was a flight from contamination, not solidarity with the state’s victims.

This is the way of the romantics. They give their hearts and souls to love in the sense of pure affection until they lose themselves in it and forget everything else.

So too did Umar ibn al-Farid, the Sultan of Lovers. Beauty captivated him in human faces and in the landscapes of nature. During the Nile flood he would visit a mosque on the river called al-Mushtaha to watch the scene in the evening. He would also visit the singing girls of Banu al-Hans, who sang for him to the tambourine and reed pipe while he danced and entered ecstasy.

Sufi singing itself, however, was performed by *qawwāls*, not by women.

Ibn al-Farid’s poetry also contains mystical knowledge, and he was one of those who taught the unity of being.

Returning to Rabi'a and her sisters, we find that her romantic passion did not prevent her from thinking about questions that properly belong to philosophical Sufism, above all the afterlife.

We have already seen that she worshipped God out of love, not from desire for reward or fear of punishment.

As I said before, the Sufis do not fear God in the ordinary sense. They stand in awe of Him. Awe means respect, reverence, and honour between beings whose stations are not wholly remote from one another.

It is reported that Rabi'a once heard a reciter read:

> “The people of Paradise are occupied there in delight.”

She replied:

“Poor people of Paradise, occupied with themselves and their spouses.”

(*Al-Kawākib al-Durriyya*, 8/203)

Ibn Arabi placed her at the same rank as Abd al-Qadir al-Jili.

The only thing that puts her beside him is her rejection of Paradise as a motive. She surpasses him in love but does not share his communal concerns.

She had a stronger will than he did because she endured life without marriage and he did not. I am not saying that his marriage and his many children arose from weakness. He possessed enough strength to do what he wanted and leave what he did not want.

I consider it most likely that Rabi'a herself never married.

The historians of Sufism became confused and told a story about her and her “husband,” but that story belongs to the other Rabi'a: Rabi'a bint Isma'il al-Shamiyya.

Rabi'a was also accused of believing in divine indwelling because of these verses:

> I have made You the one I speak with in my heart,
> while I let my body sit with whoever comes.
>
> My body keeps company with the one beside me,
> but the Beloved of my heart is my intimate within.

There is no evidence of indwelling here. Lovers say such things about those they love.

The doctrine of indwelling did not belong to her time; nearly a century separates her from al-Bistami.

Ibn Kathir nevertheless mentions, in his biography of her in *al-Bidāya wa-l-Nihāya*, that Dawud al-Sijistani accused her of heresy.

Ibn Kathir himself does not confirm the charge. He merely says that perhaps something concerning her had reached him.

The accusation does have some basis in her views on the afterlife and Paradise.

There may have been other things as well that have not reached us. We have lost a great deal about her life because the book Ibn al-Jawzi devoted entirely to her has disappeared.

There is also disagreement over the date of her death at the age of eighty. Some give 135 AH and others 185.

I favour the second date. The first would place her in the Umayyad period, when Sufism, as such, had not yet appeared. We have already argued in these orbits that Sufism begins with Ibrahim ibn Adham, who lived until 165 AH.

#### Flashes from Rabi'a

One man said:

> “I used to pray for Rabi'a. Then I saw her in a dream, and she said to me: ‘Your gifts reach us on trays of light, covered with cloths of light.’”

She also said:

> “Any deed of mine that becomes visible to people, I count as nothing.”

She meant that a good deed should remain unknown, so that it does not bring fame and status to the person who performs it.

In her private prayers she would say:

> “My God, would You burn in the Fire a heart that loves You?”

There is no doubt that she was not afraid of the fire of Hell. The fire she knew was the fire of her own heart, burning with love.

The light of love passed from Rabi'a to a woman who served her, Maryam al-Basriyya. Rabi'a herself was from Basra.

Maryam became one of the martyrs of divine love. Whenever she heard teachings about love she would lose control of herself.

She once attended the gathering of a Sufi teacher. As he continued speaking about love, she died there in the gathering.

This is how Ibn al-Jawzi tells her story in *Sifat al-Safwa* (4/33).

As for Burda al-Daririyya, when movement had ceased at night and every eye had grown still, she would call out in a sorrowful voice:

> “The eyes have grown quiet, the stars have disappeared, and every lover is alone with the beloved. I too am alone with You, my Beloved. Will You really punish me while Your love is in my heart? Do not do it, my Beloved...”

The woman mystic Umm Talq—her kunya has reached us, but not her personal name—spoke of strength of will as one of the foundations of firmness in Sufism:

> “Since God gave me power over my self, I have never let it possess what it desires. The self is a king if you follow it, and a slave if it follows you.”

Umm al-Harish is also known only by her kunya. She was a mystic who married a soldier.

The sources tell us nothing about the circumstances of this marriage. They merely say that she was afflicted with him.

She refused to eat from his food. She prepared something separately for herself, using none of the money he earned.

When he pressured her to eat with him, she would place her fingers outside the shared bowl and make him think she was eating.

What was the story of Umm al-Harish? How did she live with this soldier? Why did she not seek a separation from him when she possessed the right to do so?

There are no answers.

Hasana the Worshipper was a beautiful woman mystic who never married.

A woman once told her:

“Get married.”

Hasana answered:

> “Bring me an ascetic man who will demand nothing of me from this world. And I do not think you will find one. If you find a man who weeps and makes me weep, who fasts and tells me to fast, who gives charity and urges me to give charity, then wonderful. Otherwise, farewell to men.”

That is a judgment on men delivered by a woman Sufi who turns the table over on their heads and leaves them unable to answer the challenge.

The women mystics possessed enough strength and force of character to silence even the strongest men.

And who was stronger than Sufyan al-Thawri?

Yet several times he retreated before Rabi'a and before another woman mystic, the daughter of Umm Hassan al-Asadiyya.

Once he saw the latter living in great hardship and said:

“If you asked so-and-so for a little money, it would improve your condition.”

She cut him short and told him that she asked nothing from any human being.

Riyah al-Qaysi had a real footing in Sufism and married a woman mystic.

On the morning after their wedding, the bride went to prepare her dough.

He said to her:

“Why not find a woman to do this for you?”

She answered:

> “I married Riyah al-Qaysi. I did not marry a stubborn tyrant.”

There is a deep meaning in this.

She rejects domestic servitude. She does not want to degrade another human being by making that person her servant.

It was this high spirit in a woman whose name we do not even know that led me to include in the programme of my Communalist Committee a demand for a law prohibiting citizens from being employed as domestic servants, in defence of their dignity and of the human substance within them.

Ibn Kathir says that Maymuna bint Shaqula once mentioned in a sermon that she had been wearing the garment she had on for forty-seven years and that it had never needed replacing. Her mother had spun its thread.

Then she said:

> “A garment in which God is not disobeyed does not wear out quickly.”

What she means is that if the person wearing a garment lives uprightly—does not steal, seize what belongs to others, commit adultery, oppress anyone, take money from the state, or serve it—the garment lasts longer.

The clothes of upright and righteous people therefore last longer than the clothes of the corrupt.

---

Sufi love poetry varies greatly in how accurately it expresses mystical ecstasy.

With the exception of Ibn al-Farid, the Sufis generally wrote poetry through craft rather than natural poetic talent, because poetry was not their real field. Much of it resembles the verse composed by scholars.

A great deal of it is clumsy and overly explicit.

Yet even such poetry may contain an idea so powerful or startling that the weakness of the verse is absorbed into its beauty. It can become eloquent despite its clumsiness.

At that point its roughness approaches the taste of ordinary spoken language.

There are many verses of this kind in *al-Ta'arruf*:

> When she appears to me, her greatness overwhelms me,
> and I withdraw like one who has lost all will.
>
> Through her, all of me is swept away;
> through her, what I feel is veiled from me.
>
> I find Him when, through Him, I am absent from myself,
> and I witness my ecstasy only when it is gone.
>
> In union I witness no one but Him,
> yet I do not witness Him as something separate.
>
> Through Him I am gathered, and from myself divided:
> the single in union becomes two, and the two are one.

In the first verse we have the awe inspired by the beloved, which makes speech impossible in her presence because the lover disappears entirely into her.

The remaining verses contain the dialectics of love.

He exists when he is absent, because in ecstasy he disappears both from the Beloved and from himself.

Union is the state of annihilation: I find no one but Him.

When I return to ordinary consciousness, I become absent from Him.

But in union, when I see no one except Him, He has gathered me into Himself while separating me from myself at the same moment.

I am He in the singular, and in the dual we are one within two...

These awkward verses—even containing a grammatical irregularity in *ajiduhu*, where the poet gives the word the stillness of spoken language although grammar requires otherwise—carry very high ideas about mystical experience. They cannot be understood unless we accept the union of opposites.

At other times we encounter poetry that is well made, elegantly arranged, eloquent, and sweet in its language, like these verses, also from *al-Ta'arruf*:

> You guarded me with such care
> that I was kept from a deadly pasture.
>
> You are my defence when I am challenged,
> and in my thirst I call: My Lord.
>
> When the exalted mystic rides in secret
> toward a lofty vision,
>
> and dives into abundant seas,
> revelation begins to pulse within the thought,
>
> while the sealed mysteries fall away
> before the grieving heart of the saint.
>
> Whoever wanders bewildered in the shock of meeting—
> you will see him dead, yet living.

These are verses to be tasted and drunk, not explained.

Their music is light and delicate, apart from a little strain in the rhyme of the fourth line.

Stretching the word *ẓamāʾ*—thirst—to satisfy the metre is one of those poetic necessities avoided by the great masters but common in Sufi verse. It becomes one of the features of that roughness which brings the poetry closer to popular speech.

We find the same thing in these verses quoted in the Book of Love in al-Ghazali’s *Revival of the Religious Sciences*:

> Once my heart’s desires were scattered everywhere;
> since my eyes saw You, all my desires have become one.
>
> Those I once envied now envy me,
> and I became master of creation when You became my Master.
>
> I left the people their world and their religion,
> occupied with remembering You—my religion and my world.

The first verse is the finest and highest.

The other two contain separate concessions to the demands of poetry. The second expresses the joy of intimacy with the Beloved, while the third gives us a conventional idea repeated by lovers everywhere.

If anything surprises me, it is that Shaykh Muhyi al-Din’s great love for Nizam did not produce poetry equal to his beautiful, rich prose.

His poems to his beloved Nizam follow the conventional language of love. They fall far below the love poetry of actual poets and cannot compete with the poems of Majnun of the Banu Amir.

Still, the Shaykh has an excuse. Poetry was never really his possession.

I did come across one line from a poem about Nizam that astonished me in both sight and sound:

> She made me real: I was lost in love with her,
> though my eyes had never seen her.

This is a perfect statement of Sufi love.

Had he continued along this path and gone deeper into it, he could have given us something ordinary poets had failed to produce.

But his “poetic talent” carried him no further than this single line. The rest of the poem returns to the usual style and contains nothing new.

Al-Hallaj is more of a poet than Ibn Arabi.

His poetry flows. It is alive and true to his feelings, enough for me to count him among the poets as well as the Sufis.

The only reason poetry did not become his first concern was that he was killed.

Read these lines:

> I want You—not because I want reward;
> I want You even for punishment.
>
> Every other desire of mine has been fulfilled
> except the pleasure I find in suffering.

His companion and supporter Ibn Ata commented on this:

> “The torment of longing increased in him, along with the madness of passion and the burning of regret. Then, when he became pure and true, he rose to a sweet height, while a constant rain poured upon him from the Real.”

Al-Hallaj had no human beloved, so far as his outward life tells us.

We have already encountered Sumnun the Lover of Baghdad in the Orbit of Love, and his words on love—prose whose beauty is almost unsurpassed.

Some of his thought has also reached us in verse.

These four flowing lines use a shortened form of the *basīṭ* metre, so gentle that they resemble the murmuring of someone falling asleep:

> My heart pointed me toward You
> so it might see what my eyes cannot.
>
> You cast into my deepest mind
> the sweetness of asking and longing.
>
> You ask me to choose something,
> though You already know what I desire.
>
> I have no share in anything but You,
> so test me however You wish.

Here too is mystical ecstasy, in two lines recited by al-Tadifi and attributed to Abu al-Hasan al-Sabbagh (*Qalāʾid al-Jawāhir*, p. 123):

> He gave me a drink that brought my heart to life,
> in the cup of nearness, from the sea of love.
>
> Were God not guarding those who know Him,
> the mystics would wander madly through every valley.

I have made one alteration to the first line.

I replaced “the cup of love” with “the cup of nearness,” because “the cup of love” is a conventional phrase that does not fit the spirit of Sufi literature, which breaks through the familiar.

The Sufis also composed a poem praising the Prophet and placed it on the tongue of his uncle al-Abbas.

Its style would have been impossible in pre-Islamic Arabia or the earliest Islamic period. Its Sufi flavour itself proves that it is a later composition.

Its true author was a Sufi lover addressing a cosmic Beloved who bore the name of the Prophet.

It should be counted among the jewels of Sufi poetry:

> Before all this, you were among the shadows,
> in the hidden keeping-place where leaves were sewn.
>
> Then you descended into the world—not yet human,
> neither lump of flesh nor clot of blood,
>
> but a drop riding aboard the ark
> when the flood overwhelmed Nasr and his people.
>
> You passed from loins to womb,
> one generation rising as another passed away.
>
> And when you were born, the earth shone bright,
> and the horizon glowed with your light.[^ms-v1-020]
>
> So in that radiance, in that light,
> we make our way along the paths of guidance.
>
> O coolness of Abraham’s fire,
> O cause that makes the fire harmless even as it burns.

Some Sufi poetry overlaps with the poetry of ordinary poets, especially the romantic poets of Udhri love, in whose work the beloved takes on the qualities of a spiritual being freed from bodily relations.

The Sufis borrowed such poetry for recitation and quotation, until it becomes almost impossible to distinguish it from their own:

> Someone asked me for Layla’s secret.
> I answered blindly, uncertain even of Layla.
>
> They say, “Tell us. You are the keeper of her trust.”
> But if I told them, I would no longer be trustworthy.

***

> They gave me drink and said, “Do not sing.”
> Had they given the mountains of Hunayn what they gave me,
> the mountains themselves would have sung.

***

> Then through the wilderness I travelled all night,
> fearing neither lion nor wolf.
>
> Longing overcomes me, so I devour the night-road;
> the one who longs has always been overcome.

The prophets, according to the Sufis, were denied *shawq*—longing—while the saints experienced it, and in this respect the saints possessed an advantage over them.

They define longing as the heart surging when the Beloved is mentioned.

They compare it to the flame in a lamp. The lamp shines because of that flame; without longing, the heart is dark.

They also distinguish between *shawq*, longing, and *ishtiyāq*, intensified yearning.

Longing quiets down when the beloved is met.

Intensified yearning does not. It grows stronger and burns more fiercely.

This means that *ishtiyāq* is more intense than *shawq* and belongs to another order.

To understand the difference, think of ordinary longing as the wick that gives light to a lamp. The wick slowly consumes itself until it disappears. When it disappears, it separates from the lamp.

So too with longing. When it enters the heart, the heart shines for a time. Then the flame goes out and the heart returns to darkness and dullness.

Intensified yearning is different.

It is like the sun. The closer you come to it, the more fiercely you burn, and the sun is an eternal furnace that does not go out.

So this deeper yearning grows stronger through nearness.

This is why, in the two verses above, I changed “the cup of love” to “the cup of nearness.” Nearness contains something love by itself does not: continuing contact and constant exposure to the radiance of the Beloved.

There are moments when a poet in love reaches so deeply into mystical ecstasy that you can no longer tell whether he is a poet or a Sufi.

People formed by cities and the money economy rarely reach this level. It belongs above all to people close to nature.

Read these verses written by a poet from the interior of Arabia during the pilgrimage season, after he had passed through a place called al-Na'm and then left it:

> By Him whose House the pilgrims answering the call have visited,
> and who knows the days of sacrifice and slaughter,
>
> my love for al-Na'm and its people only grew
> through the nights Layla stayed there.
>
> May God give me hope whenever I remember her
> and soothe my companions with her memory on the night of departure.
>
> The weariness and sleepiness within me grew still,
> and so did the restlessness and fear of the riding beasts.

This is a state of feeling only a Sufi normally reaches.

Layla is no longer a bodily being whom the poet hopes to meet. She has become the source of a spiritual energy that cures him of exhaustion and drowsiness and restores his ability to live.

Her spiritual fuel even flows into the camels, calming and reviving them.

This is the ecstasy that remakes nature so that it becomes more responsive to the lover, giving him the power to act whenever helplessness overtakes him.

The greatest Sufis reach the same point at the height of divine longing.

Abu al-Hasan al-Alawi, a disciple of Ibrahim al-Khawwas, tells us:

> “I saw him in the mosque at Dinawar, sitting in the middle of it while snow fell on him. I felt sorry for him and said, ‘Why not move somewhere sheltered?’”

He replied in verse:

> The road to You lies clearly straight;
> no one who truly wants You needs a guide.
>
> If winter comes, with You it is summer;
> and if summer comes, with You there is shade.

Such is the force of his effect upon nature.

The romantic Sufis considered Majnun of the Banu Amir one of their own masters.

One of them saw him in a dream and asked:

“What did God do with you?”

Majnun replied:

> “He forgave me and made me a proof by which lovers are judged.”

His authority comes from the purity and faithfulness of his love: from the fact that Layla eventually became for him an abstract spiritual being rather than a body.

This transformation is essential to Sufi love.

Qays did not begin that way. At first he loved Layla because he wanted to marry her.

When marriage became impossible, he spiritualised her. His ordinary love turned into mystical ecstasy.

In its ordinary meaning, love is the love of a man for a woman that leads him to seek marriage with her.

If he marries her, the love becomes the kind of marital affection we discussed in the chapter on love.

If he cannot marry her, he becomes consumed with longing.

That longing is not for the body but for the spirit.

This is why my word *ihtiyāmī* corresponds to “romantic.” Arab romantic lovers and Western romantics agree in spiritualising the woman they cannot marry.

That is the essence of this kind of love.

Love that ends instead in a bodily encounter outside marriage does not belong in the category at all. It belongs to adultery.

The lover does not commit adultery.

#### A Story of Ecstasy

In al-Qushayri’s *Risāla* we read that a Sufi once arrived among an Arab tribe and was taken in by a young man.

While the young man was serving him, he suddenly fainted.

The Sufi asked what was wrong with him.

They told him:

“He has a cousin he is passionately in love with. She moved inside her tent, he saw the dust rise from her dress, and he fainted.”

The Shaykh went to the entrance of the girl’s tent and said:

“Among your people a stranger has rights and protection. I have come to intercede with you for this young man. Have some tenderness toward him in this love he bears you.”

She replied:

“Glory be to God. You have a very innocent heart. He cannot bear even the sight of the dust from my dress. How could he possibly bear my company?”

#### A Lover’s Calls

Abu Abd Allah al-Isfahani, one of the disciples of Shaykh Umar al-Suhrawardi of Baghdad, recited several verses about longing and then began to call out:

> O you who feel safe, is there anyone among you who can climb to heaven?
>
> O you imprisoned in the dungeons of your names, is there anyone among you whose understanding is sound enough to grasp the signs of wild beasts and birds?
>
> Is there among you anyone whose longing is like Moses, who can say with the tongue of his heart:
>
> “Show Yourself to me, that I may look upon You. The waiting has been long...”

#### A Rebuke

Rabi'a fell ill.

Her companions came to visit her and asked what had happened.

She replied:

> “By God, I know no cause for my illness except this: Paradise was shown to me, and my heart leaned toward it. I think my Master became jealous over me and rebuked me.”

*Today, Friday, 24 Sha'ban 1417 AH, I went to the mosque of my master Muhyi al-Din on the slope of Mount Qasioun and greeted his beloved with a Confucian blessing. From there I recited the prayer over the Name.*

---

### The Sufis and Singing

Most jurists agree that listening to singing is forbidden, even though there is no decisive text prohibiting it.

The Quran itself does not forbid singing.

There is only the verse:

> “Among people are those who purchase idle talk in order to mislead others from the path of God without knowledge.”

They interpreted “idle talk” as singing.

This interpretation is forced.

The verse plainly refers to **misleading**, not **entertaining**. Misleading is done through propaganda. Entertainment is done through song.

“Idle talk” here means false or empty speech with no value in it.

People in Medina sang during the Prophet’s lifetime. The city contained noisy gatherings for singing, such as the gathering of Hassan ibn Thabit.

The sources of hadith and jurisprudence also contain reports in which the Prophet encouraged people to make marriages publicly known through song rather than conducting them in silence, so that marriage could be distinguished from illicit sex.

Umar ibn al-Khattab may bear responsibility for the later prohibition. This would fit his general tendency to focus Muslim attention on the heavy tasks of building the state and society and expanding Islam through conquest.

Umar was also the first to become severe in prohibiting wine and punishing people for drinking it. The Prophet did not impose such a punishment.

The earliest report I have found of specifically Sufi singing dates from the time of Junayd al-Baghdadi, in the second half of the third Islamic century.

It is usually accompanied by dancing.

The dancing does not begin as soon as the singing begins. It comes later, when those present enter ecstasy, rise, and dance.

The dancers are therefore the Sufis themselves.

The singers do not dance.

The Sufi singer is called a **qawwāl**, literally one who “says” or recites. The singing itself is called **samāʿ**, listening.

Poetry of mystical ecstasy or praise of the Prophet is recited in it.

One report about a *samāʿ* gathering attended by Junayd says that Ibn Marzuq entered ecstasy, rose, and began to dance, while Junayd remained sitting perfectly still.

They asked Junayd why he did not move.

He recited the verse:

> “You see the mountains and think them motionless, while they pass like clouds.”

Explaining the meaning of song in the chapter on the etiquette of *samāʿ* in his *Revival*, al-Ghazali writes:

> “There is a noble faculty in the heart that the power of speech cannot bring out in words. The soul therefore brings it out in melodies. When it appears, the soul delights in it and is moved by it. So listen to the soul and converse with it, and leave conversation with outward things.”

This is a Sufi understanding of song.

It takes music down into the far depths of the self, into feelings, emotions, and vague thoughts that language cannot reach and that can therefore be expressed only through melody.

Clearly this applies only to the higher forms of song and music: Sufi singing, the music of al-Nazarani, and in our own time symphonies and the singing of Muhammad Abd al-Wahhab, Umm Kulthum, and Fairuz.

The only problem with those three is that they often choose poor texts to sing. Most of their songs are love poetry, because their cultural formation is not broad enough to absorb texts of the highest order.

Al-Ghazali set strict conditions for *samāʿ*. We can summarise them as follows:

1. **The right time.** The heart must be free to attend to the music. It should not be a time for eating, argument, prayer, or anything else that would distract the heart.

2. **The right place.** The gathering should not take place on a busy street, in an ugly setting, or anywhere containing something likely to distract the heart. In other words, singing needs a suitable space in which people can concentrate fully on listening.

3. **The right company.** Those present should not include religious people who condemn music because they are bankrupt in the subtleties of the heart; their presence weighs heavily on the gathering. Nor should there be an arrogant worldly person whom everyone else must flatter and accommodate. Nor should there be a Sufi who theatrically pretends to be in ecstasy, showing off through dancing and tearing his clothes. All these things spoil the gathering.

4. **The right manner of listening.** The listener should attend closely to what the *qawwāl* is singing. His heart should be present. He should not keep glancing from side to side or watching other people’s faces to see what signs of ecstasy appear on them. He should concern himself with his own state and watch his own heart. He should avoid movements that disturb people whose hearts are truly present. Outwardly—not inwardly—he should remain still, his limbs calm. He should avoid clearing his throat and yawning. He should sit with his head lowered, as though deep in thought, and resist clapping, dancing, and every other movement performed artificially for display.

Al-Ghazali then raises a strange question.

The question suggests that the Sufis prefer listening to song over listening to the Quran, even though the word of God must obviously be superior to song.

His answer is that singing arouses mystical ecstasy more strongly than the Quran does.

He gives six reasons:

1. **Not every Quranic verse suits the listener’s emotional state.** Someone overwhelmed by grief, longing, or regret will not find his state reflected in a verse such as, “God instructs you concerning your children: the male shall have the share of two females,” or, “Those who accuse chaste women...” What stirs what already exists in the heart is something suited to that state. Poets compose verses precisely to express the states of the heart.

2. **Most people know the Quran by heart and hear it repeatedly.** The first time something is heard, its effect on the heart can be great. The second time the effect weakens. By the third it may disappear. Poetry, by contrast, can always offer unfamiliar wording and composition even when the underlying meaning is the same. A Quran reciter cannot produce a new and unfamiliar Quran every time, because the Quran is fixed and cannot be added to. Abu Bakr the Truthful is said to have pointed to this when he saw Bedouins hearing the Quran and weeping. He said, “We were once as you are, but our hearts grew hard.” This report is one of the Sufis’ lies! It does not mean that Abu Bakr’s heart was harder than the hearts of uncultivated Bedouins, or that he loved God and His word less than they did. It means that repetition had made his heart accustomed to the Quran and reduced its immediate effect. It is contrary to ordinary experience for someone to hear a verse for the first time and weep, then continue weeping at the same verse for twenty years. In short, a singer can always produce unfamiliar verses capable of stirring ecstasy. A reciter cannot always produce an unfamiliar Quranic verse.

3. **The rhythm of words has an effect on the soul.** Measured sound is not the same as unmeasured sound. Poetry has metre. The Quran does not.

4. **The effect of metered poetry can be changed by melody.** A singer may lengthen a short vowel, shorten a long one, pause inside words, break phrases apart, or join them together. Such freedom is permitted in poetry. It is not permitted in the Quran, which must be recited as it was revealed. If the Quran is recited exactly as revealed, it does not possess the additional effect created by musical metre. Musical pattern has an effect of its own even where no verbal meaning is understood, as with strings, pipes, and other musical sounds whose power comes from melody alone.

5. **Measured melodies become still more pleasing when strengthened by rhythms and other measured sounds: the sounds of instruments.** Such accompaniment cannot be applied to the Quran.

6. **A singer may sing a verse that does not suit the listener’s state.** The listener can dislike it and ask for another. He cannot do this with the Quran. He cannot reject one verse and demand a different one.

Abu Hayyan al-Tawhidi reinforces this difference between the effect of singing and the effect of Quranic recitation with a story in *al-Imtāʿ wa-l-Muʾānasa*.

A man was once circling the Kaaba on a donkey while singing.

People said to him:

“Are you not ashamed to sing in the Sacred Sanctuary?”

He replied:

“I am afraid I will become drowsy, fall from my donkey, and hurt myself. I sing so I will not fall asleep.”

They asked:

“Why not recite the Quran?”

He answered:

“We tried that. We found it made us sleepier.”

The truth about the Quran is that it is high literature. Only a minority of people—those with eloquence and highly developed artistic and rhetorical taste—can fully appreciate it.

Not everyone can be affected by it in the way Egyptians and others may be affected by Quranic recitation at their ceremonies and begin to weep.

The Quran does not easily submit to melody, except in the hands of the great Egyptian masters of recitation, who achieve extraordinary beauty with it.

I knew an Iraqi who used to drink wine while listening to Shaykh Mustafa Ismail.

Even those great reciters, however, do not eliminate the need for song, for the reasons al-Ghazali has explained.

Al-Ghazali tells another story.

One Sufi visited another and found him reading from the Quran.

When the host saw his visitor, he closed the book and asked him to recite some poetry he knew by heart.

The visitor recited two lines.

The Sufi threw the Quran from his hand and began to weep until his beard was soaked.

Then he said:

> “And you blame people for calling me a heretic? I have been reading the Quran since the dawn prayer and not a single tear fell from my eye. These two lines have brought the Resurrection down upon me...”

Not all Sufis, however, fit the orderly picture al-Ghazali gives us.

Their gatherings are often noisy. Frenzied dancing mixes with the singing, and people who understand nothing of Sufi matters may attend the *samāʿ*.

We will return to this when we come to the critique of Sufism.

Some of the great masters abstain from *samāʿ*, not because they consider it forbidden but because they consider restraint more dignified.

*Samāʿ* belongs above all to the romantic Sufis. It appears much less often among the great masters of social Sufism.

#### A Flash

Ruwaym al-Baghdadi was asked about the ecstasy Sufis experience during *samāʿ*.

He replied:

> “They take on the forms of meanings that remain hidden from everyone else, and those meanings call to them: ‘To Me! To Me!’”

What Ruwaym is really saying is that the Sufi is consumed by the beauty of art—which is to say, by the beauty of things.

When beauty is seen by the physical eye, imagined by the inner eye, or heard by the heart, the Sufi disappears into it. This is one part of his greater disappearance into the oceans of the unseen kingdom.

The Sufi’s heart is more delicate than the poet’s. His taste is finer.

When he senses beauty in a word, a melody, or an image, he does not simply admire it. He becomes absorbed in it.

This is why some people have explained the word “Sufism” through its supposed connection with *safāʾ*, purity.

Even when the Sufi is dealing with philosophical ideas, his language remains open to free forms of eloquence. It is filled with visions: images he may actually see in dreams, or describe without deliberate invention.

They therefore possess the easy flow of a truthful report while remaining strange.

The sentences uttered by the Sufi swim beyond the familiar. They do not obey ordinary logic.

From the standpoint of formal logic, they belong to the irrational.

But they possess a logic of their own within a method of unrestricted intuition.

Through this method, the Sufi uncovers and imagines truths whose validity is judged through laws of intuition working together with the laws of dialectic.

The Sufi’s intuitions are inseparable from the beauty of ecstasy.

This is why he experiences truth itself as one expression of cosmic beauty—what he calls divine beauty.

His relationship with truth is one of passionate love because truth, for him, is the product of the heart rather than the intellect.

Sufism, then, is a beautiful art in its own right, and the Sufi is an artist.

The Sufi imagination has wings. Neither religious law nor rational limits can contain it.

André Chouriet discovered its relationship with Surrealism—or rather Surrealism’s relationship with it.

If there is to be a comparison, the debt runs from Surrealism toward Sufism, not the other way around, because Sufism is older.

Even so, Sufism does not match Surrealism in the latter’s closed and opaque visual expressions.

Sufi language is scattered, fragmented, and illogical, but it can still be tasted.

The Sufis shaped it within limits that aesthetic taste can receive. From there the reader emerges either with an intuitive truth, when the subject is one of knowledge, or with an emotional response, when the subject is romantic passion.

The Sufi has no taste for ugliness.

This is why they required *samāʿ* to take place somewhere pleasant to look at and free of burdensome people, poseurs, or arrogant figures whom everyone must humour.

Sufi art, which recoils from ugliness, also recoils from violence.

At its core Sufism possesses a Christ-like tenderness of heart.

This is why it can oppose religious wars while at the same time supporting defensive vigilance and standing guard: it hates violence, but it loves justice.

Westerners have brought both ugliness and violence into painting and cinema.

Sufi art escaped both because it remained innocent of barbarism and everything connected with it.

### Farewell to the Orbit of Habāʾ

Love reaches perfection when every obstacle is removed from the lover.

And when love reaches the fullness of its nature, the qualities of the Beloved bend tenderly toward the lover and draw him in, freeing him from whatever still compromises the truth of his love.

The lover then returns bearing something of the Beloved’s own qualities, and can say:

> I am the one I love, and the one I love is I.
> We are two spirits dwelling in a single body.
>
> If you see us, you have seen Him;
> and if you see Him, you have seen us.

*ʿAwārif al-Maʿārif*

## The Orbit of Zayn al-Abidin

They say that when Zayn al-Abidin, Ali ibn al-Husayn, made the pilgrimage from Medina and returned, he would not remove his mount’s saddle. The round trip was close to a thousand kilometres.

Why? Because this animal had been his companion. She had carried the burden of taking him to the House of God and bringing him home again. She, too, was a living spirit, and a living spirit was not to be insulted or harmed.

And what would he do if she suddenly refused to move somewhere along the road? He would simply go along with her. If she stopped, he stopped. He would wait until her mood improved, and then they would continue.

But kindness to animals does not necessarily prove that someone is humane. Plenty of Westerners treat animals gently and pamper them, while in every other respect behaving like predators toward other human beings—especially toward people of another colour.

That was not Zayn al-Abidin.

Another story tells us that one of his slaves was roasting meat in an oven while a young son of Zayn al-Abidin stood nearby. The skewer slipped from the slave’s hand, struck the child on the head, and killed him.

Zayn al-Abidin heard the cry and rushed over. When he saw his son covered in blood, he did not turn to him first. He turned to the slave and said:

“Don’t be afraid. Don’t be afraid. You are free. You are free.”

Only then did he turn to his son. The child was already dead. He picked him up and covered him.

For Zayn al-Abidin, the urgent problem at that moment was the slave, who was almost dying of terror at what had happened. Zayn al-Abidin understood this and dealt with the man’s fear before attending to his own son. To make him feel completely safe, he freed him on the spot.

Slaves were normally freed as a reward for some great service. They were not freed because they had accidentally—or deliberately—killed their master’s child.

Zayn al-Abidin was a believing Muslim, deeply committed to doctrine and religious law. One sign of that commitment was that he owned slaves, because slavery was legally permitted. He saw no defect in the law so long as it came from God.

But Zayn al-Abidin’s ethics rose above his beliefs.

His conduct shows the role ethics can play in softening the cruelty of law. He gives us an example of something every legal system needs: moral principles capable of tempering its rigidity and strict literalism.

I had been looking for a title for this Orbit on Sufi ethics, and I chose Zayn al-Abidin.

I could just as well have chosen Plekhanov, the Russian Marxist philosopher.

What made me think of him was something he wrote in the introduction to his book *The Monist View of History*, which contained his debates with a Russian bourgeois thinker named Mikhailovsky. Plekhanov said that further ideas and replies had occurred to him after the original exchange, but he refused to add them to the book because Mikhailovsky had died. It would not be right, he said, to add new arguments to replies written while Mikhailovsky was alive, since the man would no longer be able to read them or answer them.

That is the same ethic we see in Zayn al-Abidin.

I chose Zayn al-Abidin simply because he came earlier.

Plekhanov has another standing of his own, one that Zayn al-Abidin does not share outside questions of ethics, and his proper place is elsewhere in this book.

I had wanted to include him among my list of God’s cherished ones, but some friends suggested that, among the Russians, I should limit myself to Plekhanov and Tolstoy, since between them they could stand for the others of their kind. I accepted their advice, while keeping my affection for him.

---

### Ethics

**Khuluq**—moral character—is a quality in a person. It may be stable or changing, innate or acquired.

In Arabic the word is pronounced *khuluq*, though the middle consonant can also be left unstressed. Popular speech later produced from it the meaning of mood or psychological state. Iraqis say, “My *khuluq* has narrowed,” meaning that they feel distressed or upset. People in the Levant may say, “Let me ease my *khuluq*,” meaning: let me relieve this inner pressure.

But ordinary people also use the original moral sense.

The positive meaning predominates. Shawqi writes:

> Nations endure only as long as their morals endure;
> when their morals disappear, they disappear as well.

Ordinary people say, “So-and-so has no morals.”

Educated people say instead that someone has “bad morals” or “high morals.”

It is better to preserve the positive sense as the word’s primary meaning. For the opposite we can say: bad traits and noble traits, bad conduct and good conduct.

Arabic has several near-synonyms.

*Shamāʾil* usually refers to good qualities.

*Sajaya* means deeply rooted traits in a person, and again usually carries a positive sense.

*Shiyam*, the plural of *shima*, is similar to *shamāʾil*. Ordinary people use it as a synonym for honour, gallantry, and generosity. Someone might appeal to another by saying, “O man of honour.”

Iraqis have a saying: “Appeal to the peasant’s honour and take his cloak.”

In other words, stir his sense of pride and generosity and he will give you even the cloak from his own shoulders, though he owns nothing else.

Here *gharami* means the peasant or Bedouin, as distinct from the town-dweller, the city man who—according to the joke—has no such sense of honour.

Sufi ethics has three main sources:

**pre-Islamic Arab values — Islamic teachings — Christian ethics.**

Among the basic values inherited from the pre-Islamic period and broadly accepted by the Sufis were the following.

#### Noncooperation

This means rejecting the state, resisting it, and refusing submission to it.

It is a social and political position, and we have already discussed it in the preceding Orbits.

#### Dignity

Abd al-Qadir al-Jili says in *Futuh al-Ghayb*:

> Be the servant of the Lord, not the servant of other human beings.

This expresses one of the worldly purposes of worshipping God, as it developed in both Christianity and Islam: a human being should be a servant of God, not the servant of another human being.

Sovereignty belongs to God alone. God is the only master.

Human beings are equal in their servitude to God. One cannot properly become the slave of another.

The obvious difficulty is slavery itself, where one human being is legally the master of another.

Jesus dealt with this by abolishing slavery in practice within the community of his movement. Its members were absolutely equal: neither wealth nor mastery gave one superiority over another.

But he did not dare issue a formal declaration abolishing slavery because he had to avoid direct confrontation with the Roman Empire.

As for Muhammad, one report in Shiite sources says that he considered abolishing slavery altogether (*Makarim al-Akhlaq* by the younger al-Tabrisi), but he did not do so.

Muhammad was pragmatic. His politics rested on balances and compromises, and his reason was stronger than his heart.

Still, he tried to lessen the suffering of slaves. He gave them rights unavailable under other slave systems. He prohibited beating and harming them, overworking them, and starving them. Masters were required to feed slaves from the same food they themselves ate, without distinction.

He even introduced a different language for speaking about them. Instead of saying “slave,” one was to say “young man” or “young woman.” A master speaking of a male or female slave should say “my young man” or “my young woman,” rather than “my slave” or “my bondwoman.”

It was a compromise between the slave’s right to freedom and the master’s supposed “right” of ownership.

The Sufis, for their part, did not call for the abolition of slavery either.

Instead they held to the principle of **moral character**, applying it to everyone: slave and free, Muslim and non-Muslim alike. In moral terms they gave them equal rights.

They also abolished slavery in their own personal lives. The great Sufi masters, in the overwhelming majority of cases, did not own male or female slaves.

This practice goes back to Abu Dharr al-Ghifari and Ruzbeh al-Isfahani—Salman the Persian—both of whom forbade themselves from owning slaves.

Each, however, had a freed woman who served him.

I have not been able to discover where these women came from: whether they had been purchased, or given to them as gifts and then freed, after which they remained as free servants.

I wish they had done without this service altogether. Even when it exists outside the legal institution of slavery, domestic service still carries something of slavery’s smell.

I mentioned in the previous Orbit that I included in the programme of my communalist organisation a demand for a law forbidding citizens from working as domestic servants in private homes.

Modern communist systems never achieved this.

It must be achieved under the communalist systems that will be built on **grassroots communism**, rather than communism imposed from above.

And, God willing, we will begin from this book.

Human dignity—personal honour—is almost as central a Sufi concern as divine love.

In *Awarif al-Ma'arif*, dignity is defined as:

> A person’s knowledge of his own true worth, and his refusal to lower it for immediate worldly gain.

Their concern with dignity was so exacting that they distinguished between receiving obligatory alms and accepting voluntary charity.

A poor person, they said, may take what is due from the obligatory alms, but should not take voluntary charity.

Why?

Because obligatory alms are a legal right. No one can claim personal credit for giving them. They are taken from the rich by obligation, if necessary by force. The poor person who receives them has therefore received something by virtue of his own right, not through the generosity of the rich.

Voluntary charity is different. It comes as a gift from the wealthy person, and with it can come condescension, favouritism, or even bribery. Accepting it can injure a person’s dignity or compromise his independence.

I once found myself facing a similar problem in China over a passport.

Some friends offered to obtain one for me through the Iraqi embassy there. I refused.

I told them:

“I will accept it in one of two ways. Either I raid the embassy and take it by force, or I bribe one of its employees to get it for me. In the first case I will have obtained it through my own strength, and in the second through my own money.”

Our masters suffered greatly under the burden of this idea of dignity.

Bishr al-Hafi said:

> What is lawful cannot bear extravagance, and taking from people is humiliation.

According to Ibn Asakir’s biography of him, Bishr made handkerchiefs with his own hands, sold them, and lived on the proceeds.

Their preference for manual work rested on two principles: refusal of the state and preservation of dignity.

Some took this to an extreme and rejected not only financial help but even personal favours.

Muhammad ibn Ali al-Tirmidhi, as quoted by al-Sulami in the *Tabaqat*, said:

> There is no burden in this world heavier than a favour. Whoever does you a kindness has tied you down; whoever neglects you has set you free.

(al-Sulami, p. 219)

He meant the sense of obligation created when someone does something for you. You then feel indebted to him.

This is why they said that the Sufi is someone who has no need of other people.

Yet he is also someone who serves other people and meets their needs.

One Sufi would refuse invitations to banquets from his friends, saying:

> Waiting for the gravy is humiliation.

He meant the humiliation of sitting in another man’s house waiting for the food to arrive.

Food is not worth waiting for. A Sufi eats because he needs to, not for pleasure. A need should be met simply, with whatever happens to be available, without ceremony or elaborate preparation.

If he sits waiting for food, he becomes like a hungry man staring into the faces of those who will feed him and waiting for them to do so.

Ibn Sirin took a middle course. If he was invited to a meal, he would drink some *sawiq* at home first so that he would not arrive hungry.

Dignity, however, is not absolute.

A Sufi should show dignity before rulers, rich people, and the powerful.

Toward the poor, the opposite applies: he should humble himself.

Abu Uthman al-Hiri of Nishapur said:

> To hold yourself high before the rich is humility, and to humble yourself before the poor is honour.

(al-Sulami, p. 175)

In modern terms, this means preserving the independence of culture in the face of state power and money, while surrendering that superiority before ordinary people.

The reverse is unacceptable: surrendering intellectual independence to the state while acting superior to ordinary people because “we are educated and they are illiterate.”

Dignity was a pre-Islamic Arab principle that Islam accepted, but Islam restricted it to believers:

> Honour belongs to God, His Messenger, and the believers.

The Sufis made it universal. They extended dignity to all creation.

One consequence of dignity is that a person should ask nothing from anyone.

This must be watched carefully, not only in the obvious form of direct begging, but also in indirect forms—even vague sentences that might be interpreted as a request or as announcing some need.

Al-Qushayri reports this instruction from Abu Turab al-Nakhshabi to his disciples:

> Whoever among you has no patched cloak has already asked. Whoever sits in a khanqah[^ms-v1-021] or a mosque has already asked. Whoever reads the Quran from a written copy where people can see or hear him has already asked.

The issue here is not dignity alone.

There is also the principle that a person must not join the Sufi path in pursuit of gain, prestige, or status.

The demands reinforce one another. A person cannot be genuinely self-respecting and opportunistic at the same time.

### Relations with People and the Sufi Personality

Moral character is the goal of the Sufi’s striving.

His annihilation in God is also an annihilation in creation.

Dhu al-Nun al-Misri describes the Sufi’s relations with others like this. He is:

> a helper to the stranger,
> a father to the orphan,
> a husband to the widow,
> gentle with the poor,
> and someone people can turn to in every hardship.

In everyday dealings with people, he is cheerful and welcoming.

To call him “a husband to the widow” means that he helps make up for the support she has lost with her husband, so that she is not left dependent and unprotected.

And by cheerfulness and warmth we mean an easy personality, free of the psychological knots of religious men, the arrogance of state officials and the rich, and the educated person’s habit of looking down on ordinary people.

Abd al-Qadir al-Jili listed the elements of the Sufi personality by assigning to it qualities scattered among the prophets:

generosity from Abraham, patience from Job, gesture from Zechariah, estrangement from John, wool from Moses, wandering from Jesus, and poverty from Muhammad (*Futuh al-Ghayb*, p. 124).

This is, of course, an Islamic projection onto those figures, giving them qualities drawn from stories in the Torah or from reports passed down by Jewish rabbis.

The “gesture” of Zechariah refers to his three-day silence, during which he communicated by signs.

As for the estrangement of John the Baptist, I do not know what is meant beyond the fact that he was unjustly killed.

Moses did not wear wool. He lived more like a king.

Muhammad was not poor except in Mecca. Perhaps what was meant was simplicity of life, and that Muhammad certainly had.

Christ’s wandering was part of preaching his message, and it did not extend beyond the towns of Palestine.

Still, the Sufi personality that combines generosity, patience, asceticism, and poverty is, in this sense, a prophetic personality.

There is some ambiguity in the idea of generosity, because generosity assumes that one possesses money to give away.

The Sufi is not supposed to possess money.

Still, he can practise generosity with whatever happens to come into his hands.

One example is the *futuh*: unsolicited gifts from wealthy people sympathetic to Sufism. A shaykh might accept such gifts and then distribute them among the poor.

One story about Ibn Arabi says that a Turkish ruler in Anatolia gave him a magnificent palace worth one hundred thousand dirhams.

Ibn Arabi lived in it for a time.

Then a beggar came asking him for something.

At that moment Ibn Arabi had nothing else to give, so he gave the man the entire palace and walked out.

I believe the story.

The madness of the Sufis in conduct is the same madness we find in their speech.

We have already seen, however, that Ibn Arabi did accept gifts from rulers, unlike the great masters of social Sufism. He distributed most of what he received and kept only enough to support his family. He had two sons and a daughter.

The quality of “gesture” may also mean economy in speech and a preference for silence. The Sufis were not fond of long speeches or chatter.

The Sufi’s estrangement comes from seeing what others do not see. He is therefore a stranger in his own homeland.

Wandering points to their constant movement and travel.

Travel is one of the enduring urges of civilised people.

Islam also gives it a special emphasis through the principle of migration:

> Whoever migrates in the path of God will find many places of refuge on the earth, and abundance.

The original meaning of migration here is defiance: challenging the source of persecution in one’s homeland. The first migration was from Mecca to Medina.

Wandering is migration at any time and from any place that has become too narrow for a person, allowing him to find freedom elsewhere.

When I left Baghdad twenty years ago, my migration was an act of defiance toward those who had seized control of it, not a search for comfort.

We have prophetic sayings encouraging travel, and others discouraging it or showing little enthusiasm for it.

The first were probably invented by merchants and Sufis, the second by jurists.

Chinese Muslims circulate a saying about travel in two forms:

> Whoever dies a stranger dies a martyr.

And:

> The death of a stranger is martyrdom.

The saying used to be written on the tombs of early Muslims in China before their descendants became fully settled there. The latest examples I have seen date from the middle of the eighth Islamic century.

Those who came to China during the Islamic period included merchants, ordinary travellers, and political refugees.

In one Chinese museum I saw a pottery figure of an Arab traveller, which suggests that such travellers were common in China at the time.

Travel became one of the marks of Sufi life. For this reason they established lodges where travelling Sufis—and other travellers as well—could stay free of charge.

It is said that Ibrahim al-Khawwas never remained in a single town for more than forty days.

The number forty is not meant literally here. Such numbers are often used simply to suggest either a short or a long period, and in this case it means a short one.

On his journeys he carried a water flask, a robe, and a needle.

He carried no food. He ate whatever he found along the way.

Even that was more luggage than Jesus is said to have carried. Christ had only a comb and a cup.

Then he threw away the cup when he saw someone drinking from a river with his hands.

And he threw away the comb when he saw someone combing his beard with his fingers.

Al-Ghazali devoted an entire book of more than a hundred large pages in the *Ihya* to travel.

Travel, of course, is not exclusive to the Sufis. As I said, it is one of the urges of civilised people. A great deal of poetry has been written about it outside Sufi literature.

Another moral quality expected of the Sufi is gentleness toward people, together with a refusal to oppress or attack them.

By “people” I mean ordinary people: creation, the Sufis’ people, the general public—not rulers and the rich.

In his commentary on *Nahj al-Balagha*, Ibn Abi al-Hadid quotes a Christian-like teaching attributed to Junayd:

> The knower is not truly a knower until he is like the earth, walked upon by both the righteous and the wicked; like the cloud, giving shade to everything; and like the rain, watering what grows and what does not grow.

In other words, he deals generously with both decent and indecent people.

This does not mean kindness toward corrupt rulers and wealthy men. It means tolerance toward people whose conduct differs from your own in matters of religion and personal behaviour.

The great jurists[^ms-v1-022] distinguished between violating the rights of God and violating the rights of other human beings.

They held that the first category should be treated with tolerance, while the second required strictness.

Violations of God’s rights include neglect of acts of worship and personal prohibitions such as drinking wine or committing adultery.

Violations of people’s rights include oppression, theft, murder, assault, and anything else that harms another person’s interests or rights.

These must be treated much more seriously.

Within this distinction, Sufyan al-Thawri—as we mentioned earlier—would not even look at the palaces of the rich or of state officials. This was an extension of the principle of refusing contact with them.

At the same time, he dealt normally with sinners such as drinkers and libertines.

He even praised the conduct of the young **ayyars**, bands who specialised in robbing the rich and government officials.

Sufyan is counted by the Sufis among the prophets of Sufism, even though he lived before Sufism appeared as a movement.

Junayd’s phrase “like the cloud giving shade to everything” means serving all people without asking about their religion or beliefs.

And his phrase “like the rain, watering what grows and what does not grow” means doing good for those who deserve it and those who do not.

It resembles the popular saying today:

> Do good and throw it into the river.

In other words, do not wait to be rewarded for kindness.

This teaching is Islamic but derives from Christianity.

The pre-Islamic Arabs did not approve of helping someone who did not deserve help. Zuhayr says in his famous ode:

> Whoever does good for one unworthy of it
> will see his praise turn to blame, and will regret it.

The prophetic tradition revised this:

> Do good to those who deserve it and those who do not. If the person you help does not deserve good, you yourself are still one of those who do.

All this concerns service to people in ordinary life.

Junayd’s teaching also calls for decent treatment of people despite differences in conduct and belief—with the exception, again, of rulers, the wealthy, and those who spread corruption.

The Sufi is expected to repair whatever fault he sees in another person, covering for him and concealing his shortcomings.

Hamdan al-Qassar expressed this beautifully:

> If you see a drunk man, stagger yourself, so that you do not become arrogant toward him and then find yourself tested by the same thing.

The point is that you should display in yourself the defect you see in another person, so that you do not imagine yourself superior to him, and so that you can help protect him from ridicule and humiliation.

The Sufis were extremely sensitive about such things. They were anxious not to hurt people because of mistakes or embarrassing accidents.

One story tells of a woman who came to Hatim al-Asamm to ask him a question.

While she was there, she accidentally passed wind.

She froze in embarrassment.

Hatim immediately said:

“Speak louder.”

He pretended to be deaf.

The woman relaxed because she believed he had not heard what had happened.

Hatim was an Afghan from Balkh. When he came to Baghdad, his Arabic carried a heavy accent. Yet no one could debate him without being defeated by his argument.

Some Baghdadis asked him how this was possible when he was a foreigner and spoke Arabic with difficulty.

He replied:

“I have three qualities. I am happy when my opponent is right. I am sad when he is wrong. And I guard myself against treating him ignorantly”—meaning that he would not insult or abuse him in argument.

What people today call democratic dialogue, Hatim regarded as the reason he could defeat an opponent.

Stories like this about the Sufi masters were circulated in Sufi books for educational purposes. They understood that an example or a story can teach more effectively than a long sermon.

Sufi conduct also requires relationships free of needless ceremony and formality.

A Sufi should be easy company. He should not burden the people around him or force them to manage his moods. He should not be an unpleasant guest.

As Dhu al-Nun al-Misri put it, he should be cheerful and welcoming.

They disliked excessive politeness.

*Qut al-Qulub* says that the earlier generations, when they met one another, rarely said more than:

“How are you?”

or:

“How are all of you?”

They also disapproved of prying into other people’s affairs.

It was considered contrary to proper conduct to meet someone in the road and ask:

“Where are you going?”

or:

“Where have you come from?”

If a Sufi knocked at someone’s door, he would pause for a little while to make sure the person inside was ready to receive him.

They had no way of arranging appointments beforehand.

If permission to enter was not given, he simply left without becoming upset.

Riyah al-Qaysi once suffered a severe crisis of conscience after visiting a friend and finding him asleep.

He criticised the man for sleeping.

Then he realised what he had said.

He went off to the cemetery and began rebuking himself.

This belongs to what they called **self-accounting**—what people today would call self-criticism—carried out privately between a person and himself.

Alongside this, or completing it, was indifference to what other people said about you.

Praise and blame should become equal.

The Sufi himself neither praises nor condemns—again, meaning ordinary people, not rulers and the rich. He certainly condemns rulers, wealthy men, and soldiers, and naturally does not praise them.

He may, however, praise an ordinary person who deserves it, in order to encourage good qualities.

Indifference to praise and blame is one measure of strength in the Sufi personality.

It also expresses independence: the seeker refuses to submit to social standards he considers false or inauthentic.

A man once asked Abu Yazid al-Bistami how he might attain inner peace.

Abu Yazid told him to go out into the market riding a stick like a hobbyhorse, running along while children shouted after him.

The point was to destroy his solemn public image, break pride within him, and free him from the obsession with reputation and status.

They also cited Umar ibn al-Khattab, who used to walk quickly and say that walking quickly got things done and kept him further from vanity.

This conflicts with a saying that claims:

> Walking quickly takes away the believer’s dignity.

But reports of Umar’s quick pace are strengthened by Aisha’s description of him: whenever he walked, she said, he moved fast.

Umar was tall and his feet turned outward. When he walked, he looked almost as though he were riding while everyone else was walking.

Members of ruling families, beginning with the Umayyads, had a special gait they trained themselves to use.

Tyrannical rulers, rich men, and military officers likewise have a way of walking marked by heaviness and swagger.

The Sufis wanted the walk of ordinary people: natural, without ceremony or display.

Another requirement of Sufi independence is that the Sufi must not worship his teachers.

That does not mean failing to respect and honour them. A teacher has rights over a student.

It means refusing to believe that the teacher is infallible.

A disciple needs a teacher.

Al-Bistami said:

> Whoever has no shaykh has Satan for a shaykh.

But the disciple should receive his teacher’s teaching with an awake mind. Education should not be rote indoctrination. It should be understood and absorbed.

At the same time, they discouraged constant argument with the teacher because it showed poor manners and could make teaching impossible.

But education has a limit.

In *Futuh al-Ghayb* that point is called **the stage of weaning**. The disciple separates from his teacher and becomes a teacher in his own right.

Tolerance is a central ethical principle in the Sufi personality.

We have already seen how it works in everyday social relations.

But it also applies to questions of belief.

We have discussed the principle of equal rights among religions, and between religious and non-religious people.

Abd al-Qadir al-Jili is particularly strict on the question of declaring people unbelievers.

Among the qualities required of people undergoing spiritual discipline, he says, is this:

> Never state with certainty that any person who belongs to the Muslim community is guilty of idolatry, unbelief, or hypocrisy. This is closer to mercy, higher in rank, more faithful to the Prophetic way, and further from intruding into God’s knowledge.

(*Futuh al-Ghayb*, p. 128, discourse 78.)

Al-Jili raised the matter because he was a jurist as well as a Sufi.

His statement is therefore also a legal judgment against declaring people unbelievers.

Most jurists did not agree, though some leading ones did. Taj al-Din al-Subki, for example, agreed that a Muslim should not be declared an unbeliever merely because of an opinion he expresses.

He limited such rulings to explicit apostasy: openly leaving Islam and entering another religion.

When dealing with people of different religions and different forms of religious conduct, al-Jili also told his disciples never to consider themselves better than anyone else.

A Sufi should deal with sinful or immoral Muslims, and with non-Muslims, on the assumption that they may all be better than he is.

Why?

Because the sinner may repent while he himself later falls into sin.

And the non-Muslim may become Muslim while he himself leaves Islam.

(*Futuh al-Ghayb*, p. 129.)

Through teachings like these, the Sufis confronted a deep problem in the religious personality: the tendency to see itself as superior to everyone else and to claim exclusive possession of truth and virtue.

The Sufi is open toward people who differ from him in conduct or belief.

He does not pursue people by branding them sinful, heretical, or unbelieving.

In the same spirit, the Sufis rejected the excessive ritualism of Islam and tried to reduce its weight.

As we saw in earlier Orbits, they criticised acts of worship and even accused the prophets of burdening people with too many obligations.

Al-Sha'rani says in *Tanbih al-Mughtarrin* that among their ethical principles was this:

spending money to feed the hungry, clothe the naked, and pay the debts of people unable to repay them should take priority over building Sufi lodges, religious houses, and similar structures—that is, over building mosques. (p. 168)

Abd Allah ibn al-Mubarak said:

> A mouthful of food in the stomach of a hungry person weighs more heavily in my scale than the building of a mosque.

Sufi books say a great deal about **trust in God**, and they give it different meanings.

Some interpreted it passively: a person should not pursue livelihood too aggressively, because livelihood comes from God.

This view is usually found among Sufis closer to Sunni orthodoxy.

But the more common Sufi position is expressed in the Prophet’s saying:

> Tie your camel, then trust in God.

He is said to have told this to a Bedouin who asked whether he could leave his camel untied and rely on God to protect it.

The general principle is simple:

**work first, trust second.**

In this order, trust in God is psychological. It helps a person deal with the effects of failure, so that disappointment does not destroy him or darken his spirit.

It also encourages him to keep working, because he feels that God remains with him.

The Sufis made work a duty.

They required the disciple to earn a living before devoting himself fully to Sufism. He was not permitted simply to wait for someone else to feed him.

As we have already seen, the best livelihood in their eyes was that earned by one’s own hands—the work of craftsmen and wage labourers.

In the section on proper social conduct in the *Ihya*, al-Ghazali says:

> The purest food a servant of God can eat is what a craftsman earns with his own hands, provided he works honestly.

That is, provided he does not cheat in his craft.

Among their instructions to disciples, as reported by al-Qushayri, was this:

> Whoever desires what ordinary people desire must obtain it in the way ordinary people obtain it: through the labour of his hands and the sweat of his brow.

(p. 183.)

They also gave “trust in God” characteristically Sufi interpretations, often stretching words beyond their obvious meaning.

*Qut al-Qulub* defines it as:

> The heart’s stillness toward what people possess, and the ending of concern with what lies in their hands.

In other words: not needing other people and not longing for what they own.

Another definition says:

> No one reaches the station of trust until praise and blame from other people become equal to him.

(2/5–9.)

This adds another element to the personality we have already been describing.

Do not care excessively about what other people say about you, so long as you know yourself, recognise your own faults, and judge yourself honestly.

There is also a streak of madness in the Sufi personality, just as there is in Sufi speech.

We have already discussed it.

Seen in that light, the stories of Sufis giving away everything they owned and living instead from manual work—or from the very little required for survival—become perfectly believable.

We need not be surprised that Ibrahim ibn Adham handed over ten thousand dinars to the slave whom his family sent to him after his father’s death, then freed the man and continued earning his own living by harvesting crops and cleaning.

Nor need we be surprised that Yusuf ibn Asbat inherited seventy thousand dirhams and refused to take any of it.

Or that Ibn Arabi gave his royal palace to a beggar, as though determined to outdo his ancestor Hatim, who slaughtered his only horse when he had nothing else with which to feed a guest.

Ibn Arabi was proud of this connection and called himself **al-Hatimi**.

When the Sufi speaks of absolute unity, he also applies that unity to the intimate bond between thought and conduct.

The two become one in their madness, in their departure from the logic accepted by the surrounding world.

And once he reaches that point, he does not care if people throw stones at him.

### Ordering Rights and Duties

Al-Harith al-Muhasibi, a third-century figure and one of the masters of Sunni Sufism, arranged a number of basic rights and duties in the following order:

* **A mother’s claim to care comes before a father’s.** A child should attend to his mother first because she is usually more in need of care.

* **Supporting one’s family comes before pilgrimage or the lesser pilgrimage.** A person must first secure his family’s livelihood before travelling to Mecca.

* **A debt that has fallen due comes before pilgrimage.** If someone owes money and payment is due, he should not go on pilgrimage. He must pay the debt first, and then perform the pilgrimage if he can still afford to do so.

* **A wife’s rights come before satisfying one’s parents.**

Al-Muhasibi does not say that a wife’s rights come before the rights of one’s parents. He says they come before **pleasing** them.

This is precise.

A wife’s rights and the rights of parents are equally binding. One set of rights cannot simply be cancelled for the sake of the other.

But parental approval is something different.

Parents may treat a wife unjustly. In that case, her rights cannot be violated merely to keep them happy.

This follows the Quranic teaching:

> If they pressure you to associate with Me what you have no knowledge of, do not obey them; but continue to live with them in this world with kindness.

Parents may order someone to do wrong. They may themselves act unjustly. They are not to be obeyed in such matters, while their legitimate rights are still preserved.

Al-Harith gives another instruction on the same subject.

A person must not please his parents by cutting off relatives.

Parents may be involved in grudges and disputes with other members of the family. Their child is not therefore permitted to withhold help or service from those relatives simply to satisfy them.

* **Friday prayer comes before returning a deposit held in trust.**

This belongs to the rights of worship.

Perhaps al-Muhasibi assumed that delaying the return of the deposit until after prayer would cause no harm.

Otherwise the ruling would conflict with the general principle that one should be lenient in matters involving God’s rights and strict in matters involving the rights of people.

Al-Muhasibi has other teachings as well. Among the most important are these:

* **Do not beat or humiliate your children to please your wife.**

The child meant here is a child from another wife who has died or been divorced.

Some fathers mistreat such children in order to please a new wife.

Women in this situation can sometimes lose all compassion and acquire a harshness almost military in character, making it difficult to apply to them the old description “the gentle sex.”

Al-Harith al-Muhasibi recognised these realities and tried to deal with them through his Sufi moral sense.

* **Do not fulfil moral obligations using suspect money.**

By obligatory rights he means money a person is required to provide to parents, relatives, or some public interest.

“Suspect money” means wealth tainted by illegality: money obtained through seizure, embezzlement, bribery, or similar means.

Much of what Sufis accepted from rulers fell into this category.

The poet alludes to the contradiction:

> Like a woman seeking to do good with money earned from selling her body—
> damn you, neither fornicate nor give alms.

The Sufis had no settled position on distributing money stolen from the rich in the manner of the ayyars.

And Abd al-Qadir al-Jili’s call to seize wealth and distribute it among the poor remains too vague to tell us exactly what he had in mind.

### Guidance for Solving a Problem

In the fourth discourse of *Futuh al-Ghayb*, al-Jili gives three successive options for dealing with a problem.

The first is to rely on yourself.

Try to solve the problem through your own abilities and resources.

If you cannot solve it yourself, turn to other people: the state and its employees, people with influence or wealth, respected figures in society—or doctors, if you are ill.

If none of them can solve the problem, then turn to your Lord through prayer and supplication.

The sequence is deliberate:

so long as you can help yourself, do not turn to other people;

so long as other people can help you, do not turn to the Creator.

Several basic principles of life follow from this teaching.

**1. Rely on yourself first, and try not to depend on others.**

**2. It is permissible to seek the state’s help in solving a personal problem.**

This does not contradict the principle of keeping one’s distance from the state.

The governed have rights over the state, and they are entitled to demand them.

Security is an obvious example. If someone is attacked, he may turn to the authorities to stop the attacker.

In disputes of every kind, the state may have to intervene through the police and courts.

But what about asking the state for financial help?

An ordinary person has the right to do so.

A Sufi, a jurist, or any other learned person—that is, anyone we would now call an intellectual—does not.

It is useful to keep one distinction in mind.

The principle of noncooperation with the state is binding on the intellectual, not on ordinary people.

The exception is when an organised movement is under way to remove a ruler and ordinary people have joined it. At that point, the ordinary participant has effectively become a political or union activist and must also refrain from contact or cooperation with the state.
