Something like this happened during the reform movement, when the people of Baghdad mobilised against al-Muqtadir and his minister Hamid ibn al-Abbas.

At that point, any person who established contact with the caliph or his minister was effectively breaking with the collective position.

Similar questions arose in the modern Iraqi national movement, first led by jurists and later by communists. They were really continuations of old traditions of political opposition.

When Shaykh Mahdi al-Khalisi issued a ruling calling for a boycott of King Faisal’s government, any politician who accepted a ministerial or administrative office came to be accused of betrayal.

But no one objected when an ordinary citizen submitted a petition asking the state to stop an assault, remove an injustice, or remedy some harm.

The same general pattern has continued to the present day.

**3. Turning to God and placing one’s trust in Him comes only after one has lost hope of solving the problem by ordinary means.**

The sequence ends in surrender to God.

From this we can understand God’s action as bringing peace to the heart of the desperate person, so that despair does not destroy him.

#### Summary

The Sufi personality is positive, not negative.

It acts as much as it receives.

It remains open to life even while renouncing sensual pleasures and turning away from luxury.

The Sufi is free.

He loves nature, and for that reason he travels. Wandering is one of the foundations of his freedom.

That freedom rests on other foundations as well, the first of which is **renunciation**:

**own nothing, so that nothing can own you.**

The Sufi is independent.

He follows no one blindly, obeys no one absolutely, and imitates no one.

He has self-respect. He guards his dignity and his human essence.

He is hard toward those who dominate others: rulers, wealthy men, and soldiers.

Toward ordinary people he is tolerant. He loves them, defends them, and serves them.

He is not fanatical about a religious school, a religion, or a group.

He does not pursue people by calling them foolish or inferior.

He does not condemn ordinary people. His condemnation is directed toward rulers, the rich, and soldiers.

He may praise an ordinary person to encourage upright conduct, but he does not praise those in power.

He is free of malice without being naive.

Peaceful without being weak.

He smiles at people, but his heart is sad and full of cares.

The Sufi also cares about cleanliness, sometimes to an extreme.

It is said that Abu Yazid al-Bistami had three sets of clothes: one for going outside, one for wearing at home, and a third for entering the lavatory.

The same was true of his shoes.

Flies were said not to approach Abd al-Qadir al-Jilani because he was so clean and used perfume.

Yet he sat with the poor and picked lice from their clothes.

He did this because he could not provide them with proper means of cleanliness, so he shared the burden with them himself.

Some Sufis, however, discouraged excessive bathing. Such figures seem to have been influenced by Indian asceticism.

The Sufi speaks little.

He does not indulge in endless disputes or insist on argument.

They often repeat a saying attributed to Ma'ruf al-Karkhi and others:

> When God wishes good for a people, He opens the door of action and closes the door of argument. When He wishes the opposite, He opens the door of argument and closes the door of action.

The Sufi is sensitive.

He does not burden anyone.

People do not feel uncomfortable in his company.

He makes no grand claims and does not boast.

He sees himself as less than others and does not believe that anyone owes him recognition for his virtues.

He is honest with himself and with other people.

For that reason, he does not remain fixed in one state. He changes constantly as new truths become visible to him.

Junayd expressed this by saying:

> The truthful person changes forty times in a day. The hypocrite remains in one condition for forty years.

Why?

Because the Sufi accepts truth when it comes from another person, while recognising error when it comes from himself and correcting it.

He learns from what he reads and from what he experiences, developing both his ideas and his practice.

This quality belongs to the great intellectual—the universal intellectual.

It marks a movement from merely being shaped by things to becoming someone capable of shaping them: from being governed by the world to becoming capable of judging it.

The Palestinian critic Yusuf Sami al-Yusuf once published an essay on Sufism in which he discussed Sufi ethics and argued for its superiority to the ethics of the German philosopher Immanuel Kant.

His reason was that Sufi ethics contains a living, active element, whereas Kant’s ethics, in his view, remains purely abstract, rigid, and alienated from life.

This is really a broader problem with philosophers and ethics, including the philosophers of Islam.

Their ethics tends to consist more of abstract moral instruction than of living ethical systems.

Abu Bakr al-Razi’s *Philosophical Life* took a more practical direction because it began from his own conduct and his life as a doctor and philosopher.

Al-Razi shared something of the Sufi spirit in his deistic spirituality, which rejected organised religion, and in his relationship with ordinary people: he served them through medicine and defended them in his philosophy.

He founded the independent line in Islamic philosophy.

Greek teachers did not exercise a one-way influence over him. His metaphysical system also drew on Eastern sources, which he developed through his own creative approach.

Al-Razi could easily be placed among the **Abdal**: those exemplary figures who respected ordinary people, defended them, and served them through their own field of expertise.

But the philosophical way of life described by al-Razi is not typical of philosophers in general, especially in European philosophy beginning with the Greeks.

Few Greek or modern European philosophers combined the roles of philosopher and sage.

Most lived ordinary lives with little in their conduct to distinguish them from anyone else.

Their relationship with ordinary people was blocked by the complexity of their thought and by their intellectual aristocracy.

The same is true of the philosophers of Islam.

They had little relationship with ordinary people, though their personal lives varied.

Al-Farabi and al-Shirazi were spiritually inclined.

Ibn Sina was pleasure-loving.

Most of the others lived ordinary lives, without anything especially distinctive in their stance or conduct.

### A Warning and a Reminder

Adonis drew a sharp comparison between Sufism and Surrealism from several angles.

Both break out into the space of the irrational and the limitless.

Both rebel against reality, its social conventions, and the laws imposed by the state.

Both seek the human freedom that existed before the state enslaved the human being.

But from what Adonis himself writes about Surrealism in his important book *Sufism and Surrealism*, I have come to believe that Surrealism bears a considerable share of responsibility for the disintegration visible in the West today.

It provided an ideology for an unrestrained idea of freedom: the idea that the individual may realise his individuality at any moment, in any place, by any means; that every barrier standing before overwhelming desire should be smashed in the name of freedom; that the body should become the vehicle of experiment.

Under this logic, unrestricted sex merges with divine love, and killing according to personal whim is joined to rejection of the state.

From there one enters the theatre of organised crime.

Sufi ethics has nothing to do with this.

Different doctrines must not be carelessly mixed together.

Adonis should have approached the Western text cautiously—with his own critical caution, not in the manner of translators.

Sufism seeks to build a good world free of religion, superstition, the state, and aggression.

A free world.

But one governed by values people accept voluntarily.

In such a world, a person does not kill because he feels like it.

Nor is a woman’s body violated in the name of rebellion against the family.

When the Sufi loves a woman, she becomes spiritual in his eyes.

In that spiritualisation, human love and divine love merge into one another.

#### Postscript

The letters have informed me that one reason for the extreme emphasis some Sufis placed on trust in God was the fierce greed of the rich and of state officials in accumulating wealth.

Some Sufis responded by stressing renunciation in order to counter that drive.

This negative or passive tendency does not appear in other matters such as seeking knowledge, struggle, or resistance to the three powers.

### Farewell to the Orbit of Zayn al-Abidin

During the Battle of Siffin between Ali and Muawiya, Muawiya seized the only usable watering place on that stretch of the Euphrates and prevented Ali’s army from reaching it.

Ali organised an attack, drove Muawiya’s forces away, and took control of the water.

But then he ordered that a path be opened so that Muawiya’s men could come down to the river and drink.

This was an extreme application of moral rules in war.

Ali would have been within his rights to deny the enemy water and use thirst as a means of forcing them to surrender.

The ethical rules of war should go no further than prohibiting treacherous weapons of surprise—which in ancient times meant burning and poisoning, both forbidden in Islam—and requiring humane treatment of prisoners.

No more than that.

## The Orbit of Orbits

### Some Sufi Terms

The Sufis broke with ordinary, logical ways of writing. They plunged into madness, linguistic delirium, and strained interpretation. They also broke free of the limits imposed by dictionaries and grammar. They drew new meanings out of existing words, sometimes through an obvious connection, sometimes through a distant one, and sometimes with no visible connection at all. They coined new words from roots and derivatives, bending language to their purposes.

They paid little attention to the grammarians. To them, grammarians belonged to the outside world. The grammarian and the Sufi had no common ear with which to hear one another and no common tongue with which to speak.

They made context, not dictionary meaning or grammatical form, decide what a word meant. Some of their terms never settled on a single meaning at all. They used them according to what the argument required at that moment, which is why their own explanations of these terms often differ.

Here are some of them, drawn from al-Sarraj al-Tusi’s *al-Lumaʿ* and other works.

**State:** Something that descends on a person in a particular moment, remains briefly, then passes. Most Sufis held that a state does not last. A moment of clarity may flash into being and disappear almost at once. Joy, sadness, contraction, expansion, longing, anxiety, and stillness are all states.

**Station:** Something that becomes firmly established in a person and endures. Reaching a station requires effort and discipline. It may begin as a passing state, recur again and again, and eventually become permanent.

A station in this sense is what the existentialist philosopher Kierkegaard called an “eternal disposition.”

One station can also rise into a higher one. Stations include self-examination, love, asceticism, opposition, closeness to people, and bringing them comfort.

**Place:** The level reached by people who have attained perfection, firmness, and the final stages of the path. Once a person has completed the inner meanings and passed through states and stations, a lasting place becomes established for him.

The “place” is therefore the highest position, associated with the rank of the Pole.

**Witnessing:** Nearness and direct presence. It is close in meaning to unveiling, though unveiling is fuller.

At the beginning of witnessing, the marks of particular existence shine out through disclosures of presence, while still remaining beneath the cover of the unseen. The heart then seeks an unbroken presence with what the unseen has given it.

Witnessing means disclosure: seeing the truth, living it, and experiencing it directly rather than thinking about it. Thought belongs to reason and logic. Witnessing is seeing, touching, and living.

It belongs to the world of intuition, reached through contemplation and complete absorption in the hidden depths of existence.

Al-Qushayri says that by “unveiling” the Sufis may have meant something close to what a person sees in the state between waking and sleep.

This may also be one source of the delirium in which logic breaks apart and the distance between opposites disappears. The person in delirium stands somewhere between waking awareness and the hiddenness of intoxication.

**Glimpses:** Secrets that flash into view and lift the seeker upward from one state to a higher one. They appear during his progress, but they are fleeting and do not last.

After them come the **gleams**, which last longer, and then the **rising lights**, which are settled states.

**Shares and rights:** Rights are what a person is entitled to by religious law or accepted custom, especially what is needed for life and survival.

Shares are whatever lies beyond those rights. Among the Sufis they are usually spiritual things, such as mystical drinking or divine manifestation.

The two stand in tension. When rights appear, shares disappear; when shares appear, rights disappear.

The tension is between a person’s material rights and his spiritual shares. The higher a Sufi rises through the stages of nearness and manifestation, the more he gives up his own claims.

**The elite of the elite:** A rank reached by those who have crossed through the states and stations, travelled their paths, and passed through their deserts.

**Allusion:** Something whose meaning is too subtle for the speaker to reveal in a direct statement. It hints rather than explains.

Al-Rudhabari said:

> Our knowledge is allusion. Once it becomes explicit statement, it disappears.

This is also what al-Niffari meant when he said:

> The wider the vision, the narrower the words.

Language therefore has to be exceeded by allusion.

Here, “allusion” means symbol and whatever works like it. It does not mean bodily gesture. Bodily gesture is **signalling**.

**Symbol:** A hidden meaning stored beneath visible words, recognised only by those who belong to the tradition.

Al-Naqqad said:

> When they speak, the reach of their symbols defeats you;
> when they fall silent, how far you are from reaching them.

The word in the first half-line violates the expected grammar. The Sufis did not care much about grammar.

Someone once said that if you want to understand the symbols of our masters, look through their letters and correspondence. Their real symbols are there, not in their formal books.

Unfortunately, almost none of their correspondence has survived.

**Clarity:** A state purified of interference from ordinary temperament, in which realities are seen as the acts of the Real in the present moment.

For some Sufis it simply means leaving blameworthy qualities behind.

**The clarity of clarity:** Passing beyond states and stations and entering the final stages.

It also means freeing the secrets from created things so that the Real is witnessed through the Real, continuously and without cause.

What is meant is the knower reaching the furthest limit of knowledge.

**Gathering:** A state of union in which the person sees nothing but the Real. Whoever still sees something other than the Real has not gathered.

It means that awareness of the Real completely takes possession of a person’s inner life. When he returns to ordinary action, he returns to separation.

We might put it this way:

Seeing acts is separation.
Seeing attributes is gathering.
Seeing the Essence is the gathering of gathering.

The gathering of gathering is annihilation.

There is also the **eye of gathering**, which is itself the eye of annihilation.

This level corresponds to pure unity: complete identification between the knower and the Real.

Some claimed that reaching it required leaving creed and religious law behind. Umar al-Suhrawardi rejected this. Gathering, he said, belongs to the spirit, while separation belongs to the human frame. As long as the human being remains made of both, gathering and separation must remain together.

In other words, the knower moves back and forth between moments of annihilation and moments of awareness. He does not dissolve completely.

What matters here is that the great Sufi masters did not accept release from the religious law, even though they had all moved beyond fixed creed.

Leaving religious law altogether would mean falling into lawlessness and permissiveness.

Al-Tahanawi tells us in his *Kashshaf* (1/116) that some false Sufis said:

> We have no power to avoid sins or carry out commandments. No one in this world owns another person or another person’s labour. Everyone shares wealth and spouses alike.

They confused communal ownership of wealth with communal ownership of women.

The great masters of social Sufism accepted the first, not the second.

Those who preached this kind of permissiveness were minor Sufis who claimed to have reached absolute gathering and the furthest edge of knowledge. In reality they had not. Only the great ones reached it.

And the great ones did not abandon the rules of religion and ethics. They freed themselves from creeds and from fanaticism toward them.

They could ignore religious rules they rejected—slavery, polygamy, commerce—but they remained committed to other rules governing conduct, social relations, rights, and things that must not be violated.

According to al-Junayd, Abu Yazid reached the eye of gathering. He left creed behind, but he did not abandon the ethical obligations of religious law. In fact, he added moral obligations that the law itself did not contain.

He completed what it lacked.

**Sobriety and intoxication:** Two opposite states.

Intoxication is the astonishment that overtakes the lover when the beauty of the beloved suddenly appears before him. More generally, it means being overwhelmed by the power of a state.

It belongs to attraction and annihilation.

After intoxication comes sobriety: the knower returns to ordinary sensation after having been absent from it.

“Intoxication” is a metaphor for being so completely absorbed in contemplation that things being contemplated become disclosed.

Something like this can happen to any serious thinker absorbed in investigation. Among Sufis, however, it can become complete immersion in the cosmic spirit—in the divine Essence understood in the Sufi, rather than conventional religious, sense.

Then intoxication passes. A state, as we have said, does not last. The knower returns to sobriety.

It is in this return that knowledge reaches completion, because the intuitive disclosure produced by deep contemplation becomes fully realised.

This is the method of Sufi philosophy: intuition.

There are two kinds of sobriety.

The first comes before intoxication. It is the ordinary state shared by everyone.

The second comes after intoxication. In it the knower returns filled with majesty and beauty, together with the truths discovered during contemplation.

The second sobriety may not arrive immediately. The knower may pass through a process of fluctuation, moving back and forth between intoxication and sobriety until the state settles and becomes firm.

They also associated **ecstatic utterance** with intoxication.

An ecstatic utterance is a statement that breaks with creed. Many of the famous sayings attributed to Abu Yazid and al-Hallaj belong to this category. It is one of the features of irreligious Sufism.

But it can also appear in orthodox Sufism. There are many examples.

The truth is that ecstatic utterance is common to all the great Sufi masters, whatever category we place them in.

The ecstatic sayings of al-Jili the lover and al-Jili the grandson resemble one another. In the grandson they are spread out; in the ancestor they are brief.

Linking ecstatic utterance to intoxication can be a way of escaping responsibility for what was said. But the utterance also belongs to sobriety, because what the ecstatic says while intoxicated does not fundamentally change when he returns to sobriety.

That is what they mean when they say that the second sobriety is the summit of perfection.

**Presence:** Close in meaning to sobriety. Sobriety moves between intoxication and sobriety, while presence is continuous.

Presence belongs to the heart, not the body.

When Bishr al-Hafi was asked why he did not pray in the first row, he said:

> I know what He wants. He wants hearts brought near, not bodies brought near.

They also said that presence can exist in absence.

Abu al-Husayn al-Nuri put it this way:

> When I disappear, He appears;
> when He appears, He makes me disappear.

**Rushing in and overpowering impulses:** Someone seized by a powerful desire for what he seeks will rush toward it.

If he believes what he wants lies across a sea, he swims into the sea. If he believes it lies in a wilderness, he enters the wilderness.

When desire overwhelms him and the demand becomes strong enough, he may even throw himself into fire.

Whether this destroys him, costs him his life, brings him to what he seeks, or fails to bring him there makes no difference.

This is an answer to outsiders who treat Sufism as pure negation, and treat Tao in the same way.

No. As our master Abd al-Qadir said:

> In every field of glory they rise to a lofty rank,
> and in every army they march at the head of the procession.

**Annihilation and abiding:** These have many definitions.

Annihilation can be relative or absolute. Absolute annihilation occurs when the power of reality completely takes possession of the knower, so that he disappears from his own interests and from everything else.

In *al-Lumaʿ*, annihilation is the disappearance of the self’s attributes, the disappearance of resistance, and coming to rest in the state that has descended.

**Abiding** means the endurance of annihilation.

The disappearance of the self’s qualities may be a passing state, or it may settle and remain for a long time.

There can also be annihilation in the Real, in which the qualities of the created being disappear into the qualities of the Creator.

This is the eye of gathering: the stage in which the human being becomes divine.

Many Sufis proclaimed it, among them al-Hallaj and Abu Yazid.

This stage corresponds to reaching the highest degree of strength.

Al-Hallaj was in such a state when he began his activity in Baghdad, was arrested, and was killed. He endured what was done to him almost as though he found sweetness in it.

Sufism is heroism—but not individual heroism.

It is courage—but not courage confined to the battlefield.

It is courage in the broadest sense: present at every moment, in every place, whoever may be standing before you.

It is not the courage of the soldier who fights the enemy and then returns obediently to his commander.

It is the courage of free people.

Annihilation has also been defined as complete absorption in the greatness of the Creator and the witnessing of the Real: that is, immersion in absolute existence and attachment to the spirit of the universe.

It is the highest degree of contemplation.

**Beginner:** Someone who begins the path with firm resolve. He is trained by someone who already knows the state.

**Seeker:** Someone whose beginning has proved genuine. He has entered the company of those who have cut themselves off from outside powers, and the hearts of the sincere testify to the truth of his intention.

But he has not yet acquired a state or station.

**The sought one:** The knower who no longer has a will of his own.

Before becoming the sought one, he is a traveller. The traveller comes after the seeker, or alongside him.

The knower is the person who has reached the final stages. He becomes sought after first having been a seeker.

**Ecstasy:** The heart suddenly recovering a purity of remembrance that had been lost.

Its substance is love overpowering the lover.

Longing and grief meet in it.

The Sufi’s longing contains sorrow. His love contains tears. His body suffers while his spirit rejoices.

If the person in ecstasy has a human beloved and is given the choice between meeting her and remaining apart, he chooses separation.

Meeting contains the possibility of complaint about the pain of longing. It also contains the possibility of physical union.

This is why the great Sufi does not marry the woman he loves. If he married her, ecstasy would turn into loss. The spirit would sink into ordinary affection and lose the power to carry him toward his cosmic throne.

Al-Wasiti said that ecstasy has four stages:

bewilderment, confusion, intoxication, and sobriety.

It is like someone who first hears about the sea, then approaches it, then enters it, then is seized by the waves, and finally emerges again.

Emerging corresponds to sobriety.

It belongs to those to whom the realities of the unseen have been disclosed.

**Showing ecstasy:** Making ecstasy visible.

It can also mean acting intoxicated, whether by imitation or by genuinely falling into intoxication.

This often happens during musical listening, when melody and words stir something in a person’s inner being, move him, and perhaps make him dance.

The knower, however, maintains his composure. Ecstasy does not show itself outwardly except when appropriate, and it does not draw attention to him.

We have already seen this in the story of al-Junayd at a gathering of music.

**First stirring:** What first appears in the heart at a particular moment, according to the knower’s state.

**Incoming state:** What comes into the heart after the first stirring and then absorbs it.

An incoming state has an action and an effect. The first stirring does not.

Some limited incoming states to praiseworthy things such as joy, sadness, contraction, and expansion.

The Sufis use the word **joy**, not **exultation**, because exultation can carry the sense of arrogance and excess.

**Sudden visitations:** Things that strike the heart unexpectedly and bring either expansion or contraction.

The word comes from the same root as *badīha*, sudden intuition, or at least shares its root.

In *ʿAwārif al-Maʿārif*, sudden knocks, first visitations, sudden visitations, rising lights, gleams, glimpses, and sparks are all treated as closely related terms.

We have already discussed glimpses.

They defined **sudden knocks** as things that strike the hearts of people of truth through hearing and renew their inner realities.

Some of these experiences may conflict with religious law.

**Contraction and expansion:** Two states of the knowers.

When the Real contracts them, they become reluctant to take advantage even of permitted pleasures such as food, drink, and similar things.

When the Real expands them, He returns them to these things.

That is one definition.

I smell opportunism in it.

Minor Sufis could use “expansion” as an excuse to enjoy permitted pleasures, and perhaps go beyond them as well.

Al-Junayd defined contraction and expansion as fear and hope.

Hope expands a person toward obedience.

Fear contracts him away from wrongdoing.

Contraction has also been defined by the saying:

> Contraction for the knower is like fear for someone seeking safety.

In the “hidden station,” expansion means that God opens the knower outwardly toward people while drawing him inwardly toward Himself, as an act of mercy toward creation.

The idea is that the knower remains among people and looks after what is due to them, while giving up his own share for his Lord.

Bishr al-Hafi once applied this contrast to himself and Maʿruf al-Karkhi. The two lived at the same time and were close companions. Maʿruf attended banquets; Bishr did not.

Bishr said:

> Maʿruf eats from the expansion of knowledge. I refrain from the contraction of scrupulousness.

**Inner prompting:** A message that enters the mind.

Sometimes it is cast into it by angels, sometimes by devils, sometimes by the self, and sometimes by God.

An inner prompting can therefore contain good or evil.

Angels symbolise what is good, as does God. Devils symbolise evil.

The devil is a symbol. He is not Iblis.

Iblis, after all, is one of their masters.

Indeed, he is the first Pole.

**Knowledge of certainty:** Knowledge based on evidence and proof.

It means theoretical knowledge founded on observation and reasoning.

**Eye of certainty:** Knowledge reached through disclosures and descending intuitions.

**Truth of certainty:** Knowledge reached by witnessing and direct seeing.

It is the highest degree of disclosure, and therefore the highest level of intuition.

Some Sufis said:

Knowledge of certainty belongs to separation.
The eye of certainty belongs to gathering.
The truth of certainty belongs to the gathering of gathering.

**Domineering:** When a seeker or someone at an intermediate stage uses his spiritual states to lord it verbally over people like himself.

It is blameworthy.

This agrees with what we said earlier: people of knowledge do not exercise their power over people. They exercise it over the forces that dominate people.

**Unity:** The Sufis use this word in a sense different from the ordinary religious meaning of God’s oneness against polytheism.

They call it **the unity of the elect**.

It means that God makes you one through Himself, sets you apart for Himself, lets you witness this, and makes you absent through Him from everything else you witness.

The ordinary religious meaning of unity as the opposite of polytheism does not appear among the characteristic Sufi meanings of the word. For them, God is absolute existence, and categories such as one and two do not properly apply to Him.

Sufi unity is therefore **union**.

**Singling out:** To isolate the unique by removing createdness from it in order to realise the eternal, and to isolate the Eternal by recognising the realities of uniqueness.

Its meaning is more exact than ordinary religious monotheism.

Someone said:

> Those who affirm unity are many. Those who truly single out the One among them are few.

It is the final form of unity, completely free of any suspicion of partnership.

Al-Hallaj said:

> It is enough for the One that the One be singled out.[^ms-v1-023]

Uniqueness is more exact than simple oneness.

But uniqueness does not prevent union. Union comes only after **stripping away**, when signs of divinity become clear to hearts once they have been purified of cloudiness.

Spirit contains neither partnership nor multiplicity.

“Singling out” can also mean stripping oneself of forms and states and becoming single in one’s actions.

Here we are dealing only with ethics.

A person’s actions should be for the Real, not for the self—that is vanity—and not for other people—that is flattery.

Nor should he act in hope of compensation.

He should seek no personal gain from what he does.

The **undivided concern** and **undivided secret** are the knower’s concern and inner secret once they have been freed from all distractions and devoted completely to watching the Real.

No passing thought cuts across them, and no obstacle prevents attention, approach, nearness, or connection.

The greatest concern is what our noble master identified as:

**bringing comfort to people.**

This belongs to undivided concern because the knower, absorbed in the Real, gives up his own share for the sake of others.

**Conversation:** A description of the final state of the utterly truthful.

It resembles revelation, except that it comes without an intermediary.

They even fashioned a hadith for it:

> In my community there are people who are spoken to and inspired, and Umar is one of them.

But Umar never claimed sainthood, and he had no such direct conversation.

He belonged to Muhammad’s mission in the field of politics, not religion. He was the man who consolidated and expanded the Islamic state.

That kind of work does not require revelation.

**Intimate prayer:** The secrets speaking in the clarity that follows remembrance.

It normally comes after remembrance and often takes place at night.

One of al-Junayd’s intimate prayers says:

> My God and my Master, do You wish to cut me off from You by joining me to You?

Divine love contains neither cutting off nor joining.

If either occurs to the lover, his ecstasy becomes loss, as we have already explained.

**Night converse:** Prolonged intimate reproach while secrecy remains intact.

It can also mean the Real addressing the knowers from the world of secrets and the unseen.

Or it can mean spirits alone with the hidden intimacy of their private conversation.

**Name:** Letters used to point from the act of naming toward the existence of the thing named.

Abu al-Husayn used to illustrate this with the following line:

> When a child’s hunger touches his mother,
> she feeds him with the child’s own name, and the child finds shelter.

Al-Shibli said:

> Reality was lost in knowledge, knowledge was lost in the name, and the name was lost in the Essence.

The mother whose child is hungry—and there are many like her today in our country, Iraq—feeds him from the very essence of being.

That essence is his name from childhood.

The child grows hungry and cries out for help, seeking shelter. But he finds no one to protect him except me, and the state stands as a veil between us.

So he dies.

Even as I say this, I seek refuge from saying it—unless the mother is not merely an individual mother, but the mother of a child who has absorbed the secret of existence, allowing her child to become rooted in the Essence.

Childhood then becomes his food.

Childhood itself needs food in order to survive, but the essence of childhood feeds on itself.

This is why I say that the essence of childhood will remain in Iraq without food until the veil is removed and childhood returns, multiplying and reproducing itself through nourishment.

The essence can do without food.

Its individual embodiments cannot.[^ms-v1-024]

**Essence:** A thing that stands by itself.

Name, description, and attribute are signs pointing toward the Essence.

Nothing has an essence without being named, described, and given attributes.

**Veil:** Something that comes between the thing being sought and the person seeking it.

Sari al-Saqati used to pray:

> My God, whatever else You punish me with, do not punish me with the humiliation of the veil.

Sahl al-Tustari said:

> The thickest veil between the servant and God is the claim.

He means claiming to have arrived when one has not arrived.

Or perhaps he meant something else that has not reached us.

**Trial:** The Real putting the servant’s true state to the test through affliction: what descends on him in the form of suffering, so that the degree of his patience becomes known.

A poet said:

> Poor man, the trial is on me—I mean
> that among all trials, this one is light to me.

One form of trial is illness in the body without panic.

Another is losing something dear, or someone whose loss is painful.

Another is finding oneself in a struggle and remaining firm.

Patience under such trials is praiseworthy.

But patience can also be blameworthy when the affliction consists in people being robbed of their rights, or a person being deprived of the basic necessities of life.

Al-Niffari said:

> I am amazed by anyone who finds nothing to eat in his house and does not go out against the people with his sword drawn.

Here patience is blameworthy.

Outrage is required.

**Secret:** What the Real has hidden and not disclosed to creation.

Sahl al-Tustari said:

> The self has a secret which the Real revealed only through Pharaoh’s tongue when he said: “I am your highest lord.”

This is one of al-Tustari’s ecstatic utterances.

The Sufis stand with Pharaoh against Moses because they reject intermediaries.

As for Pharaoh’s words, “I am your highest lord,” this is the secret of the self when it strips itself bare, drinks from the cup of nearness, and unites with the Real, so that the human being becomes divine.

But Shaykh al-Tustari failed to notice that Pharaoh’s claim to lordship does not follow the Sufi pattern.

Pharaoh wanted to make himself a god **over** people.

The Sufis wanted to become divine **for** people.

**Secret of the secret:** What the Real keeps entirely to Himself, beyond even the servant.

Here lies the motive that drives the search for the unknown.

If the secret of the secret were known, knowledge itself would come to an end.

**The pact, or pact of the secret:** Something the knower commits himself to in his heart, between himself and God: that he will do this or that.

Al-Tirmidhi said:

> For thirty years I have made no pact between myself and God Almighty, for fear that He might overturn it and make my own tongue prove me false.

The pact of the heart belongs to the elect.

A spoken pledge belongs to ordinary people.

Here “ordinary people” does not mean the ignorant masses. It means people outside the Sufi path.

**Glance:** The inward eye noticing what flashes before it as an increase in certainty regarding what it believes of the unseen.

**Effacement:** The disappearance of something until no trace remains.

If a trace remains, it is **blotting out**.

**Eradication** has the same general meaning as effacement, but is more complete.

These are degrees of annihilation.

Al-Shibli said:

> I am effaced in what He is...

Meaning:

Nothing comes from anything.
Nothing is in me.
Nothing comes from me.
Everything is from Him, through Him, and for Him.

Sufi effacement is the removal of the attributes of the self.

Some distinguished more carefully between the terms.

Effacement is the annihilation of one’s actions in the action of the Real, so that action proceeds from beyond ordinary reason.

Eradication is the annihilation of one’s very existence in the Essence of the Real.

Blotting out is the annihilation of one’s attributes in the attributes of the Real.

The **effacement of gathering** is the annihilation of multiplicity in unity.

These are the various degrees in which Sufi annihilation can be understood.

People in our own age sometimes confuse these terms because they have become distant from us—or because we have become distant from them—across many centuries.

So let us put it plainly.

The knower is annihilated in the spirit of the universe, which has been given many names: God, the Creator, the Real, Tao, the Absolute.

All are names for one reality.

This annihilation has two sides.

First, the knower strips away his ordinary qualities, allowing him to become absorbed in the Absolute. He moves through the kingdom of the universe, wandering among its qualities of beauty and kindling his spirit and the energies hidden within it.

Then he emerges from effacement into the second side, ready to act.

The action is his action and, at the same time, the action of others.

It is his because it is carried out through him, by his own decision and choice.

But it is not his alone, because he acts while immersed in something greater.

Suppose, for example, that he joins the poor in confronting the states of the rich.

He acts by a decision of his own self.

But the action does not belong to him alone, because he acts with those around him.

Without them, he would have no action.

Alongside this, the knower experiences happiness as a knowing self spiritualised in the Absolute.

The Absolute is beautiful.

For the Sufis, the world too is beautiful.

As for me, I find the world beautiful when I am alone with it.

I do not find it beautiful when I am among people.

They are being tortured in it.

**Trace:** A sign left by something.

Someone said:

> Whoever is prevented from direct vision finds comfort in the trace.

But a trace can also be something that must be escaped, when it is the trace of something that should itself be removed.

Al-Khawwas was asked about the unity of the elect and said that it meant turning away from the effects that things leave on their souls.

The trace in which a person deprived of vision finds comfort, on the other hand, means the visible forms and manifestations of existence.

**Being and separation:** Al-Junayd said of those who had realised unity:

> They were without being, and separated without separation.

The meaning is that they are among things as though they were not among them, and apart from things as though they were not apart.

They are among things bodily.

They are apart from them in their secrets.

Someone wrote:

> Alone, he wandered lost in the wilderness of unity,
> and Husayn disappeared in the glory of seeking You.
>
> You appeared to one who affirmed You after his distance,
> and he was without being—as though You had become him.

**Union and separation:** Union means the arrival of what was absent.

Some defined it as cutting oneself off from everything except the Real.

It does not mean one physical essence joining another, because that kind of contact belongs only to bodies.

The same is true in human love.

When the Sufi speaks of union, he understands it as the woman who came at the hour of death understood it, not as the ordinary love poet understands it.

We have already discussed this.

Union and separation overlap.

Anyone who thinks he has arrived should know that he has already separated.

As one poet said:

> No union and no separation,
> no despair and no hope.

Al-Jawahiri touched the same meaning when he wrote of Zahle in Lebanon:

> Love is Zahle-like: no joining,
> no turning away, no withholding, no giving.

There is nothing about Zahle itself that makes it so, nor did the poet deliberately create this meaning.

The Merciful simply overflowed through his tongue.

Our Sufis used **separation** rather than **abandonment**, because abandonment belongs to the vocabulary of ordinary love poetry.

**Adorning oneself:** Taking on the speech of the sincere and displaying their actions outwardly.

**Manifestation:** What is disclosed to hearts from the lights of the unseen.

It has three forms:

manifestation of the Essence,
manifestation of the attributes of the Essence,
and manifestation of the judgment of the Essence.

The first is disclosure and vision.

The second is the place of light.

When divine power manifests itself to the knower, he fears no one else.

And when he fears no one, his own action becomes something no one need fear.

At this level manifestation also brings self-sufficiency, because the knower needs no one else.

The manifestation of the judgment of the Essence belongs to the next world.

Manifestation through attributes is a state descending on the knower through the attributes that are the divine names.

These names also become descriptions of the person receiving the manifestation.

If manifestation comes through the name **the Merciful**, mercy becomes one of his attributes.

If it comes through **the Generous**, he becomes generous.

If it comes through **the Powerful**, he possesses power.

The Beautiful Names are therefore attributes shared, in this sense, by the One who manifests and the person to whom He manifests.

Ahmad al-Rifaʿi said that when the servant becomes firmly established in the states, he becomes an attribute of the Real and nothing can defeat him.

He cited a sacred saying:

> O son of Adam, obey Me and I will obey you. I will make you able to say to a thing, “Be,” and it will be.

Abu al-Husayn al-Nuri said:

> He manifested Himself to His creation through His creation, and hid Himself from His creation through His creation.

Here manifestation is understood through things in their relations with one another.

Creation is His image and the field of His action.

At the same time, creation is His veil.

Strictly speaking, He appears and acts only through their appearance and action.

Our Sufis knew this just as the philosophers did.

But the higher human being does not accept nature exactly as it is.

He rebuilds it so that it serves needs greater than those supplied by nature alone.

Sufism does not explain nature.

It changes it.

**Emptying oneself:** Turning away from the things that distract a person from the Real.

This is also a Buddhist idea.

It is usually linked to withdrawal.

What must be left behind are bodily demands beyond natural need, together with the self’s desire for status, power, and fame.

**Everlasting:** Permanent and unchanging.

Al-Shibli said:

> My time in You has become everlasting, and it is everlasting.

Al-Sarraj al-Tusi comments that al-Shibli is describing the quality of his secret, not the quality of his ordinary attributes.

Attributes change.

The secret endures.

**Subtle insight:** Something that flashes before understanding and gleams in the mind, but is too fine in meaning for language to contain.

**Overwhelming seizure:** A force that descends on the mind and carries it away through the strength of its domination.

**Freedom:** A sign of the final fulfilment of servanthood to God.

It means that nothing in creation owns you.

You are free when you are a servant of God.

Bishr al-Hafi said:

> God created you free, so remain as He created you. Do not perform for your family when you are at home or for your companions when you travel. Work for God and leave people alone.

Al-Junayd said:

> The final station of the knower is freedom.

Someone else said:

> A servant cannot truly be a servant while still enslaved by anything other than God.

Servanthood to God is therefore freedom among people.

Freedom also means the knower becoming free of his own self.

Shuʿayb Abu Madyan pointed to this when he said:

> No one reaches pure freedom while anything of himself still remains.

Becoming free of the self means becoming free of its excessive claims on life and wealth.

What a person owns also owns him, as the Sufis used to say.

His desires enslave him.

Abu al-Atahiya wrote:

> I obeyed my desires, and they enslaved me.

They claimed that al-Hallaj recited this while crucified.

Hardly.

Desire was never capable of enslaving him.

Freedom, as al-Junayd said, is the knower’s station at the final stages.

It is the highest goal of travelling the path.

**Rust:** The corrosion that settles on hearts.

Someone said that hearts can be veiled in four ways.

Then he listed six.

There is sealing and imprinting, which belong to the hearts of unbelievers.

There is rust and hardness, which belong to hypocrites.

And there is tarnish and covering, which belong to believers.

Six, not four.

**Clouding:** A film or haze.

A hadith says:

> A clouding comes over my heart, and I ask God’s forgiveness and repent to Him a hundred times each day.

The saying does not appear in the major primary collections, but it is old. It appears in Abu Ubayd al-Qasim ibn Sallam’s *Gharib al-Hadith*.

The Sufis took hold of it. Al-Sarraj al-Tusi included it.

They explained the clouding by comparing it to a mirror breathed upon by someone looking into it. Its brightness briefly fades, then returns.

**Intermediaries:** Things standing between the servant and his Lord among the causes of this world and the next.

There are three kinds:

intermediaries in constant communication,
connected intermediaries,
and disconnected intermediaries.

The first are sudden visitations from the Real.

The second are acts of worship.

The third are the shares of the self.

**The outward face of possibilities:** The Real manifesting through the individual forms and attributes of possible things.

The idea of “possible things” comes from Ibn Sina. It means things that may either exist or not exist.

Its opposite is the impossible, which cannot exist, and necessary existence, which is absolute existence.

But when all the conditions required for a possible thing come together, its existence becomes necessary.

Plant a seed in suitable soil and a suitable climate, and its development into a tree becomes necessary.

**The outward face of existence:** The manifestations of the divine names, where unity is real and difference is relative.

This belongs to the philosophy of the unity of being.

Existence is truly one.

Existing things differ in their forms, not in their underlying essence.

**The outward face of knowledge:** The individual realities of possible things.

That is, the actual existing things whose forms are reflected in the human mind, producing knowledge of their outward appearance.

Knowing their inner reality requires inward knowledge.

**Worship:** Turning toward God through ritual acts. Its place is the body.

**Servanthood:** Acknowledging His lordship. Its place is the spirit.

**Utter servanthood:** The most hidden secret.

Perhaps they separated this last category because it returns to a relation of equality.

**Unveiling:** Ahmad al-Rifaʿi defined it as:

> A power which, by its special nature, draws the light of an eye or inner sight toward the overflowing unseen. Its light joins that unseen as a ray joins clear glass when the glass faces the source of light. The light then rebounds and is reflected onto the purity of the heart. From there it rises, shining, into the world of the intellect and joins it through an immaterial connection whose effect is to pour the light of the intellect back onto the field of the heart. The light of the intellect then shines upon the pupil of the inner secret, and the person sees what ordinary eyes cannot locate and ordinary understanding cannot conceive.

This is a good description of the power of intuition.

I apologise to specialists in the art.

This is the kind of passage one is meant to taste and absorb, not explain.

But I am defining terms here, so I have no choice.

**Fluctuation and settledness:** Two stages in the knower’s path.

In the first, his qualities are unstable and change according to the different states passing through him.

He then rises from fluctuation to settledness.

Here realities are disclosed to him and become firmly established.

Of course, nothing in a human being can ever be absolutely fixed. Human beings continue to change.

What is meant is that a truth once disclosed to him never again disappears or grows weaker. It only deepens.

Someone still in fluctuation may contradict himself when the qualities of his ordinary self reappear. He remains exposed to change and forgetting.

Settledness belongs with witnessing.

**Taste:** In ordinary usage, a faculty that recognises subtle qualities of speech and delicate forms of beauty.

Among the Sufis, it is a light of knowledge cast by the Real, through manifestation, into the hearts of His friends. By it they distinguish truth from falsehood.

It is the first manifestation.

It belongs to people of sudden visitations.

But taste is also a general means of understanding and one of the powers of intuition.

**The olive:** A symbol of the self when it is ready to ignite with the light of holiness because of the strength of its oil.

The oil represents its original preparedness.

**Qahwāʾiyya:** The Real addressing a person directly, face-to-face, in the imaginal world.

Perhaps the term was formed from words relating to the mouth and its opening.

**Meeting-place of opposites:** Absolute identity: the presence in which the furthest extremes embrace and opposites meet beneath a single identity.

**Meeting-place of the two seas:** The presence of “two bow-lengths,” in which the two seas of necessity and possibility meet.

“Two bow-lengths” means the nearness of the meeting.

The meeting is between possibility and necessity.

It is also defined as the presence in which existence is gathered together through the meeting of divine names and cosmic realities.

**Veiling and manifestation:** Al-Junayd said:

> It is discipline, refinement, and dissolution.

Discipline belongs to veiling and is for ordinary people.

Refinement belongs to the elect and is manifestation.

Dissolution belongs to the saints and is witnessing.

Veiling draws the saints back out of the gathering of gathering so that people can benefit from them.

If they remained permanently there, they would be cut off from creation.

The knower therefore has times for himself and times for people.

His time for himself is the gathering of gathering.

His time for people is separation.

**Helper:** The Pole when people turn to him for help.

He is not called the Helper at other times.

From this we can understand the function of the Helper as action taken to confront some difficulty that has fallen on an individual or a group.

**The present moment:** The state in which the Sufi is present in his heart with God.

A saying attributed to the Prophet runs:

> I have a moment with God in which neither an angel brought near nor a prophet sent with a message has room to enter.

The present moment is **now**.

It is a word suspended between two non-existent things: the past and the future.

The past has gone.

The future has not yet arrived.

The faqir is the child of his moment.

This concentrates the Sufi’s attention on the present, turning him away from dwelling on the past or looking toward the future.

But this should be understood only as a state of attention.

A human being cannot truly cut himself off from the past.

He is the child of his past, not merely the child of the present moment.

His existence arose in the past, whose time is not fundamentally separate from the time of the present. Moments of time and what accompanies them are distinct, as Aristotle counted them, but they form a sequence.

As for thinking about the future, sometimes we do and sometimes we do not.

But removing the future entirely from human thought also removes one of the motives for action.

That is a negative thing.

Ibn Arabi defined the present moment as:

> Your state in the time of the present state.

That is, a passing state.

The best definition, to my mind, is the one given by someone who said:

> Your moment is whatever has mastery over you in this instant.

**The exalted letters:** The hidden affairs of the Essence concealed in the unseen of the unseen, like the tree hidden in the seed.

Ibn Arabi wrote:

> We were exalted letters, never spoken,
> suspended on the summits of the highest peaks.

**The self-reproaching soul:** The soul illuminated enough by the light of the heart to wake from the sleep of heedlessness.

Whenever it commits a wrong through the darkness built into its nature, it begins to blame itself.

What the Sufis called the self-reproaching soul is what people today call the **conscience** or the **superego**.

**Awe and intimacy:** Two states higher than contraction and expansion.

Awe requires absence.

Intimacy requires sobriety and waking awareness.

Awe prevents intimacy.

**The red ruby:** The universal soul, whose light is mixed with the darkness that comes from attachment to the body.

The separate intellect, by contrast, is pure light.

For the Sufis, the individual soul is the site of blameworthy qualities and one of the veils covering the intellect.

The universal soul is a mixture of the light of absolute existence and the darkness of particular existence, which in this relation is the body.

**Witnessing:** Presence, sometimes in the form of watchfulness and sometimes in the form of direct vision.

The **unity of witnessing** means creation merging into the Real until no distinction is experienced between them.

It can sometimes approach what has been called the **unity of the existent**, an idea I reject because things have their own independence and distinction from absolute existence.

The unity of the existent means that nothing truly exists except God, while all other beings are illusions and shadows.

This comes close to the subjective idealism of some modern European philosophers.

A few Sufis expressed ideas like this.

But most of them held to the **unity of being**, not the unity of the existent.

That means absolute existence multiplying itself through existing things.

Those things become many and distinct and acquire an existence independent from absolute existence, even though they continue to draw existence from it.

In nature, the relationship resembles that between a seed and a tree.

The seed is the original existence.

The tree derives from it.

Yet the tree becomes independent.

A tree in its essence is not the same thing as a seed in its essence.

Those who believe in the unity of the existent make the tree the seed and the seed the tree.

Dialectical logic allows this in one sense: the tree is absent in the seed and the seed is absent in the tree.

But this does not abolish their separation in essence.

If we move from this example to the relation between the Real and creation, we see the Real flowing through existing things or manifesting Himself in them.

Those manifestations take different forms.

Our senses testify to those differences.

The things are therefore not identical with the Real.

For this reason, subjective idealism never became widespread among the Sufis.

References to it appear only occasionally, usually in loose, passing remarks or in attempts to complete an unfinished expression.

**Overpowering state:** Ecstasy that continues without interruption.

Ecstasy may die away quickly.

When it lasts and continues, it becomes an overpowering state.

### Explanation of Some Phrases and Expressions

**Drawing the spirits:** Abu Saʿid al-Kharraz said:

> God drew the spirits of His friends toward Himself and gave them pleasure in remembering Him—

he should have said **delighted them**, because pleasure is bodily—

> and in reaching nearness to Him. He hastened to give their bodies pleasure in everything, so that their bodies live the life of animals while their spirits live the life of those close to the divine.

His phrase “He hastened to give their bodies pleasure” is a bad one.

The bodies of the knowers do not take pleasure.

Their spirits are what rejoice.

**My sea has no shore:** A phrase of al-Shibli’s, spoken during one of his gatherings:

> Your moments are cut off. My moment has no two ends, and my sea has no shore.

He means that the state God has given him has neither end nor interruption.

**A person of the heart:** Someone unable to express in eloquent speech the knowledge gathered in his heart.

Al-Junayd used to say:

> The people of Khurasan are people of hearts.

He meant the Sufis of Khurasan.

They could not express themselves fluently in Arabic, so their hearts spoke instead of their tongues.

**A person of a state:** Someone connected to a particular state, such as love, fear, hope, or longing.

**A person of a station:** Someone who has acquired a particular lasting station, such as scrupulousness, asceticism, patience, or opposition.

**Without a self:** To say that someone is “without a self” means that the ordinary qualities of the self no longer appear in him.

These include anger, harshness, pride, greed, craving, and envy.

**A person of allusion:** Someone whose speech contains subtle meanings and indirect signs.

**I without I:** Said by someone who has given up ownership of his actions within the very act of doing them.

That is, he does not act for himself.

It also describes someone who has given up his personal claims.

And it can mean surrendering the self to the beloved.

A poet wrote:

> O desire of every desirer,
> You took me through You away from me.

That is:

You stripped me of my “I.”

The Taoists also speak of giving up ownership of action within action.

The sage acts, then withdraws.

### Ranks of Those Who Enter the Path

**Seeker:** Defined above.

**Intermediate:** Someone who has travelled some distance as a seeker and acquired states, but has not yet acquired stations.

**Traveller:** Someone who has arrived after passing through the stages of seeker and intermediate.

Travellers themselves have different levels and ranks according to their capacities.

**Knower:** The traveller at the highest level.

He is also the sought one.

**Pole:** The highest knower, around whom the movement of creation comes to revolve.

He is called the **Helper** when people turn to him for help.

**Poleship** is the rank of the Pole.

I use the term **Sufism of the Poles** to distinguish this from ordinary Sufism practised by the general mass of Sufis and their lesser figures.

As we have already seen, the function of the Helper means action by the Pole to solve a difficulty facing an individual or a group.

**Faqir:** The common name for a Sufi, because poverty is the pattern of his personality once he has freed himself from servitude to money and ownership.

Its plural is **faqirs**, or **people of poverty**.

**People of the Real:** The Sufis’ name for themselves.

**The aspiring Sufi and the Sufi:** Usually two names for the same thing.

Some, however, treat them as two stages: first the aspiring Sufi, then the Sufi, taking this from the grammatical form of the Arabic word, which can suggest gradually becoming something.

**Guide of the path:** Someone who teaches seekers the road into Sufism—that is, someone who brings them into the path.

### Assessment

There has been a great deal of disagreement over where the word *Sufism* comes from. The most common view derives it from *ṣūf*, wool. Wool was what poor people and ascetics wore, provided it was not finely woven. Coarse wool was the norm. Fine wool, by contrast, was sought after by the rich, by rulers, and by people accustomed to comfort. Ordinary wool was rough and crude. Such people found no pleasure in wearing it and preferred silk, linen, and cotton.

Islam forbade men to wear silk, and Umar ibn al-Khattab forbade his governors to wear linen, leaving them wool and cotton. Even today, our peasants wear a rough woollen cloth called the *bisht*. Iraqis have a saying about the man in the bisht, used for the poor and powerless person who gets into a dispute with someone who has authority—a merchant, a soldier, an official, or an intellectual.

It is also possible that the word *Sufi* is related to the Arabic *ṣūfiyā*, carrying the sense of a seer, revealer, or diviner. The word is native to the Semitic languages; perhaps the Greek *sophia* is related to it.

But I am more inclined to connect Sufism with wool, because Sufism began as a social movement founded on asceticism and opposition to the feudal way of life of state officials and the property-owning classes. It did not begin as an intellectual movement. Philosophy entered it later.

Modern researchers who have tried to get away from wool and search for some other origin with more philosophical or cultural associations are really expressing the intellectual’s dislike of wool—or embarrassment at having to wear it.

The history of Sufism today suffers from outside interventions that have tried to appropriate it for culture in the narrow sense. New books appear, or old books are edited and republished, financed by rich people and governments, while their authors and editors bask in the comforts of both worlds.

Worse still are groups who claim family descent from the great communalist Sufi masters, or claim to follow their path, then use their names to grow rich at the expense of hungry people. Aristocratic families are formed around a name while knowing nothing of what the name once stood for.

Sufism, in both its social and philosophical forms, came to an end with Abd al-Karim al-Jili, who died in 832 AH. After him came books of biographies that recorded the tradition, explained it, or repeated what its great masters had written and said during the Islamic age, which in this sense does not extend beyond the generation of the last great master, Abd al-Karim.

No one has the right to claim membership in the path when both of its real paths have disappeared.

We have returned to Sufism as part of our return to the heritage. We are trying to revive and develop it by joining it to social Marxism and enriching it with the Chinese form of Sufism known as Taoism.

Our own social Sufism and Taoism together provide the foundation for a **bottom-up communism, a grassroots communism**, to be built on the ruins of top-down communism—the communism of bourgeois Marxists.

In the final orbit devoted to Sufism, we put forward a project for a Communalist Association of Iraq. It begins from social Sufism and Abd al-Qadir al-Jilani’s principle of **“bringing comfort to the people,”** together with the Taoist project of the **Great Society**. It joins these to Marxist organisation and draws inspiration from the Paris Commune, the Qarmatians of Iraq, the movement of Jesus, Mazdak, and Abu Dharr al-Ghifari.

---

Our writers today endlessly repeat the language of **“failure,”** translated from the Latin and Germanic languages.

Taoism failed. Jesus failed. The philosophers of Islam failed. The Sufis failed. Marxism failed. Guevara failed. The October Revolution failed. The Bolsheviks failed. Chinese communism failed.

The only people who succeeded, apparently, are our writers and their own little projects—projects isolated from one another and at war with one another over who owns the patent.

Every researcher now plants himself at the centre of life and history, master of the whole spectacle, looking down on the dead: all of them failures. Civilisations failed. The people who made them failed. Their martyrs failed. History itself withdrew.

Only the successful researcher remained.

Perhaps Abu Yazid al-Bistami foresaw this farce in one of his visions.

A man once told him that he had spent thirty years in worship and had still not arrived.

Bistami explained to him that arrival would not come through worship. He should go out dressed exactly as he was, mount a donkey, carry walnuts through the marketplace, and let the children follow behind him, whistling and clapping.

Then he would become a Sufi master.

Yes.

When souls are great, bodies grow weary in pursuing their aims.

And the reverse is also true.

Sufism is a social culture with a communalist root. It inhabits a large soul before which the prestige of the ruler and his state collapses, along with the prestige of money and its owners. Anxiety over reputation, fame, success, and intellectual patents disappears.

Cultural identity is then realised by sitting with the poor and helping them clean the lice from their clothes when the unsuccessful cosmopolitan intellectual cannot even provide them with soap—because, like them, he owns nothing.

Sufism began with Ibrahim ibn Adham.

Before him there were people who did not belong to the Sufi path but carried some of its concerns. They were, so to speak, its forerunners. They helped draw a line within Islamic culture that identified itself with ordinary people and stood in conflict with the state.

They included theologians, jurists, and religious scholars whose consciences were communalist and fiercely independent. They hated the state and its masters and stood with its victims.

The founders of speculative theology were all killed in the struggle against Umayyad despotism. The overwhelming majority of the jurists of the first Islamic century sided with the opposition. They carried out their role as defenders of rights, not as professional men of religion.

The Umayyads had little on their side except poets.

This explains why the Sufi biographical collections, and similar books, include lives of people who lived before Sufism existed. They were counted among the Sufis even though they were not actually Sufis. It is more accurate to call them forerunners.

This is why Sufi books quote them as though they were among the tradition’s own teachers.

Among them were Amir al-Anbari, whom the biographical works describe as a Sufi even though he was one of the generation after the Companions and a student of Salman al-Farisi; Malik ibn Dinar; Abd Allah ibn al-Mubarak; Sufyan al-Thawri; Sufyan ibn Uyayna; Ayyub al-Sakhtiyani; Tawus al-Yamani; Dawud al-Ta’i; Ibn al-Sammak al-Kufi; and others like them.

They were ascetics, opponents of power, and people deeply concerned for others.

But they were not Sufis.

Al-Qushayri says in his *Epistle* that the name *Sufi* first appeared in the third Islamic century. Yet the real beginning should be placed with Ibrahim ibn Adham in the middle of the second century. He was the first to use recognisably Sufi terminology, speak in the language of the tradition, and express its basic principles.

We have already seen that he came from Balkh, in what is now Afghanistan, close to China. That invites us to imagine that he may have encountered something of Taoism.

But, as I have said, this is only an idea. We have no evidence that allows us to go further.

We can, however, imagine oral transmission. Ibn Adham may have heard about the Taoists and their ideas from Chinese sages who were known in Central Asia before Islam.

Central Asia lay within China’s political and cultural sphere to such an extent that when al-Tabari describes Qutayba’s arrival at Kashgar, the capital of East Turkestan, he gives the passage the heading:

**“Qutayba’s Arrival in the Land of China.”**

Whatever the case, Sufism arose naturally from the growth of tendencies that already contained its basic principles, even though they had not yet come together in a distinct language of their own.

It did not appear in a vacuum.

Sufism continued along its two main lines through a history of roughly six centuries.

The social form came first. That was the path of its “founder,” Ibn Adham.

Philosophical Sufism began with Abu Yazid al-Bistami, though it did not become a fully distinct school until Ibn Arabi in the sixth Islamic century.

Bistami’s philosophy is contained in his ecstatic sayings. Those sayings broke down the walls of inherited belief and created a way of thinking outside traditional religious frameworks.

With them, Bistami shocked the Islamic mind out of its skull and sent it soaring into an immense open space where it could move with almost complete freedom.

He struck directly at the habit of thinking through sacred things.

Sacred objects and ideas fell from their pedestal in his speech and became ordinary things he could play with according to wherever the current of his thought carried him.

Once he had brought prophetic sacredness down to size, he did not stop for long before divinity itself.

His wild ascent continued until he collided with the Throne.

Either he brought the Throne down to earth or climbed onto it himself, depending on whether his vision happened to be rising or descending.

He removed the barrier between earth and heaven by placing the human being and the Lord on equal terms.

Philosophical Sufism denies that the spirit was created. It must therefore also deny slavery as the proper relation between Creator and created.

Bistami rejected it in the ecstatic saying in which he addressed God:

**“The whole world is my slave except You.”**

But he did not erase God from existence.

He still needed a cosmic spirit to serve as a Buraq carrying him upward through the higher kingdom, freeing him from the slavery of earthly sensation.

He did not deprive God of action. Instead, he made the divine act through his own humanity.

The Real acts through the human being—but not through just any human being.

It needs a human being capable of breaking through the necessities imposed by nature and reshaping them through reason, thereby matching the process of creation itself, which produced nature as this mixture of perfection and imperfection.

Humanity is one essence, but it takes form in different individuals. Not every individual human being can do what another can do.

When we speak of a human being becoming divine, we do not mean just anyone.

That stage requires certain conditions, reached by a particular person through labour and struggle.

Everyone struggles, but toward different ends.

If someone works to become a blacksmith or carpenter, he reaches that goal, while another person exactly like him may be incapable of it. Becoming a blacksmith or carpenter requires time, effort, and skill that another person may not possess.

The person who labours toward divinity can also reach his goal—a goal the blacksmith and carpenter will not reach.

The division of labour does not exist in economics alone. It applies to every part of life.

What Bistami attained cannot be reached by someone else except under the same conditions that carried him to his goal.[^ms-v1-025]

Just as people benefit from the labour of the blacksmith and carpenter, they benefit from the labour of Bistami and others in the same profession.

Every profession benefits people in its own way, through its own methods, levels, stages, and degrees, depending on the field in which it operates and the kind of expertise it requires.

Human liberation from slavery passes through two stages.

The first is to replace slavery to other human beings with servitude to God, following the Islamic-Christian principle:

**Be the servant of the Lord, not the servant of creation.**

Even this liberation has not yet been completed.

In most of the world, human beings are still slaves to other human beings.

Religious corruption makes matters worse by combining servitude to God with servitude to people, directly contradicting the Islamic-Christian principle.

This is why Islamic thinkers repeatedly insisted on distinguishing the two kinds of servitude and making servitude to God conditional on freedom from servitude to human beings.

The Sufis made this effort more consistently than anyone else.

Perhaps this explains why they so often called a human being an **abd**, a servant, and much less often a *believer*.

*Servant* is a description shared by all people, regardless of religion or faith. It makes them equal before God and requires that they be slaves to no one else.

It seems that some of the great masters became impatient, or simply grew tired of waiting for humanity to complete its liberation from human slavery before moving on to the end of servitude to God.

Perhaps Bistami’s declaration came out of that impatience.

That is his own affair.

Once he had freed himself from slavery to others, broken free from the outside powers that ruled him, and become master of himself, he turned toward the final chain limiting his sense of complete sovereignty.

He abolished his servitude to God.

Later masters in the atheistic current of Sufism followed the same decision. They declared:

**“The spirit never fell beneath the humiliation of ‘Be.’”**

That is: the human spirit is not created.

And if it is not created, it is not the slave of a creator.

In the older Arab language, createdness itself carried an implication of subjection, while servitude to God placed a chain around the human essence.

Some Sufis therefore objected even to being called **Abd Allah**, servant of God.

The expression became common in Islam as a way of addressing a stranger whose name you did not know: “O Abd Allah”—in other words, “you there.”

We are still a very long way from freeing human beings from slavery to one another.

That liberation must come before liberation from servitude to God.

But this does not mean Bistami failed at this stage.

He attained it for himself and created a model that others after him could struggle toward.

Humanity’s rescuers are always early.

They sleep lightly.

Bistami is the master of philosophical Sufism.

The closest students to him were Abu Bakr al-Shibli and then al-Hallaj: Shibli through ecstatic utterance, and Hallaj through both ecstatic utterance and writing.

Shibli combined intellectual Sufism with the Sufism of passionate love. As we have suggested, he was in love with a human woman whose name he never revealed.

Hallaj loved without attaching that love to one identifiable human beloved. He was also an intellectual Sufi and a social-communalist Sufi.

He was a plant watered by all three rivers.

Intellectual Sufism developed slowly through al-Makki in *The Nourishment of Hearts*, al-Sarraj al-Tusi in *The Book of Flashes*, and al-Kalabadhi in *The Doctrine of the Sufis*, before reaching its summit in Muhyi al-Din Ibn Arabi, the greatest philosopher of Sufism.

By coincidence or otherwise, he lived at the same time as the great master of passionate Sufism, Umar ibn al-Farid.

Ibn Arabi was a philosopher and should be counted among the philosophers of Islam. His philosophical theories, above all the unity of being, should be studied as achievements of Islamic philosophy.

The martyred scholar should really have discussed him in the philosophy section of *Materialist Tendencies*, not under Sufism.

Had I ever met him—may God sanctify his secret—I would have pointed this out.

With Ibn Arabi, principles first established by earlier Sufis reached maturity. They took settled form in his work and continued after him in Ibn Sabʿin and Abd al-Karim al-Jili.

We have discussed them in detail throughout these orbits. Here they are in brief:

1. **All religions stand on equal ground with respect to truth.** Religions and atheism do too, as do revealed religions and pagan religions. No party has the right to monopolise truth. It follows that one dominant religion must no longer persecute or suppress another, and that the tide of religious warfare and conflict must be stopped.

2. **Sainthood is placed on a level with prophecy.** Alongside the Seal of the Prophets appears the idea of the Seal of the Saints, together with an impulse to proclaim the great Sufi master himself a prophet—an impulse blocked by the saying, “There is no prophet after me.”

3. **The walls of doctrine are broken down so that the mind can escape its skull and think without restraints.** To complete this liberation of thought, Sufism took another step and broke through the laws of formal logic as well. This was done in favour of intuition and dialectics. The Sufis and Taoists share in the ancestry of dialectical logic, to which the Greek philosopher Heraclitus also made a major contribution.

   Intuition and dialectics made possible al-Shirazi’s philosophical revolution. He was both philosopher and illuminationist, and it was through illumination that this method reached him. It carried him beyond the walls of formal logic and allowed him to see the movement of substance and the unity of opposites that drives that movement. Had Shirazi remained inside Aristotelian logic, he would have remained just another commentator on Aristotle.

4. **The principle of happiness in both worlds is rejected, at least for the great Sufi master.** Ibn Arabi was less explicit on this point than the masters of social Sufism. He rejected eternal torment in hell, either interpreting it as a special form of pleasure experienced by the people of the Fire, or as punishment lasting only as long as their sins require before turning into pleasure. He also made the pleasures of paradise spiritual rather than material. Shirazi reports him as saying that life continues and never stops, a position that leads toward denial of a final resurrection.

These foundations of philosophical Sufism were inherited by Ibn Sabʿin in the generation immediately after Ibn Arabi.

He reproduced them in his books and letters and helped spread them more widely.

Like Ibn Arabi, Ibn Sabʿin was a believer in the unity of being, a dialectical thinker, a deist, and a universalist in matters of belief.

He left behind a group named after him. They treated religious rituals lightly, though without falling into moral libertinism.
