A hundred years later came Abd al-Karim al-Jili, who renewed Ibn Arabi’s whole system in all its elements and became the last great master in the line of philosophical Sufism.

One thing has always puzzled me.

Why do the great biographical dictionaries and collections of Sufi lives ignore this magnificent figure?

Al-Sha'rani gives him no biography. Nor do Ibn al-Mulaqqin, al-Sakhawi, Ibn al-Imad, or al-Munawi.

Did they simply not know him?

Or did they seek refuge in God from him and leave him in the dark?

I cannot say for certain, especially because al-Munawi embraced Hallaj and defended him—and Hallaj was more dangerous than Jili to states and social systems, while being just as dangerous to established religions.

Social Sufism was a movement of struggle within the field of Asian communalism.

Its companions there were the Taoist philosophers and Jesus.

I know of no equivalent companion in the other great civilisations of the East.

Take India, for example. I looked through its philosophy and then closed the book again. I found religion, and little else.

The Hungarian communist Lukács was entitled to lament its passivity and submissiveness, and to see the same tendency behind both the Nobel Prize awarded to Tagore, the poet who admired ancient Indian wisdom, and Britain’s conquest of India.

The ancient Semites and Egyptians did touch on a form of struggle between communalism on one side and slavery and feudalism on the other. But their cultures did not rise to the point of fully grasping that struggle and creating a wisdom that embraced it, together with sages who would fight openly against the state and the rich in defence of people’s rights.

The idea of **people** is a common thread between Sufism and Taoism.

The Taoist word is *ren*.

The Sufis gave the Arabic word *khalq*—creation, or people—its social meaning. Through them it passed into the languages of Muslim peoples.

So we find expressions such as **Afghani Khalq**, meaning the Afghan people; **Khalq Bank**, the People’s Bank; and **Khalq Party**, the People’s Party.

In Taoist usage, *ren* combines with *min* to mean the people, while *ren* on its own refers to the human being and to humanity.

The Sufis also used words such as *the common people* and *ordinary people*.

They rejected the habit of treating these expressions as synonyms for **the mob**.

They made a distinction.

The ordinary people are those whose hearts are sound, whose conduct is upright, and whose character is good.

If those qualities are absent, then we are dealing with the mob, not the common people.

Once we understand this difference, a number of things that used to seem confused become clearer.

We often hear that “the common people” fight philosophy, persecute philosophers, and rise up at the command of religious authorities against heretics.

In reality, ordinary people respect an honest and modest intellectual.

It is the mob that persecutes.

Every society and every social group contains a mob that can be moved by religious men—or anyone else—for purposes of their own.

A religious mob existed during the Islamic period, and religious authorities used it against thinkers. But the field was never completely open to them because they did not themselves control the state. They had to wait for moments when the state looked the other way before harassing people they accused of heresy.

Ordinary people, on the other hand, normally respond only to calls for jihad against a foreign enemy.

That is the one point on which they may join religious authorities and the state.

The ordinary people I knew in Iraq still had the same attitude toward intellectuals.

They called the intellectual an **arifa**, “a knowing one,” in a particular familiar tone.

If they sensed that he cared about them and was interested in their affairs, they approached him, asked him questions, and sought his advice.

If a mullah then pronounced him a heretic or something similar, they hesitated. They found excuses for him. They tried to explain him away.

The social struggle of the Sufis was directed toward lifting oppression from people and securing the means of life for them.

At first glance, some of their language about trust in God, asceticism, and patience may appear contradictory.

The contradiction disappears once we understand what they meant by those words and distinguish their meaning from the religious meaning given to them by professional religious authorities.

Religious authorities demand trust, asceticism, and patience from ordinary people.

And religious culture constantly distinguishes between elite and commoner, ruler and ruled, owner and deprived.

This merges easily with the corruption that infects clerical institutions in every religion.

The burden of religious obligations is thrown onto the people and lifted from the rulers and wealthy who form the clergy’s own social circle.

Most religious authorities are not ascetics.

The public display of religion is itself lavish. It shines with decoration, luxury, and ornament.

This produces real crimes against humanity.

Look at the vast amounts spent by Iraqis and Iranians on shrines belonging to all kinds of figures, major and minor, some whose graves are known and others whose association with a site is doubtful.

The waste reaches almost unimaginable levels while the Iranian and Iraqi peoples endure severe crises of daily life, reaching famine in Iraq and crushing poverty in Iran.

The old Muslim social fighters rejected this.

They accused those responsible of violating both justice and religious law.

Religious authorities have no evidence in scripture, prophetic practice, or the traditions of the Imams permitting them to transfer the rights of the poor to shrines.

They are simply following a pattern found among clerical establishments in every religion.

European churches and Buddhist and Hindu temples are no different in this respect from Islamic mosques and shrines, especially Shiite ones.

This is religion everywhere, in every period, across religions and civilisations.

So when religious circles speak of asceticism and trust in God, the message is directed almost entirely at the poor.

Among social fighters and advocates of justice, the equation is reversed.

Very early in Islamic history, Umar ibn Abd al-Aziz refused to renew the columns of the Umayyad Mosque because the poor had a greater right to the money than the columns did.

The same principle was maintained by religious scholars, theologians, and jurists whom we have treated as forerunners of Sufism.

We have already discussed Abd Allah ibn al-Mubarak, a wealthy man who distributed everything beyond his own needs among the poor and refused to spend money on building mosques.

Sufyan al-Thawri spoke painfully of people who erected lavish and decorated buildings, whether mosque or palace.

Al-Ghazali rejected the building of mosques while poor people remained in need of money, as he says in *Disclosure and Clarification*.

Al-Sha'rani’s *Warning to the Deluded* contains a great deal of material along the same lines.

Opposition was therefore not confined to palaces.

It extended to mosques and shrines whenever poor people existed who had a greater need for the money.[^ms-v1-026]

Hunger occupied a central place in Sufi concern.

They made it the mother of all problems, and they considered relieving it better than any religious or worldly act.

They did not postpone it to the next world.

The Sufi heart was pressed constantly against the daily sight of hungry people.

The Sufi finds happiness in feeding someone who is hungry: in creating a material, physical happiness for a deprived human being.

Their awareness of popular hunger produced their uncompromising opposition to both the state and the wealthy, because they held these two groups responsible for hunger.

This followed from the position of the state in Asian society.

The state was the effective owner of social wealth. It controlled that wealth, distributed it to a minority, and withheld it from the majority.

The Sufis could see with their own eyes what the state did with money: what it spent on palaces, what it poured into pleasures, and the astonishing channels of waste it opened—waste so extreme that it fell under the legal concept of reckless incompetence for which a person could be denied control of his own property.

The Sufis and the religious scholars close to them therefore fought their class struggle first and foremost against the state.

They cursed it.

They regarded it as pure contamination, something that could not be approached safely.

Whoever touched it became polluted, lost his heart, and turned into one of the human devils.

Their attack on the rich came from the same battlefield of class conflict.

Their opposition to wealth follows the same path as the sages who preceded them: the Taoists, Jesus, and Mazdak among the Persians.

That opposition rests on two arguments.

The first is that concentration of wealth in the hands of a small group necessarily deprives the majority.

As Fakhr al-Din al-Razi observed in his Quranic commentary, the existence of surplus wealth in one person’s hands corresponds to the deprivation of someone else, because wealth is limited. If it is concentrated in a few hands, it cannot reach everyone.

Today some people try to disprove this theory by pointing to the West, where some rich people possess fortunes once enjoyed only by emperors and sultans, while at the same time broad sections of the population enjoy a reasonable standard of living.

But this is an exceptional condition.

The West takes possession of the wealth of the whole world and pumps it into its own countries.

Because the accumulation is so enormous, what the rich take for themselves does not prevent some part of it from reaching the people—not all the people, but a wider section of them.

I once discussed this with a British leftist.

I told him:

“We could start the revolution in Europe ourselves.”

He asked:

“How?”

I said:

“Cut off the oil. Withdraw our money from your banks. Close our markets to your products. Then the capitalists will no longer be able to bribe a large section of the people into keeping quiet.”

He accepted the argument in theory.

But he replied:

“Then the capitalists would turn into the kind of tyrants you have in your countries and drown the people in blood.”

However much resources increase through industrial development, concentration of wealth in a few hands still reduces the share that reaches the people and prevents sufficient resources from being available to meet their needs.

The second reason Sufis and Eastern thinkers in general opposed wealth is that wealth leads to luxury, and luxury corrupts the human being.

Once a person possesses more money than he needs, the range of his appetites expands.

An animal impulse grows inside him, tightening the grip of selfishness and excessive self-concern, blocking his awareness of other people’s interests, rights, and feelings, and drowning him in pleasure-seeking.

This disfigures the human essence.

Through reason, human beings became able to reorganise the demands of nature and restrict them to what is necessary, creating space for a spiritual development that nature itself does not care about.

From nature’s point of view, a human being differs little from an animal or plant.

His basic needs are food, drink, and sex.

Money causes these needs to expand until they cross the necessary limits. Once they begin to be pursued for their own sake as a result of wealth, the human being turns into an animal that eats and drinks.

I will not say **mates**, because an animal practices sex to preserve the species, whereas the human being also practices it for pleasure.

On this point, the human being is less civilised than the animal.

That, in any case, is nature’s judgment.

The ordinary person keeps his appetites under control and spends according to his means.

The rich person lets them loose.

He corrupts himself and others.

Money always carries the possibility of tyranny, as the Quran says:

> **Man becomes arrogant when he sees himself as self-sufficient.**

Tyranny has two sources: power and money.

The first appears in *Nahj al-Balagha*:

> **Whoever gains power becomes a despot.**

That is the nature of power, as Ibn Khaldun diagnosed it, and the nature of wealth, as the Quran diagnosed it.

We see an example of it today in the power of financial oligarchies over Western politics, driving aggression against other peoples to the point of threatening entire countries with nuclear destruction.

There is no remedy for this disaster except to answer the call of Eastern thinkers and redistribute wealth among the people so that it cannot accumulate in a few hands.

In other words: prevent hoarding and confiscate concentrated wealth wherever it appears.

Western bourgeois culture portrays this principle as a form of religious asceticism.

That is an act of appropriation that ignores the actual relationship between religion and luxury, and ignores the staggering cost of religion itself: its clergy, its institutions, its buildings, its ceremonies, and the comfortable lives of religious officials.

Western bourgeois culture also confuses Sufism with religion and Taoism with religion.

As we have now seen, Sufism is religion’s opposite and its greatest rival.

Taoism did not confront religion in the first place, because China and Chinese civilisation had no religion in the relevant sense.

The Eurasian struggle between religion and philosophy was unknown in China.

We need to stop randomly merging **religion** and **spirit**.

That confusion is one of the fantasies and falsifications produced by people who distort history.

Eastern thought is not Eastern theology.

The distance between the two is the distance between reason and faith.

Religion exercised enormous power and domination, but thought had its own enormous field of counter-movement.

The life of the East cannot be reduced to the prevailing political and religious authorities.

It also contains opposition: that distant current ignored by Orientalists and by their Eastern commentators because acknowledging it would destroy the myth of the religious East.

The French Enlightenment of the eighteenth century is a small chapter beside the many chapters of Islamic and Chinese enlightenment.

For every Rousseau, Voltaire, and Montesquieu, the East has figures of comparable number and force.

But Westerners understood spirituality only through religion.

When some of them rebelled against religion, they therefore fell into crude materialism rather than scientific materialism.

Eastern thinkers, by contrast, rebelled against religion by separating religion from spirituality.

The enlightenment thinkers of Islam and China could therefore be deeply spiritual at the same time as they were heretics.

Heresy did not detach itself from the spirit.

In fact, it remained rooted in it.

Eastern history contains a sharp contrast between the pleasure-seeking clergy and the spirituality of heretics.

The Eastern ascetic—at least within the traditions of Islam and China—is modest, restrained, and loving toward ordinary people.

The religious official and the official of the state are greedy, arrogant, and hostile to them.

Religiosity does not require asceticism.

The Muslim sultans who came after Ali ibn Abi Talib were religious, perhaps in many cases more overtly religious than Ali himself, apart from a small number of openly dissolute rulers such as al-Walid ibn Yazid.

At the same time, they were pleasure-seekers.

Harun al-Rashid used to pray one hundred additional units of prayer each day, even though Muhammad’s own prayer did not exceed eleven. He would make pilgrimage one year and wage jihad the next.

At the same time, Harun was a sexual brute and an animal of pleasure.

This is the general description that applies to the rulers of Islamic states, apart from a handful such as Nur al-Din the Martyr and Salah al-Din al-Ayyubi.

Salah al-Din was both devout and ascetic, perhaps because of his Kurdish formation.

But the general pattern among the great majority of sultans was religiosity combined with pleasure-seeking.

Religious authorities, meanwhile, preached asceticism and made it a requirement of popular religion—that is, the religion of ordinary people.

This is one of their tricks.

Material enjoyment is left to the elite while deprivation is imposed on everyone else.

The relationship between elites and the clergy requires no explanation.

It is one of the most persistent facts of history.

Sufism was not a perfectly coherent movement.

In fact, as a movement, its negative side was larger than its positive side.

All sorts of people entered it: good and corrupt, honest and treacherous, ascetic and opportunist.

The lesser Sufis cannot be praised as a group. We have to choose among them.

There were young people who dissolved into the Sufi sensibility, cared deeply, and sincerely loved God through the human women they loved.

Many others mixed the Sufi path with foreign additions they had brought in from outside.

The movement was subjected to a great deal of harsh criticism.

Sufis became proverbial examples of objectionable conduct.

One saying quoted by al-Zabidi in *Taj al-Arus* calls them, in a rhyming insult, **“dancers and platter-men”**—people who dance and pile food onto their plates.

There are amusing stories about the tricks some used to get food.

One Sufi supposedly had engraved on his ring:

**“Its food is everlasting.”**

Another had:

**“Bring us our lunch.”**

A Sufi from Merv was once preaching to people and made them cry.

When the crying went on too long, he took out a small lute and began to play, saying:

**“After all this long grief, we need an hour of happiness.”**

An amusing and delightful man.

But hardly one of the best Sufis.

Very early in the Sufi period, Shaqiq al-Balkhi said:

“I found people talking about poverty, with a new story every night. I saw the people of Damascus talking and elaborating theories, but I gained nothing from their knowledge. In Egypt I found a group I did not like. In Baghdad I saw people describing the states of the knowers, though I do not know that any of them ever reached what they talked about.”

(Ibn Asakir, 8/333)

This is what happens in a movement that gathers all kinds of people together.

They were mocked for claiming purity and for the idea that their very name came from purity.

Two satirical lines were attributed to one of their critics:

> I see the Sufis as the worst of tribes—
> go tell them so, and spare me their pretence.
> Eat like the beasts if that is what you worship,
> and dance for me.

We examined the attribution elsewhere in our book on al-Maʿarri.

The criticism is directed at conduct contradicting the very meaning of Sufism.

Among the poems written against them are lines by Ibn Sabir al-Manjaniqi, a catapult officer from Baghdad in the first third of the seventh Islamic century.

He complains about Sufis whom he had the misfortune of receiving as guests:

> My lord, O shaykh of the ribat,
> whose excellence and rank are plain,
> I come to complain of Sufi tyranny:
> they spent the night as guests and friends.
>
> I brought them food and asked their leave,
> while all night my own belly cried with hunger.
> They walked all over the bread—
> and ascetics, after all, are famous for walking on water.

In another poem he mentions their excess in dancing and drinking and blames their shaykhs:

> They put on wool and left purity behind,
> the shaykhs of our age, drinking their wine.
>
> Dancing and pretty boys are their concern:
> long hair hanging beneath a short hem.

With Ibn Sabir the situation is neatly reversed.

Instead of a Sufi condemning the soldier, we have a soldier attacking the Sufis and accusing them of corruption.

We have to accept this as part of the movement.

It is why I prefer to speak of **the Sufism of the great masters** rather than of Sufism in general.

The truth of Sufism is found among its great masters, not among the mass of lesser followers.

In this respect Sufism resembles philosophy.

Philosophy developed through individual philosophers rather than through some moving current that hardened into a sect.

Sufi truth, like philosophical truth, was the work of outstanding individuals.

We should therefore avoid identifying the whole phenomenon with the movement around it.

Like philosophy, Sufism remained fundamentally an individual phenomenon whose greatness and historical influence came from its great figures, not from the miscellaneous crowds gathered beneath their name.

The same is true of Taoist philosophy.

There were minor Taoists just as there were minor Sufis, and some exploited the name of the Tao to make a living.

The greatness and historical force of Taoism lay in its exceptional figures: Laozi, Zhuangzi, Liezi, Huainanzi, and others of their rank.

Al-Sarraj al-Tusi observes in *The Book of Flashes* that lesser Sufis imitated the great masters before they themselves had become firmly grounded in Sufism.

He counted this among the causes of corruption and deviation among them.

The great masters differed in how directly they entered the field of social struggle.

The masters of philosophical Sufism did not immerse themselves in the daily hardships of ordinary people, although they remained committed to freedom of belief and opposed religious persecution.

They were also more flexible about relations with the state.

Like the philosophers, they built bridges toward it.

Their concerns were largely those of intellectuals: searching for the truth of things, defending freedom of thought, and protecting human life from the violence of the state.

Their own lifestyles ranged from asceticism to moderation.

None of them, however, seems to have left substantial wealth to his children.

Ibn Arabi, the one closest to rulers, left no fortune for his sons because he distributed whatever remained beyond his needs from the gifts rulers gave him.

In the field of knowledge, Ibn Arabi added enough to Islamic philosophy to place him at the head of any list of its major philosophers.

His greatest achievement was the unity of being.

Dialectical logic belongs there too.

His writings, especially the *Futuhat*, contain an enormous range of knowledge, some belonging to astronomy, some to the natural sciences, some to medicine, and some to other theoretical and practical fields.

He even discussed the possibility of manufacturing a human being, a scientific luxury that Jabir ibn Hayyan had dreamed of and that some modern scientists have discussed again.

It would be more useful to search for medicines capable of treating incurable diseases and extending human life.

The great masters of social Sufism also differed among themselves.

Hallaj tried the path of revolution.

He wanted to establish the **state of the people** on the ruins of the sultan’s state.

When they crucified him, Abu Bakr al-Shibli came to him and said:

**“Did I not warn you about the world?”**

The bloodshed of that experiment discouraged the others from becoming directly involved.

They turned instead toward a firm principle of boycott and opposition.

They tried to ease the condition of the people with whatever resources became available to them, while continuing to argue for making wealth common, preventing hoarding, and extending its benefits to everyone.

It was almost as though they lived waiting for someone still to come who would finally carry these principles out.

They insisted that the human tragedy lies in **poverty and humiliation**.

Poverty deprives a person of his right to live.

Humiliation destroys his human essence.

Poverty comes from the state.

Humiliation also arises in ordinary relationships between people, and its source is taking from others and becoming dependent on them.

Whoever takes something from another person, or asks that person to meet a need, places himself in humiliation.

The Sufis did not reject mutual exchange, provided it took place between equals.

A poor person should not take from a rich person, nor a weak person from someone powerful.

Their own distribution of gifts to poor people fell, in their view, within relations of equality.

They did not boast about what they gave or use it to establish superiority, because they themselves bore the mark of poverty.

Most of the great masters accepted **openings**—money sent to them by wealthy people whose character and piety they trusted. But they did not use that money for themselves. They distributed everything that came to them and lived from their own resources, such as manual work or an inheritance from their family.

When a great master had enough strength and standing, he could even wring money out of the ruler, provided it was taken by pressure rather than received as a favour. This seems to have been the way Abd al-Qadir al-Jilani operated. He was one of the strongest and most formidable of the great masters. As we saw earlier, he enjoyed intense popular support among the people of Baghdad, which put him in a position to confront the caliph. One could almost say that Baghdad in his time had two caliphs: Abd al-Qadir al-Kilani and the Abbasid caliph.

So although the Sufis forbade a poor person from taking help directly from a rich person, in order to protect his dignity, they themselves would give him money that had come into their hands and did not regard this as humiliating him.

Naturally, if the state itself had been in their hands, the problem would disappear altogether, because public money belongs to the people. This is also why they allowed the poor to take from alms-tax funds without treating the money as charity. The alms tax is an obligation and can be taken from the rich by force; voluntary charity is a gift and a favour.

Their concern with feeding the poor was matched by their concern with preserving their dignity.

They were also fairly close to one another in the degree of asceticism they practised. Obviously they ate enough to keep their bodies alive. Without that, they could not have survived. I tried otherwise myself, as I have already explained.

Their choice lay in giving up delicacies and whatever went beyond the amount needed to satisfy hunger. They could do without these things.

The same applied to clothing. They did not dress for luxury or display, but for covering and warmth. Ordinary wool and cotton were enough, and cleanliness was the condition they insisted on.

The great masters did not approve of patched robes. Those belonged to lesser Sufis who used them to attract attention. The masters dressed simply, but not in rags.

Naturally they did not live in palaces. They lived in the same kind of houses as ordinary people.

Their mount was the donkey, the animal of the poor. They reported that the Prophet rode a bare donkey without even a saddlecloth, and that riding a donkey protected a person from arrogance because aristocrats rode fine horses.

In everyday life they mixed with the poor in order to know their circumstances and give whatever help they could.

We have already seen that al-Jilani helped poor people clean lice from their clothes.

Their teaching gatherings were normally for the poor. They were uncomfortable when rich people or officials attended, and the same applied to their gatherings of music.

It was said of Sufyan al-Thawri that the poor sat in his gathering like sultans, while any rich man who attended sat at the back.

If they heard that a rich man was devout, they mocked him. The worship demanded of a rich man, in their view, was to distribute whatever wealth remained beyond his needs. Prayer and fasting would do him no good in place of that.

The great masters did not look shabby in the manner of later dervishes. As I have said, they did not wear patched clothing. Their clothes were simply inexpensive: cotton or ordinary wool.

They cleaned their teeth with the *siwak*, following the established practice, and among the small things they carried were a comb and a tooth-stick: the comb to keep the beard from matting and tangling, and the *siwak* to clean the teeth.

They also used perfume, following the practice of the son of Amina.

For them, the line between simplicity and squalor was clear. The distinction disappeared only in the later dervish age that began with the Ottomans.

A small number among them did regard bathing as a luxury, and some walked barefoot.

Bishr al-Hafi—Bishr the Barefoot—was one of these, and there is a story behind his bare feet.

He once went to a cobbler and asked him to mend a small tear in his sandal.

The cobbler said:

“How much trouble you poor Sufis cause other people!”

Bishr took off his sandals, handed them to the cobbler, and walked away barefoot. He remained barefoot after that.

Once he went to visit one of his companions and knocked at the door. A little girl came out and asked who he was.

“Bishr al-Hafi,” he said.

She went to tell her father, then asked:

“Why is he barefoot? If he bought a pair of sandals for one dirham, he would lose the name!”

Bishr was closer to severe austerity than ordinary asceticism. He lived in Baghdad at the same time as Maruf al-Karkhi, who was an ascetic without being an extreme one.

The Sufis were also accused of smoking hashish. The accusation is old; it appears in *The Devil’s Delusion* (p. 373).

It was probably not entirely invented. A Sufi who had not yet become firmly established on the path, had not mastered meditation, and had not developed the spiritual ability to commune with the spirit of the universe might turn to a drug in the hope of reaching the state more quickly.

A great master had no need for it. His spirit had been trained in meditation and union, and the depth of his feeling and the strength of his inward intuition made drugs unnecessary.

I would not rule out, however, that some of them drank wine for ecstasy.

Ibn al-Farid’s great wine poem strongly suggests that he drank, and Ibn al-Farid was a rapturous lover: sensitive, delicate, and intoxicated with beauty.

In truth, it is difficult to read that magnificent wine poem without wanting a drink.

***

Did Sufism fail?

If we accept the theory of failure so beloved by our writers, then of course it failed.

Zaki Mubarak had already announced this verdict in his foolish book on Sufism in literature. Unfortunately for him, he did not discover what Adonis later discovered through his fine critical sense and his deep literary and artistic culture.

One of Zaki Mubarak’s signs of Sufism’s failure was that his shoes were stolen whenever he entered a mosque, whereas no one stole them in modern institutions. His point was that modern culture or civilisation was therefore more successful and more useful.

His argument echoes the Orientalism that swept violently through his generation. Taha Husayn was almost the only one to escape it. Like Adonis, he combined a deep command of the heritage with serious engagement with modern culture. He was not the product of a single source or a single set of teachers. He also possessed a personal awareness and quickness of mind joined to a sound natural intelligence.

People differ in their gifts.

But Zaki Mubarak did not know how Westerners steal, or what they steal. Nor did he know how we steal, or how the theft of the poor differs from the theft of the rich.

Writers often know very little about these things because they take their ideas from books. They know little about society, its sciences, or its problems. Of the people living right beside them, they know only that they are “the common people.” Of distant peoples, they know only what they have read.

This makes it difficult for them to grasp the fine differences between civilisations, continents, and historical periods: what it means for a civilisation to rise, what it means for one to recede, and what follows from either process.

Among the people of that generation, the only one who really deserved the name *thinker* was Taha Husayn. The rest were critics, prose writers, narrators, and translators—with translation the profession most common among them.

Civilisations do not fall. They move from one place to another.

If we spoke of the “fall of civilisation” in the way our writers do, we would have to ask where modern civilisation itself is now.

What people call the fall of a civilisation is simply its coming to a stop in the place where it first arose and its movement somewhere else.

Several civilisations may also succeed one another in the same place, as happened in Mesopotamia.

Islamic civilisation arose during a period of civilisational interruption across the Fertile Crescent following the end of the last Babylonian age in the fifth century BCE. In Europe, this was also a period of interruption after Roman civilisation came to an end and the continent entered the darkness of its medieval centuries.

Muslims took up the Semitic inheritance together with the inheritances of the Greeks, Persians, and Indians and made from them an Islamic civilisation.

When the expansion of that civilisation slowed, its achievements had already passed into Europe.

While Muslims forgot their own scientists and philosophers, Europeans studied and developed their work. While the European intellectual proudly traced himself back to Ibn Rushd, Ibn Rushd was almost unknown in the Ottoman age.

Islamic civilisation did not fall and did not fail.

It changed its home.

After a civilisation moves elsewhere, something of it remains latent in its old home.

This survives in two storehouses.

The first is everyday life: people inherit the values of the civilisation that has moved on, preserving some of them and altering others.

The second is its written record. Texts remain in their repositories until someone discovers them and brings them back to readers, just as Muslims recovered Greek books from Roman collections.

In our own age we rediscovered the written record of Islamic civilisation. We came to know it again and fashioned from it what we now call the heritage.

Its absence from us was never as complete as the absence of Greek books had been from Muslims. Islamic culture continued through its dead period by way of commentators and transmitters. Later generations still knew the names of al-Hallaj, Ibn Sina, al-Jilani, Ibn Arabi, and others, even when they knew the names without knowing the actual history behind them.

In this sense we can speak of Sufism continuing—not as an unbroken line, but as a heritage rediscovered.

Along the way, it was misunderstood from several directions.

The Orientalists, who were the first to take a serious interest in it, treated it as part of Eastern folklore.

Modern writers such as Zaki Mubarak wrote within the Orientalist framework.

The Salafis saw it through the lens of dervish practice.

Writers on the left and liberals treated it as another form of supernaturalism.

A great deal of credit for reopening the meaning of Sufism belongs to Adonis and his modernist school. They were among the first to recognise it as a distinct current within the heritage, marked by intense poetic sensitivity and by impulses toward rebellion and freedom.

Under Adonis’s influence, a generation of writers emerged who studied Sufism, searched out its great masters, and brought it into their own writing.

We should not, however, deny the influence of Louis Massignon.

In the case of al-Hallaj, Massignon published his writings and devoted studies to him in which the valuable and the worthless, knowledge and ignorance, are all mixed together.

The present generation encountered al-Hallaj through that work, and al-Hallaj took on an image resembling that of Christ and Husayn.

But like Christ and Husayn, he was not understood through the relationship between his movement and society—as a social fighter whose movement belonged to social struggle. Instead he was presented as a victim of freedom of thought, because that was how Massignon introduced him.

This has left us with the heavy task of returning al-Hallaj to what I regard as his historical place: among the victims of social revolution, not the victims of freedom of thought.[^ms-v1-027]

The rediscovery of **social Sufism** goes back to the martyred scholar, though for him it appeared mainly as a line of separation from the state.

The line of struggle for ordinary people—the communalist field—is our contribution.

Once rediscovered, Sufism can enter modern culture as a source of independence and rootedness instead of dependence on Western culture.

The communalist line within social Sufism can help sharpen our culture, free it from the dominance of the Western commercial law, and form intellectuals who do not merely talk about the people and the hungry but live among them and confront, beside them, the power of the state and money.

Our present culture, translated from the West, is a culture of slogans and chants shouted by an intellectual sitting on his sofa with an imported pack of cigarettes and a bottle of red wine beside him.

He may write these slogans or debate them in five-star halls, where cultural conferences are usually held.

The intellectual demands his own rights as though they were the rights of the people, and measures the patriotism of the state by how well it supports culture.

These are familiar European ideas. In Europe, culture is supported through official and semi-official institutions and universities, and intellectuals enjoy a status above that of ordinary people, greater resources, and better living conditions.

In the Eastern understanding, culture is a commitment toward **people**—*ren*.

The seriousness and sincerity of an intellectual are measured by the strength of his bond with the poor, by his willingness to surrender what the Sufis call the claims of the self, and by his ability to bring his standard of living down to that of ordinary people in keeping with the popular meaning of asceticism.

The intellectual’s personal happiness should come from serving people and struggling beside them for their rights. His pleasure comes from that achievement.

The Eastern intellectual is thin in body and strong in will.

Stoutness is suspicious. So are visible signs of comfort: expensive clothing, a luxurious vehicle grander than a donkey, or an elegant house.

No Taoist or Sufi could display such things and remain accepted within his own circle.

The presence of Sufism in our modern culture, and in the communalist struggle we expect to begin, will show that Sufism did not fail.

Nor did the communal wisdom of China fail—the wisdom from which modern Chinese communists emerged in order to root Marxism locally and put it into action through an experiment that showed that Marxism could work in the East only through the East’s own communal heritage, while in a purely Western form it remained weak and obstructive.

I saw with my own eyes what Chinese communism accomplished: it rescued a continent from servitude and a billion people from hunger.

Whatever is said about its crises, if true, comes from outside sources: Soviet or Western.

Chinese communism did not, in my view, retreat for economic reasons, as capitalist media and their followers among our own people claim. What happened was the result of a backward political system inherited from the Soviets, combined with American plotting alongside a Westernised Chinese bureaucracy.

Under the new Western dominance, the Chinese people lost many of their rights, and signs of hunger, poverty, begging, prostitution, and opium returned—things the communists had removed in order to build the clean society Zhuangzi called the **Great Society**.

Our intellectuals are unimpressed by Chinese communism because it is not polished, because it grants no special rights or cultural privileges, and because its peasant appearance exposes their own European taste.

They passed judgment on China’s economic development using Western statistics and accused the communists of holding it back.

But China advanced in every field under the communists and under their People’s Republic, which in its first thirty years provided food security for a billion people, who came to eat three meals a day without relying on imported food.

What should we call an economic system capable of achieving such a thing in twenty years?

After this great agricultural revolution, the communists turned toward industrialisation using their own resources.

They discovered oil after foreign experts had told them that China had none. China then achieved self-sufficiency in this strategic resource and began exporting the surplus.

The communists also achieved four major technologies: aviation, missiles, nuclear technology, and satellites.

They made major advances in light industry as well.

Had it not been for the mutual childishness of Mao Zedong and the Soviet leaders, they might even have been able to prevent the collapse of the Soviet Union by helping solve the consumer crisis that overwhelmed it.

#### Glimmer

Uthman al-Marin said:

> The drunkenness of ecstasy is, in its meaning, sobriety;  
> and the sobriety of ecstasy is drunkenness in union.

## The Final Orbit

### Texts and Positions

* Ibn Asakir relates a saying attributed to Abu Hurayra (6/16):

> The light of wisdom is hunger. The foundation of religion is giving up the world. Repentance before God means loving the poor and staying close to them.

This saying has no authentic basis. Nor is it one of the inventions of Abu Hurayra, who himself has been accused of fabricating hadith. It was invented by the Sufis.

* Shaqiq al-Balkhi said:

> I entered the home of Abbad ibn Kathir and found pots bubbling away, some sour and some sweet. I was surprised by what I saw. His servant said to me: “Man of Khurasan, he has not eaten meat for years. Every day he has seven pots prepared, sour and sweet, and uses them to feed the poor, the sick, and those who have no way of supporting themselves.”

Ibn Asakir, in his biography of Shaqiq (6/322).

The story suggests that Abbad ibn Kathir had money and used it to relieve other people’s suffering.

* Shaqiq al-Balkhi once met Ibrahim ibn Adham. Ibrahim asked him:

“What first set you on the path that brought you here?”

Shaqiq replied:

> I was travelling through the wilderness when I saw a bird with two broken wings stranded in a barren place. I said to myself: Let me see where its food comes from.
>
> I sat opposite it. Before long another bird appeared with a locust in its beak and placed it in the beak of the bird with the broken wings.
>
> I said to myself: The One who sent this healthy bird to feed a bird with broken wings in the middle of nowhere can provide for me wherever I may be.
>
> So I stopped earning a living and devoted myself to worship.

Ibrahim said:

> Shaqiq, why not be the healthy bird that feeds the injured one, and so be better than him? Have you not heard that the Prophet said, “The upper hand is better than the lower”? One sign of the believer is that in everything he does, he seeks the higher of two stations until he reaches the rank of the righteous.

Shaqiq took Ibrahim’s hand, kissed it, and said:

“You are our teacher, Abu Ishaq.”

Ibn Asakir, in his biography of Shaqiq.

*Some Sufis who came out of a legal-religious background insisted on complete trust in God and on giving up work, relying on God to provide. Shaqiq’s story reflects this religious meaning of trust. Ibrahim ibn Adham, by contrast, belonged to the working Sufis. He lived from the labour of his own hands and waited for neither God nor other people to provide his livelihood. Shaqiq admitted his mistake and agreed with Ibrahim that a person should not live in dependence but should work for his food.*

*Junayd was asked what a person should do to earn a living. He replied: “Draw water and gather date stones.”* (*al-Lumaʿ*, 195.) *That was his answer for someone who could find no other work.*

* In *al-Lumaʿ*, Ibrahim ibn Shayban says:

> We would not keep company with anyone who said, “My sandal.”

Because the word **my** implies ownership. Someone who speaks that way has stepped outside the communal relationship binding them together.

The possessive **my** has no place in the Sufi.

* The People of Permissibility said that things are, by their original nature, open to everyone. Restrictions arose only because people began taking more than their share. Where there is no such encroachment, things return to their original condition of free availability.

They appealed to verses such as:

> “…and We caused grain to grow in it, and grapes and fresh vegetation… as provision for you and your livestock.”

They said that this was addressed to people collectively, without assigning these things exclusively to one person rather than another. (*al-Lumaʿ*, 424–425.)

This corresponds to the communist organisation of human needs: things are held in common and are open to all. Each person takes enough for their needs without violating anyone else’s right and without taking more than necessary.

According to the Sufi doctrine of permissibility, things became enclosed and forbidden because people began taking them excessively: taking what they did not need, out of greed and selfishness. Remove that aggression and things return to their original state of common availability.

Some accounts confuse common ownership of wealth with women being held in common. This may simply have been an accusation added later rather than something these people themselves taught. Defenders of private property have always accused communalists of sharing women as well as wealth. The Qarmatians were accused of it, as were the Sufis, and the Mazdakites before them.

Eastern communalism means sharing wealth, not sharing women.

* Hallaj said:

> Our secrets are virgins. No deluded imagination has broken their seal.

(*al-Lumaʿ*, 321.)

* In *ʿAwārif al-Maʿārif*:

> The one who reaches the end stands on the narrow path between excess and deficiency.

The qutb—the supreme Sufi saint—orders his conduct, his private life, and his dealings with others according to the law of moderation. This includes the way he lives. His material life falls somewhere between extreme austerity and luxury, in keeping with their understanding of asceticism.

They differed among themselves. Some lived very austerely, while others were content with ordinary renunciation. Some married and others did not. Most of those who married took one wife; a smaller number had several.

The people I am concerned with here are those who lived austerely and either did not marry or married one woman.

* Junayd was asked what the end of the path was.

He replied:

> Returning to the beginning.

Some explained this by saying that a person begins in ignorance, reaches knowledge, and then returns to bewilderment and ignorance.

This concerns knowledge of God.

One of them said:

> Those who know God best are the most bewildered by Him.

The reason is the problem of divinity itself, which has no philosophical or physical foundation.

But Junayd’s words can also be understood differently, through their doctrine of opposites. The more a person knows, the more ignorant he becomes. The more firmly established he becomes on the path, the more he returns to simplicity. Whoever reaches the road of truth becomes bewildered. Whoever is illuminated by the sun falls into darkness.

Nothing in existence is absolute except the First Being.

* A saying:

> A servant does not reach the reality of faith until people think he is mad.

This saying was invented by the Sufis.

Madness is one of the effects of intoxication and ecstasy: breaking free from the restraints of reason and entering the open space of the irrational.

#### Why Does He Not Remember God?

Al-Rudhbari:

> God knows that I do not remember Him.
> How could I remember Him when I never forget Him?

The second half-line is awkward. Its syntax has been forced into shape to preserve the metre. I tried to repair it and could not.

What the poet means is that he does not “remember” God because he has never forgotten Him.

But there is a sleight of hand here. Normally, you remember something after having forgotten it. Yet the “remembrance of God” means praising and invoking Him; it has nothing to do with having forgotten Him in the first place.

What the poet really means is **unity**.

Once someone becomes one with God, becomes part of Him, and shares being with Him, how can he remember God when he himself is God?

At that point worship also loses its meaning.

He would only be worshipping himself.

* A Sufi saying:

> Nothing of the unseen appears
> while anything remains in the pocket.

Money blocks vision.

**The rich intellectual is blind.**

A story:

Shaykh Abu al-Qasim al-Iskandarani sold an animal to a man. A few days later the man came back and said:

“My master, the animal I bought from you refuses to eat anything in my house.”

The shaykh asked:

“What do you do for a living?”

The man replied:

“I dance for the governor.”

The shaykh said:

“Our animal does not eat unlawful earnings…”

The Sufis dance too.

But they dance in their own gatherings, not at the governor’s court.

* **Annihilation in humanity.** Ibn Asakir relates:

> Sari al-Saqati said: “I was returning from the Eid prayer when I saw a dishevelled little boy with Maʿruf al-Karkhi. I asked him, ‘Who is this?’
>
> “He said: ‘I saw the other children playing while he stood apart, miserable. I asked him why he was not playing.
>
> “‘He said: “I am an orphan. I have no walnuts.”
>
> “‘So I thought perhaps I could go out, gather date stones, sell them, and buy him some walnuts.’”

Children still play with walnuts in our own time. Several walnuts are thrown toward a hole in the ground. If they all go in, the player takes them.

Collecting date stones also survived into our time. As children, my friends and I used to gather them and sell them to blacksmiths so that we could buy sweets and other things.

Maʿruf al-Karkhi must have reached into his pocket and found nothing he could give the boy to buy walnuts.

So he thought of going out with him, gathering date stones, selling them, and giving him the money so that he could buy his walnuts.

What would Maʿruf have done if the wealth of the state had fallen into his hands?

That is what **we** will do when the wealth of the state falls into ours.

Then everything will return to the people, and the worldly powers will have nothing left with which to lord it over the hungry.

The day of the oppressed against the oppressor is harder than the day of the oppressor against the oppressed.

* **The Price of Arrival.** Junayd is quoted by al-Shaʿrani in *al-Anwār al-Qudsiyya*, in the margin of the *Tabaqāt*:

> Among us, a man does not reach the rank of a true man until a thousand friends among the formal religious scholars testify that he is a heretic.

*The “formal scholars” are the scholars of religion.*

The remark suggests that the end of Sufism is the beginning of heresy.

* **What Is Sufism?**

A man asked Hallaj what Sufism was while Hallaj was hanging on the cross.

He answered:

> What you see is the easiest part of it.

* **Hallaj’s Communalism**

> A person who truly trusts in God does not eat while anyone in the town has a greater claim to that food.

(*al-Anwār al-Qudsiyya*.)

Here trust in God has a special meaning: leave the food to whoever needs it more, and entrust your own satisfaction to hunger.

> I have a soul that will perish or rise—
> by your life, through me—to something great.

God’s peace and blessings be upon him, and upon us.

We are walking toward the same destination.

* **Suhrawardi the Martyr: The Shrine Is Near**

> I say to my neighbour, tears running down:
> I have resolved to leave these homes.
>
> Let me go, and do not mourn.
> Of shooting stars, the noblest are those that travel.
>
> In darkness I saw a light,
> as though the night had dressed itself in day.
>
> How long must fish be my companions?
> How long must the dragon be my neighbour?
>
> How long should I accept a home in the wasteland
> when I have built my house above the two Farqad stars?
>
> A flash comes to me from Sanaʿa,
> reminding me how near the shrine has become.

* **Dreams**

Ayyub al-Sakhtiyani once saw the funeral of a sinner and slipped into a hallway so that he would not have to pray over him.

Later someone saw the dead man in a dream and asked:

“What did God do with you?”

He replied:

“He forgave me.”

Then he said:

> Tell Ayyub: “Say: If you possessed the treasuries of my Lord’s mercy, you would hold them back for fear of spending them.”

A fierce dream.

It rebukes the person who puts punishment before mercy and condemns everyone who differs from him to destruction.

* **A Reproach in a Dream**

Abu Bakr al-Rashidi said:

I saw Muhammad al-Tusi the teacher in a dream. He told me:

“Say to Abu Saʿid al-Saffar:

> We pledged never to turn away from love.
> Yet, by the life of love, you left us—we did not leave you.”

I woke up and told Abu Saʿid.

He said:

“I used to visit his grave every Friday. This was the one Friday I failed to go…”

* They said—and wrongly attributed it to Sufyan al-Thawri:

> Those who neglect the foundations are barred from arrival. Anyone who does not hold to necessity in what he says and does will never be able to keep food, drink, and sleep within the measure of need. Once he goes beyond necessity, the resolve of his heart begins to collapse.

In other words, natural needs must not be exceeded in the search for perfection and advancement.

The foundation is strength of resolve. A person must not do anything that weakens it.

Strength is one of the qualities of the Sufi.

* **Where Does Prayer Lead?**

A man asked Shibli about what mattered in prayer.

He replied:

> Cast your whole concern toward the higher world, and from there toward the lower world, until it breaks open in your heart that there is nothing but God…

Ibn Asakir, 2/245.

* **Their State at Death**

Junayd was dying. Someone said:

“Say: There is no god but God.”

He replied:

> I never forgot Him. Why should I remember Him now?

Abu Yaʿqub al-Nahrajuri was dying. They asked the same of him.

He replied:

> By the might of the One who never tastes death, nothing stands between Him and me except the veil of His majesty.

Dhu al-Nun al-Misri was dying. They asked him for some final advice.

He said:

> Do not distract me. I am astonished by the beauty of His gentleness.

Ibn Sahl al-Isfahani said:

> Do you imagine that I will die the way ordinary people die, from illness or something of that kind? I will simply be called, and I will answer.

One day he was walking when he suddenly said:

> At Your service.

And died.

Abu Amr al-Istakhri said:

> I saw Abu Turab al-Nakhshabi in the desert, standing upright and dead, with nothing holding him up.

* **The Challenge**

Ibrahim al-Khawwas said:

> Nothing ever frightened me without my going straight into it.

Whatever terrified him, he confronted.

* **Sufi Ethics**

Abu al-Hasan al-Warraq said:

> Among the most important rules we followed when we first gathered in the mosque of Abu Uthman were these:
>
> Whatever came to us, we gave others the first claim to it.
>
> We never went to sleep with anything guaranteed in our possession—that is, we did not sleep with money in our pockets that we had not distributed.
>
> If someone treated us badly, we did not take revenge for ourselves. We apologised to him and humbled ourselves before him.
>
> And whenever we felt contempt for anyone in our hearts, we got up and served him.

(*al-Ghunya* by al-Kilani, 2/183.)

* **Christian Echoes**

In al-Qushayri:

> If someone asks: What predominates in the saint when he is sober?
>
> The answer is: truthfulness in fulfilling what he owes to God; then gentleness and compassion toward creation in every state; then a mercy broad enough to include all creation; then patient endurance of them with good character; then eagerness to ask God to show them kindness without waiting for them to ask; then concern for their salvation; refusal to retaliate against them; guarding himself against resentment toward them; keeping his hands away from their wealth; abandoning every kind of greed; restraining his tongue from speaking badly of them; refusing to dwell on their faults; and refusing to be anyone’s enemy either in this world or in the next.

(p. 160.)

* **Absolute Rights**

Sufyan ibn Uyayna said:

> There are four things for which you owe no account: satisfying hunger, quenching thirst, covering nakedness, and finding shelter.

Ibn Qutayba, *ʿUyūn al-Akhbār*, Book of Asceticism.

That means:

**food, drink, clothing, and housing.**

These are absolute human claims. A person has the right to obtain them by whatever means are necessary and cannot be held to account for doing so.

The principle includes stealing and forcibly taking money from those who possess it when a person needs that money to obtain these necessities.

Does it also mean that a woman may sell her body to obtain the money she needs for these four things?

Certainly not.

Sufyan’s words mean taking money from those who possess it by force if necessary. They do not permit a person to sell honour or dignity, because that contradicts the essence of being human.

Within these limits, theft and fraud do not.

The point is therefore one of resistance, not humiliation or surrender.

* **The Hunger of the Ruler**

The Prophet Joseph was asked:

“Why do you go hungry when you control the treasuries of the land?”

He replied:

> I am afraid that if I eat my fill, I will forget the hungry.

This story was created by the opposition to establish a principle: the ruler must live austerely and remains responsible for the hungry.

Its meaning is also contained in the Iraqi saying about the full man and the hungry man: the people go hungry because their rulers and their intellectuals are full.

* **He Dies at the Front**

Ibrahim ibn Adham died in Byzantine territory, fighting the Europeans.

When he felt death approaching, he said:

> String my bow for me.

He took hold of it and died with the bow in his hand.

I should add that the tomb bearing his name in Jableh, in the Latakia region, cannot really be his grave.

It can only be treated as a memorial.

* **Read Them!**

Abu al-Qasim al-Nasrabadi:

> Comfort is a vessel filled with reproach.

Abu Saʿid al-Kharraz:

> My greatest sin toward Him is that I know Him.

Junayd:

> I once fell ill and asked God to cure me. He said to me inwardly: “Do not come between Me and yourself.”

Sahl al-Tustari:

> For thirty years I have been speaking to God, while people imagine I am speaking to them.

Abu al-Husayn al-Nuri:

> Fire burns whoever can feel it.
> But if a man is himself the fire, how can he burn?

Another said:

> God, You know how incapable I am of thanking You as You deserve. So thank Yourself on my behalf!

Abu Salih Muflih al-Hanbali:

> The world is forbidden to the heart but lawful to the self. Everything you are permitted to look at with the eye in your head, you are forbidden to look at with the eye of your heart.

The body is the garment of the heart.

The heart is the garment of the inner heart.

The inner heart is the garment of consciousness.

Consciousness is the garment of the secret.

The secret is the garment of knowledge.

Hallaj:

> I saw a bird among the birds of Sufism. It had two wings. It broke away from me just as it was about to fly.
>
> It asked me about purity.
>
> I said: “Cut off your wing with the shears of annihilation, or do not follow me.”
>
> It said: “Then what wing am I to fly with?”
>
> I said: “Poor fool! Nothing is like Him, and He is the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing.”
>
> That day the bird stopped in the sea of understanding and drowned.

(*Tā-Sīn of the Point*.)

* **Ibn Ata Allah al-Iskandari**

> The whole universe is darkness. It is lit only by the appearance of the Real within it.

> To ask Him for something is to accuse Him.

> Hope is hope only when accompanied by action. Otherwise it is merely a wish.

> Every word that comes out of a person wears the clothing of the heart it came from.

> Whatever you love, you become its slave.

> The less there is to delight you, the less there will be to grieve you.

> He lets harm come to you through their hands so that you will not rest your heart in them. He wants to unsettle you from everything, so that nothing can distract you from Him.

> The universe is larger than you only in bodily terms. In spiritual terms, it cannot contain you.

The human spirit is wider than the universe.

> Anyone living inside the universe whose horizons toward the unseen have not opened remains imprisoned by his surroundings and confined within the structure of his own self.

* **Knowledge and Ordinary People**

Malik ibn Dinar said:

> When a servant learns in order to put his knowledge into practice, knowledge increases him in knowledge. When he learns for some other purpose, it only increases him in corruption and arrogance, and he begins to despise ordinary people.

He is describing the **intellectual** who turns the authority of culture against the people, looks down on them, and treats them with contempt because he calls them ignorant.

Ordinary people may be illiterate.

That does not make them ignorant.

An illiterate person can be alert and aware of many things that escape the intellectual.

Ignorance can coexist with education.

Knowledge can coexist with illiteracy.

* Junayd:

> When love becomes true, the rules of propriety fall away.

*Perhaps this points toward freedom from formal worship at the stage of qutbhood, the highest rank of sainthood.*

* Abu Uthman al-Maghribi:

> The knower is consumed in the seas of realisation.

* Hallaj:

> The mark of the knower is that he has emptied himself of both this world and the next.

* **The Sufi and the Soldier**

In al-Shaʿrani’s *Tabaqāt*:

Shaykh Abd al-Rahman al-Dawudi was eating fish when someone told him that some soldiers had eaten on the bank of the river where his fish were caught. When they finished, they shook the remains from their tablecloth into the river, and the fish rushed to eat them.

The shaykh never ate fish again.

*Some of the soldiers’ food had entered the bodies of the fish. They were no longer fit to eat…*

* **From Shuʿayb Abu Madyan**

> Three of the most harmful companions are a heedless scholar, an ignorant Sufi, and a preacher who flatters power.

He places ignorance beside education.

An ignorant Sufi.

> No one reaches pure freedom while any remnant of himself remains.

> The traveller is going toward Him. The knower is going from Him.

The knower stands at a higher stage than the traveller.

The end returns to the beginning.

> Discipline of the body means keeping the limbs from what harms them. Discipline of the heart means refusing to rest in the worldly powers. Discipline of the self means giving up claims about oneself.

Keeping the limbs from harm means not taking into the body what damages it.

The “worldly powers” are the state, the rich, and the religious establishment.

Giving up claims means humility: not boasting of having “arrived.”

When the knower drowns in the seas of realisation, his personality disappears. There is no longer a self there from which a boast could arise.

> Spiritual chivalry means seeing what is good in people and becoming absent from their faults.

> Spirits are care; bodies are protection. This is tasted, not read.

> This path is founded on seriousness, effort, and breaking with what is familiar and habitual.

Sufism breaks through the familiar.

It leaves the rational behind.

It is an emigration from home.

> Any truth that does not erase the servant’s traces and fixed forms is not truth.

Thought is a process of erasure and change.

Anyone whom truth does not change does not belong to it.

* **An Example**

Ibn Kathir says of Shaykh Abd Allah al-Yunini:

> He was known as the Lion of Syria. He came from a village near Baalbek called Yunin. He had a Sufi lodge there which people visited.
>
> He was one of the great righteous men, famous for worship, spiritual discipline, commanding what is right and forbidding what is wrong.
>
> His commitment to asceticism and moral scruple was extraordinary. He accumulated nothing. He owned neither money nor clothing. What he wore was borrowed. In summer he wore no more than a shirt; in winter he put a fur garment over it. On his head he wore a cap made of goatskin.
>
> He regularly went out to fight—against the Crusaders and Byzantines—and shot with a bow whose draw weight was eighty ratls.
>
> At times he withdrew to Mount Lebanon. In winter he went to the springs of Ayn al-Asal, on the eastern side of the mountain overlooking the village of Duma east of Damascus, because the water there was warm. People would come to visit him there.
>
> At other times he came to Damascus and stayed at the foot of Mount Qasioun.
>
> He was known for unusual spiritual states and visions.
>
> People called him the Lion of Syria.
>
> He would never stand up for any ruler. He said:
>
> “You stand only for the Lord of the Worlds.”
>
> When al-Amjad, the ruler of Baalbek, came to him, the ruler would sit before him. The shaykh would say:
>
> “Amjad, you did this and this.”
>
> He would command him and forbid him, and al-Amjad would obey everything he said.
>
> He accepted gifts but kept nothing until the next day.
>
> When hunger became severe, he took almond leaves, rubbed them together, swallowed them, and drank cold water afterward.

They also claimed that in some years he travelled to the pilgrimage through the air.

The day before his death, he climbed to his lodge. He spent the night remembering God and remembering his companions and everyone who had ever shown him even the smallest kindness, and he prayed for them.

When morning came, he prayed with his companions. Then he leaned back and continued remembering God, holding his prayer beads.

He died sitting like that.

He did not fall.

The beads did not fall from his hand.

Al-Amjad came and saw him in that condition. He said:

“Why not build a structure around him like this, so that people can continue to see the miracle?”

They told him:

“That is not the Prophetic practice.”

So the body was moved and buried beneath the almond tree under which he used to sit remembering God.

He had lived beyond the age of eighty.

He died in 617 AH.

---

### Abu Yazid al-Bistami

> I am amazed that anyone who knows God can still worship Him.

This resembles another saying:

> Whoever knows the Real does not worship the Real.

> I kept watch over the heart for forty years. Then I looked into myself and saw that it was the Lord, and the Lord was the servant.

> God, You created these people without their knowledge and placed Your trust upon them without their consent. If You do not help them, who will?

> The wish of the knower in this world is that faith survive. His wish in the next is that creation be forgiven.

> As long as a servant believes there is someone in creation worse than himself, he is arrogant.

A person should never imagine that he has some superiority over other people or that he is better than they are.

He should look instead at himself and say:

“I am worse than the others.”

* Someone asked him:

“Can anything veil the knower from his Lord?”

He replied:

> If he himself is the veil, what else could possibly veil him?

> The Real looked into the inner secrets of the whole world and saw them empty of Him, except for my secret. He found it filled with Him. Then He addressed me in honour and said:
>
> “All the world are My servants except you…”

* He once set out for the pilgrimage.

Somewhere along the road, a man met him and asked:

“Where are you going?”

“To the pilgrimage.”

“How much money do you have?”

“Two hundred dirhams.”

The man said:

> Walk around me seven times and give me the two hundred dirhams. I have a family to support.

So Abu Yazid walked around him seven times and gave him the two hundred dirhams.

* A man asked him:

“How are you this morning?”

He replied:

> There is no morning and no evening. Morning and evening belong only to someone who is confined by attributes. I have no attribute.

> One night I went looking for my heart and could not find it.
>
> Near dawn I heard a voice say:
>
> “Abu Yazid, here you are looking for something other than Us?”

> The most complete quality of the knower is that the qualities of the Real flow through him, and a kind of lordship flows from him.

He was asked:

“What is Sufism?”

He replied:

> A quality of the Real that the servant puts on like a garment.

> There are two paradises: the paradise of bliss and the paradise of knowledge. The paradise of knowledge is eternal. The paradise of bliss is temporary.

A man asked:

“How do I reach Him?”

He replied:

> Disappear from the road and you will reach Him.

A man came and knocked on his door.

Abu Yazid asked:

“Who are you looking for?”

“Abu Yazid.”

Abu Yazid replied:

> So am I. I have been looking for Abu Yazid for twenty years.

> When a person finds himself, he is **given a choice**. When he loses himself, he becomes **the chooser**.

The first is passive.

The second is active.

And activity is closer to dignity than passivity.

> I wish the Day of Resurrection would come so that I could pitch my tent at the gate of Hell.

Someone asked:

“Why, Abu Yazid?”

He replied:

> Because I know that when Hell sees me it will go out, and I will become a mercy to creation.

> I crossed the barren places until I reached the wilderness. I crossed the wilderness until I reached the heavenly kingdom. I crossed the heavenly kingdom until I reached the King.
>
> I said: “With permission?”
>
> He said: “I have given you everything you have seen.”
>
> I said: “You know that I saw none of it.”
>
> He said: “Then what do you want?”
>
> I said: “I want not to want.”
>
> He said: “Granted.”

> When I reached His Oneness, and the first glimpse of unity came to me, I travelled through Him with understanding for ten years.
>
> Then my understanding itself became a bird whose body was made of Oneness and whose wing was made of eternity.
>
> I flew through the air of how-ness for another ten years. I flew a distance eight hundred million times greater than the distance between the Throne and the earth.
>
> I continued until I passed beyond permanence itself.
>
> Then I looked out over unity, where creation disappears from the knower and the knower disappears from creation.

> I vanished into the world of divine power, crossed the seas of the heavenly kingdom, and passed through the veils of divinity until I reached the Throne.
>
> I found it empty.
>
> So I threw myself upon it.

> Moses wanted to see God.
>
> I never wanted to see God.
>
> He wanted to see me.

> In this world He tricks you with the marketplace, and in the next world He tricks you with the marketplace.
>
> You are forever a slave of the marketplace.

> What is Hellfire?
>
> By God, if I saw it, I would put it out with the edge of this patched cloak.

> My heart is one.
>
> My concern is one.
>
> My spirit is one.

He heard a man say:

“All creation stands beneath the banner of our master Muhammad.”

Abu Yazid said:

> By God, my banner is greater than Muhammad’s banner. My banner is made of light. Beneath it stand all humans and jinn, together with the prophets.

> Glory be to me! Glory be to me! How great is my majesty!
>
> Nothing like me exists in heaven, and no quality like mine is known on earth.
>
> I am He, and He is I, and He is He.

* One day Abu Yazid washed his shirt outdoors. A companion said:

“Hang it on the vineyard wall.”

He replied:

“Its owner has not given me permission.”

“Then hang it on the tree.”

“The branches might break and be damaged.”

“Then spread it on the ground.”

“The grass would dry beneath it.”

So Abu Yazid turned his back to the sun, laid the shirt across his own back and head, waited until it dried, and put it on again.

* A Zoroastrian was told in Abu Yazid’s time:

“Become a Muslim.”

He replied:

> If Islam is what Abu Yazid practises, I could never bear it.
>
> And if Islam is what you practise, I have no desire for it.

---

### Women and Sufism

The Sufis allowed a woman to receive the Sufi cloak of initiation from an unrelated man.

If her husband tried to prevent her, she could seek separation from him and go to her shaykh.

### Sufism and Heresy

Ibn Aqil, one of the Hanbali theologians, said that much Sufi language points toward doing away with religious mediation and even prophecy itself.

When the Sufis spoke of hadith scholars, he said, they mocked them for having “taken their knowledge from one dead man after another.”

In this way, Ibn Aqil argued, they had already undermined prophecy.

And anyone who says:

> My heart told me directly from my Lord

has openly declared that he has no need of the Messenger.

Ibn Aqil also said:

> The heretics never dared openly reject religious law until the Sufis came along.

(*Talbīs Iblīs*, 372–374.)

### From the Words of the Sultan of Lovers

> I pray, and sing of her. When I recite,
> it is her remembrance that fills me.
>
> I sway with joy inside the prayer niche
> while she stands there before me.
>
> On pilgrimage, when I enter consecration for the House,
> I enter in her name.
>
> To abstain from her
> would be the breaking of my fast.
>
> Whom could I follow in love
> if I ever sought escape from it?
>
> Every imam in love
> follows me.
>
> In every limb there is a longing for her,
> a yearning that pulls me by the reins.

---

> Increase my bewilderment
> through the excess of love for You,
>
> and let Your burning heat
> run through the veins of the earth.[^ms-v1-028]
>
> And when I ask to see You as You truly are,
> then appear—
>
> and do not answer me:
> “You shall not see.”[^ms-v1-029]
>
> O heart, you promised me patience
> in loving them.
>
> Take care, then,
> not to grow cramped or weary.
>
> Tell those who came before me,
> and those who will come after,
>
> and everyone who looks
> upon my grief:
>
> take from me,
> follow my example,
>
> listen to me,
> and speak of my affliction among the people.
>
> I was alone with the Beloved.
> Between us was a secret
>
> finer than the breeze
> when it begins to move.
>
> I stood bewildered
> between His beauty and His majesty,
>
> until the language of the state itself
> began to speak for me.

---

> Be coy—you are made for it.
> Rule me: beauty has given you the right.
>
> The command is yours.
> Judge my soul however you wish,
>
> for beauty itself
> has appointed you over me.
>
> Test me in your love
> however you please.
>
> My only choice
> is whatever pleases you.
>
> In every state you have a greater claim on me
> than I have on myself,
>
> for without you
> I would never have been.
>
> Beauty veiled in majesty
> leaves the lover lost,
>
> until even torment
> becomes sweet.
>
> Leave me my eyes.
> Perhaps one day before I die
>
> I may see with them
> someone who has seen you.
>
> How far I am
> from what I sought!
>
> And how could my eye receive its share
> when it has never seen you?
>
> Everyone touched by beauty loves you,
> but I alone love everyone within your sanctuary.
>
> The lovers will be gathered
> beneath my banner,
>
> and every beautiful one
> beneath yours.
>
> Your distance from me
> is itself a kind of nearness,
>
> and inside your coldness
> I have found tenderness.
>
> Whenever you disappear outwardly
> from my sight,
>
> I turn inward in a thousand directions
> and find you a thousand times.
>
> It is no wonder that light
> is borrowed from my outward form,
>
> when my inward being
> is your home.

### From the Great Ode Rhyming in Tāʾ

#### The Jewel of Cosmic Beauty

> Speak of beauty as absolute.
> Do not confine it
>
> by tying it
> to ornament and decoration.
>
> The beauty of every beautiful man
> is borrowed from hers,
>
> and so is the beauty
> of every beautiful woman.
>
> Through her, Qays fell mad for Lubna.
> Through her, every lover has fallen—
>
> Majnun for Layla,
> Kuthayyir for Azza.
>
> Every one of them longed
> for an attribute she had put on,
>
> a beauty appearing
> inside a beautiful form.
>
> It was only she
> appearing through different manifestations.
>
> They imagined someone else,
> although it was she who appeared within them.
>
> She appeared by hiding
> and hid by appearing,
>
> taking on one form after another
> in every manifestation.
>
> At the beginning of creation
> she appeared to Adam
>
> in the form of Eve,
> before Eve became a mother.
>
> He fell in love with her
> so that through her he might become a father,
>
> and through the pair
> the law of children might appear.
>
> She has never stopped
> appearing and disappearing,
>
> according to the needs of every time
> and every age.
>
> She appears to lovers
> in every form,
>
> clothed in astonishing shapes
> of beauty.
>
> At one time she is Lubna.
> At another, Buthayna.
>
> For a while
> she is called Azza.
>
> They are not other than her,
> yet neither are they simply her.
>
> No one shares
> her beauty with her.
>
> So it is through union
> with her beauty
>
> that she appeared to me
> adorned within others.
>
> And I appeared for her
> inside every passionate lover,
>
> whatever beautiful face
> and whatever name he bore.
>
> They are not other than me
> in love.
>
> I came before them,
> in the ancient nights.
>
> These people are nothing but me
> in their love for her.
>
> I merely appeared to them
> clothed in every form:
>
> now as Qays,
> now as Kuthayyir,
>
> now as Jamil,
> the lover of Buthayna.
>
> Under all those names
> it was really I who was named.
>
> I was the one appearing to myself
> through selves that disappeared.
>
> I have never ceased to be her,
> and she has never ceased to be me.
>
> There is no difference:
>
> my own being
> loved itself.

### The Strength of the Sufi

> My soul went on feeding itself with longing,
> erasing power through weakness
>
> until weakness itself
> became strength.
>
> There I found all beings
> united in declaring
>
> that they were branches
> of a trunk that was mine.
>
> With one thought
> I swept across the horizons,
>
> and with one step
> I pierced the seven heavens.
>
> The bodies of those
> in whom no remnant of self remained
>
> became light as spirits
> in my gathering.
>
> Whoever spoke,
> whoever rose,
>
> whoever displayed power,
> did so through a subtle thread of strength from me.
>
> No one ever walked on water,
> flew through the air,
>
> or entered fire
> except through my spiritual force.
>
> Whoever received from me
> even one subtle thread
>
> could act upon his whole gathering
> in a moment.
>
> If the slightest subtle force from me
> were to enter a dead man,
>
> his soul would return
> and he would live again.
>
> Such is the soul:
>
> when it casts away its desires,
> its powers multiply,
>
> until every atom
> yields its strength to it.

### All Religions Equal in Truth

> If revelation lights
> the prayer niche of a mosque,
>
> the Gospel likewise blesses
> the sanctuary of a church.[^ms-v1-030]
>
> And there are the books
> of Moses’ Torah,[^ms-v1-031]
>
> with which the rabbis commune
> every night.
>
> And if a devotee bows before stones
> inside the Buddhist temple,[^ms-v1-032]
>
> there is no ground
> for sectarian condemnation.
>
> Through me the warning reached
> everyone who sought it,
>
> and through me
> every community has its excuse.
>
> The eyes of no religion
> have gone astray,
>
> nor the thoughts
> of any sect.
>
> The Magians worshipped fire,
> and for a thousand ages it did not go out,
>
> as the old accounts tell us.
>
> Yet they intended none but me—
>
> even when they thought
