> they intended someone else,
>
> and even when they themselves
> did not know the intention in their hearts.

Ibn al-Farid is saying what all the great Sufi poles say:

Muslims, Christians, Jews, Magians, Buddhists, and pagans are **all in the right!**

### *The Interpreter of Desires*

Muhyi al-Din Ibn Arabi dedicated this poem to a young woman named Nizam. He speaks of his longing for her in a way that shows both Sufi abstraction and poetic embodiment, and from there moves into his doctrine of the unity of religions, his rejection of religious conflict, and his view of the pilgrimage.

I have rearranged the poem—cutting some lines and moving others—so that it comes closer to the Sufi sensibility:

> O doves among the trees and branches,
> be gentle.
>
> Do not double my sorrow
> with your grief.
>
> Be gentle.
> Do not reveal through mourning and tears
>
> the hidden force of my passion,
> the grief buried inside me.
>
> Who will give me back
> the gathering at al-Muhassab in Mina?
>
> Who will give me Dhat al-Athl?
> Who will give me Nuʿman?
>
> Hour after hour
> she circles my heart,
>
> in longing and torment,
> kissing all its corners,
>
> just as the best of messengers
> circled the Kaaba—
>
> though the proof of reason itself
> would call that House deficient.
>
> He kissed its stones
> though he could speak,
>
> and what is the rank of a house
> beside the worth of a human being?
>
> The spirits lamented
> in the grove of gazelles.
>
> The branches bent over me
> until I too was undone.
>
> I lay fallen
> between breastbone and heart.
>
> What a wonder:
> a garden in the middle of fire.
>
> Before today
> I used to reject my companion
>
> if his religion
> was not close to mine.
>
> But now my heart
> has become open to every form:
>
> a pasture for gazelles,
> a monastery for monks,
>
> a temple of fire,
> a Kaaba for the pilgrim,
>
> tablets of Torah
> and the pages of the Quran.
>
> I follow the religion of Love.
>
> Wherever Love’s caravan turns,
> that is my religion
>
> and that is my faith.

This is one of the most important poetic texts in the whole tradition.

We read it for what it says, not for its poetry as such. As I noted earlier, Ibn Arabi’s poetic gift is not as spontaneous or overflowing as that of his contemporary Ibn al-Farid. Ibn al-Farid was a true professional poet.

Ibn Arabi writes poetry the way scholars do.

---

### Sufi Figures

In *Shadharāt al-Dhahab*, under the events of 386 AH, we read of Abu Talib al-Makki:

> He came from the mountain region and settled in Mecca, which is why he became known as al-Makki.
>
> He practised severe spiritual discipline. It was even said that for a time he gave up ordinary food altogether and lived on wild plants that were lawful to eat, until his skin turned green.
>
> He came to Baghdad and preached to the people, but began saying strange and confused things, and they abandoned him.
>
> Muhammad ibn Tahir al-Maqdisi says in his book on genealogies that when Abu Talib al-Makki entered Baghdad, people gathered around him. He began mixing things together in his speech, and one statement in particular was remembered:
>
> “Nothing is more harmful to the creature than the Creator.”
>
> After this, people abandoned him and he stopped preaching.

Abu Talib al-Makki is normally classified as a **Sunni Sufi**.

But Sufis resemble one another in their ecstatic excesses because they all return to the same source.

Even a Sunni, believing Sufi remains closer to a deist Sufi—or even an atheist Sufi—than he is to the jurist.

#### Abu Madyan Frightens the Sultan

According to *al-Istiqsāʾ*, Abu Madyan Shuʿayb ibn al-Husayn settled in Bejaia toward the end of his life.

Large numbers of people gathered around him, and miracles were attributed to him.

Some of the formal religious scholars denounced him to Yaʿqub al-Mansur.

They told the ruler:

> We fear for your state because of this man. He resembles the Mahdi, and he has followers in every country.

Yaʿqub wrote to the governor of Bejaia ordering him to send Abu Madyan to him. He instructed the governor to treat him with care and not transport him like an ordinary prisoner.

Abu Madyan died on the journey near Tlemcen in 594 AH.

#### The Madness of the Sufi

Ibn Sabʿin:

> Do not be afraid of your madness at this stage.
>
> It is a more complete world.

#### On the Search for Truth

Ibn Sabʿin:

> I seek refuge in God from an aspiration that stops.
>
> Indeed, I seek refuge in God from a mind that is ever satisfied.

#### The Essence of the Human Being

Ibn Arabi:

> O white pearl,
> ruby-bright,
>
> mounted in a shell
> of human flesh.
>
> The people of the earth,
> miserable in their ignorance,
>
> failed to know its worth
> while fighting over pearls and rubies.

#### Beyond Books

Ahmad ibn Abi al-Hawari threw his books into the sea and said to them:

> You were excellent guides.
>
> But once a person has arrived,
> remaining occupied with the guide is impossible.

Hallaj said:

> Whoever points **toward** Him is merely a would-be Sufi.
>
> Whoever points **from** Him is a Sufi.

And:

> My frame is body,
> my inmost core is light.
>
> My spirit belongs to the Eternal,
> the knowing Judge.
>
> Through spirit it rises
> to the lords of spirit,
>
> while the bodily frame remains behind
> as dust and decay.

#### The Path at the End

Al-Qushayri relates that Abraham once received a Zoroastrian as a guest.

Abraham told him:

“If you become a Muslim, I will feed you.”

The Zoroastrian replied:

“What favour would you then have done me?”

And he left.

God then revealed to Abraham:

> Abraham, would you feed him only if he changed his religion?
>
> We have been feeding him for seventy years while he remained an unbeliever…

(p. 63.)

See what we wrote earlier in the Orbits about the Sufi’s universalism and his equality between religions.

---

### Reports of the Communalists

Al-Jahiz relates in *The Book of Animals*, on the authority of al-Asmaʿi:

> An elderly man from Medina told me:
>
> “This land originally belonged to people who first developed it and dug its channels.
>
> “When the fruit ripened, one of them would say:
>
> “‘Open the garden so that passers-by may eat from it, and so that anyone in need can take some.’
>
> “Then he would say:
>
> “‘Send so much to this person, and so much to that person.’
>
> “And after the fruit had been sent, he might say:
>
> “‘Send this many dinars to so-and-so, and that many to someone else.’
>
> “At that point the steward would cry out:
>
> “‘What are you doing, man? Damn you!’
>
> “Then the land was fully developed and became rich.
>
> “It was granted to a different set of people.
>
> “Now every one of them walls off his garden and hides its gate.”

The story points to a struggle between two tendencies: the communal tendency and the feudal one.

The right of ordinary passers-by to take fruit from a garden was recognised in religious law, and the owner was not permitted to prevent it.

One Syrian form of this was the **Garden of the Jar**, which survived until the beginning of this century. It disappeared only with the arrival of the capitalist economy under the French.

A large jar—something like an enormous storage pot—would be placed at the entrance to the garden and filled with its fruit. When the garden gate was shut, passers-by could take what they needed from the jar.

Unfortunately, Iraq never developed this kind of garden because of the destruction of social values inflicted on it during the Ottoman period.

We will restore the principle in a more complete form:

**by making the whole state communal.**

#### The Kaaba of Najran

Before Islam, the Christians of Najran maintained an institution that provided free food and lodging both to the people of Najran and to travellers who came there.

Al-Aʿsha mentioned it in one of his poems:

> I visit Yazid and Abd al-Masih,
> and Qays—the finest of its lords—
>
> and the Kaaba of Najran,
> which calls you onward
>
> until at last
> you reach its gates.

The Arab Christians remained closer to the memory of Christ.

The European Church had not yet come between them and him.

### Address, Glad Tidings, and the Announcement of the Time

**Muhammad ibn Abd al-Jabbar al-Niffari**

He made me stand before Him and said:

Tell the night: Do not become morning. You will not return after this. I will bring up the sun from where it vanished beyond the earth and hold it back from moving. It will burn whatever once sheltered in your shade and make plants grow without water. I will reveal them from every side. I will let the beasts graze on your growth, while My own growth rises tall and beautiful. Its eyes will open and they will see Me. I will make My case, and they will write down My proof through their faith.

The towering mountain will split from its base, though the waters had once stood upon its summit and it would not drink. I will lower the bed of the water. I will stretch out the noonday heat and not let afternoon follow it.

There they will gather, and I will overturn every vessel.

You will see the bird roaming freely in its own nest. You will see the one at rest purchase wakefulness with sleep, and ransom war with peace.

And He said to me:

Tell the one spread wide and stretched out:

Prepare yourself for your rule. Adorn yourself for your station. Veil your face with something translucent. Keep company with the one who shelters you with his own face, for you are My face rising from every face.

Take faith as the pledge of your covenant.

When you go out, enter into Me, until I kiss you between the eyes and whisper to you what no one but you should know. I will go out with you onto the road, and your companions will be adorned as though they were hearts without bodies.

When you are firmly upon the road, stop there, for that is your destination.

Thus says the Lord:

Stretch out your right hand and raise your banner with it.

Do not sleep and do not wake until I come to you.

Servant, stand for Me. You are My bridge and the stairway of My remembrance. Across you I pass to My companions.

I have set you in place and cast over you a shoulder of wind. I want to bring forth My knowledge that has never yet gone forth, array it as an army, and send them across you. They will follow what lies beyond you without a road.

I will reveal it, and you will not know where it came from: whether it stood before them or arose along their path.

When you see Me, move, and they will move with you. I will set you upon My hand.

Let everything pass behind you.

Whoever crosses over you, receive him and carry him.

Whoever goes around you is utterly lost.

Servant, stand within the Law, for I have made you stand there.

Leap to avenge your longing as a starving lion leaps upon its prey.

Rise and reach through Me. Seek Me through My sustaining power in everything you attain.

Whoever sees Me sees what neither appears nor hides.

Servant, your time has come.

Gather My people around you. Store My treasures with the keys I have given you.

Hold firm, and firmer still. You have reached the threshold of your full strength.

Appear before Me in what I have made you appear in.

Speak of Me through My merciful grace, so that whoever hears you remember Me will love Me.

Thus says the Lord:

I am rising over the horizon, smiling.

They will gather around Me. The weak will ask Me for help. All of them will put their trust in Me.

I will send out My light, walking among them. They will greet it, and it will greet them.

Wake, sleeping one, and rise.

And you who are already standing, move toward what lies before you.

Cast your stars against the houses. Make the Pole grow beneath your finger.

Put on the ascetic robe of the Truth, and hold nothing back.

Rule belongs to you, and the staff of blessing is in your right hand.

This is what I want, and I am witness to it.

These are the lights of God. Who can take light from His light except by His permission?

This is the Truth.

Conjecture cannot grasp you through Us, and none disputes it except the ignorant.

Thus says the Lord:

Come forward. Do not turn back.

I will string the necklace for you.

Go out to the earth by My hand, and they will see Me with you and ahead of you.

Come out from behind your curtain, for I am bringing the sun up over you.

Take your destiny in your right hand.

Find your way as the winds do.

Arm yourself with the mercy that came before all things.

Do not sleep. I have lengthened your dawn, and morning has drawn close.

This is one of the signs of your Lord.

It concerns the descent of Jesus son of Mary from heaven to earth. A near time will announce it: a sign for those who have been given knowledge, and a guidance by which God guides people toward Him.

You will rescue many who do not know.

Thus says the Lord:

I have told you this so that right conduct may appear.

Lift the veils from your face.

Mount the creature that wanders the earth. Raise up My buried foundations. Carry them to Me in your own hands.

Whoever stands with you is on the right.

Whoever opposes you is on the left.

Wake, sorrowful one.

Come forth, hidden one.

Gather up your clothes and throw your wrap across your shoulder.

I will carry you across every mountain pass.

Spread yourself wide as the full moon and the sea.

Rise like the high sky.

I will send fire ahead of you. It will spare nothing and settle nowhere.

In this is a sign: the Word of God will appear, and God will reveal His friend upon the earth. He will take the friends of God as his allies, and the believers will pledge allegiance to him in Mecca.

Those are the beloved of God.

God will support them, and they will support Him.

His companions will be the entrusted guardians, equal in number to those who witnessed Badr.

They will act and be true:

three hundred and thirteen.

They are the ones who will prevail.

Thus the Lord made me stand before Him and said:

Tell the sun:

Sun, written into the order of the Lord, uncover your face. Unfold yourself from your folds.

Travel wherever your joy triumphs over your sorrow.

Send the moon ahead of you. Let the fixed stars gather around you.

Travel beneath the clouds. Rise over the depths of the waters.

Do not set in the west and do not rise in the east.

Stand still for the shade.

You are nothing but the Lord's mercy, a portion He sends to whomever He wills.

This is God's guidance. He guides by it whomever He wills.

This is how God sends down revelation.

Turn, you who have remained where you are.

Be at peace, hidden one.

I have let go the reins, and the Lord has sent His secret counsel ahead of you.

Thus says the Lord:

Rise, shining sun. I have stripped away the night.

Spread yourself over everything.

The crops will grow, and every tree will give its fruit by permission of its Lord.

The orphan will come out to you and grow tall.

The callers will gather around you.

You will see how My light blossoms.

Take your people with you, you who are setting out.

Prepare for the journey.

You are the light of the Lord.

The Lord has said:

You are to establish just rule among the people.

The hearts of the believers will lean toward you.

Through you the weak will grow strong and defend themselves against what they fear.

Sleeping one, come and wake.

Receive the good news.

I have sent down the table, and springs of food and drink have burst from it.

They will come to you and stand to your right and left. They will become your helpers, and they will prevail, because whoever fights them fights Me.

And I am the hearts.

Make room, you who have been confined. I have released you from captivity and opened the doors before you.

Adorn yourself, and adorn the peoples with My splendour.

I have taken sorrow away from you and filled your heart with joy.

They will line up in a single row for My coming, and I will come with clear proof.

Do not be stunned. Do not lose your way.

After this I will disappear only once.

Then I will appear and never disappear again.

You will see My ancient friends rise and rejoice.

And He said to me:

My time has come. The appointed hour of My appearance is near.

I will reveal Myself.

The weak will gather around Me and grow strong through My strength.

I will feed them and give them drink, and you will see their gratitude to Me.

Rise, sleeper.

Sleep, you who stand.

I have made calamity the captive of consolation.

I have sent down My guidance, My light, My pillar, and My signs.

Then He said to me:

Set up the couches for Me.

Spread the earth before Me with habitation and flourishing life.

Raise the hanging curtains for My arrival.

I am coming out, and My companions are with Me.

I will raise My voice. The callers will come and ask Me to shepherd them, and I will protect them.

Blessing will descend.

The tree of abundance will grow upon the earth.

Rule will belong to Me alone.

That is the measure.

That is what I desire.

---

### From Kurdish Sufism

The Kurds are an ancient people who have inhabited the mountain region since very early times. In a later period the region became known by their name, Kurdistan. Muslim geographers had called it either the Mountain Region or the Land of the Kurds, the latter being precisely what *Kurdistan* means.

The Kurds never established a unified state across their whole region because of its mountainous geography. The terrain makes it extremely difficult to create a central government able to extend its authority across areas separated by severe natural barriers.

This did not, however, prevent the Kurds from remaining a single people, preserving shared characteristics, qualities, and values.

The Kurdish language survived in this politically divided environment and remained one language despite its different dialects. In this respect it resembles Arabic and Chinese, both historically and today. Some dialects became so distant from one another that mutual understanding grew difficult without a common standard language, like Standard Arabic or Putonghua in China.

The Kurds have not yet developed a single standard language that can serve everyone and become a common language of culture in the way Standard Arabic and Putonghua have done.

Because of their tribal and mountain-based social order, the Kurds did not absorb the cultural currents of the Islamic age deeply enough to produce a learned culture of their own on the same scale as the Persians and Turks.

Kurdish literature therefore remained oral until the eleventh Islamic century, the seventeenth century CE, when Ahmad Khani launched what may be called the first cultural revolution in Kurdish history.

He wrote down the old Kurdish epic *Mem and Zin* in Kurdish. Until his time it had been transmitted orally. He then reshaped it through his own poetic imagination and Sufi culture, so that the work bears the personal creative mark of a major writer.

Khani opened the epic with an impassioned discussion of the Kurdish question, perhaps the first of its kind in Kurdish history.

He asked:

Who made the Kurds a people deprived, ruled, and enslaved as a whole?

He spoke of their virtues: their courage, honour, noble character, and love of what is good. He argued that they did not deserve the deprivation, division, and foreign occupation under which they lived.

He called on the Kurds to revolt, on condition that they joined the sword to virtue.

The world, he said, is like a bride. You win her only through the power of the drawn sword—when, that is, a stronger rival is competing with you for her and trying to seize her from you.

The first instrument of revolution was Kurdish unity.

The Kurds were known for the instability of relations among their tribal groups, a weakness that continues to shape the course of Kurdish revolution and helps prevent it from reaching its goal.

Arab historians noticed this as well. Al-Qifti said that the Kurds were a great nation and that, were it not for their internal feuding and fighting, they would have prevailed across the lands.

Khani called for the Kurds to unite under a central leadership, liberate their country, and build their state.

Khani also said that he wrote the epic partly to rouse the Kurds and partly to answer people who claimed that Kurds knew nothing of passionate love—that among them there was neither a heart capable of love nor a beautiful woman worth loving.

Passionate love is one of the great orbits of Sufism, as we have seen throughout this book. It is one of the famous mysteries of the Sufi path.

Khani's purpose, however, went well beyond proving that Kurdish hearts were tender and Kurdish women beautiful. He wanted to establish a written Kurdish culture capable of standing alongside Persian and Arabic culture.

He succeeded in both his project and his revolution.

Over the last two centuries Kurdish literature and writing have poured forth in a great stream. A Kurdish lexicon and Kurdish technical vocabulary have taken shape. Kurds have become able to deal with every field of knowledge in their own language, after long depending on Arabic and Persian for culture and literature.

Khani wrote in the Arabic script and adapted it successfully to Kurdish.

The move by some Kurds today toward the Latin alphabet does not, in my view, arise from any defect in the Arabic script. It belongs instead to the current fashion for imitating everything European.

It would have been better for the Kurds to return to their old script, develop it, and continue writing in it.

Ancient peoples do not lightly abandon their writing systems. The Japanese, Chinese, and Koreans continue to write in their own systems; Tibetans in Tibetan script; Mongols in Mongolian; Armenians in Armenian; and Arabs in Arabic.

And so on.

Sufism spread through Kurdistan as part of the Islamic culture written in Arabic. Some of its great masters established their lodges in the Kurdish mountains.

The best known was Adi ibn Musafir, who lived among the Īzīdīs, an old esoteric community whose name was altered in a later period into Yazīdīs.

Adi had an influence on their veneration of Satan, whose standing had already been rehabilitated by certain Sufis.

Sufi orders appeared in Kurdistan during the Ottoman age of dervishism, which followed the end of the great age of the Sufi masters.

Some of these orders nevertheless adopted Kurdish national demands.

Even in the age of dervishism, Sufism retained positive elements capable of inspiring action toward just ends. The Bektashi order played such a role in Albania, and the Sanusi order in Libya.

In Kurdistan this burden was taken up by the Naqshbandi order, which led the Kurdish revolution from the 1930s through the 1970s through the Naqshbandi master Mulla Mustafa Barzani.

After Mulla Mustafa, the Kurdish revolution in southern Kurdistan—Iraqi Kurdistan—grew weaker when leadership passed to the party he had founded to give the revolution an organisational structure.

This was a new and imported kind of party that did not express Kurdish traditions. Its leaders were educated in the West, loyal to the global capitalist system, and did not possess the hearts of the Sufis: their firmness and courage, humanised by love and by a transparent sense of humanity.

Ahmad Khani was a Sufi master of the dervish age.

He knew the different schools of Sufism thoroughly and absorbed them deeply. When he wrote his epic, he seemed almost to be taking up a broken line that had ended with Abd al-Karim al-Jili in the ninth Islamic century.

Much of his Sufism, however, was indebted to the Persian Sufis who came after al-Jili.

Among them a rapturous Sufism of love had become dominant, well suited to the Persian passion for human beauty and the beauty of nature.

They were, for the most part, lovers. They wrote about divine love through human love, continuing the ecstatic tradition of Ibn al-Farid within the Persian landscape, whose beauty seems itself to speak in art.

Ahmad Khani appears more the student of Farid al-Din Attar and Hafez of Shiraz than of the philosopher Ibn Arabi.

Literature and an overflowing sensitivity to beauty dominate his work, just as beauty fills the Kurdish landscape: a landscape combining beauty with majesty—green plains, deep and imposing valleys, towering forest-covered mountains, and springs pouring clear running water.

The epic is full of Kurdish beauty expressed through art: song, music, and dance.

It presents scenes of extravagant celebration, overflowing with art, beauty, and excessive luxury, which can remind us of the Chinese epic *Dream of the Red Chamber*.

There may even be a kind of conversation between the landscape of some parts of China and that of Kurdistan: the interweaving of plain and mountain, greenery and height, deep valleys and flowing springs.

Khani's ecstatic aesthetics remain completely at home in a Sufi atmosphere.

He is faithful to his teachers and to his school, which for him becomes a way of sensing, remembering, and forming the spirit, all of it saturated with love.

He wrote the epic to prove that the Kurds were a nation of lovers, like the people for whom Abu Firas al-Hamdani spoke:

> We are a people whom dark eyes melt,
> though we ourselves can melt iron.

The epic contains the vocabulary of Sufi doctrine at the level of the great masters.

Ahmad Khani defended Iblis. He justified Iblis's refusal to bow before Adam on the ground that God had been premature in proclaiming Adam's rank before Adam had displayed any great deed that would justify such elevation.

Iblis, for his part, refuses to bow to anyone but God—that is, he refuses to bow to a human being.

Yet in another chapter of the epic, Khani asks God to leave Iblis in the Fire forever so that humanity may be redeemed through him: let Iblis enter the Fire alone and no human being enter it.

Khani begins from the love of creation and compassion for people on which Sufism was founded.

He sees no distinction among human beings on account of their beliefs:

> All creation, from first to last,
> believer and unbeliever alike,
> has become a manifestation
> of Your greatness and Your lordship.

Like the Sufis before him, he says that the whole of existence has been placed at the service of humanity, and that the human being is the centre and purpose of the world.

He also embraced the unity of being, addressing the Truth:

> You cannot occupy a place,
> yet You dwell within Your creatures.

This is divine indwelling in existing things, as Zhuangzi also said.

Khani's use of the word *dwelling* here is new within the Sufi literature of the unity of being.

God also appears as light in beautiful faces and as fire in the hearts of lovers.

It is God's beauty that adorns the faces of beloved women and draws lovers toward them, because His heavenly attraction is embodied in them.

It was God who afflicted Khosrow with the love of Shirin, Qays with the love of Layla, and Mem with the love of Zin.

Khani classified young male lovers as mirrors of the beauty of the Divine Essence, and beloved women as manifestations of the lights of the Divine Attributes.

On this basis he rejected same-sex love, because in his scheme love is a relation between Essence and Attributes, not between Essence and Essence or between Attributes and Attributes.

He also made spirituality a condition of true love.

This is what separates the lover from the mere pleasure-seeker.

Some people who claim to love are looking for an advantage. They are not lovers.

Others seek union with the beloved. These are lovers of the body.

Still others choose suffering: a spiritual torment that becomes their true bliss.

That is Sufi love.

Those who possess it are the gnostics.

On the basis of this classification, Khani made the love of Mem and Zin a love belonging to the next world rather than this one.

Here the epic introduces an element of theological compensation, and Ahmad Khani falls into it because, despite his Sufism, he maintained his connection to religion.

After describing the hardships inflicted upon Mem and Zin by the ruler who separated them and imprisoned Mem, Khani opens a new chapter for them in the afterlife.

There they marry and live together in the bliss of paradise.

He even makes the plots of the villain Bakr serve the purpose of delivering them into the next world, after love has raised them upward and carried them to heaven.

In the course of this, Khani gives a religious interpretation of Bakr's killing at the hands of Tajdin, Mem's friend, comparing it to the killing of the boy in the story of Moses and al-Khidr.

But the Sufis do not place the two cases on the same level.

Bakr is a villain and conspirator, an agent of the state.

The boy in the story of al-Khidr is innocent. Al-Khidr kills him so that he will not become a cause of corruption for his parents.

The killing of the boy cannot be justified by any law except a religious law that measures the value of human life according to righteousness and belief.

Khani moved a long way from the social line of Sufism when he spoke without discomfort of the beautiful princess Zin, served by a hundred attendants while another hundred maidens dressed and adorned her.

What he ought to have spoken of was the poor Kurdish girl who owns nothing with which to cover her body or satisfy her hunger.

This came from the influence of Persian Sufism, with its ecstatic love and its aesthetics so close to the splendour of palaces and so distant from the suffering of the hungry.

Still, Khani did not entirely abandon the Sufi doctrine of poverty.

He wrote against inheritance, regarding the heir as someone who possesses wealth he did not labour to acquire. He called on the wealthy to spend their money on good works rather than pass it on as inheritance.

Whatever the case, I am not asking Ahmad Khani to travel centuries backward and become another al-Hallaj or al-Kilani.

He grew up in the age of Ottoman dervishism. When he tried to free himself from it, he turned to Persian Sufism and became one of the lovers.

What distinguished him was his national consciousness.

He raised the banner of revolt against those who had seized his country and enslaved his people.

He is therefore the father of the Kurdish revolution just as he is the father of Kurdish culture.

The Kurds owe him a debt greater than the crushing debts they continue to pay every day to their leaders.

A successful Arabic translation of *Mem and Zin* has been produced by the Kurdish disciple Jan Dost, son of Shaykh Mulla Bashir Shaykh Salih.

He introduced it with a short study of the author and the epic, and added notes explaining difficult meanings and symbols that show his strong command of the Sufi tradition.

**Zin** is pronounced with a clear *z* and an *i*-vowel.

#### Tree

**Adonis**

> Two stars fell.
>
> Above the traveller's head
> a cloud passed.
>
> He bent down

>
> A palm tree falls,

> within the fence

#### The Star's Insomnia — Exile

A poem by **Abd al-Ilah al-Yasiri**, a poet from Iraq.

A traveller on the path of steadfastness.

His conscience has four great preoccupations:

the state, colonialism, the turban, and the intellectuals.

He is an active fighter in our communalist field.

He supports taking over state palaces and hotels in order to house the poor.

Like Abd al-Qadir al-Jili, he believes that **“the comfort of creation”** is the root of everything.

He wants to take possession of Iraq so that he can distribute it among its hungry.

He lives by the work of his own hands.

> Others drink from cups
> filled to the brim with wine.
>
> My own cup holds
> labyrinths and grief.
>
> Tired eyelids
> find their rest in sleep.
>
> My eyes find rest
> in the company of stars.
>
> If only my cares
> were like other people's:
>
> possessions,
> swords,
> money,
> office,
> profit.
>
> My heart melts among them
> though they have hardened,
>
> as a lamp melts
> in the depth of night.
>
> I almost weep for them
> whenever they laugh.
>
> I almost laugh at them
> whenever they mourn.
>
> Against the wind,
> by some enchantment,
> without a compass,
>
> I travel where there are
> no ships
> and no sailor.
>
> Alone amid the uproar of night,
> forever silent:
>
> when will I reach morning?
>
> When will the night
> give way to dawn?
>
> I am the stranger
> with a wound
>
> that union with the beloved
> cannot quiet
>
> and no surgeon
> can heal.

Today I received a card from him for the birthday of Jesus.

He wrote:

My sorrow and pain—not congratulations and not joy—on the birthday of our teacher, which the world celebrates by crucifying him all over again.

What can a grieving lover like me offer on this yearly catastrophe except flowers draped in black and steeped in tears?

His birth has been turned into an annual death of the spirit, an excuse for more profit in trade and more pleasure for the body.

Forgive my madness and the strange rhythm in which I move.

I have nothing to console me in what I am going through except my cup, which I drank to the voice of the Beloved.

I still drink from it.

I have not sobered from its wine, the wine that set me alight in this frozen world around me.

And while I was drunk, our teacher called to me and made me weep.

He said:

“What reward is there in loving someone who loves you?”

I answered:

“In him I have gathered all humanity, because he alone is still alive among these graves.”

Then he wept, and the light around me disappeared...

#### The Drunkard

> Drunken one,
> grow still more awake
> through drunkenness.
>
> Become alert.
>
> Buy the cup
> with everything you own
> in this world,
>
> and gain through it
> honour and splendour.
>
> Discover in the wine
> the meaning within you
> that reason lost.
>
> Reach the depth
> that revealed itself in you
> and reached its furthest point.
>
> Sip from it
>
> and see the light
> enthroned within you
> as God.
>
> Erase from yourself
> every attribute,
>
> every doubt
> and every confusion.
>
> Merge into its spirit
> through love,
>
> for you are nothing
> but that spirit.
>
> Lose yourself in Him
> through Him:
>
> in ecstasy,
> sorrow,
> and sighs.
>
> You came to earth
>
> so that silence
> might grow lips through you;
>
> so that you might hold
> water inside stone
> for the trees;
>
> so that you might clothe
> a bird in safety
> and in dreams.
>
> Be nothing
> but what you choose to be,
>
> in your own choosing
> and your own direction.

#### Prisoner of Water

**Riyad al-Numani — an Iraqi communalist**

> Beneath the pearls of night
> in my master's garden,
>
> O father of the lily,
>
> the daughter of light slept,
>
> the third...
>
> seven lilies
> in the blue.
>
> All the gazelles of the earth
> gathered
>
> to breathe her in,
>
> rippling into perfume,
>
> tracing tiny patterns
> from mysterious letters
>
> with no dots upon them.
>
> The rose tore open its clothes.
>
> I broke
> every jar of wine.
>
> Silence cried out.
>
> Drunkenness surged.
>
> The Absolute went mad.
>
> My God...
>
> save me.
>
> I cannot bear cities.
>
> You prolong
> Your coyness in me.
>
> You turned abundance
> against me,
>
> and I became like someone
> imprisoned
>
> beneath a boat.

### The Final Clause

**By the Author**

> The morning stars disappear
> quickly before the face of the sun,
>
> for the life of a star is limited.
>
> And even to the one
> who is certain of the sun,
>
> the life of the sun is limited.
>
> In the Book of Certainties:
>
> whoever seeks some possession
> on the earth
> will find it.
>
> The deprived questioner
> is the one who asks about something
> and receives no answer.
>
> Through negation
> he sees things.
>
> Certainty is a sultan
> who sees heresy in negation.
>
> And sultans,
> when you fail to understand them,
>
> exile you.

So walk the roads of estrangement.

Do not submit to any depth.

Do not search for some distant end stored away in the Tablet, and do not write with any eyelid but your own.

Tablets have no memory.

There is no human being at the bottom.

The circle of the sky grows narrow when it is filled with anything other than thought: thought burning with the fire of ecstasy, kindled in the lands of care and intimate whispering.

Ecstasy contains hidden things that slip past all baggage.

The keeper of seals and power cannot see them.

Read them to him and he calls for the sword.

The poet, meanwhile, loses nothing of what he hopes for, because ecstasy is water mixed with fire.

With his eternal cup he fills the emptiness of every state.

It presses hard against the heart and forbids it everything other than the Beloved.

The heart says:

Sleep itself is an “other.”

So do not lose sight of the fire.

Your earthly heaven is adorned before you. The eye looks upon it and does not return humbled and defeated, as it recoils from a well suddenly blazing with flame.

If not, then let the walls fall.

The balance of things can be read.

There are mountains on the earth that do not sway with it.

Things themselves sway with the people who inhabit them.

Beginnings enchant the beating heart.

Yet at the beginning there is no human companion.

Fire begins from its end.

The root of the earth has no house in the belly of the earth where it can settle.

And you have no permanent resting place beneath the rule of the sun.

Abu Yazid, even beside the House, knows neither the Lord of the House nor the House itself.

For the Lord has lost His throne and His water in the wilderness.

He has entered evening in the deserts of bewilderment, without angels, a stranger within His own kingdoms, as though the earth had never come to Him willingly, the wind had never humbled itself before Him, and the ages had never submitted.

Return, questioner.

Thought is rebellion.

Thought has its stations, separated from one another by the Book of Poverty, moving from one day to the next in full wakefulness like al-Khawwas, without wings and without strings.

You are fertile seed in your sacred deprivation, and all creation is spread open before you.

From the spring of true existence comes the letter, climbing the peaks of the names.

The names are something hidden in the conscience of the universe.

The one who intends knows their essence when he holds fast to negation.

He walks through the hidden places of the letter *tāʾ*, full as the sand of the sea.

The laws deny him.

Action does not know him.

At the root of civilisations he has a voice that calls out to the people:

No wage belongs to the one who builds.

No deed belongs to the one who strives.

No rule belongs to the protected.

To conquer the ten thousand is humiliation.

Laws are like the morning star.

They do not understand the meaning of night.

Night is latency.

Those outside it do not know what night is:

there, separation is joined;

union is broken;

and the letter *tāʾ* stands at the heart of beginnings like water abandoned by the wind, all its sides becoming one.

In the deep sea are dimensions whose root the traveller circles in the *suwayyira*[^ms-v1-033] of absolute nothingness.

Do not ask about forms when they appear.

Do not ask when they flee.

Today you are *insī*—a human companion—and from the same root comes *uns*, intimacy.

You emerged naked from creation.

Thought is a wanderer.

Faith has shades of grey.

Will you be satisfied with merely being human?

Absolute nothingness surrounds the earth.

It is that thing without which the earth would not turn, the winds would not run across its shoulders, the sun would not pass over it, and you yourself would never have been.

The secret of the earth is before you like a lamp lit with its eastern oil.

Thought is rebellion.

You are beyond the summons of everything other than the Truth, beyond the voice that calls:

**Be.**

The passivity of things does not apply to your being.

Fire moves toward the sun as one equal moves toward another.

Fire has no equal but the sun.

Body, you who concern yourself with creation:

the time has come for the fire to consume you.

Through fire you will see the Truth without shadow.

Beyond distance there will be no further distance.

Dimensions will disappear.

Distances will dissolve.

And through fire, thought becomes rebellion.

If every human being is drowned beneath the wave of silence,

you will not drown...

---

## Appendix

### Supreme Communalist Council of Iraq

## Programme of the Iraqi Communalist Movement

> “I am amazed that a person who finds nothing to eat in his own house does not go out against the people with his sword drawn.”
>
> **Abu Dharr al-Ghifari**

Immediately upon the collapse of the existing regime, the Council will launch a nationwide popular campaign to impose the following demands upon the new government.

This will form part of its broader struggle to establish a communalist state in Iraq.

1. A new ministry shall be created under the name **Ministry for the Communal Organization of Society**.

2. The ministry and its institutions shall be entrusted to compassionate people known for tender hearts and clean consciences, regardless of their political affiliations or religious beliefs.

   Its minister shall not receive a minister's salary, but only a salary sufficient to meet ordinary necessities, like any other citizen.

   The ministry shall not use expensive luxury cars, but cheap cars of the kind ordinary people use.

3. The ministry shall establish communal committees at neighbourhood, village, and city level to carry out the tasks included in its programme.

4. The ministry shall establish people's restaurants at city, neighbourhood, and village level, managed by the communal committees, to provide food either free of charge or at cost, according to what resources permit.

5. The ministry shall establish people's markets at the same levels to provide basic necessities.

   Goods shall be sold either at cost or with a maintenance charge of no more than one percent.

6. The Ministry for the Communal Organization of Society shall coordinate with the Ministry of Health to reorganise government hospitals so that their standards of care, cleanliness, and treatment equal those of private hospitals.

   For this purpose, the ministry shall appoint **communal monitors** to ensure that hospitals work as they should, that doctors and other medical staff fulfil their duties toward patients, and that patients' dignity is protected from abusive treatment by some hospital workers.

7. The ministry shall carry out a programme to build brick homes for peasants and the inhabitants of the marshes.

   It shall make use of Iraq's advanced brick industry and the long-standing skill of Iraqis in this craft, together with the ready availability of raw materials and cheap fuel from petroleum products.

8. The ministry shall establish **marriage committees** to help young women marry, drawing inspiration from Japanese and Muslim traditions in this field.

   These committees shall also deal with family problems, reconcile married couples, work against divorce, and prevent second marriages.

9. The ministry shall seek legislation preventing citizens from working as domestic servants.

   Service work in public institutions such as restaurants, hotels, and hospitals shall be organised in ways that guarantee the dignity of workers, including rules requiring respectful forms of address.

   The word **servant** shall be abolished, along with the job category of office servant in government and private institutions.

### Immediate Measures to Provide Housing for the People

1. The Council shall lead a popular campaign to take over state palaces and tourist hotels and use them to house citizens.

   It shall prevent the palaces from being distributed to intellectuals, writers, and politicians who dream of inheriting them from the people of the existing regime.

2. This shall be done through careful and well-planned measures ensuring that the palaces and hotels remain clean and that their beauty and artistic appearance are preserved.

---

The ministry shall carry out its programmes using the funds allocated to it in the general state budget.

The Council believes that the existing sources of public expenditure are sufficient for this purpose.

It therefore rejects the theories of those who tell the people that they must be patient and wait for development plans to bear fruit decades from now.

To provide adequate funds, the Council proposes a practical redistribution of state spending as follows:

1. The Ministries of Information and Culture shall be abolished, and their budgets transferred to the Ministry for the Communal Organization of Society.

   Abolishing the Ministry of Information will do no harm to official media, which are handled by radio and television, the news agency, and the press, just as they were before this ministry was created.

   As for culture, it developed in every civilisation and throughout every historical age without a Ministry of Culture.

   Writers and authors can publish their work through local and Arab publishing houses.

2. The Ministry of Labour and Social Affairs shall be merged into the Ministry for the Communal Organization of Society because the two operate in the same field.

   The budget of the abolished ministry shall be transferred to the new one.

3. In order to reduce public expenditure and transfer the surplus to the Ministry for the Communal Organization of Society:

   the Ministry of Higher Education shall be merged with the Ministry of Education;

   the Ministry of Agrarian Reform with the Ministry of Agriculture;

   and the Ministry of Trade with the Ministry of Economy.

   Consideration shall also be given to abolishing the Ministry of Municipalities and dividing its responsibilities between the Ministry for the Communal Organization of Society, the Baghdad municipal authority, and the municipal departments in the provinces, which shall be placed under provincial administration.

   The Ministry of Military Industrialisation shall be merged with the Ministry of Defence.

If the movement grows strong enough to take control of state power, it will be able to confiscate the presidential palaces and their contents and put them to use for the ministry's work.

It will also be able to cut the enormous expenditure devoted to the president, his family, his deputies, his entourage and their families; to the cabinet; and to ministers and their families.

The surplus will be transferred to the Ministry for the Communal Organization of Society.

As we have already explained, the public resources of the state—of any state—are sufficient to meet the goals of communal social organisation without waiting for long-term development plans.

The Council will, in any case, work to strengthen development within the limits of its resources and responsibilities.

It can also intervene to protect the public sector from corruption and turn it into a model of good management, strong performance, honesty, and integrity.

The Council shall organise the masses at different levels and arm them to defend their communal gains against class enemies within the state and society.

The Council shall remain in existence alongside the Ministry for the Communal Organization of Society in order to monitor its performance and mobilise the armed population against any attempt to stop or obstruct the ministry's work.

The Council shall coordinate and form alliances with trade unions and peasant organisations in order to fight their common battles against the same class enemy in both state and society.

---

Membership in the Council is open to all social activists regardless of political affiliation or religious belief.

Major merchants, large landowners, and members of aristocratic families shall not be admitted unless they surrender their property and place themselves on an equal footing with the people.

Preference shall be given to workers, peasants, ordinary wage earners, and craftspeople.

Written on the Nativity of Jesus, 1996

For the Founding Committee

**Hadi al-Alawi**

### A Word at the Edge of Annihilation

The communalist revolution ascends through the stations of the seekers, from the day of Jesus to the day of Johansson.

Its true homeland is here among us, after its tribal foundations dried up in the West.

Johansson was only an exceptional formula dropped by the Jesuit clouds onto the soil of Sweden—an accident, not a law.

The law is here, in the great continent of communalism, which produced the teachers of communist consciousness three thousand years ago.

At that time Europe was still moving along the road toward feudalism, then toward the dog-eat-dog capitalism called free competition, and finally toward monopoly capitalism, which moulds the Westerner in its own canine and predatory image.

To clear the road for the communalist revolution, translated culture must be swept away, along with its advocates and its vehicles.

With it must go the politics of both government and opposition: two faces united behind the dominant American model.

This means joining the communalist revolution to the national revolution for the liberation of the country from imperialism and from its mother, capitalism, together with all their dependants of every colour, taste, and class.

Speaking in the voice of al-Hallaj, I proclaim communal ownership of wealth and declare it an exclusive right of the people: its workers, peasants, displaced people, hungry, and sick.

Speaking in the voice of Saladin, I proclaim a nationalism defined by opposition to the West and its soldiers and politicians, while affirming our right to strike it by every painful means available to us.

Our enemy is the West, the one enemy of humanity.

Its most violent instrument in its continuing aggression against humanity is the United States, which is waging against us a war of extermination:

either it or us.

And it will be us.

Alongside the West stand our own rich: those inside the local state, around it, and attached to it.

The rich and the neighbours of the rich.

The state and its democratic institutions.

Its intellectuals, eaten hollow by spiritual emptiness, bodily materialism, and intellectual servitude.

A shoe offered by a peasant is worth more than all their libraries.

The Sufi has died at the front since the days of Ibrahim ibn Adham.

And he has lived among the poor since the days of Abd al-Qadir al-Jili.

### The Awaited Dawn of Communalism in the Sufi Heart

> My time has come.
>
> The hour has come for Me
> to uncover My face
> and reveal My glory.
>
> My light will reach the courtyards
> and what lies beyond them.
>

> will rise toward Me.

> because I do not repay him in kind.
>

>

>

>

> with the adornment of Truth.
>

> My justice—
> drives everything else away.
>

> into ease and plenty.
>

>
> I will bring out My treasure.
>

> of My news and My promise
> will be fulfilled.
>
> My rising has drawn near.
>
> I will rise.
>
> The stars will gather around Me.
>
> I will bring together
> the sun and the moon.
>
> I will enter every house.
>
> They will greet Me,
>
> and I will greet them...

**Muhammad ibn Abd al-Jabbar al-Niffari**

An awaited dawn.

A thousand years have passed since it was promised.

Its time has come to appear.

These orbits describe how it will appear.

Sufism is a project for social revolution just as it is an intellectual revolution: a breaking-open of accepted certainties...

## Footnotes

[^ms-v1-001]: His epithet *al-Jili* comes from al-Jil, in northern Iran, pronounced with a short *i* on the *j*. The form *al-Jilani* is also used. In the Ottoman period the name shifted to *Kilani*: with Persian *gāf* in Iraq and ordinary *kāf* elsewhere.

[^ms-v1-002]: The theologians and philosophers restricted themselves to the term *al-Bāriʾ*.

[^ms-v1-003]: I owe this beautiful term to the al-Barrās dictionary by Hani Labada, where he gives it as a synonym for Romanticism. If only all his terminology were this good.

[^ms-v1-004]: *Mirʾāt al-Zamān* remains in manuscript. A complete copy is held in the Feyzullah Efendi Library in Istanbul. I arrived in that great Islamic capital after fleeing Lebanon, where Saudi intelligence had been pursuing me after abducting my companion Nasser al-Saeed. I began consulting the manuscript of *Mirʾāt al-Zamān* in that library. Before I could finish its first volume, I was approached by a double Saudi-Iraqi intelligence agent. I fled the library and left the next day, returning to Syria. What I quote here from *Mirʾāt al-Zamān* therefore depends on Abd al-Rahman Badawi’s *Sufi Ecstatic Utterances*, cited in the text, p. 214; he took it from the Paris manuscript.

[^ms-v1-005]: Al-Hallaj’s condemned *Tawasin* has survived, while al-Junayd’s book opposing al-Hallaj has not. The two men belonged to the same generation. This is one piece of evidence that the loss of Islamic books did not follow a systematic distinction between approved and rejected doctrines; much of it was simply accidental. Among those accidents were burnings of books by Turkish powers, beginning with the Seljuks and the Turks of the later Abbasid age; by the Byzantines during their occupations of parts of Syria and Mosul; and by the Spaniards. Many different kinds of books disappeared in these burnings, approved and disapproved alike. More works of jurisprudence and literature were lost than works of philosophy.

[^ms-v1-006]: Islamic sources spoke of Pharaoh’s justice in matters of judgment and taxation and treated those matters as separate from his unbelief...

[^ms-v1-007]: Ibn Arabi’s statement that as long as a person is alive there remains hope that he can attain perfection, whereas killing him prevents him from reaching it, appears in another form in Mao Zedong. In *On the Ten Major Relationships*, Mao says that a human being is not like a tree: if his head is cut off, it does not grow back. The death penalty should therefore be approached with caution.

[^ms-v1-008]: This is the Marxist definition of **the people** as the totality of the working classes, unlike **the nation**, which includes everyone.

[^ms-v1-009]: The word *dialectic* became common during the Cultural Revolution on the tongues of Red Guards between roughly twelve and twenty years old. One of them claimed that he had used dialectics to produce an improved variety of potato. A Lebanese activist working with a Palestinian organisation once told me that he saw, above the desk of an official from South Yemen, a Soviet school report card. His grade in dialectics was 100 percent... a mark Karl Marx himself might have struggled to obtain in a university examination.

[^ms-v1-010]: This refers to a hadith fabricated by jurists forbidding people from probing too deeply into the essence of God. Ibn Arabi was not good at hadith criticism. But he did not simply stop at the prohibition; he used it formally as a way of throwing believers off the trail.

[^ms-v1-011]: By **anti-movement** I mean what prevailing usage calls **reactionary**, translating the English word *reactionary*. In this sense the usual Arabic translation is broken and alien, another lesson imposed on Easterners about their own sophisticated civilisation. The Chinese noticed the problem and used *fan-dong*, literally **against the movement**, for this term. The anti-movement type is the political criminal who lacks the resolve and courage of the activist. An anti-movement figure can be a progressive intellectual, while the activist may be an illiterate peasant.

[^ms-v1-012]: *On Conflict and Dispute* is an important short work by al-Maqrizi. By “the Black man” he means Ammar ibn Yasir.

[^ms-v1-013]: The existence of a religion called Taoism does not contradict my statement that China did not know religion in the same sense. Taoist religion has no daily ritual obligations or regular worship, and there is no population of adherents called “Taoists” in that sense. Individual Chinese people may visit its temples to make a vow or pray, but they do not do so periodically. A person may go only once in a lifetime, or never at all. Taoism also never developed a unified clerical institution.

[^ms-v1-014]: The Palestinian thinker Ahmad Barqawi once told me that Salman was a fabricated figure. I find this sweeping claim difficult to accept. Fabricated figures usually appear in disconnected reports; their lives do not form a continuous whole, and they contain gaps that cannot be filled. Historians did raise doubts about Uways al-Qarani and Abd Allah ibn Saba, but none of the major historians from al-Tabari to al-Dhahabi raised such doubts about Salman. My friend Ahmad’s imagination wandered too far...

[^ms-v1-015]: Ibn Kathir also says that the Tigris flooded that year, and that the people of Baghdad said it flooded because al-Hallaj’s ashes had mixed with its waters...

[^ms-v1-016]: The verb here was traditionally pronounced in the passive, although most people today pronounce it in the active. The difference is theological: in the passive, God is the one who lengthens a person’s life; in the active, the person himself lives it out.

[^ms-v1-017]: *Nashkūtiyyāt* is a term coined by Abd al-Salam Benabdelali from *nashʾa* (origination) + *kawn* (cosmos), for the study of the origin and formation of the world, corresponding to **cosmogony**. It differs from *kawniyyāt*, **cosmology**, the study of the universe and its problems after its formation.

[^ms-v1-018]: R. F. Christian, *Tolstoy: A Critical Introduction*, Arabic translation by Abd al-Hamid al-Hasan, Ministry of Culture, Damascus, 1983, p. 59.

[^ms-v1-019]: Christian, pp. 275–276. The *khafāfa* is the wooden last on which a shoemaker makes shoes.

[^ms-v1-020]: The Arabic poem uses *ḍawā*, a forced form in place of the normal *ḍāʾat* (“shone”). The poet altered the word so that its sound would fit the music of the metre.

[^ms-v1-021]: *Khānaqāh*: a Sufi lodge. The word is commonly explained as a compound of Turkish *khān*, meaning a place or premises, and Persian *gāh*, a suffix used to form names of places. It may instead be the plural of *khānak*, a Kurdish word meaning a hut, small house, or broken-down house of the kind poor people live in.

[^ms-v1-022]: Jurists fall into two groups: one functions as men of religion and the other as men of law, in the modern legal sense. Their doctrines differ according to which of these roles is more characteristic of them.

[^ms-v1-023]: The word appears once with *jīm* and once with *ḥāʾ*. The meaning is the same.

[^ms-v1-024]: This time of mine is a time of veiling. That is why I write. Yet I should not write. At the very moment I am writing, children are dying in Iraq for lack of food. I hold on to the name only so that it will survive and multiply again when the veil is lifted. My writing at this moment is itself the veil. My time is not the time for writing; it is the time of Husayn ibn Mansur. I should be there, not here inside this time. Writing is a state of estrangement; being there would be purity. Writing is contraction; being there would be expansion.

[^ms-v1-025]: From this standpoint, some of them said that the spirits of ordinary people are created while the spirits of the elect are uncreated. “Ordinary people” here does not mean commoners, but those outside the Sufi path.

[^ms-v1-026]: This view was repeated by an Iraqi opposition newspaper in the late 1940s, *al-Ahali*, associated with the nationalist leader Kamil al-Chadirji. The government shut the paper down. That same government established the public brothel and flooded Baghdad with nightclubs...

[^ms-v1-027]: Al-Jawahiri reinforced this misconception in the line, “The history of the revolution of thought tells us that a thousand Christs were crucified for it.” Christ’s revolution was the revolution of the unlettered common people, not a revolution of intellectuals.

[^ms-v1-028]: The original line reads: “Have mercy on a breast set ablaze by the fire of your love.” I changed it to suit the language of Sufism. The Sufi lover does not complain of the flame burning inside him. How can someone absent between Beauty and Majesty complain of love? That is what poets do.

[^ms-v1-029]: The original reads *fa-smaḥ* (“then permit”). I have adopted *fa-aẓhir* (“then reveal/show”), which better suits the Sufi.

[^ms-v1-030]: *Bīʿa*: a church. From Syriac.

[^ms-v1-031]: *Al-Kalīm*: Moses.

[^ms-v1-032]: *Al-budd*: a corrupted form of **Buddha**. Arabs used the word both for a Buddhist temple and for statues of the Buddha.

[^ms-v1-033]: *Suwayyira*: a diminutive of *sawra*, with the *w* doubled. It is the point where river water surges and curls back on itself for a time, until the current grows stronger and more forceful and collides with whatever blocks its flow. In the *suwayyira*, attraction and repulsion become one movement. Its centre is empty, and because of the power of that emptiness it swallows anything that comes near its mouth. *Sawra*, with a short *a* after the *s*, means a sudden violent rising and belongs to the same family as *thawra* (“revolution”), *fawra* (“surge, boiling”), and the English verb *soar*. I doubled the *w* against the normal rule to embody the thrusting force of the water at that critical point. The term is common along the banks of the Tigris and Euphrates. (Published in *Mawāqif*.)
